MATCH. HOMO SAPIENS (BASELINE). REFERENCE No. 12. Location of record: Upper House.

Location of sample, I wrote underneath, in my own hand: a sky-grazer, floating.

I sat down on his stone and did a thing I have done perhaps six times in eleven years. An Examiner doesn't suggest that the Register is wrong; she says it, formally, and the saying goes up the line to the Chief Examiner's desk with her name on it, and if she's wrong it stays on her record for the rest of her life.

I opened a Discrepancy.

In the matter of Reference No. 12, of Firstfield. The Register holds the subject transferred to the Upper House and present there. The world holds the subject's living trace present in the Spill, sector 40, as of this morning, confirmed by assay. Class: Unrecorded. Further classes pending. Case open.

Then I began to enter evidence.

One scrap of grey knit shirt, labelled PROPERTY OF THE MORIEL HERITAGE SERVICE. One sleeping hollow under a fur-tree, lined with shed fur. One reed stem, knotted eight times. Thirteen crusts of chewed seed. Eight flattened patches of grass and the bank between them. Seven tomato plants, baseline, red-stemmed, and one notched stick. The plated footprints along the stream and the pond, wherever they could still be found. One fallen sky-grazer on the bank below 40-K, bladder empty, as a control.

And the flock.

Every sky-grazer in the valley bearing the subject's trace, entered as a living exhibit, in situ, together with the roost and forage they depend on for their condition. I counted them twice from the bank as they stirred on the far slope, and got sixty-three both times, and wrote sixty-three. The Register asked me, very politely, if I wished to enter sixty-three exhibits under one number.

I said yes.

The Register thought about it for no time at all.

EVIDENCE ENTERED. Under open case. May not be destroyed, altered or cleared.

"Hask," I said. "I need a witness."

He had been waiting for this, I think, for a very long time. I held the slate against his pale mandate stone, worn smooth by centuries of other people's thumbs, and the Register recorded an Orrun Proxy's attestation. And Hask made the small grinding sound deep in his stack that is, in an Orrun, roughly equivalent to applause.

* * *

The burn crew came at nine. A day early.

I heard them first: the hum of a water tender along the farm track, and the clank of drip-torches coming off a truck, like saucepans having an argument. Somebody upstairs had heard overnight that an Examiner was sitting in a valley asking questions, and had brought the order forward. That was a plain fact too. I wrote it down.

I walked down to the cut to meet them. My coat, fished out of the stream at first light where it had snagged on a root, hung off me in damp folds with pond weed still in the pocket. My hair had dried in the shape of the inside of a stone. I smelled, quite strongly, of goat.

There were eleven of them in fire-orange, lined along the grain like a hedge of marigolds, and a crew boss, a lean man with burn scars like lace up both forearms and eyebrows that had been singed off and grown back thin and surprised. He had a drip-torch in one hand, and when he saw an Orrun cairn come out of the Spill behind me he looked from one of us to the other, working out which to address.

"Inspectorate," I said, and showed him the warrant. "Morning."

He read it. He sniffed, and tried to pretend he hadn't.

"Examiner," he said. "Order's approved. You'll want to stand back of the cut. We light at ten, wind permitting."

"You don't, I'm afraid."

"Ma'am, I've got a clearance order from the Heritage Service with the Register's seal on it—"

"So do I," I said. "A different one."

I held out my slate so he could see the case. He read it carefully, lips moving a little at the long words.

"Sixty-three exhibits," he said.

"Living."

"You've entered a flock of goats as evidence."

"Sky-grazers. In situ. Together with their roost and their forage." I pointed up the valley, where the flock still hung along the far slope like washing, beginning to stir in the sun. "The roost you're about to burn. Evidence under an open case may not be destroyed, altered or cleared. You can burn it, of course. And the Register will put your name on it, and mine, and the Service's, for longer than either of us is going to live."

He looked at the flock for a long moment.

"They're goats," he said, but not with much hope.

"They're the only place on Moriel where that boy is still on record as being anywhere at all," I said. "Look at your order."

He looked down at his slate: the same clearance order, the same seal, and under it a new line the Register had added on its own, without being asked, because it does not care which of us is right; it cares only that it agrees with itself.

SUSPENDED. Conflicts with open Discrepancy. Area contains evidence.

"Thirty years," said the crew boss slowly. "I've never once seen an order suspended."

"You've never had an Examiner sit in your valley."

Behind him, his crew had started to grin, the way crews do when the boss has been out-argued by somebody they'll never see again.

The crew boss handed the drip-torch to the man behind him without looking round. Then he looked at me, and his surprised eyebrows did something complicated.

"You do smell like a goat, you know," he said. "Ma'am."

"Evidence," I said.

He laughed. It came out of him unwillingly, like a cork, and then he couldn't stop it, and he shook his head and turned round and made a circling motion over his head with one scarred arm, and eleven people in fire-orange began, not at all reluctantly, to load the torches back onto the truck.

Hask stood beside me and watched them go, the tender whining off down the farm track in a long plume of gold dust.

"Examiner," he said. "I have been wondering what the Spill would have done, if it had been able to object."

"It doesn't get to. It's Unrecorded."

"Yes." The leaf between his seventh and eighth stones turned, very slightly, towards the sun. "I believe it has just done so anyway. Through you."

I didn't answer that. I unscrewed the flask, refilled that morning on the driver's spirit stove, and took a mouthful, and it was too hot, and I let it be.

"Item fourteen," said Hask, comfortably.

* * *

That evening I sat by the tomatoes.

The flock had come home at dusk and gone round the hollow twice looking for a boy who wasn't there, and settled. My grazer had wiped its face on me on the way past and gone to sleep with its chin on my boot. I had washed in the pond three times that day. I could still smell hay and wet wool and honey and vinegar on my wrist, and under it, very faintly, bread.

"Examiner," said Hask.

"Yes, Hask."

"I have considered." The grinding settled into silence. "I wish to declare it."

"The leaf?"

"Under the attachment. As an amendment." He said the word amendment the way other people say sunrise. "I would not have it said that the record was wrong about me, and I knew, and did nothing."

So I opened the attachment the customs officer at the spaceport had filed, Seed, provenance unknown, lodged in Proxy, and under it, on the slate in the last of the light, with Hask dictating at the speed of continental drift, I added: Now leaf. One. Pale. Folded like a ticket. Hask said the last part was not strictly necessary. I left it in. He let me.

He made the small grinding sound again, deep in his stack.

The stars came out over the ridge, one and then another and then a great many. Down at the stream the bells began. The seven tomato plants sat in their row in the dark, small and red-stemmed and doing nothing, with a notched stick at one end, and I thought that there are worse things to be the evidence of.

My slate chimed.

Not the sensor. The Register: the soft double note it makes when a record I'm following has changed. I picked it up expecting a filing note, my Discrepancy going up the line.

HOMO SAPIENS (BASELINE). REFERENCE No. 12. TRANSFERRED: UPPER HOUSE.

And then, as I watched, the last word of the line below dissolved and reformed itself, letter by letter, very neatly, the way the Register does everything.

STATUS: RETIRED.

I looked for the next line. The one that tells you where a retired thing goes.

There was no next line.

The timestamp said it had been entered eleven hours after I opened my case. Someone upstairs, a long way from this valley and these stars and these seven small red stems, had signed a form saying the boy was finished; and the Register, which has never in its life been uncertain about anything, had read the form, and found it in order, and serenely agreed.


## Chapter Five: Keep Moving

> CLAIM UC-114 (Ridge Undercroft, north face). Workings: suspended. Wall consolidant: BINDER line B-4, self-setting, sugar-fed; left in place. Biological status: none (subsurface; inert). Renewal: automatic. [scratched into a bolt-plate, in a different hand:] B4 HOLDS. KEEP MOVING.
>
> — Register of Mineral Claims, Margin district, entry unchanged since filing; and an anonymous miner
>

I wake up because a sky-grazer is breathing bread into my ear.

It's the one with the nicked wing. It has folded itself down in the moss so close that its soft blunt face is lying across my shoulder, and every breath it lets out is warm and yeasty and faintly sour, like the bakehouse with the door just opened. The other one is a little way off, legs tucked, float creaking as the morning warms it. The moss under me is wet with dew and still holds the shape of my landing, a long boy-shaped dent with a sigh at the bottom of it.

I lie there and don't move, because it's the best I've felt in ten days, and because everything hurts. My arms feel as if someone has tried to take them home. There's grazer down in my teeth.

The sky over the ridge goes from grey to the colour of weak tea, and then the sun comes up behind me and lays itself down the slope, and the floating seed starts to lift out of the valleys below like breath off a horse. Gold, rising. All over the far hills, little glittering clouds of it, going up.

The nicked-wing grazer lifts its head. It smells the seed before I see it. Its float gives a long low creak, filling.

That's when the grass starts.

I taste it before I understand it, the way you taste milk on the turn before you've thought the word sour. Down at the bottom of the slope, a ripple runs through the grass, sweet and quick on the front of my tongue: warm here. That's me. That's always me. But behind it, coming down from the ridge we flew over, passed blade to blade the whole long way, is the other thing. Fainter. Darker. Bitter at the back.

Warm. Moving.

I sit up. The grazers pipe, low and worried.

"It's alive," I say. I kicked it in the bulb and watched it fall off a cliff, and it's coming over the ridge, slowly, because it never hurries.

Something is, says the voice. I'd go somewhere.

"Where?"

It doesn't answer, because it doesn't know, and then the morning wind turns, and I find out where.

Cold air. Pouring up the slope out of nowhere, against the sun, smelling of a cellar. Not Gran's crock-under-the-stairs cellar: a deeper one, stone and wet and mushroom, the smell of the bottom step in the mill where nobody goes. I follow it with my face, the way a dog does, and twenty paces up the slope from where I landed there's a ring of fern I hadn't seen in the dark, and inside the ring of fern, the moss just stops.

It's a hole. A round black throat in the hillside, wider than our kitchen, with roots hanging down into it like unravelled rope. The cold is coming out of it in long slow breaths. In. Out.

I think it through, crouching at the edge with my teeth starting to go. The thing hates cold water: I watched it pull its foot out of the pond as if the pond had insulted its mother. It finds me through the grass. And down there, wherever down there goes, there's no sun, so there's no grass. Nothing to point.

The grazers are rising behind me. I hear their floats fill, a slow double creak like two people settling into chairs, and I turn round.

They're a hand off the moss already, hanging, legs unfolding. The seed is going up and they have to go with it.

"Go on," I tell them. "Go on, then."

The other one goes first, straight up into the gold, piping. The nicked-wing one hangs a moment longer. It drifts close and bumps my forehead with its soft nose, once, hard enough to rock me back on my heels. It smells of bread and of me. Then it rises after the other, turning slowly, up and up, until it's a pale speck in a glittering cloud, and then it's one pale speck among a lot of pale specks, way off over the valleys, and I can't tell any more which one is mine.

They aren't mine. I know that. I watch until my eyes water anyway.

Behind me, along the ridge, the grass is still passing it on. Warm. Moving.

I fill both trouser pockets with the fat white roots that grow round the fern, the ones that taste of wet bread and pennies. I take the reed stem out of my shirt and tie the tenth knot. Then I sit down on the lip of the hole, put my plated feet on the first of the hanging roots, and go down into the cold.

* * *

The light goes out like a door closing.

For a while there's only the roots in my fists, wet and stringy, and the cold climbing up my legs like water filling a bath. The circle of sky above me shrinks to a plate, then a coin, then a button. My daylight eyes are no use down here. They keep trying, which is worse; they keep making shapes out of nothing, faces, doorways, Ma.

So I use the rest of me.

The rock is cold and slick under my left palm, then, a little further down, it isn't. It's soft. Furred, like the inside of a mitten, and faintly warm, and when I leave my hand on it, something comes up through it into my wrist. A hum. Lower than the fur-trees, lower than the dome on its quietest night. I don't hear it so much as feel it arrive in my back teeth, the way you feel the mill wheel through the floor of the mill.

Oh, says the voice. It's woken right up. Oh, this is big.

The air tastes of stone. Not the dusty dead stone of the drone hatch; wet stone, mineral, like licking a key. And underneath it, with my new tongue, something else, a slow taste, sweet going one way and bitter coming back, so faint and so slow it's like listening to two people talking in the next house through a wall.

My feet find a floor, gritty and cold enough to bite even through the plating. I let go of the roots and stand, blind, and take one step.

And the dark lights up.

Not all at once. In front of me, on the wall where my warmth is, a shelf of something pale starts to glow. Blue-green, soft, like the last of a summer evening caught in a jar. Then another shelf, higher up, catches it. Then another. The light runs away from me up the walls and over my head, shelf after shelf, gill after gill, like somebody walking ahead of me lighting lamps, and I tip my head back and keep tipping it, and it doesn't stop.

It's a room. It's the biggest room I've ever been in. Bigger than the inside of the dome. The walls go up and lean in and meet far above me in arches, and the arches are made of fungus: great ribbed shelves of it, pleated like the underside of a mushroom cap, each pleat a little brighter at the edge than the middle. Between them the rock is threaded with pale lines, thousands of them, branching and joining and branching again, and I realise I'm looking at roots. Not the fungus's. The Spill's. The roots of every fur-tree and fern and glass-bell bush on the hill above me, coming down through the ceiling and hanging there, wound through with the pale threads. A whole forest, seen from underneath.

And everywhere, everywhere, drips. Every one rings. Some high, some low, tink, tonk, tink, never twice the same, like a choir that's been told to tune up and has decided to stay tuning forever.

Somewhere much further in, under all of it, there's a long dull roar. Water. A lot of water, going somewhere fast.

My breath is smoking in the blue light. I didn't notice I'd stopped walking.

It's talking, says the voice. It sounds hungry and pleased, both. All of it. Every one of those threads. It's been saying the same sentence since before your gran was born, and it's only halfway through.

"What's it saying?"

I put my palm on the furred wall and the hum comes up into my teeth again, and the glow brightens round my fingers like a cat leaning into a hand. Up close it smells sweet. Not flower sweet; bruised sweet, like the apples in the bottom of Saul's barrow at the end of the week.

Sugar's down by the water, the voice says. Pass it on. Something heavy fell over that way. Pass it on. Warm thing at the door. Pass it on. A pause. That's you. You're news.

"Hello," I say to a mountain, and laugh, and the laugh goes up into the arches and comes back down a long time later, very small, as if the mountain is trying it out.

* * *

I walk for what feels like a whole day. The glow lights ahead of me and fades behind me, so I'm always walking in a moving room of blue-green light, and when I stop and sit, it lowers itself, slowly, the way Gran lowers the lamp.

There are things that were made here, once. A rail on the floor, rusted to lace, running off into the dark. Iron rods driven into the walls, with plates on them furred over like buttons on a coat. On one plate, somebody has scratched letters with a nail. I rub the fur off with my sleeve.

B4 HOLDS. KEEP MOVING.

"Holds what?"

No idea. It's very old. The voice sounds bored again; it doesn't care much for things that aren't alive. The rock doesn't remember who wrote it.

I keep moving. It seems polite.

I sleep in a dry niche above a pool, with my back against the furred wall so the hum can go into my spine, and when I wake I don't know if it's morning. I tie a knot anyway.

There's a light in the pool.

Not the blue-green of the shelves. Warmer. A soft amber pulse, under the black water, slow, like someone breathing on a coal. It goes bright, dim, bright. It moves.

I lie very still on my ledge.

The light comes up through the water and becomes a throat. A round, glowing pouch under a sleek dark chin, and above it a face, flat and whiskered, with two eyes like drops of oil, and then the rest of it slides out onto the stone, the length of my forearm. It's brown-black and so wet it looks poured. It has webbed paws, and I count them twice, because the first time I get six and think I've made a mistake. I haven't. Six legs, two at the front and four behind, and it walks like water running downhill, every leg doing something slightly different.

Its whiskers are in the pool, trailing. It keeps them there while it looks at me, as if it's tasting the water and the boy at the same time.

Hungry, says the voice. Curious. Wet. A pause. It thinks you smell like a disappointing fruit.

"How can it think that? It hasn't smelled me."

It's smelling you now. It's very disappointed.

The throat pulses brighter. Its nose goes up and works, and then it isn't looking at me any more. It's looking at my pocket. At the last fat white root in my pocket, which I was saving, which is the last thing I own that tastes even a bit like bread.

"No," I say.

It's already moving.

It's up the rock and onto my legs and into my pocket faster than anything has a right to be, a cold wet weight that smells of river and lamp oil, and out again with the root in its teeth, and off down the slope in a long brown pour of legs. I grab. I get a fistful of air and a whisker-flick across the back of my hand that tingles like nettle.

"Oi!"

I go after it. Of course I do.

It goes over a rock pool and I go into it, and it goes under a shelf and I go into the shelf, and the glow lights up as I come, so the whole chase happens in bursts of blue-green. A lit cave with a brown streak in it. Dark. A lit cave with a brown streak and a wet boy in it, swearing. Dark.

It stops on a flat stone in the middle of a stream, where it thinks I can't get to it, and sits up on its four back legs, holding the root in its two front ones like Saul holds a sandwich, and starts to eat.

I wade straight in, waist deep, so cold it stops my breath, and I grab the other end of the root.

It holds on. I hold on. It growls, a little rattling purr in its glowing throat. I growl back, which I haven't done since Tilly was small enough to wrestle. We stare at each other over the root, nose to nose, in the water, in the dark. Its breath smells of my breakfast.

It says, says the voice, that this is its root. It also says that all roots are its roots.

"Tell it that's not how roots work."

I can't tell it anything. I can only taste what it means. It means teeth, mostly.

The root breaks.

We both fall over backwards. When I come up, spluttering, it's on its stone with half a root and I've got the other half, and its throat pulses slowly, amber, amber, as if it's thinking hard.

It eats its half. Then it looks at mine.

I'm cold to the bone and starving, and the root in my hand is a soggy pale stub. I break it again and hold out one piece on my flat palm, the way you give a carrot to Saul's horse.

The creature looks at my palm for a long moment. Then it leans out off its stone, delicately, all its whiskers forward, and takes the piece so gently I barely feel it. It eats that too. Then it slides into the water, swims round me once, and climbs up my shirt onto my shoulder, where it sits, dripping down my neck, pulsing.

"You're a thief," I tell it.

Its throat glows. Its whiskers are in my ear.

The last thing I had that tasted even a bit like home was a root, and it stole that, and now it's on my shoulder like the root was a fair price. In Firstfield the bakehouse heel is the best bit and the first bit to go, and you never, ever see who took it.

"Biscuit," I say. "You're Biscuit."

It doesn't care what you call it, says the voice. A pause. It likes your ear.

* * *

Biscuit knows the way to everything that can be eaten, and that turns out to be most of the way to everything.

It leads, in long wet pours, and waits for me with obvious contempt. It eats a beetle off a wall, and a second off my sleeve. It finds a pool with tiny clear shrimp in it and fishes them out with its whiskers and won't share, and when I try a shrimp anyway they taste, to my noisy tongue, of cold and stone and a very small amount of panic.

That day, or whatever it is, we come to a skylight.

A column of real light, standing in the middle of a cave like a white tree. It comes down a shaft from far overhead, where a fur-tree must have fallen and torn its roots out of the hill, and lands on a mound of earth and leaf-litter, and the mound is green. Ferns. Moss. Tiny pale seedlings, all of them pointing up the shaft at the sky.

I put my hand into the light, and it's warm. Actually warm, like the windowsill at home. I hadn't known how cold I was until then.

Biscuit won't come into it. It sits at the edge of the column with its throat dark, squinting, offended by the whole idea.

I take out Saul's twist of paper. It's damp through and soft as a rag, and when I shake it the seeds still rattle, but fewer now. I kneel on the mound, where the earth is crumbly and warm and smells of leaf-rot and sun, and push five of them in, first knuckle deep and not a hair more, and pinch the soil shut.

These again, says the voice. Still very quiet.

"They're meant to be."

Biscuit comes into the light then, after all, squinting, and digs up the nearest seed with one paw and eats it.

"Biscuit."

It chews, looks thoughtful, and spits it out onto my knee. Not food, apparently. I push the seed back in with my finger, and pat the earth flat, and say it, the thing I've said once already, to a pond.

"In case wherever you're going hasn't got proper ones."

Biscuit sneezes. Up in the shaft, a long way off, a bird I've never heard before says something in four notes, and stops, and says it again.

* * *

The next day the caves start going quiet.

I notice it with my hand first. I've got into the habit of trailing my palm along the walls, so the hum comes into my teeth. And then the wall is cold. Not furred. Wet and slick, like the outside of a jug of well water. No hum. The glow doesn't come.

The quiet isn't empty, quite. It's full of drips, and the drips ring differently here. Flatter. Like knocking on a table instead of a bell. And it smells.

It smells of nothing.

I know that nothing. It was in the bottom of the bakehouse crock in the hard winter, and it was on the fallen grazer in the shallows, wet down and stream water and nothing else. The smell of something that has stopped.

Biscuit's throat has gone dim. It presses itself against my ankle, all six legs, damp and shivering.

We go on, because the river's roar is that way. The arches overhead stay dark as we pass under them, dead shelves, grey at the edges like burnt paper. And growing over them, over the walls and the dead gills and the rusted rods, there's something else.

It's grey. Smooth. Rubbery, like the skin on boiled milk, if boiled milk were the size of a barn. It lies in folds and drapes over everything, filling the cracks, sealing the pale threads under it the way wax seals a jar. It doesn't glow. When I bend close to it, it doesn't smell of anything at all, not even stone, which shouldn't be possible.

It isn't talking, says the voice, slowly. It's not saying anything. It's just eating.

"Eating what?"

It tastes through my fingertip, from a careful distance. The talk. It's grown over the threads and it's drinking whatever comes down them. Sugar mostly. A pause, and something in the pause I haven't heard from it before, a sort of distaste. Somebody wrote it. On purpose. It's like your feet: somebody gave it a shape. Only it's been copied and copied and the shape's gone wrong. It was meant to hold something. Now it holds everything.

B4 HOLDS. KEEP MOVING.

I'm still frowning at that, standing with my weight on my right foot, when something takes hold of my left heel.

I look down. The grey is round my foot. I don't know when it got there; I didn't feel it come. It's a smooth grey lip, closed round my heel to the ankle bone like a mouth round a spoon, and it isn't soft any more. It's hard. It's going hard as I watch, the way Ma's plaster goes hard on the wall in the time it takes to say a prayer.

I pull. It holds.

I pull harder, and there's a sound like a cork leaving a bottle and my foot comes out of it, plated sole and all, and I go over backwards onto the wet rock.

Where my foot was, there's a grey lump, already as hard as a step. In the top of it is a hole the exact shape of my heel. I can see every ridge of the plating pressed into it, sharp as a seal in wax. If it had been my old pink heel, with skin you can pinch, I'd still be standing in it.

"Keep moving," I say, to nobody. "Right. Got it."

That's when the mountain coughs.

First, my tongue. A wave of bitter comes through the air, sharp at the back, so strong I gag: down the threads from somewhere off to the left, the furthest end of the living wall, passed hand to hand, heavy, falling, heavy, falling, the way the grass passed on the hunter. I've got just long enough to think that's a warning before the floor jumps.

The noise is like the end of the world, and then it's like a sack of potatoes, and then it's dust. Off in the dark to my left, a piece of the ceiling the size of our house has come down. Grit rains on my head. Biscuit is up my back and on top of my skull before the dust reaches us, all six feet in my hair, its throat blazing so bright the whole cave goes amber.

When the rumbling stops, I'm crouched with my arms over my head, coughing. The air tastes of rock flour. Behind us I can taste the network passing the warning on into the living caves. Heavy, falling. Pass it on.

That bit fell because it was dead. I'm sure of it, the way you're sure which tooth hurts. The grey drank the talk out of it, and when the talk stopped, the threads stopped holding. And the dead bits don't hum, and I can't hear which ones are about to fall until they're falling, because I'm only hearing with a hand.

I need to hear it. All of it. The whole mountain, at once.

* * *

There was a watch in Firstfield. One. It was Master Aldous's, and it was older than the schoolhouse, silver gone soft and brown with handling, and he wound it every morning at the start of lessons with a tiny key on a string while we said our names.

One winter afternoon, when the dome was doing its grey and the lamps were lit and nobody could sit still, he took it out of his waistcoat and held it up by its ring.

"Who can hear it tick?"

We all leaned in. I could, just. A thin little scratch of a sound, like a beetle walking on paper.

"Not much of a voice," said Master Aldous. "Bram. Come here." He smelled of chalk and lamp oil and the pear drops he kept in his drawer for when someone had been brave. He held the watch out by the ring. "Put that between your teeth. Gently. It's worth more than the meeting house. Now put your fingers in your ears."

I did. The schoolroom went quiet and woolly, the way it goes under the bath.

And then the watch was enormous.

It wasn't in my mouth. It was in my head. Tock. Tock. Tock. Like a cart going over cobbles behind my eyes, like someone knocking from the inside of my skull to be let out. I jerked so hard I nearly bit it, and the whole school laughed, and Master Aldous laughed too, and caught the watch as it fell.

"Where did you hear it?" he said.

"In my teeth."

"Further in than that." He tapped my jaw, by the hinge. Then the bone behind my ear, the hard lump Dr. Ommery had made us all find on ourselves. "Jaw. Skull. And three little bones in there, smaller than seeds, that pass the sound along hand to hand. The hammer, the anvil, the stirrup." He made us say them. All of us, all together, the way we'd said the parts of Gerald in the jar: hammer, anvil, stirrup. Jaw. Skull. "Ears are only the porch, Bram. The house is further in."

Jory wanted to know if it would tick louder if he swallowed it. Master Aldous said we'd all find out together if he tried.

* * *

Hammer. Anvil. Stirrup.

I say them aloud in the dead cave, with the dust still settling and Biscuit on my head, and the words go out flat and come back flat. Jaw. Skull. The lump behind my ear.

"Can you," I say, "make all of that hear? Through the rock. Through the threads. Like the watch."

Yes, says the voice, at once, and very gently. Now that you've told me where the house is. Shall I?

It always asks. Sometimes I wish it wouldn't.

"What does it cost?"

Food. Heat. Sleep. A pause. And you'll hear things. All the time. I don't think you'll be able to stop.

The bitter comes down the threads again, faint, from somewhere far off. Another bit going.

"Yes," I say.

It starts in my jaw. A deep ache, like having a tooth pulled backwards, and then it spreads round behind my ears, and it itches, terribly, on the inside of my head where no fingernail can get. I hit the side of my head with the heel of my hand, which does nothing, which Biscuit objects to. My eyes stream. I'm so hungry suddenly that I eat a beetle off the wall without thinking about it and then think about it.

Then it stops, and I put both hands flat on the rock, and I lean down, and I put my teeth against the stone.

And the mountain opens.

I hear Biscuit's heart. It's sitting on my head and I hear its heart through my own skull, quick and light as rain on thatch. I hear my own heart come back to me off the walls. I hear all the drips, the whole choir of them, so I can tell where every single one falls, like seeing in the dark with the back of my head. I hear the river: not a roar now but a hundred voices of water, one deep fast one and a lot of small ones running into it, and I know it's off to my right and down, and I know how far. I hear the living network, the whole web of it, humming, branching, every thread a slightly different note, sweet and busy and slow, further than I can follow, like standing in the middle of all of Firstfield on market day with your eyes shut and hearing every single person in it.

And I hear the quiet.

It's a hole in the sound. Ahead of me, and spreading, a great ragged ring of nothing where the hum just stops, the way a field of crickets stops when you walk into it. I can hear its edges. I can hear the edges moving, slow as a tide coming in, the hum going out thread by thread. And every so often, inside the quiet, there's a tick, a faint dry tick, like a cooling stove. Rock deciding.

I take my teeth off the stone. My whole face is buzzing.

Well? says the voice.

"It's huge," I say, which isn't anything like enough.

On top of my head, Biscuit's heart is still going, a tiny quick drum I can hear without even trying now, right through my scalp. I'll never not hear it. I find, wiping my nose on my sleeve, that I don't mind.

* * *

The quiet has a middle. That's the next thing. Rings do.

I go in towards it with my teeth to the rock every fifty paces, listening, the way Saul listens to a tomato frame before he leans on it. Where the stone ticks, I go round. Where it's silent and the grey hangs in thick folds from the roof, I go round wider. Twice I feel the bitter come down the threads, and I'm already somewhere else when the roof comes down behind me.

Biscuit leads half the time. Its whiskers keep going out into the air, twitching, its throat lit, and I realise after a while it's smelling something it wants, the same way the quiet goes.

Then I smell it too. Not bruised-apple sweet, like the glow. Sticky sweet. Glass-bell fruit and fur-tree sap and something like honey left in the sun.

The middle of the quiet is a cave shaped like a bowl, and in the bottom of the bowl is the grey. All of it. More grey than I've seen anywhere, heaped and folded and fat, glossy, the size of a haystack, sitting in its own lap. And above it, out of a crack in the wall at about the height of my head, water is weeping.

A thin steady trickle, glinting, running down a groove worn in the rock and straight down into the grey, into a sort of grey pool where the folds have shaped themselves into a basin to catch it. The air over it is so sweet I can taste it with my eyes.

Biscuit makes a noise like a kettle and goes for it.

"No!"

I grab it round the middle, all six legs going, and it twists round and bites me in the thumb, not hard, and keeps reaching for the trickle with every whisker it owns.

It says you are the worst thing that has ever happened, says the voice. It says it hates you forever. It says that's sugar.

I hold the furious otter against my chest and look at the water and think.

The water comes down off the hill above, through all those roots. Once, the threads would've passed it on, sugar's down by the water, pass it on, a little to every cave for miles. Now it all runs into one mouth.

Nothing in Firstfield is free. Ma's honey costs the bees their summer. The grazers pay for floating with seed, and when their seed stopped working, they fell. So what's this paying? It doesn't glow. It doesn't talk. It just holds, and holding the roof of a mountain is dear. All of it is paid for with that trickle.

"If it doesn't get the sugar," I say slowly, "it can't hold."

No.

"So it lets go."

Yes. The voice has gone very interested. And then the talk comes back. And also, it adds, possibly the ceiling.

I look up. The roof over the bowl is grey from edge to edge, and silent, and ticking.

"Can you fix it? The grey. Starve it, or—"

I can only write in you, the voice says. You know that. And then, almost kindly: You don't need me for this one. You need a wall.

I need a wall. I need the trickle to go anywhere but into that basin. And the groove it runs in has a lip on the low side, and below the lip, I can hear it with my teeth to the floor, there's a crack in the rock running off down towards the river, a dark slit where the water would go, all on its own, if something just turned it.

I put Biscuit down. It goes straight for the trickle. I let it; at least it's busy.

I gather flat stones from the old roof falls. I lay them across the groove one at a time, slantwise, the way Saul lays a sod to turn rain off a bed, so the water hits them and spills sideways over the lip and down into the crack. The trickle bends. Most of it goes over the lip and down the slit, glinting, but a little still seeps between the stones into the basin, and a haystack can live forever on a little. And every time I let go of a stone, the water nudges it.

I need mortar. I need something that sets.

I look at my left heel, and the little pale ridges on it, and then at the grey, and then at the hole in the step back there, the perfect shape of my foot.

Anything that stays still.

"Oh, you won't like this," I tell the voice.

I already don't.

I step down into the groove, onto the stones, with the sweet water running cold over my plated feet. I crouch and put my hands on the top stone and press it hard into the gap, and I put my weight on the others with my feet, and I hold them there. And I stop moving.

I hear it with my jaw when it starts: a soft, thick creeping, under the drips, like dough rising. The grey comes up out of the basin towards the stones, because the stones are still, because I'm still, because that's what it was made for. It closes over the lowest stone. Over the gaps between them. Round the edges of my feet. I feel it reach the plating and grip, cold and then tight, and every bone in my legs wants to run.

Gran's count. Five hundred, before you peek at bread.

One. Two. Three.

At a hundred and forty, it's over both my feet to the ankle bones, hard as a step. At two hundred, it's up past the edge of the plating, onto my shins. Onto skin. Plain pink Bram skin with nothing on it, the kind you can pinch. I feel the difference at once: on the plating it was gripping; on skin it's sucking, sealing, like a cup on a wet table. If it sets there, I won't come out of it with a pop. I won't come out of it.

I keep counting. My voice is doing something wobbly. The stones under my hands have stopped shifting. The water's all going over the lip now, every drop, glittering down into the crack, and the seeping between the stones has stopped, because the grey has filled every gap. It's building its own wall. It's sealing itself off from its own supper.

Two hundred and sixty. It's at my knees.

"Biscuit," I say, very calmly. "Biscuit, I could do with you."

Biscuit is sitting on the lip of the groove, eating the last sweet drips off the stone, ignoring me with its whole body.

Two hundred and eighty. It's over my knees.

"Biscuit."

The water's stopped coming into the basin. Biscuit notices that. It looks at the stones. It looks at me. Its throat goes from amber to a dark, sulky red, and it comes down off the lip in one wet pour and lands on my leg, and it bites the grey.

It bites like it bit the root. It worries at the grey where it's still soft, above my knee, tearing it away in rubbery strips, spitting them out, biting again, all six feet braced on my shin. It's fast. It's faster than the grey is. Every bit that's crept onto skin, it tears off, and every bit on the plating it leaves, and I can't tell if it knows the difference or if the grey on the plating has just gone too hard to bite.

Four hundred. Four hundred and ninety-nine.

Five hundred.

I let go of the stones. They don't move. They're a wall now, a grey solid bank across the groove, smooth as a dam on the mill race, and the sweet water is running politely over the lip of it and away down into the dark towards the river, every drop, and the basin below is still and dry.

I pull my left foot. Nothing. I pull harder, and the plating does its job, and it comes out with a pop like a cork, and then the right one, and I fall over backwards onto the floor of the bowl, which is getting to be a habit.

Biscuit stands on my chest, dripping, red-throated, chewing a strip of grey with deep dissatisfaction.

It says, says the voice, that you taste worse than you smell. And that it would die for you. A pause. Or possibly die near you. Its grammar is mostly teeth.

I lie there and laugh till my ribs hurt and my new skull rings with it.

* * *

Then I put my teeth to the floor of the bowl, because I can't not, and I listen.

At first, nothing different. The quiet, ticking.

Then, a long way off, at the very edge of it, a thread starts to hum.

One note, like the first bird before dawn. Then another thread picks it up, and another, and the hum is coming in from the edge of the ring towards the middle, slow as a rumour, thread by thread. The sugar is going down the crack into the river, and the river runs past a hundred caves, and the threads at the edges are drinking, and passing it on.

And in front of me, the haystack of grey shivers.

It gives a sound I feel more than hear, a deep wet crack, and a seam opens in its side, and it slumps. Just a little. Like Gran sitting down after the long stairs. A fold of it high on the wall peels away from the rock, and from under the fold a shower of grit pours down.

The voice says, Ah.

The roof ticks.

Not one tick. All of it, the whole grey ceiling of the bowl, like the dome's ribs on a cold night, and the bitter comes down the threads so strong it makes my eyes water. Not from far off this time. From over my head. Heavy, falling, heavy, falling, every living thread around the bowl shouting it and passing it on.

It was holding up what it killed.

I grab Biscuit and run.

* * *

I run with my mouth open, and every time my feet hit the floor the floor tells me something. Dead, dead, tick, dead. Hum. I go towards the hum. Behind me the bowl comes down with a noise that comes up through my feet and slams my teeth together. The dust comes after us like a flood down a lane. Biscuit is on my head again, its throat blazing, and in its light the grey on the walls is peeling and the rock behind it starting to move.

And the arches light.

Not behind me. In front. A shelf of gills on the right-hand wall flares blue-green, though I'm nowhere near it. Then another beyond it. Then another, further, making a line. The living network, drinking its first sugar in who knows how long, talking, passing it ahead of me in a running ribbon of light, and on the front of my tongue it tastes of one thing over and over.

This way. This way. This way.

I go that way.

The floor drops. I slide down a slope of wet scree on my backside with the roof coming down behind me, and the roar of the river grows from a sound into a wall of sound, and the line of lit shelves bends down and down, and then there's no more floor.

There's water. Black and fast and so loud I feel it in my eyeballs, running through a channel in the rock with the network glowing on the walls above it, blue-green, rushing past.

The ceiling behind me lets go.

"Hold on," I tell Biscuit, which is pointless, because it already has hold of my ears, and I jump.

The cold hits me like Saul's barrow. It's colder than the pond, cold that gets into your teeth and your new skull and your heart and squeezes. The river takes me like a hand and throws me.

I go under. I come up. I can hear the river through my jaw even under the water, every rock, every turn, where it's deep and where it's shallow before I get there, and I kick away from the shallow places, the places that grind. Biscuit is on my head, its webbed feet flat on my scalp, its whiskers trailing in the water beside my ear, tasting it, and every time we go under its throat lights up in the black like a lamp in a flooded house.

And above us, on the walls, the network is lit. All the way. A running ribbon of blue-green, cave after cave, rushing past overhead like the lit windows of the lane when you run home late. The drips ring. The river roars. The light says this way, this way, and the river says it too, and for once, for one long cold rushing minute, everything I can hear is saying the same thing.

That's when I taste it.

Under the cold, under the stone and the sugar and the bitter, carried on the hum through my teeth, very far off and very faint, from somewhere deeper in the network than I'll ever go. Green. Very old. Very patient. A taste I know the way I know Ma's voice through a wall.

It tastes of my pond.

The voice in my head goes absolutely silent. Not bored silent. Not asleep. Silent the way you go silent when someone says your name in a crowd and you can't see who.

Then the river turns, and turns again, and the taste is gone, and there's light ahead.

Not blue-green. White.

* * *

It's like having a bucket of light thrown in my face.

The river spits me out of the hill in a long fall of white water, Biscuit shrieking on my head, into a pool, and the light is everywhere. It's in my eyes like sand. It's under my eyelids when I shut them. I crawl out onto warm rock by feel, with my hands over my face, crying from the glare and coughing up river, and lie there.

Biscuit climbs off my head and shakes, all over me, like a dog with too many legs. Then it gets into my shirt, out of the sun, and sits on my stomach, dripping, its throat pulsing dull red against my skin, and refuses to come out.

I can't see. I've been in the dark three days and my eyes have forgotten what the sun's for.

But I can hear.

I can hear everything. The rock under my back is warm and it's talking: the waterfall pounding into it, the roots of some tree up the bank creaking as they drink, a beetle going across it a stride from my ear, its six feet like a drumroll. Under all that, the mountain I just came out of, still settling. And, if I press my jaw to the stone, the hum, inside the hill. Wider than before. Coming back.

And I can't stop hearing it. I try. I lift my head off the rock and it's still there, in my feet, in my spine, the whole ground going about its business right through me. I think of plain porridge, and how I can't remember what plain tasted like, and I understand I'm not going to remember what quiet sounded like either.

I tie a knot. Thirteen. By feel.

"More edge," I say to Master Aldous, who isn't here, with my eyes shut against the whole bright world. My voice comes back to me off the rock, through my skull, and it doesn't sound quite like mine.

Then the ground speaks.

It comes from far off, out across the valley, through the stone and up into my back. A single footfall. Deep. Slow. So heavy I feel it in my ribs like the bass note of the Friday thunder. I wait. I count. At fifty-one, it comes again. At a hundred and two, again. Something enormous, walking, one step every fifty breaths, out there on the hills, and I can hear it now, and I'll be able to hear it, I suppose, forever.

Biscuit's throat goes from red to amber, against my belly. Curious.

Bram, says the voice.

It hasn't used my name before. I don't think it ever has.

When you're rested, it says, carefully, the way you ask a thing you're afraid of the answer to. Would you put your hand on the stone again? On the hum. Just for a little while.

"Why?"

It doesn't answer for a long time. Out across the valley, the enormous thing takes another step.

I'd like to taste that again, it says. I think I know it.


## Chapter Six: Biological Status: None

> UNDER. Dead rock is quiet. A living cave drips, ticks, breathes and talks to anyone with a hand on it; a dead one only waits. If the cave stops talking to you, stop talking to it, and leave by the way you came. [pencilled in the margin, in a large untidy hand:] And if it hums, say thank you.
>
> — Field Primer of the Survey, fourth edition, the chapter headed "Under"; a crew copy, Vessany lanes
>

The Chief Examiner called at dawn, which is how I knew he hadn't been to bed.

I was sitting on the boy's stone by the pond with the flask between my knees and the seven tomato plants at my feet when the slate gave the three flat notes of a relay call from Margin Station, nine hours of hop away. It sounded like somebody frying bacon in a tin bath.

"Pastern."

"Chief Examiner."

There was a pause, and in it, very clearly, I heard a small dry tock: a sunflower-seed shell landing in the tin on Halloran's desk.

"You opened a Discrepancy," he said, "on a boy."

"On a record."

"On a boy who, as of last night, the Register has down as Retired." Tock. "Which means there's no discrepancy, Pastern. The Upper House says he's finished. The Register says he's finished. Retired is a status, not a whereabouts. Close it."

The grazer that had marked me came down the bank while he was talking and wiped its face along my jaw again, ear to chin, as if it suspected I'd been washing. Hay, wet wool, honey gone to vinegar, and under it, faint, the loaf.

"Sir," I said. "The record says Retired. And then there's no next line."

"There often isn't."

"There always is. Retired to a pension. Retired to the stud book. Retired, decommissioned, rendered. The Register is a filing system; it has never in its life left a thing without a drawer. This one hasn't got one." "And I have his living trace on a sky-grazer twenty-two metres up, confirmed by assay, entered under seal, eleven hours before somebody upstairs signed him off. Either he was retired while he was still floating about the Spill on the back of a goat, or the Upper House filed a form about a boy it didn't have. One of those is a Forgery, sir. Possibly both."

Tock.

"You've got a goat," Halloran said at last, "that tastes of a dead boy."

"Sky-grazer. And he isn't dead. He planted tomatoes."

That pause was longer.

"Ten days," said Halloran. "From now. And no budget, Pastern. None. Don't send me receipts. I don't want to see a sandwich. If you can't put it on a form in ten days, you close it yourself and you sign it yourself, and it stays on your record."

"Yes, sir."

"And Pastern." Tock. "The company's filed. Varro-Tandem. Some sort of recovery claim on the remains. They'll have someone on you by lunch." He spat another shell. "Be civil."

"I'm always civil."

"I've read your file," said the Chief Examiner, and the call closed on a crackle like a log falling in a grate.

Ten days and no budget. In eleven years Halloran had never once said well done, and he had never once given anyone ten days.

"Hask," I said. "We've been given a great deal of trust and no money at all."

"I have never had money," Hask said, from the shade of the fur-tree, where he had spent the night with a leaf in his joint and a crack in his third stone. "I have found trust heavier."

* * *

The company came at noon, by air, in grey.

The skiff was the colour of a filing cabinet and nearly as quiet, and it set down beyond the farm cut without disturbing a single seed-head. A man got out and walked up the valley towards me with the step of a person who has never in his life arrived anywhere early or late.

He was tall, and thin, and very neat. Company-grey suit, still without a crease after half a kilometre of Spill, which ought not to be possible. Silver hair combed flat to his skull, though the face under it couldn't have been much past forty. He carried a slim ledger case and a stylus, and when the grass leaned at him he looked down at it and wrote something. He smelled of cedar and of the warm breath of a copier that has been running all morning.

"Examiner Pastern," he said. A pleasant baritone, the kind they use on the hop to tell you the oxygen masks are perfectly routine. "Associate Pell. Varro-Tandem Bioreference. Recovery." He inclined his head to Hask. "Proxy. I'm told the Orrun prefer not to be touched, so I shan't offer."

Hask regarded him from his full height. "You are well informed."

"I am documented," said Associate Pell, and smiled.

On his lapel there was a small enamel pin, white on grey, with a numeral on it. 7.

"Seven?" I said.

Something went across his pleasant face and was filed away. "Associate Pell," he said again, a little more distinctly. "There are, I'm afraid, a number of us. The licence calls each of us the Associate. The pin is a convenience for accounting." He opened his ledger case on his forearm. "Examiner, I'll be brief; I know your budget is nil. My company holds a claim for recovery of materials in respect of the specimen designated Reference Twelve, status Retired. Retired specimens revert to the licensee. I'm here to recover them."

"Them?"

"The materials. I wouldn't dream of asking you to alter evidence, Examiner. But I notice that you've entered the boy's traces. You haven't entered the boy." He looked up, mild as milk. "He isn't evidence. He's materials. And materials, wherever found, are ours."

It was exactly the hole I'd have gone through myself.

"You can't recover what you can't find," I said.

"No," Pell agreed. "But I can document where I've looked. Our survey drones picked up a heavy, low-flying shape at dusk on the night of the ninth, leaving this valley with the flock. North, over the ridge. I imagine you saw it too." He had read my notes, of course; filing is publishing, if you know where to stand. "I've staked a route. I'll be ahead of you, Examiner. Nothing personal."

"Everything documented," I said.

His face did the thing again. "That's mine," he said.

"I read it on your skiff."

He wrote that down. Then he closed the ledger case with a precise click, bowed to Hask, and went back down the valley. At the edge of the trees he took a slim grey peg from his case, pushed it into the earth with his heel, and walked on.

"Hask," I said. "Did he just mark his trail for us?"

"For the company," said Hask. A long grinding pause. "Following it is not," he added, with satisfaction, "enumerated."

The skiff driver came at four with the last hamper I could not afford. I asked for rope. She looked at me for a long time, then fetched forty metres of good brown line, coiled like a sleeping snake, and dropped it at my feet.

"Rope's yours," she said. "Bill's under it."

* * *

We went north the next morning in a cold wind, up the stream, past the ravine, and onto the ridge.

The pegs were every hundred metres, exactly: slim grey stakes with orange caps, each stamped VT and a number, running up the slope in a line so straight it looked ruled on. There's a particular pleasure, for a woman with no budget, in being guided across a wilderness by her opponent's surveying.

The fur-trees grew shorter and shaggier as we climbed, and then gave up and became a tufted scrub that rattled in the wind like a crowd shaking dice. The air tasted thinner and colder, resinous, like a pencil being sharpened.

Two-thirds of the way to the top, the pegs swung east in a long careful curve, though the slope ahead was easy, and I stopped.

The grass was pointing.

Not at me; not since a grazer wiped its face on my jaw. A patch fifty paces up the slope was leaning, every blade, inward at a tangle of fern and broken stone, like iron filings round a magnet. And in the shadow under the fern, swinging very slowly from side to side, was a small blue light.

Pell's drone, I thought, had seen the heat from the air and drawn a polite curve round it. Pell had not been wearing goat.

The wind came down the ridge at me, and I smelled it: a butcher's drain in high summer, with a new note now of something scalded.

"Examiner," said Hask, very low. "I am obliged to mention that I have already measured that one."

"I know."

"I may act at once."

"Don't," I said. "It doesn't know we're here."

It didn't. I walked up that slope in plain sight, the grass round my boots standing straight up as if I were weather, and the blue bulb swung across me, and paused, and swung on. It was waiting for something that would make the grass point. I passed it at twenty paces. I could hear it breathing, a dry tic... tic... of plates. My own heart was going so hard I thought it must show on the grass from a kilometre off.

The grass didn't care. To the Spill, I was flock.

Hask came after me in a long slow detour, and when he was well past he said, "Item three thousand three hundred and one does not cover passing it."

"No."

"I find I should like it to."

The ridge top was all wind and a view that knocked the breath out of me: valley after valley to the north, folded like a blanket kicked off a bed, green going blue going violet, a river laid along one like a dropped silver chain. On the far side the slope went down in deep moss, and below the crest the moss held a long dent.

It was the length of a boy lying down. Grazer down was caught in it, a whole drift, and when I put my face close the moss still smelled, four days on, faintly and unmistakably, of bread.

I wrote: Landing site. I wrote: Two grazers, or one big one. Then, because I could, I wrote: He flew.

Twenty paces further up there was a ring of fern, and inside the ring of fern, the moss simply stopped.

It was a hole, round and black and wider than a kitchen, with roots hanging into it like unravelled rope, and out of it, in long slow breaths, came cold. It smelled of a cellar: stone and wet and mushroom, and something sweet underneath, like apples bruised in the bottom of a sack.

Pell's pegs ran straight to it and stopped, and at the end of the line, on a folding stool beside a lamp and a coil of orange company line, sat Associate Pell, writing.

"Examiner," he said, without looking up. "You came through the warm patch. How very brave. I've entered it." He turned his ledger case round so I could see the Register entry glowing on it.

CLAIM UC-114 (Ridge Undercroft, north face). Workings: suspended. Wall consolidant: BINDER line B-4, self-setting, sugar-fed; left in place. Biological status: none (subsurface; inert). Renewal: automatic.

"A mining claim," said Pell pleasantly. "Unworked for sixty years, but very much on the books. Inert. Which means whatever I find down there is nobody's business but mine. I can net it. I can blast for it." He capped his stylus. "There's nothing down there to object."

The hole breathed out at us, slow and cold and sweet.

* * *

Hask couldn't come.

We had the argument at the lip of the sinkhole in the twilight, while Pell pretended to write and listened with every inch of his neat grey suit.

"Old workings," I said. "Undercut rock, sixty years untended, and you weigh a third of a tonne."

"Three hundred and forty kilograms," said Hask, "less the leaf."

"I've been underground with the Survey. What brings a ceiling down is weight on a hollowed floor. I'm not having you go through it."

"Item one thousand one hundred and seventy," said Hask. "Entombment, partial or entire." He brought it out slowly, as if it hurt. "It is enumerated, Examiner. I cannot shield you from it from up here."

"Then shield me from up here as best you can, and I'll try very hard not to be entombed."

"It is not enumerated," he said at last, "that I should be left behind."

"No," I said. "I'm sorry, Hask."

"I shall walk above you," he said. "Stone hears stone. If the hill moves, I shall know where." And then, after a pause so long the first stars came out over the ridge: "Take the rope."

I took the rope.

We went down at first light, Pell and I, a little apart. He had his company line, his lamp, a long grey case on a strap that held a net-launcher, and on his belt, discreetly, a short black rod with a brass cap, which the catalogues call a compliance wand and everyone else calls a stun baton. I had forty metres of brown rope over my shoulder, my coat, my slate, my flask, and a pad of blank paper forms, because Fallow taught me that paper doesn't need charging.

The roots were wet and stringy in my fists, and the cold climbed my legs like water filling a bath. The circle of sky shrank to a plate, then a coin. Pell's lamp made a hard white tunnel through the dark and showed me nothing but the next root.

Then my boots found grit, and I let go, and took one step off the bottom of the roots, and the cave lit up.

* * *

It did not light for Pell's lamp. I want to be exact about that. His lamp swept the walls and showed wet black rock and pale shapes on it, dull as dried mushrooms in a jar. But at my shoulder, a shelf of those shapes began to glow. Blue-green, soft as the last of a summer evening, and the glow ran away from me up the wall, shelf after shelf, until it was over my head and then gone round the curve of the roof. I took a step and it followed me, lighting ahead and fading behind. I stood still and it lowered itself, slowly, the way you turn down a lamp for somebody sleeping.

It liked warmth. It liked me. It didn't care for a white beam in the least.

"Fascinating," said Pell, and wrote, and turned his lamp off.

It was a room the size of a cathedral, except that cathedrals are made to look large and this was simply large, the way weather is. The walls went up and leaned in and met far overhead in ribbed arches, and the arches were fungus: great pleated shelves, each pleat brighter at the edge than the middle, like the inside of a shell held to a lamp. Through the rock ran thousands of pale threads, branching and rejoining, and down from the roof hung roots, the roots of every living thing on the hill above us, wound about with those threads, so that I was standing under the whole of the Spill and looking up at its underside.

Drips fell everywhere, and every one rang, tink, tonk, never the same twice, and under it all ran a long roar like a crowd in another street. Water. A great deal of it.

The air tasted of stone, of wet keys and clean cold, and when I drank from a pool it hurt my teeth to the roots. I put my bare palm flat on a furred patch of wall, purely for the record.

It was faintly warm, soft as the inside of a mitten, and something came up through it into my wrist and settled in my back teeth: a hum, very low, steady and slow, like a mill wheel felt through the floor of the mill.

"Biological status," I said, "none."

"Inert," Pell agreed placidly, beside me in the blue dark, and wrote it down.

* * *

We walked for most of a day. Pell followed a map from the claim file, drawn in a fine dead hand; I followed the glow, which went more or less where his map did; and we bickered the whole way at a low pleasant volume, like two clerks sharing a desk.

"Sixty-three exhibits under one number," he said, stepping over a rail rusted to lace. "I've filed a note that it's improper."

"Which Pell's note? Yours?"

"The Associate's note," he said, with frost on it.

He saw things; I'll give him that. He wrote down the number of every rod and every plate bolted to the wall, furred over like coat buttons. He did not see that one of them, at shoulder height, had been rubbed clean. A sleeve's width of the fur wiped away, and the iron plate under it bare and dark, and on it, scratched with a nail by someone long ago:

B4 HOLDS. KEEP MOVING.

The fur was creeping back only at the edge of the wipe: a few days' growth. Someone shorter than me had stood here and wondered what it meant.

I said nothing to Pell. He was writing down the number of the plate.

We came to the skylight in the afternoon, if there were afternoons down there.

A column of real light stood in a cave like a white tree, coming down a shaft where something big had torn its roots out of the hill. It landed on a mound of leaf-litter, and the mound was green, ferns and moss and tiny pale things straining upward. I put my hand in the light. It was warm as a windowsill, and I hadn't known until then how cold I was.

Up the shaft, a long way off, a bird I didn't know said something in four notes, and stopped, and said it again.

I nearly walked past them, the way I nearly had at the pond. They did nothing.

On the top of the mound, in a little row, there were five dimples in the soil, where a finger had gone in first knuckle deep and no further. Four of them had cracked open, and out of each crack there came a tiny bent stem, folded over like the handle of a walking stick, with its two seed leaves still caught in the husk. The stems were red. A dull, dark, earthy red, the colour of a rained-on brick.

In the middle of the row, one dimple had been dug up and roughly patted shut again, and beside it in the soft earth was a print I didn't know: small, webbed, with a ragged scuff of toes behind it, as if something had walked through on more feet than it strictly needed.

"Weeds," said Pell, behind me, looking at the stems.

"Tomatoes."

"Is that significant?"

"They don't move," I said.

He wrote tomatoes down, with a small doubtful frown, as if he suspected me of a joke at the company's expense. I took out my slate and added them to the open case: Five plants, baseline, red-stemmed, planted by the subject; one disturbed and replanted. Then I sat on my heels in the warm light with the bird singing its four notes, and for a moment I didn't want to be an Examiner at all. I wanted to see his face when he knew they'd come up.

* * *

The quiet started an hour past the skylight.

By then I was trailing my palm along the furred walls for the hum, and then the wall was cold, slick as the outside of a jug of well water. No hum. When I stopped, the glow didn't come.

The drips didn't ring here. They knocked, flat, like knuckles on a table, and the air smelled of nothing at all: a closed-room nothing, like the bladder of the fallen grazer below 40-K. Something had stopped here.

Pell turned his lamp back on. The arches overhead were dead, grey at the edges like burnt paper, and over everything hung something else: grey, smooth, rubbery, like the skin on boiled milk if boiled milk were the size of a barn, lying over the pale threads the way wax lies over a jar.

Only it was coming off.

All along the gallery the grey was peeling, folds of it hanging slack like wallpaper in a house nobody has heated for a winter, and under the folds the threads showed pale again. I put my palm to a bare patch and it was cold, and then, very faintly, it wasn't. A hum, thin as a single violin string in another room.

"B-4," said Pell, reading his claim file. "Wall consolidant. Self-setting." He sounded, for the first time, slightly uncertain. "It's meant to be holding the roof."

"It isn't holding anything. It's starving."

"How on earth would you know that?"

I didn't, then. I knew it twenty minutes later.

The gallery opened into a field of rubble, fresh, the dust still soft and pale and unmarked by drips. A whole cave had come down here days ago, not years. Pell's lamp swept over it and the dust rose in the beam like flour.

At the near edge of the fall, a grey lump stood on the floor, hard as a step, and in the top of it was a hole.

I knelt by it. The hole was the exact shape of a heel. Not a boot heel: a bare one, small, and every ridge of a fine broad tread was printed into the grey, sharp as a seal in wax. I had seen that tread in the mud of a stream a day's walk south, coming out of a run of bloody bare prints and going on as if the boy had grown boots.

I put two fingers into it. Cold, smooth, perfect. Something had grabbed his heel here and set, and the heel had come out like a cork from a bottle and left this behind like a calling card.

"Footprint," said Pell, reading over my shoulder. "Of what?"

"Of materials," I said, and he had the grace to say nothing.

The other thing was in the far wall.

Above the rubble, where a slice of the old cave wall still stood, a thin trickle of water was weeping out of a crack at about the height of my head and running down a groove worn smooth in the rock. The air over it was sweet. Not the bruised-apple sweet of the glow. Sticky sweet: fruit and tree sap and honey left in the sun. I put my finger in it and tasted it, which you should never do in a cave, and which I'll do again.

Sugar.

Low down, where the groove met the edge of the fall, someone had built a wall across it. Flat stones from the old roof falls, laid slantwise, the way you'd lay a sod to turn rain off a garden bed, and the gaps between them packed solid with grey binder gone as hard as mortar. A dam, the length of my arm, smooth and grey and neat. The sweet water hit it and ran politely sideways, over the lip of the groove, and away down a dark crack in the rock, glinting, towards the sound of the river.

In the top of the dam there were two more holes. A left heel and a right heel, side by side, ridged and perfect.

And all over the grey round them, low down, there were small sharp bite marks, dozens of them, and strips of grey lay in the rubble below, chewed and spat out, as if something with small teeth had been eating the stuff and thinking very poorly of it.

I sat down on a rock with my palm on the wall, where a pale thread was showing through the peeling grey, and let the logic come.

The grey fed on sugar; the claim said so. The sugar came down from the Spill's roots on the hill above, and it should have gone out along all those threads, and instead it had all been running into one mouth. Then a boy who knew how to turn rain off a garden bed had laid a wall of stones across the groove, and when the water kept shifting them he had stood on them, still, and let the grey do the one thing it was made for. It set round anything that stayed still. It set round his stones, and his feet, and sealed itself off from its own supper.

He had stood in it long enough for it to set. I thought of what it would have done to the bleeding pink feet from the stream, and had to look at the bite marks instead. Not alone, then. Something with small teeth, on his side.

After that the sugar went down the crack to the river, and past a hundred caves, and the threads were drinking again; and the grey, starved, was letting go of the roof it had been holding up.

Under my palm the hum came up into my teeth, a little stronger than before. I dug my thumbnail into the bare pale fur at the edge of the grey to take a sample. It came away cold and faintly greasy and packed itself up under my nail, and the wall glowed round my hand, briefly, like a cat leaning into a stroke.

"He fixed it," I said.

"Who did?"

"The boy. Your materials." I held up my thumb to the lamp. "Biological status: none. The whole mountain's been talking under your feet since we came in. He found the one thing killing it and starved it with a wall of stones and his own two feet. That's a living network, Associate, and somebody put it in the wrong drawer sixty years ago and let it renew itself every year since."

Pell looked at the dam and the heel marks for a long time. I watched him want to write something down and not know what.

Then the rock under his feet went quiet.

* * *

The hum in my teeth stopped. Not faded: stopped, as if a hand had been laid flat on a string. And in the silence, from somewhere under the floor, a tick. A dry small tick, like a stove cooling. Then another.

The drips round Pell's feet were knocking flat on dead stone. There wasn't a glimmer on the walls on his side, not a thread showing. The grey hung over him in slack folds, letting go.

On my side, a pace behind me, a shelf of gills glowed faintly blue.

"Pell," I said. "Step towards me."

He looked up from his ledger case. "I beg your pardon?"

"Now. The rock's dead under you."

I saw the tiny pleasant pause in which an Associate decides whether an instruction from an Examiner constitutes a recordable event. He opened his mouth. I believe he was going to object.

The floor went.

It tipped, the whole slab under him, with a crack like a branch, and slid. Pell went with it, lamp swinging, and I was off my rock and across the gap before I'd decided anything, the way I went for the guard at the tank-room door, and I got a fistful of grey company suit at the collar and threw my weight backwards.

It was not enough. It was a good deal better than nothing.

The slab went out from under both of us and we fell together, a long sliding drop down rough stone in a roar of grit and dust, his lamp spinning off and going out with a small final pop, and then we hit bottom, hard, and the dark came down on top of us like a blanket.

* * *

For a while there was only rock settling over our heads, a long grinding complaint, and pebbles bouncing down the walls. Then there was only the river.

It was very loud. It was below me.

I lay on cold wet stone and took stock. Knees scraped. Left elbow unhappy. Ribs sore but present. The flask, still dented in four places and no more. The rope, still over my shoulder, bless the driver and her bill. I couldn't see my hand in front of my face. Black like the inside of a closed fist.

"Examiner." Pell's voice. Calm, pleasant, and some way off. Further than it should have been. "Are you injured?"

"Bruised. You?"

"I've lost my lamp, and the stylus, which is company property, and I seem to be sitting on the edge of something with water under it."

I felt round me on hands and knees. A narrow shelf of stone; a wall on my left, slick and cold; another on my right, close enough to touch with my boot. A slot, a long narrow cleft in the mountain, and we were at the bottom of it. I crawled towards his voice.

The floor stopped.

It ended in a sharp edge, and below it came cold air, and spray, and the roar. I dropped a pebble. It took a long time to hit water.

"Pell," I said. "Keep talking."

"About what?"

"Anything. Your grievances."

He began, in a perfectly level voice, to list them. They were mostly against Pell-4. It gave me time to work it out with my hands. I was on a shelf at one end of the slot; he was on a shelf at the other. Between us the floor had split into a black crack too wide to jump even in daylight, and far down it the river was going through the mountain like a train.

I reached up. The walls rose further than I could reach, a metre apart on my side, so I could press a palm on each. On his side, by the sound of him, they flared wider.

"Associate," I said. "Can you reach both walls?"

A pause, and the faint scuff of someone neat trying to touch two things at once. "I can reach one wall," said Pell. "The other appears to be a theory."

"Then we have a problem."

"I'd noticed." I heard a case unclip. "I have a proposal, Examiner. My net-launcher fires a weighted line. I believe I can put a line across this crack, and we could rig a crossing, and then—"

"And then?"

"And then I would, of course, have to log what the net had recovered." A small embarrassed cough in the dark. "It's not my decision. The launcher records it. It's company equipment. Anything it brings in is entered as recovered materials."

I sat on my shelf in the dark, knees bleeding through my trousers, and laughed until my ribs hurt.

"You want me to be salvage."

"It would be purely a matter of record," said Pell, wounded. "Nothing personal."

"Everything documented."

"That's mine," he said, with real feeling, and then, after a long pause in which the river went on, in a different voice, much less pleasant and much more honest: "Examiner. I don't know how to get out of here."

"No," I said. "I do."

And then I looked down at my hands, because something had changed, and I couldn't at first tell what.

They were glowing.

Not enough to light the slot. But under each nail there was a faint blue-green crescent, soft as the gills on the cathedral wall, and the thumb I'd scraped the sample with was brightest of all. Ten small moons in the dark. I moved them in front of my face, and they moved, and I could see them and nothing else in the world.

Something had come in under my nails while I had my palms on the wall, and liked it there, and stayed.

"Examiner?" said Pell. "You've gone quiet."

I took my slate out of my coat. The case had cracked in the fall; the light behind the screen was dead, and the screen was black. But a slate's screen doesn't need its own light, any more than a page does. It only needs some.

I spread my fingers over it like a woman warming her hands at a stove, and in the blue-green glow of my own fingernails the letters came up out of the dark, one line at a time.

CLAIM UC-114. Biological status: none (subsurface; inert).

I could read it as clear as a ledger on a sunny desk.

"What," said Pell, from across the dark, in a voice I would remember with pleasure for the rest of my life, "is that?"

"Evidence," I said.

I found the line, and touched it, and opened a Discrepancy, there in the bottom of a mountain, with the river under me and my hands lit like a ghost's.

In the matter of CLAIM UC-114, Ridge Undercroft. The Register holds the subsurface inert. The world holds a living network, fungal, carrying sugar and signal over kilometres, joined through root to the Spill above, and repairing itself after damage. The Examiner has felt it, been lit by it, and is presently reading by it. Class: Misfiled. Correction: biological status, LIVING NETWORK. Case open.

For evidence I entered the dam, and the heel casts, and the five red-stemmed seedlings under the skylight, and a sample of the network taken under my own right thumbnail, still glowing. Then, after a moment, I entered myself, as the witness who could prove the glow was alive: one Examiner, in situ.

The Register thought about it for no time at all.

CLAIM UC-114. RECLASSIFIED: LIVING NETWORK (subsurface). Workings may not resume. Blasting, netting and removal prohibited without survey. Renewal: SUSPENDED.

There was a short silence on the far side of the crack. Then, very faintly, I heard the soft chime of a ledger case receiving an update.

"Examiner," said Pell, at last. "That's very irritating."

"I know."

"I'll have to file something."

"I'm counting on it." I put the slate away. My nails glowed in the dark like ten small coals. "Now I'm going to climb out, Associate. And then I'm going to get you out. In that order. Don't move."

"I wasn't going to," said Pell. "I've filed a note against moving."

* * *

My first season with the Survey, I was twenty-one, on a limestone world where the rain ate the ground like sugar. One night the cook tent went into a sinkhole with the cook's boy inside it. The only person small enough to go down after him was the rope boss, who was sixty and had lost two fingers to a winch, and the only person small enough to go down after her was me.

Her name was Rook. She smelled of tobacco and lanolin. The hole was a slot like a letter box, black and dripping. When the boy had been hauled out, swearing, the rope was too frayed to take the two of us, and she looked at me in the light of her headlamp and said, "Right. We'll walk."

"Walk where?"

"Up." She put her back flat against one wall of the slot and her boots against the other, and hung there, sitting on nothing, comfortable as a cat on a chair. "Never climb with your hands, Pastern. Your legs are twice your arms. Hands are for keeping your balance and pointing at things. Push, don't pull. Back on one wall, feet on the other. Then walk up the inside of the world."

She went up a hand's breadth at a time, talking the whole way about a man she'd nearly married. I went up after her, shaking. Halfway up I looked down, and she said, without looking, "Down's not a direction, Pastern. It's a mood," and I laughed so hard I nearly fell, and didn't, and at the top she gave me a sip of something from a tin cup that took the skin off my throat.

* * *

I put my back against the near wall of the slot. I put my boots flat on the far one.

I pushed, and my weight came off the shelf, and I hung between the walls, sitting on nothing, over a river I couldn't see, my glowing hands flat on the rock beside my hips.

Push, don't pull.

Back up a hand's breadth. Boot. Boot. My thighs burned after twenty; after forty they were on fire. Where the walls were furred, the slot glowed a little round my fingers and showed me the next half-metre of black shining rock, and went dark again. I climbed up the inside of the mountain in a little travelling pool of my own light.

Below me, Pell had stopped listing grievances. I could hear him breathing.
