"Examiner," he said, once. "How far?"

"Down's not a direction," I said, through my teeth. "It's a mood."

"I'll write that down," said Pell, faintly.

At the top there was a lip, and rubble, and air that moved, and far off the faint blue-green of a living wall. I got an elbow over, then a knee, and rolled onto broken stone and lay with my legs shaking like a new foal's and my ten fingernails glowing at the roof.

Then I got up, because there was a man in a hole.

I worked along the top of the slot in the dark until Pell's voice came up from directly below me. On the rim there, half buried in the rubble, was an iron rod driven into the rock: one of the old consolidation rods, with its bolt-plate furred over like a button. I put my weight on it. It held. B4 holds, I thought, and nearly laughed again.

I tied the driver's rope round it with a hitch Rook would have checked twice and passed, and paid it out until I felt it go slack on his ledge.

Then I took out my pad of paper forms and a pencil, and knelt on the rubble, and wrote by the light of my own hands.

INSPECTORATE OF DISCREPANCIES. Form 9: Assistance Rendered. One (1) line, forty metres, brown, privately owned, lent to Associate Pell (7) of Varro-Tandem Bioreference at the bottom of a hole in CLAIM UC-114, now LIVING NETWORK. The line is lent and not given. The Associate is to acknowledge receipt below. The Associate is not recovered materials. He is a person who fell in. Signed: O. Pastern, Examiner.

I folded it once, and tied it to the rope with a twist of thread from my cuff, and let it slide down.

After a moment the rope twitched.

"Examiner," came Pell's voice, up the slot, in the dark. "There's a form."

"There is."

"I'm being asked to sign for it."

"You're welcome to object."

A long pause, filled with the river. Then the faint scratch of a spare pencil, because he is the sort of man who would carry one.

"For the record," Pell said, "I'm still going to recover him."

"For the record," I said, "you're going to have to climb out first. Hands for balance. Push with your legs."

And I left him there, with my rope and my form, and walked away towards the light.

* * *

The skylight was closer than I'd thought. The living wall led me there, glowing ahead of me, the way you'd show a guest to the door, and I came out into the white column of real light beside the mound with the five red stems in it, blinking like a mole.

The roots of the fallen tree hung down the shaft like a ladder somebody had stopped making halfway. Above them, a disc of evening sky the colour of a bruise.

And in the disc, a shape, leaning over.

"Examiner," said Hask, from the top of the shaft, in the voice of a mountain that has spent a long day trying not to clear its throat. "The hill moved."

"It did."

"I heard it in my third stone. It is the cracked one. It hears very well now." A grinding pause. "I came to where it moved."

I climbed. The roots were a staircase after the slot, but near the top my legs went, and I hung with one arm through a root and couldn't find the last two metres. Then Hask's top stone came down the shaft on its long ledge of others, the notary's ell, like an arm if you were generous about arms, and I put both my hands on it, and he lifted me out of the hill like a tooth.

He set me down in the moss. I lay in it and looked up at him, and at the bruise-coloured sky over him, and at the first star.

Between his seventh stone and his eighth, beside the first pale leaf, there was a second. Smaller. Still folded, like a ticket in a fist. The cold breath of the shaft had been coming up over it all afternoon.

"Hask," I said. "You've got another one."

"I am aware," said Hask. "I shall need to amend the amendment."

I laughed, and held my hands up against the dusk. They were dirty and grazed across every knuckle, and under every nail glowed that soft cold crescent of blue-green, like the hands of somebody who has come back from somewhere they ought not to have.

"Examiner," said Hask, after looking at them for some time. "Your hands are haunted."

"They're evidence."

"Is everything evidence?"

"Today," I said, "yes."

* * *

The grey skiff came over the ridge as the stars came out, quiet as a filing cabinet, and set down on the moss without disturbing it, and a man got out.

Tall, thin, very neat. Company-grey suit without a crease. Silver hair combed flat. Cedar and warm toner. On his lapel, a small enamel pin, white on grey.

12.

"Examiner Pastern," he said, in a pleasant baritone. "Associate Pell. Varro-Tandem Bioreference. I'm so sorry to disturb your evening." He inclined his head to Hask. "Proxy. I'm told the Orrun prefer not to be touched, so I shan't offer." "I understand there's been a slight setback in the matter of the recovery. I've been sent as a backup."

There was a sound from the shaft behind me: a scrape of good shoes on root, a grunt, a long, effortful, deeply undignified scramble. Then a hand came over the lip, and an arm, and Associate Pell, number seven, hauled himself out of the hill and onto the moss on his elbows.

He had lost a shoe. His suit was grey now in a quite different sense, dust from collar to cuff, torn down one sleeve, with a faintly glowing blue-green smear on the knee. His silver hair stood up in a crest. He had my rope coiled neatly over one shoulder, because of course he had, and my form folded into his top pocket.

He got to his feet. He saw Pell-12.

They looked at each other across three metres of moss, two identical men in the starlight, one perfect and one filthy, and the temperature on that hillside dropped by about ten degrees.

"Twelve," said Pell-7.

"Seven," said Pell-12. "I'm relieved to see you. HQ was concerned."

"Backup," said Pell-7.

"A precaution. Nothing personal."

"Everything documented," said Pell-7, and his pleasant baritone cracked across the middle like a slab of dead rock. "That's mine. You were issued after me—"

"The licence," said Pell-12 gently, "refers to all of us as the Associate."

"I am filing," said Pell-7, "a grievance."

"I've already filed one," said Pell-12, "against yours."

I sat in the moss with my glowing hands round the flask and did not help. Hask stood beside me with two leaves in his joint, enjoying himself in a way no stone in the Tally has any business doing.

Pell-12 turned to me with the air of a man who has decided to be the more professional copy. "Examiner, as a courtesy," he said, "that the company has a fix on the subject's living trace, as of this afternoon. Forty kilometres north, and moving." He smiled, very pleasantly. "Where the flowers are. I'll be going ahead by air. Seven, I'm afraid, will have to follow on foot. There's only room for one in the skiff."

He walked back to the grey skiff. Pell-7 watched him go with an expression I have only ever seen on Hask when someone sits on him.

The skiff lifted without a sound and went north, a grey speck against the stars. Behind me the hill breathed out its cold sweet cellar breath, and when I laid my palm flat on the moss the hum came up into my teeth, wider than it had been that morning.

I unscrewed the flask. Steam came up, and tar, and raisins.

"Item fourteen," said Hask.

I drank it anyway. Then I took out my slate, and held my hand over the cracked black screen, and by the light of my own fingernails I read, in the dark, on a hillside, with no budget at all, that I had eight days left.


## Chapter Seven: The Hive Remembers

> BLOCK 14 · CLOVER-VIOLET (line CV-3) · POLLINATOR: HIVE LINE 22 ALL BLOCKS SCENT-KEYED TO STANDARD. NO PERFUME. NO SWEETS. NO SUDDEN MOVEMENTS. THE HIVE REMEMBERS WHAT YOU SMELL LIKE.
>
> — enamel signboard at the edge of a pollination block, the Hiveplains; weathered silver, the company's name long since worn off
>

I put my hand on the stone, because it asked.

It's evening before I can open my eyes without crying, and the rock by the waterfall pool has gone from hot to just a rock. I lie on my front with my palm flat and my jaw on the stone, and the hum comes up out of the hill, wide and slow, thread after thread waking up and passing it on.

I wait for the voice to say something.

It doesn't, for a long time. It's just listening, behind my eyes, not hungry for once, as if it's trying to hear one voice in a crowd that's moved off round a corner.

It's gone, it says at last. The taste. It's gone too deep.

"We could go back in."

No. Quick, like Ma pulling Tilly back from the stove. Then, softer: No. Not that way. Later.

"Later where?"

It doesn't answer. Across the valley, the enormous thing takes a step. I count without meaning to. Forty-nine, fifty, fifty-one. Another. The ground carries it into my ribs like a door shutting in a big house.

I tie no knot, because it's still the thirteenth, and I sleep on the rock with the waterfall talking to my spine all night. I dream about my pond, green-gold and much deeper than it ever was, and wake up hungry.

* * *

I smell the Hiveplains before I see them.

It's halfway across the valley on the fourteenth morning, my eyes mostly working again, when the wind comes down off the hills in front of me, warm and sweet and so thick I stop walking.

Honey.

Not like honey. Honey, and hot wax, and crushed green stems, the way the bee shed smells in high summer when Ma has the lids off and the whole yard is drowsy with it. I stand in the long grass with my mouth open and my eyes shut and I'm home for one whole breath, the mill wheel going, Tilly singing the tomato song wrong on purpose, and then I open my eyes and I'm not.

Oh, that's loud, says the voice, interested for the first time all morning.

I go up the valley side through grass to my waist, and come over the top, and there they are.

The hills roll away as far as I can see, and every hill is a colour. Not mixed, the way the Spill is. Blocks. Great straight-sided blocks of flowers laid over the land like quilts on a line: violet, a whole hillside of it; then saffron, deep as an egg yolk; then a blue so blue it hurts the backs of my eyes, still raw from the dark. The edges between the blocks are knife-straight, like the farm cut where the Spill began. Somebody planned this. Somebody stood here with a map.

And standing up out of the blocks, here and there, as tall as the mill and fatter, are the hives.

The air between them is moving, specks, gold and dark, millions, a haze over every block like heat over a road. The sound goes straight into my jaw and my new skull: a hum so big and low it's less a sound than a floor I'm standing on. It's got notes in it. A long held chord, the way the meeting house sounds when everyone is humming the first line of a hymn before they find the words.

It's beautiful. It's the most beautiful thing I've ever seen, and I've seen the stars now, and the Undercroft lit up like a cathedral.

It takes me nearly an hour, sitting on the hilltop with the sun on my neck, to notice what's wrong with it.

The bees aren't landing.

* * *

I go down into the violet first, because it's nearest and because I can't not.

The flowers come to my chest, each head a ball of little purple tubes packed tight as a pincushion, so loaded with pollen it comes off on my sleeves in gold smears. Under the honey and wax there's a taste on the back of my tongue: pepper, like a cut radish, and under that, tin.

And under that, coming off the whole plain like a cold draught under a door, something I didn't expect.

Hunger.

I know hunger. I've been eating beetles. The whole beautiful place smells like the inside of an empty bowl.

There are bees everywhere. Long as my thumb, gold and black, with furred backs like a bumblebee's and long clear wings that flash when they turn. They fly low over the flowers, so close they ruffle the petals. One hovers a finger's breadth above a violet head, feelers working, tasting. Then it lifts and tries the next one, and doesn't land on that either.

There are dead ones in the stems. Lots. A brittle gold litter on the ground, crunching under my feet.

It's like watching someone walk past a table full of bread, says the voice, saying, "No, no, I'm looking for bread."

At the corner of the block there's an enamel board on a post, gone silver in the weather so the letters only show when I tip my head to the light. BLOCK 14, and CLOVER-VIOLET, and a lot of small print, and at the bottom, in big letters: THE HIVE REMEMBERS WHAT YOU SMELL LIKE.

"Cheerful," I say.

And then the hum changes key.

It's right behind me. I turn, and there's a hive in the next block over, a great lumpy tower of grey paper and gold wax, and a piece of the air in front of it has come loose and is coming towards me. A gold-and-black cloud the size of the bakehouse, with a rising, interested whine in it.

I don't run. I'd like to say I decide not to. My legs just don't get the message in time.

They hit me like warm rain.

All over. Hundreds of little hooked feet, light as dandelion fluff, the weight of them adding up until my shirt sags. They walk across my eyelids. One is on my bottom lip, and I can feel its feelers tapping, tapping, at the corner of my mouth, like Tilly trying to get a sweet out of my fist.

Hands first, says Ma, somewhere a long way behind my eyes, in her bee-yard voice. Worry after.

My hands are already coming up to swat. I make them come down, slowly, as if the air is cold syrup, until they're hanging at my sides with bees walking over the knuckles. The hum round my head is a sort of singing, and inside it I can feel them tasting me with their feet and feelers. Hundreds of little tongues.

They aren't stinging.

"What," I say, without opening my mouth, which comes out as "Mmmh."

They think you're a flower, says the voice. It sounds delighted. A very big flower that walked in from the hills. You smell of their food, a bit, in your sweat. Like a memory. It tastes, through my skin. Honey. You've eaten a lot of honey in your life.

Ma's dark honey, on porridge, in the cake, off the spoon when her back was turned. It tastes faintly of clover, because the bees in Firstfield go to the clover strip behind Saul's east rows, there being nowhere else to go.

The cloud on me shifts and grumbles, working out that I'm not a flower after all, and the singing round my head goes down two notes into something I can only call disappointed. They pour back towards their tower and leave me standing in the violet with my arms at my sides and pollen in my eyelashes and every hair on my body standing up.

I let out a breath I've been holding since Saturday.

That's when Biscuit, who has been entirely still against my stomach this whole time, puts its head out of my collar to see what's happening.

* * *

There is one bee left, on my collarbone, walking in a puzzled circle, and Biscuit's face comes up right beside it. They look at each other from a finger apart.

Biscuit's throat goes amber. Very amber. Its whiskers come forward.

"Biscuit," I say, "no."

Biscuit has never once in its life heard the word no and taken it as anything but advice.

It eats the bee.

Or it tries to. There's a crunch, and a high outraged sound like a kettle that's been insulted, and Biscuit falls out of my shirt into the violet with its paws over its mouth and all four back legs kicking. Its top lip swells as I watch, fat and shiny as a split sausage, until its whiskers stick straight out sideways and its face looks like it's carrying a small pillow.

Then its eyes go slow.

It blinks, very carefully, as if blinking is a new idea. It yawns, which with that lip is a terrible thing to see. It tips over onto its side, all six legs folded, and starts to snore.

I crouch over it with my heart going. "Is it—"

It's fine, says the voice. Numb. Sleepy. The sting doesn't kill. It makes you not care for a bit. It tastes, curious. Mostly lip. It'll be furious when it wakes up.

A bee lands on Biscuit's snoring head, stands there a moment as if to say and another thing, and flies off.

I laugh until I have to lie down too, and the hungry plain goes on humming round us in its sad, enormous chord.

* * *

By dusk I've walked three blocks with a sleeping otter in my shirt.

The hives are built of grey stuff like wasp paper, layers and layers of it, chewed and smoothed, and where the paper's worn thin there's comb showing through, whole walls of it, gold, glowing with the low sun behind. Through it, like looking through a dirty window, I can see bees, thousands, packed on the comb, doing something.

Some of them are dancing.

I watch one for a long time. She runs a little straight line up the comb, waggling her back end so hard she blurs, loops round to the right, runs the line again, loops round to the left. The bees round her lean in and touch her with their feelers. Then they turn away. She's telling them something, over and over, and they've stopped listening.

The wax is warm under my hand and smells of honey and old sweat. With my jaw to the paper, the hive is a drum, a hundred people talking in the next room, and under it, all through the tower, a thin high whine like a kettle left on low. I don't know what it is. I don't like it.

Round the hive's mouth, a ragged doorway as wide as a cart, there are bigger bees, long as my hand, dark, with shiny backs like beetle armour. When I come within ten paces, three of them walk out and stand there, feelers out. Not flying. Just standing, the way Elder Marrow stands at the meeting house door when he thinks you've been running.

I don't go any closer.

Guards, says the voice. They don't know what you are. They're deciding.

"What happens if they decide wrong?"

I'd imagine the rest of the hive comes out to discuss it.

I back away very slowly and find the corner where the hive's shadow falls, and take out Saul's twist of paper. It's so light now I can feel through it how few are left. I push four into the dark earth at the hive's foot, where old fallen wax has made the soil sweet, first knuckle deep and not a hair more.

"In case wherever you're going hasn't got proper ones," I tell them.

Bees, says the voice, don't eat tomatoes.

"Tomatoes don't need bees. Saul shakes them." I pat the soil flat. "He goes down the rows on a Thursday and taps every truss with his stick, like a man telling them off."

Does that work?

"He's got the best tomatoes in Firstfield. He hates it."

That night the hive-towers all over the plain light up gold in the last of the sun, every one, like lanterns hung in a giant's orchard. I sleep in the lee of ours with its thin hungry whine in my spine, and I tie the fourteenth knot.

* * *

There's a honey bog in block fifteen. I find it the hard way.

It's the next morning, with Biscuit still snoring into my stomach, and the grass ahead of me is green and lush and lovely, and I put my foot in it, and my foot keeps going. Under the grass there's no ground, only something thick and dark gold that smells so sweet it makes my teeth hurt, and it's got my leg to the shin and it's pulling, slowly, like a dog that doesn't want to give a stick back.

I throw myself backwards and my plated foot comes out with a long sucking sound and a rope of gold hanging off it. Sitting on solid ground, I look twice, the way Saul says. The grass over the bog is greener than the grass round it, taller, in a perfect circle like a green island, because underneath it honey has been spilling out of some fallen comb for years and the grass has been drinking it.

"Look twice," I say, in Saul's voice, which is mostly a grumble.

You looked once, says the voice, and then you stood in it.

I lick my foot, because I'm starving. It tastes of old honey and dirt and, very faintly, of a bee who has been dead a long time.

It tastes of clover.

* * *

I sit on the edge of the bog with the sticky on my fingers, tasting. Darker than Ma's, but the same note in it, sweet and green and a bit like hay.

I bite a saffron flower. Burnt sugar and the inside of a penny. I go back to the violet. Pepper, tin. I walk all morning with my mouth full of flowers to the blue, and it tastes like cold water poured over hot iron and makes my eyes water.

None of them taste of clover.

They used to, the voice says, quiet and careful, the way it tasted the grazers' guts. All of them. It's underneath. Very faint. Like the writing on that board.

I go back to the board in block fourteen and find the line I didn't bother with yesterday, tipping my head so the light catches the ghost of the letters. ALL BLOCKS SCENT-KEYED TO STANDARD.

"Scent-keyed," I say.

One smell, says the voice. Every colour they liked, all saying the same word, so one kind of bee could feed on all of them. Clever. A pause. Then they went away and left it.

And the flowers got copied, every year, copy of a copy, like the grazers' fermenters. The violet drifting off towards pepper. The saffron towards pennies. The blue towards iron. And the bees handing it down, queen to queen, comb to comb, the one smell that means food, while the flowers walked off and changed their names.

The bees mobbed me because I was the only thing on the whole plain that smelled right.

"They're starving," I say, "in a field full of dinner."

Yes.

"Because nobody told them it's the same dinner."

Yes. A pause. Could you?

I look at the hive. The dancer behind the wax, telling them and telling them.

"I don't speak bee."

No, says the voice. But your mother does.

* * *

The smoker smelled of burning sacking and dry grass, and when Ma squeezed the little leather bellows it went huff and a fat grey worm of smoke came out of its spout and lay down over the hive, slow and lazy, like a cat.

I was small. Small enough that the bee veil Ma had pinned onto my hat came down past my knees, so I saw the whole yard through a fog of black net, like being inside a raincloud.

"Slow," she said. "Everything slow. Bees think fast is the same as cross."

She lifted the lid. The smell came up like the inside of the honey cake, and the hum went up a note and settled. Ma's sleeves were rolled to the elbow. She never wore the gloves. She said they made her hands stupid.

She drew out a frame, slowly, and turned it to the light, and the comb was solid with bees, crawling, shining. "There," she said. "Watch her."

One bee, running up the comb in a straight line, waggling so hard she blurred. Loop round. Straight line again. Waggle. Loop the other way.

"She's telling them where the clover is," said Ma. "The straight line's the way to go. Straight up the comb means straight at the sun. Lean it over, and they lean their flying. And the more she waggles, the further it is." She tipped her head at it, smiling. "She's found Saul's east strip again. She's very pleased with herself."

I didn't believe her. Then I watched the bees round the dancer climb off the frame and fly out, straight towards the east rows, as if somebody had drawn them a map.

"And they talk with their noses," Ma said. "When they're happy it smells like this." Warm wax. Honey. "When they want everyone home, they stick their bottoms up and fan it, see that one, at the door? Smells like lemon balm. Come on. Come home. This way." She sniffed. "And when they're cross."

"What do they smell like when they're cross?"

"Like Master Aldous's drawer."

Pear drops. Sharp and sweet. I was going to laugh, and then something walked across my face inside the veil.

A bee. Inside. It had got in somewhere at the neck, and it walked up my cheek and onto my eyelid, and I felt every one of its feet, and I screamed. My hands went up.

"Bram."

Her hands got there first. Not fast. That's the thing I remember. The hum climbing, the air going sharp and sweet, pear drops, a cloud lifting off the open hive, and Ma's hands coming up through it as slow and steady as if she were lifting bread out of the oven. One on my wrist, bringing my hand back down. One at my veil, lifting the hem, just a finger's width, so the bee on my eye could find the way out.

The bees were all over her arms. Hundreds. A crawling gold sleeve to the elbow. She didn't look at them once. She was looking at me.

"Hands first," she said, quiet. "Worry after."

The bee walked off my eyelid and out under the hem. The cloud settled. Ma lowered her arms, slowly, and the bees on them walked down to her fingertips and dropped off, one at a time, like drops off a wet branch, back onto the comb.

Then she let out a long breath, and I saw her hands shake. Just once. Just after.

* * *

I sit down in front of the hive in block fourteen with my back to the sign and Biscuit still snoring in my lap, and I say it all out loud, so the voice can hold it.

"The dance. The straight line is the sun. The waggle is how far." I hold my hands up and turn them over, like I'm showing Ma they're clean. "Lemon balm is come home. Pear drops is cross. Honey and warm wax is happy. And clover is food."

Yes, says the voice, very still. They say it with their skin. Little pockets that make a smell and let it go. A pause. You've got the same. All over you. The places your sweat comes out.

Dr. Ommery's chart of the flayed man, with Jory's moustache on it. The skin was drawn like a slice of cake, and down in the bottom layer, little curled tubes like the springs in Gran's chair, each with a thread going up to the top. Sweat glands, she said, tapping. Two million of them, or thereabouts. Everyone's a kettle, Bram. That's how you cool.

"Sweat glands," I say. "Two million of them. In the bottom of the skin, coiled up, like springs. With a little pipe to the top."

That I can hold. It sounds pleased with me, the way Master Aldous sounded when you got a long word right. I can teach them new words. You'd only have to want to say it, and your skin would say it. Shall I?

"What does it cost?"

Food. Heat. Sleep. The same answer it always gives, and then the other part, a bit more gently. And it goes the other way too. If you can say it, you can hear it. Everything here talks with its skin. You'll smell what they feel. Everyone. All the time. You won't stop.

Biscuit snores in my lap, its lip still the size of a damson. I look at the hive, at the thin high whine in its walls. I've worked out what that is, I think. It's the sound of thousands of things being hungry at once.

"Yes," I say.

It starts in my armpits.

I'd like that to sound braver. It's an itch like the time Jory bet me I wouldn't roll in the nettle patch behind the mill, and it spreads to my palms and the back of my neck and then everywhere, all two million of them at once. I scratch so hard I tip Biscuit off my lap. I roll in the violet saying words Gran would wash my mouth for, and the hunger comes after it like a cart after a horse, so bad I pull up a violet root and eat it, dirt and all.

Then it stops.

I lie there, panting, sweating. Smelling my own sweat. It smells like me, mostly. Salt and boy and grass.

Then Biscuit wakes up.

* * *

Biscuit wakes up on its back in the violet with its lip like a damson and its whole body remembering, all at once, the bee, and the indignity, and the fact that I didn't stop it, which I did, I said no. It rolls over. It looks at me. Its throat goes a dark, dull, sullen red.

And the sulk comes off it like heat off a stove.

It smells like wet socks. Like Gran's compost cabbage water on the third day. Like the inside of Jory's boot after the harvest dance, and under all that, sour and mournful, milk that's been left in a sunny window and has thought about its life. It's not a smell. It's an opinion. It sits behind my eyes and complains.

I scrabble backwards through the violet on my elbows with my eyes watering.

Biscuit follows me, sulking. It turns its back on me, deliberately, and sits there, three paces off, glowing red and radiating.

Ah, says the voice. I did mention.

"Make it stop."

It's an otter. I can't make it stop. I can only tell you it says it is never, ever speaking to you again, and also, it would like something to eat.

I give Biscuit two beetles off the hive wall and the end of the honey from the bog on my fingers. It eats them with its back still turned. The sulk drops, slowly, from wet socks to something more like a damp dishcloth. I can live with a damp dishcloth.

Then I turn round to the plain, and breathe in.

And the whole Hiveplains talks to me at once.

The violet saying here, here, here in an accent so strong I can only just understand it. The saffron saying it in pennies. The bees overhead, each trailing a thin thread of smell like a voice, nothing, nothing here, sad and dry. And from the hive behind me, so thick I could lean on it: hunger. Not a taste now. A word. An empty bowl with a spoon going round in it, scraping.

And from the doorway, faint, from the dancer's back, from every bee that brushes past her and turns away, the other word. The old one. The one everyone remembers.

Clover. Clover. Where's the clover.

I sit there in the violet, smelling everybody's feelings, until the sun goes down. Then I tie the fifteenth knot, and lie awake with my nose full of a whole hive's hunger, and think.

* * *

I'm up before the bees.

It's the sixteenth morning, grey and wet, the flowers shut tight against the dew. Biscuit's in my shirt, sulking at a low, bearable dishcloth. I've thought it through all night, the way I thought through the grazers. The bees know one word for food. The flowers have forgotten how to say it. So somebody has to say both at once, the new smell and the old word, on one thing, until the bees learn they're the same.

Like Tilly learning her letters: Ma pointing at the picture of the apple and saying apple, and at the word and saying apple, until Tilly read it on her own and was so pleased she cried.

"Picture," I say, "and word."

You're the word, says the voice. What's the picture?

I take my shirt off, with Biscuit in it, and lay it in the grass. Then I wade into the violet, and I lie down, and I roll.

I roll in the saffron, too, and crawl through into the blue and roll in that until my eyes sting. The pollen gets in my ears and up my nose and down my trousers. When I stand up I'm gold and purple and that terrible hurting blue all over, like a painted post at the harvest fair, and I smell of pepper and pennies and hot iron, all three words, all at once.

Biscuit's sulk goes up, briefly, to wet socks. I think that's a laugh, actually. I think it's laughing at me.

"Now," I say. "Clover."

I want to say it. That's all it takes. I think of Ma's honey on the spoon, and the clover strip behind the east rows on a hot afternoon, and my skin, all two million of it, warms and prickles.

And I smell of clover.

Not the faint memory the bees found on the first day. Loud, the way the bakehouse smells of bread from three houses off.

Ooh, says the voice. Well done.

The sun comes over the hills, and the first scouts come out of the hive in block fourteen to look again for a word that isn't there. One turns in the air and comes straight at me. Then a hundred. Warm rain, little hooked feet, and this time my hands stay down. With my new nose I can smell what they're saying to each other as they crawl on me, a quick excited chatter: clover, clover, CLOVER, and then, as their feet find the pollen, puzzled, —and this? —and this?

I stand very still in the morning, covered in bees, saying clover with my whole skin.

Picture, I think at them. Word. Picture. Word.

A scout lifts off my shoulder with violet pollen on her legs, two little purple lumps like saddlebags. She hangs in front of my face, turning, tasting. Then she drops to a nodding purple head, a finger's breadth over it, the way they've been doing for years and years.

She lands.

I don't breathe.

Her whole head goes down into one of the little purple tubes. Her back end waggles, once, as if to settle in. She comes up with her face dusted gold, and goes down into the next.

Over the pepper and the tin and the hunger, faint, from one bee, I smell warm wax and honey.

Happy.

"Yes," I say, out loud, too loud, and the bees on my face all lift and settle again in a little startled wave.

* * *

She goes home. I follow, covered in bees, and put my eye to the wax where the paper's worn thin. I know her by the violet on her legs. She pushes through the crowd on the comb, and she starts to dance.

Straight line. Waggle. Loop round. Straight line. Waggle. Loop the other way.

The bees round her lean in. They touch her with their feelers, the way they've been touching every dancer for years. And this time, they don't turn away.

One of them climbs off the comb.

Then six. And the hum against my cheek, in my jaw, my skull, my teeth, starts to change. The thin kettle whine wavers. Finds another note under it, and another. A chord.

Bees pour out of the doorway past the guards, a gold river, and fly straight out over the violet in a long bright line, exactly as if somebody had drawn them a map.

I sit down where I am, against the hive wall, and watch them land.

It's like rain starting. One flower, then a dozen, then the whole near edge of the block nodding as the bees come down, and the air that's been full of dry sad nothing here goes thick and warm and busy. Here. Here. HERE.

By noon I've stood in front of two more hives, rolling and being crawled on and saying clover until my skin is raw and I'm so hungry I eat honey out of a bog with both hands. I don't know how it spreads after that. Maybe a dancer wanders into the next hive. Maybe it's the smell, rolling over the blocks on the wind. But one tower after another, across the hills, I hear the kettle-whine falter and the chord come up underneath it, and the bees come out in long lines like threads unwinding, and land.

Bee on flower, flower to seed, seed to next year's meadow. A plain that's been in bloom and dying for longer than I've been alive will be here next year.

I lie on my back in the blue, which is starting to smell a little of clover too, I swear it, where the bees have been, and the sky above me is full of gold specks going about their business, and none of them are looking for me any more.

Biscuit climbs onto my chest and sits there, glowing amber. The sulk has gone. It smells of river and lamp oil and something warm, almost like bread.

"You're speaking to me," I say.

It says, says the voice, it isn't. It says you smell like a flower and it's very embarrassed for you.

I tie the sixteenth knot lying down, and I go to sleep in the blue with the hive-towers lighting up gold over the hills like lanterns, every one, and every one of them humming.

* * *

The seventeenth morning, I smell them before I hear them.

I'm at the mouth of the hive in block fourteen. The guards know me now; I've been saying their hive's own smell back to them, and they just touch my feet with their feelers when I go past. Biscuit's asleep in my shirt, and I'm crouched by the four tomato seeds at the hive's foot, seeing if they've split yet. They haven't. Saul's never do, till they're ready.

And the wind brings me cedar.

Clean and sharp, like the inside of the good chest at the meeting house where they keep the Charter. And under the cedar, a dry, hot, dusty smell, like ink that's been cooked.

I know that smell. I don't know its name. I smelled it in the Upper House, in the corridor outside the glass room, the night I heard them say draw from him.

I stand up.

They're coming down the hill through the violet, two of them, side by side but not together, the way Jory's da and his brother walk to the meeting house the week after they've argued about a fence. Tall, thin, in grey suits the colour of the hive paper, not a crease on them, holding their arms up out of the flowers like men wading a cold river. Silver hair combed flat. The same face. Not like brothers: the same, as if somebody had cut one man out of paper twice.

One carries a slim black case. The other has a long grey stick with a silver end, and slung on his back something like a fat short gun with a bell on the end.

Under the cedar and the cooked ink, they smell of other things, now that I've got the nose for it. The one on the left smells of something tight and sour, like vinegar in a cut. The one on the right smells eager and hot, like a pan left on the stove.

I could run. But there's a little grey boat on the hilltop behind them, on legs like a beetle's, with a cold blue light on its nose, and I don't know how fast that is. So I stand in front of the hive with my hands at my sides.

They stop ten paces off and brush pollen off their sleeves with the same flick of the same fingers. Each has a little enamel pin on his lapel. A number. The vinegar one says 4. The hot-pan one says 12.

"Good morning," says the one with the 4. He has a lovely voice. Deep and kind, like the man who reads the lessons at midwinter. "You'll be Reference Twelve."

"Bram," I say. My voice goes up in the middle.

"Of course." He opens the black case on his forearm, takes out a stylus, writes something. "Associate Pell. Recovery. Varro-Tandem Bioreference." He nods his head at the other one, without looking at him. "And Twelve."

There are two of us called Twelve, then. I nearly say so. I don't think either of them would find it funny.

"Associate Pell," says the other one.

"The licence," says Four, still writing, "specifies the Associate. Singular. One of us is the Associate. One of us is a number." He smiles at me, a pleasant smile. "We'll be taking you back now. There's a recovery claim. You're company materials. Nothing personal."

"Everything documented," says Twelve, quickly, and then looks annoyed that he's said it at the same time as Four's mouth was already opening.

The tight vinegar smell comes off Four in a wave.

"I'm not materials," I say. "I'm Bram. Son of Wenna."

"I'm sure you are," says Four, and writes it down.

Twelve swings the fat gun off his back. Close to, the bell on the end isn't a bell. It's a coiled net, grey and fine as a spider's web, folded into the mouth.

I take a step back. My heel hits the hive.

The guards come out of the doorway behind me, slow heavy feet on the old wax, and stand by my ankles, heads turning. Tasting. They touch my feet, and know me, and turn their heads to the two men in grey.

And taste cedar and cooked ink. And something they've never smelled in their lives.

The hum behind me goes up a note.

"That's a hive," says Twelve, with interest, looking past me. "Look, Four. They're enormous. Is that in the brief?"

"If you'd read the brief, Twelve," says Four, "we would have been here yesterday."

"I flew all night—"

"I'll be noting that."

I'm frightened. I want to be clear about that. My knees have gone soft like dough, and I can smell my own fear coming off my skin, sour and thin. I'm frightened, and I'm thinking, the way you think when you've climbed the bell tower on a dare and suddenly remember about the way down.

I think: the hive remembers what you smell like.

And I think: Ma's hands.

"In Firstfield," I say, and my voice nearly works, "when you meet somebody, you say whose you are."

Four looks up from his case, polite. "I'm aware of the custom."

"Bram," I say. "Son of Wenna, of the house of Hale. Grandson of Gran. Brother of Tilly." I wipe my palm on my trousers, very slowly, hands first, worry after, and I hold it out to him. "And you shake hands. It's rude not to."

Four looks at my hand.

The vinegar smell goes up and down. Behind him Twelve has the net gun on my middle and is watching Four sideways, hot as a stove door opening.

"Courtesy," says Four at last, "costs nothing." He tucks the stylus behind his ear and steps forward, two neat steps, and takes my hand. His hand is dry and cool and smooth as paper. "Associate Pell."

I think about pear drops.

I think about Master Aldous's drawer, opened for someone who'd been brave. I think about my palm, all the little coiled springs in it, and I want, very much, to be cross.

My palm goes hot.

Four lets go and steps back and takes the stylus from behind his ear. He sniffs, very slightly, and frowns, like a man who's found a sweet in his coat pocket he doesn't remember putting there.

"And you," I say to Twelve, holding out my hand again.

Twelve looks at Four. Four looks at Twelve. I watch it happen: Twelve's not going to be the one who wasn't polite. Not with Four writing everything down.

Twelve shifts the net gun to his left arm and steps forward and shakes my hand so hard my teeth click. "Associate Pell," he says, loudly, looking at Four.

"Noted," says Four.

I let go. My palm's tingling. They both smell, very faintly, over the cedar and the ink, of Master Aldous's drawer.

"You might want to stand very still," I tell them.

* * *

The guards smell it first.

Their feelers go out, stiff, towards the two grey men. Then all three open their wings, great clear fans with a shine like oil on water, and buzz, deep and angry and close.

The hive behind me answers.

The chord breaks. It climbs, high and sharp, into my jaw and my skull like a saw. The paper walls shiver. And out of the doorway, out of every crack and gap in the grey, out of the top of the tower, the hive comes up.

All of it. Like smoke. Like the whole thing taking a breath out.

"Ah," says Four.

"Is that—" says Twelve.

I put my arms down at my sides, and I say warm wax and this hive's honey as hard as I can with every bit of skin I've got, and I say it to the cloud the way you'd say your own name, and I close my eyes.

They come past me like a river round a stone.

Thousands. The noise of them so loud it's not noise any more, it's weather. Little feet on my face, tasting, ours, ours, lifting off again. Not one of them stings me. They pour past me and they find the pear drops.

When I open my eyes, the Associates have become two tall shapes made of bees.

Twelve fires the net. It goes off with a thwump and opens in the air like a grey flower, and catches about four hundred bees, and wraps round Four.

"Twelve!"

"It was aimed—"

Four goes over, slowly, like a tree, inside the net and the bees. Twelve swings the grey stick, the one with the silver end, and the end crackles blue, and he hits a cloud of bees with it, which does exactly what you'd think hitting a cloud would do. Then he's hopping. Then he's sitting down. Then he's lying in the short grass with his hair still perfectly combed and his face going slowly, gently, puffy, with the stick fizzing in the grass beside him, saying, "Ow. Ow. Ow," in a lovely deep voice, more slowly each time.

The sound starts to come down. The guards, satisfied, fold their wings. The cloud thins over the two of them and pours back into the hive in a long gold stream, still grumbling, and the chord comes back underneath, ragged, then steadier.

Four is lying in the net with his face swelling up like Biscuit's lip. His eyes have gone slow. He's still got the black case. He's opened it on his chest, and he's trying to write in it, but his hand isn't working, and the stylus keeps sliding off sideways and drawing long lines. So he puts the stylus down, very carefully, and starts writing with his finger, in the pollen on the case's lid.

"Twelve," he says, thickly. "I'm filing a grievance."

"I'm filing one," says Twelve, from the grass, with his eyes shut, "against you."

"For?"

There's a long pause. "I'll think of something," says Twelve, and starts to snore.

The vinegar smell coming off Four has gone soft and woolly. But his eyes are on me, slow and puffed, and I realise, looking down, what he's looking at.

My wrist. The green-gold veins under the skin, the leaf-pattern from the pond. They're glowing, like the shelves in the Undercroft, a soft live light running up my forearm in threads, as if my skin is still talking. And my feet, grey-green and plated, ridged like bark.

"That's," says Four. He swallows. His lovely voice has gone thick and slurred. "That isn't. In the brief."

I should run. I don't. He's frightened too, I can smell it under the sleepy woolly stuff, a thin sharp thread of it, like mine.

"You should put something cold on that," I say. "Mud's good."

Then I step over Twelve, very carefully, and walk away through the violet with the bees going about their business all round me.

* * *

I go round a circle of too-green grass on the edge of the saffron, because I know what's under it, and lie flat on the far side with Biscuit beside me, its throat dimmed right down, because it isn't stupid.

From here I can see the hive. The net. The two grey heaps.

My whole body's shaking, knees and hands and teeth, so hard I put my jaw against the earth to stop them, and then I can hear everything: the hive's chord settling; Biscuit's quick drum of a heart; and far off on the hills to the north, the footfall. Fifty-one. Like it's been walking the whole time.

I count to five hundred, the way Gran counts before she peeks at bread.

That, says the voice, and stops. It doesn't usually stop. Bram. That was—

"I shook hands with them," I say into the dirt. "I shook hands with them."

You did. And then it laughs. I've never heard it laugh before. It's not a sound, exactly. It's a warm fizzing behind my eyes, like the first sip of Gran's elderflower when it's gone a bit too far. You said pear drops into a man's hand and then you said please and thank you.

"Ma would kill me."

Your mother, says the voice, would have done it slower.

Down by the hive, something moves.

Four's sitting up, halfway, still in the net, face like a pudding. He's talking into the case. I put my jaw to the ground, and the earth carries it: a slow, thick, careful voice, saying each word as if it's carrying it across a stream.

"Report. Priority. Upstairs." A pause. A breath. "Specimen is not. Baseline. Specimen exhibits. Active modification. Feet. Skin. Something. Under the skin. Lit." Another pause, longer. "Something is writing in him."

The case gives a little chime. Two notes, bright and clean, like a spoon on a glass. It carries a long way over the flowers.

Four lies back down in the net, very slowly, and folds his hands on the case.

I don't know who upstairs is. I know the Upper House had stairs, and that smell. I lie in the saffron with my glowing wrist pressed into the dirt, to put it out, and it doesn't go out, and I think about somebody, somewhere, a long way off, opening a message and reading the word writing, and sitting up.

We should go, says the voice. Quiet again.

"Yes."

Now, I'd think.

"Yes."

I tie the seventeenth knot with my fingers shaking. Biscuit climbs into my shirt and smells, for once, of nothing but river and lamp oil and otter, which I've decided is the best smell there is.

Then I go north, keeping low, through the blue, which smells of iron and of clover now, both, where the bees have been. Over the next hill. And the next, all day.

On the third hill, the wind turns.

It comes up from the far side, from somewhere beyond the last of the blocks, and it's cool, and it's wet, and it smells of something I've never smelled in my life. Salt. Not salt from the pot: a great wide salt, enormous, rotting and green and alive, like a thousand ponds that someone's left out in the sun to think. It's so big I can smell the size of it.

What is that? says the voice. It sounds young. It sounds as if it hasn't smelled anything new in a very long time.

"I don't know," I say. "Let's find out."

Behind me, the hive-towers are lighting up gold in the evening, one after another, every one of them humming. Somewhere back there a message with my name in it is going up a long set of stairs.

Ahead of me, out past the hills, something enormous takes a step. Fifty-one.

I start walking.


## Chapter Eight: Variance Within Tolerance

> POLLINATOR LINES 18–26 (Hiveplains reserve) · STATUS: STEADY · FIELD VARIANCE: 0.0% · SOURCE: TOWER TELEMETRY, CERTIFIED Where the record holds, the world holds.
>
> — Moriel Bioreference Index, public bulletin no. 6,940, as carried on the news crawl at Margin Station
>

The bees had been angry for about an hour when we came over the last hill, and Associate Pell, in two copies, was lying in the violet arguing with himself about whose fault it was.

I heard the bees first. Two days of walking north from the Undercroft had taught me the plain's sound the way you learn a neighbour's mill: by the second evening it was simply the floor of the world, a long low chord like a chapel humming the first line of a hymn before anybody remembers the words. On the morning of the seventeenth the chord had broken. What came over the hilltop to meet us was high and thin and climbing, a saw going through tin, and it got into the roots of my back teeth and stayed there.

We had left the ridge on the fifteenth, Hask grinding along behind me with two leaves in his joint. Twice he stopped dead, and when I asked why, he said, "Something walks. Every fifty-one of your counts. A long way off." I put my palm to the ground and felt nothing, and wrote it down anyway. Two hills behind us, all that day and the next, a small grey figure followed our trail, limping with great precision.

On the evening of the sixteenth the wind turned and smelled of honey, and hot wax, and crushed green stems, and we came up onto a crest in the last light and saw the towers. Fat and lumpy and taller than anything in Firstfield, standing out of hills laid in blocks of colour with edges like a ruler's, violet and saffron and a blue that hurt, and every tower lit gold from the inside by the low sun, like lanterns hung in a giant's orchard. The air between them was full of sparks, which were bees going home.

I sat and watched until dark. Then I read, by my own fingernails, that I had six days left.

* * *

So: the seventeenth, and the saw in my teeth.

Below us lay a hillside of violet, flowers to my chest, and at its foot a tower of grey paper and gold wax with a doorway as wide as a cart. Round its top and over the flowers in front of it, the air was boiling: a cloud of bees the size of a barn, turning on itself like smoke off a bonfire somebody has thrown wet leaves on. Under the cloud lay two grey heaps. One of them was in a net.

"Hask. Stay here."

"I should like to point out," said Hask, "that there appear to be several million of them."

I went down slowly, because I'm not a fool, and then, because I'm not perfect, I did a foolish thing. A bee hung in front of my face, gold-furred, as long as my thumb, the light coming through its wings in two little rainbows, and before I'd thought anything my hand went up to brush it away.

It stung me on the back of the hand.

It went in like a hot needle and then stopped being anything. My hand went numb, then my wrist, a warm spreading nothing, and with the nothing came a feeling I can only call cheerfulness, idiotic and total, climbing my arm. I stood in the violet and thought it was the funniest hand I had ever seen.

Ten more bees came to look. I lowered the hand to my side the way you lower a full cup, and held still, and after a while they lost interest and went back to the cloud.

So. Slow was not cross. Fast was.

I wrote that down left-handed, because my right had become a cheerful stranger, and went on down through the violet like a woman carrying soup.

* * *

Pell-12 lay on his back by the hive's doorway with his eyes shut, his silver hair still combed flat, his face swollen like a pudding. His compliance wand lay in the grass beside him, fizzing faintly blue, like something muttering in its sleep.

Pell-4 was in the net. It had wrapped him from knees to ears, and inside it with him were perhaps four hundred bees, trapped and saying so. He had his ledger case open on his chest and was writing, very slowly, with one finger, in the pollen on its lid.

The air round them smelled of cedar and warm copier, and of something sharp and sweet I didn't expect: pear drops, like the ones my grandmother kept in a tin for visitors who had been good.

"Examiner," said Pell-4, without turning his head. His baritone had gone thick, every word carried carefully across a stream. "How. Nice."

"Are you hurt?"

"I am. Recovered." His finger crept towards the net-launcher, lying two metres off with its little screen lit. "The launcher. Logs. What it brings in."

I bent, slowly, and read it.

RECOVERED MATERIALS: 1 (one) ASSOCIATE, grade 4. Approx. 400 (four hundred) BEES, ownership not established. Entered to company stock 9:14 am.

It is not right to laugh at a man who has been stung four hundred times. I want that on record. I bent over the launcher with my numb hand pressed to my mouth and laughed until the nearest bees came to see what the matter was, and I had to stop, and stand very still, and think about sad things.

"It was aimed," said Pell-12, eyes shut, "correctly."

"It was aimed," said Pell-4, "at me."

"It was aimed at the boy, and you were in the way, because you were shaking his hand—"

"I was. Being. Civil."

"You were being first."

Pell-4's swollen eyes found me. "He shook. Hands," he said, and under the sting he sounded honestly puzzled, a man handed a form in a language he had studied and still could not read. "He said whose. He was. Then he held out his hand. And then." His finger moved in the pollen. "Pear drops."

The bees were on Pell-4's cuffs and lapel and the backs of his hands, and nowhere else. I didn't understand it. I wrote it down. Both Associates smell of pear drops. The bees are only on the pear drops.

And under it, because I couldn't help it: He shook hands with them first.

* * *

It took most of an hour to get Pell-4 out of the net, and I did it the way you'd defuse a bomb made of honey.

Every fold I lifted let out twenty bees with the saw in their voices. I learned fast, because the price of learning slowly was a second sting, on the side of my neck, which turned half my face into a warm idiot and made me very fond of Pell-4 for ten minutes. After that I moved as if the air were cold syrup. Lift a fold. Wait. Let them walk off my fingers. Lift the next. The sun climbed, the violet steamed, the scent came off it so thick I could taste it, honey and something peppery underneath like a cut radish, and the gold dust stuck to the sweat on my arms until I looked like a saint on a cheap icon. Dead bees crunched under my knees. I noticed that, and put it away for later.

Hask, who had come down against instructions, stood ten paces off and watched a different sort of bee walk out of the doorway towards him.

It was as long as my hand, dark, with a back of shining plates like a beetle's, and it didn't fly. It walked, heavily, and two more came out behind it and stood on the threshold with their feelers out. Guards. The first climbed onto Hask's bottom stone and began, very thoroughly, to taste him.

Hask went completely still. Then his top stone came down on its ledge of the others, the notary's ell held level like the arm of a careful man measuring a doorway, and stopped a finger's breadth from the guard.

A long grinding pause.

"A little more than one tenth of an ell," said Hask. "Snout to tail-root. Item three thousand three hundred and one does not apply. It is not of the larger sort." Deeper, from the bottom of the stack: "I find I am relieved."

"Because you won't have to act?"

"Because," said Hask, "I was concerned I might have to measure all of them."

The guard found him to be a rock, which was correct, and walked back inside, unimpressed.

When Pell-4 was free I fetched mud from a seep and put a cold handful on his face, and on Pell-12's, and on my neck and hand.

"He said mud was good," said Pell-12 dreamily.

"Who did?"

"The boy. Before he walked off." Pell-12 smiled, slow and stupid, through the mud. "You should put something cold on that."

I sat back on my heels in the violet and looked at the two of them for a while.

"Did he," I said.

* * *

Pell-7 arrived at half past two, in one shoe, and went into the honey bog.

I watched it from the edge of the saffron. He came over the hill at his precise limp, gold to the knee, his hair still standing up in the crest the Undercroft had given it, my forty metres of brown rope coiled neatly on his shoulder. He took the straightest line towards us, across a lovely little circle of lawn, greener and taller than any grass round it.

The lawn took him to the knee.

"I'd look twice at that," I said.

He stood sunk in something dark gold and slow, and looked up at me with the face of a man to whom nothing will ever again be a surprise.

"Old honey," I said. "Comb gone down under there years ago, leaking. The grass grows greener over it. It's drinking." I lay flat at the edge and held out the end of my own rope. "Push with your legs. It holds harder if you pull."

He pushed. The bog let him go with a long, bottomless suck, like a giant finishing a drink through a straw, and his remaining shoe stayed behind. The gold closed over it with a small, contented gloop. I put a finger in the honey on his trouser leg and tasted it, because I couldn't not: dark and old and soft and the best I'd ever eaten, with a green summery note in it, like hay, from a flower I couldn't place.

Pell-7 sat up in his socks, coiled the rope, and handed it to me with both hands, the way you'd return a borrowed book, with my Form 9 on top. Under his signature was a new line: Returned in full, Hiveplains, day seventeen. Condition: honeyed.

Then he walked down into the violet and stood over his colleagues.

"Seven," said Pell-4.

"Seven," said Pell-12.

"I am filing," said Pell-7, in a voice like a door shut very gently on a very cold room, "a grievance. Against Four, for being recovered. Against Twelve, for recovering him. And against both of you," he took out his pencil, "for getting here first."

* * *

I left them composing and went to look at the plain, and found I'd been looking at the wrong thing all morning.

In front of the block-fourteen hive, the bees were landing. All along the near edge of the violet they dropped onto the purple heads and went in to the shoulders, and the air over the block was thick and warm and busy, and the chord was back, ragged, like a choir after a fright.

But two blocks over stood a second tower, and its bees were not landing. They flew low over the flowers, a finger's breadth above each head, tasting, lifting, moving on. When I put my ear to that tower's wall the paper was dry and warm and smelled of old wax and nothing else, and inside, under the hum, ran a thin high whine, like a kettle left on low in an empty house. Beyond it a third tower, humming its chord. Beyond that a fourth, whining. A patchwork. As if something had walked across the plain and touched some towers and not others.

I went back to block fourteen and dug.

The Survey teaches you that what's under your boots is a library, if you'll get on your knees and read it. I cut out a square of turf, and another under it, and the side of the hole was a book in layers. On top, a crisp gold litter of the newly dead. Under it a brown mat, wings gone brittle. Under that another, and another, each a pressed brown seam no thicker than a page, with thin grey bands of soil between. Lower down the seams grew thick, almost nothing but bee. I counted them with the point of my knife.

Nineteen, before plain dark earth. The hole smelled of dust and cinnamon and old paper.

I asked the Register what this place was.

HIVEPLAINS RESERVE (former pollination plantations, owner lapsed). Pollinator lines 18–26: STEADY. Field variance 0.0%. Source: tower telemetry, certified. See MORIEL BIOREFERENCE INDEX.

I had seen that word before, on the news crawl over the customs queue at Margin Station, sliding past in six scripts between a toll rise and a soap opera: MORIEL BIOREFERENCE INDEX STEADY. I'd thought nothing of it. It is a word designed so that you'll think nothing of it.

The enamel board at the corner of the block, gone silver in the weather, said this tower's bees were line 22. It also said NO SUDDEN MOVEMENTS, which I could have used at nine that morning, and, at the bottom, in letters the weather hadn't managed to rub out, THE HIVE REMEMBERS WHAT YOU SMELL LIKE. I opened the Index and pulled line 22's daily readings. Hive weight. Forage flights per hour. Hum, as a frequency. Every day for nineteen years.

They were the same.

Not similar. The same, to the last decimal. Hive weight 4,212.6 kilograms, summer and winter, for nineteen years. I scrolled until my thumb ached and the numbers never so much as twitched.

"Hask. What does that look like to you?"

Hask leaned over the slate, which took a while. "It looks very consistent."

"Nothing alive is consistent, Hask."

"I am."

"You're a cairn."

A grinding pause. "I have a leaf," said Hask, with dignity. "Two."

Somewhere at the top of every tower, if the Index was honest about its own source, something was weighing the hive and counting the bees and sending it home; and somewhere between the tower and the Index, the numbers had stopped moving. One good full day, nineteen years ago, picked up and copied out, and copied out, and certified, while the hive under it ate itself down to the comb and the dead piled up in pages under the flowers.

I looked up at the block-fourteen tower. Close to, its paper lay in thousands of chewed swirls like the inside of an oyster, worn so thin in places that the gold comb showed through, and on the comb, like shadows behind a curtain, bees danced: a straight run, a body shaken to a blur, a loop, the run again. Out of the very top, twenty-five metres up, stuck a thin dark spike like the wick of a candle. An iron mast; the bees had built round it. On its tip sat a grey box with a cracked solar cap, the same make as the post at 40-K, and on the box a small green light blinked. Once. A long while later, once.

Still awake. Still writing, every hour, to somebody who had stopped reading.

Four hundred metres west stood another tower, taller by a third, and black. It rose out of the saffron like a burnt tree, split down one side, the wax of its upper half melted and run and set again in drips like candle-grease down a bottle, its mast bent over at the top like a fused fishhook. No bees. The breeze brought me old char and caramel and something like a blown fuse.

"Lightning," said Hask, "hunts the tallest thing."

At the foot of our tower, in the corner where the fallen wax had made the earth soft and sweet, lay a row of four small dimples. A finger had gone in at each, first knuckle deep and not a hair further. They hadn't split. They did nothing whatsoever. I crouched beside them a while and didn't write anything down. I knew what they were.

That evening the towers lit gold again, the humming ones and the whining ones alike, every one beautiful, and I sat with my back to the warm paper of ours and the dead in their nineteen pages under me, and listened to the chord through the wall.

Five days left.

* * *

Halloran called at dawn on the eighteenth, bacon frying.

"Pastern." Tock. "You queried the Bioreference Index."

I hadn't told him. I'd filed the query. Filing is publishing, if you know where to stand.

"I pulled a series."

"You pulled nineteen years of a series, on an Inspectorate seal, at eleven at night, and somebody at Varro-Tandem's field office was awake to watch you, and now somebody at the Chancery's awake as well, wanting to know why an Examiner on a dead case with five days left is tugging the one number on Moriel that half the quarter's credit hangs on." Tock. "Nine million lines of stock, Pastern. If the Index says steady, the money sleeps at night."

"It's been printing one day for nineteen years. Sir, I've dug a hole. There are nineteen years of dead bees in it."

A silence, with frying in it.

"Then it's Chancery business," Halloran said, quite quietly. "Not yours. You've got five days, and they're for a boy. Send the Chancery a note." Tock. "If you're going to call a thing the size of the Index Forged, you bring me the pen it was written with. Not a hole. Not bees. The pen. Can you?"

I looked up at the mast, and the box, and the faint green blink.

"I don't know yet."

"Then send a note," said Halloran. "I've read your file," which was not the same as goodbye, and the call closed like a log falling in a grate.

Send a note. It was reasonable. It was what I'd have told a junior Examiner myself. Put your name on the bottom, send it up the line, and it sits in a drawer, and the Index goes on saying steady, and everybody sleeps.

I had done it before.

* * *

My third season on the Survey I was twenty-three, on a slow brown river world in the Corvel reach, surveying a terrace for a settler charter. Two hundred families had paid for a spring landing. The terrace ran four kilometres along the river like a shelf on a wall, and the soil was so black and soft you could push your arm in to the elbow.

The survey lead was Corran Teague. He was kind. He showed you pictures of his three children whether you asked or not, and every season he brought a jar of pickled plums into the field and shared them out on the last night, one each, like sacraments.

On the second-to-last day I was taking cores under the trees at the back of the terrace, and I put my hand on a trunk, and it came away grey.

Silt. A level band of it round every trunk, higher than my head, like a bathtub ring. Above it, in the forks of the branches, hung rags of river weed dry as paper, and wedged in one, bleached white, the shell of a crayfish as big as my hand, left there by water that had stood two metres deep where I stood. The lichen had only just begun to grow over the silt line. A year. Two at most.

I took it to Teague that night in the mess tent, which smelled of paraffin and plums. He turned the crayfish shell over for a long time.

"Pastern," he said. Tired and kind, the way you'd say it to a child holding up something sharp. "Two hundred families need a terrace by spring. The river hasn't been up there in fifty years."

"It was up there two years ago."

"Once. A freak." He gave me back the shell. "Put it in your notes, if you like."

I watched him type it. Terrace: sound. Flood risk: negligible. I watched him sign it. He gave me a plum, and I ate it, and I said nothing.

Eleven years later I looked it up, at a desk at Margin Station, at night, with nobody watching. The settlement moved uphill after its second spring. Some of the houses are still there, the record says, standing in the river to their windows. Nobody drowned. That was luck, and none of it was mine.

Years after the plum, somebody else said my name in that same tired, kind way, at the end of something. That's another file. I keep it shut.

* * *

I found I'd been gripping the flask so hard its dents had printed themselves in my palm.

"Hask," I said. "I'm going to need the pen."

He looked up the tower for a long while. "The guards have tasted me," he said, "and found me to be a rock. They have tasted you and found you to be an Examiner. I do not believe they hold Examiners in high regard."

"Then I'll have to change their minds."

Before I could start, the Associates left, and I want it recorded, because it was the funniest thing I saw in the Hiveplains, and I'd seen a man entered as stock.

There were three of them and one skiff, on the hilltop on its little beetle legs, with a seat for one. All three stood round it in the dawn and each explained, in a perfectly pleasant baritone, why he should sit in it. Four had seniority. Twelve had flown it there. Seven had suffered most recently. Four observed that he had been formally recovered, was therefore company property, and company property travels by company transport. Twelve observed that Four had just declared himself luggage. Grievances chimed on all three ledger cases until the hilltop sounded like a small, very polite bell tower. In the end Four sat in the skiff, closed the canopy on the other two with a click, and lifted off north without a sound.

"He'll be noting that," said Twelve.

"So shall I," said Seven.

They set off north on foot, side by side and very far from together, one in a perfect suit with a face like a pudding, one in his socks.

Hask watched them out of sight, and made a small sound deep in the stack that I had never heard before and couldn't have described for the Register under oath.

"Hask. Was that a laugh?"

"It is not enumerated," said Hask, "that I may not."

The plain's gold dust had settled thick in his joint overnight, and two bees were walking about in it like clerks in an archive. Beside the first leaf and the second there was a third: a pale twist no bigger than my little fingernail, pushing up through the pollen.

"Hask. You've got another one."

"I am aware," said Hask heavily. "The amendments are becoming a hedge."

* * *

The wind came up at noon out of the south-west and smelled of salt.

It flattened the violet in long silver waves and lifted the gold dust off the blocks in sheets until the whole plain smoked. I wetted my scarf and tied it over my mouth and breathed pollen and wet wool and sneezed anyway. The hum went up a note and stayed there. All over the plain the bees were coming home early in long gold threads, and far off to the west the sky had gone the colour of a bruise.

Then the squall hit, and the side came off the hive.

A gust like a shove from a giant's hand got its fingers under the thinnest paper on the tower's south face and peeled it off the way you'd tear the crust off a loaf: a curved slab of paper and comb as tall as a door. It fell slowly, and landed in the violet like a dropped mattress.

The hive screamed. There's no other word. The chord tore straight up through the saw, bees gushed from the wound and the doorway, and the air round the tower went dark.

I ran, and then I stopped running, because fast is cross, and walked.

The slab lay on its back in the crushed flowers. Gold comb, and in the cells under thin wax caps the brood, white and curled like grains of rice the size of my thumb; and at one edge, hanging like long pale acorns, three queen cells. The bees came down over it in a living blanket, layer on layer, shivering to warm it. I could feel the heat off them through the air like a stove, and smell it, wax and warm milk.

The first fat drops of rain burst on the comb.

Brood chills. I'd learned that on a Survey world where we kept pollinators in seed domes: a brood comb lying in the rain for an hour is a dead comb. The bees knew it. It wasn't going to be enough.

"Hask!"

He was already coming, in long grinding strides that left holes in the ground.

The wound in the tower was three metres up, a ragged mouth of torn paper with the raw gold comb behind it. Hold the slab in place and the hive would do the rest; they can mend anything with wax, given time, the way the Undercroft mended with thread. It only had to be held.

I knelt and put my hands into the bees.

They gloved me to the wrist at once, warm and crawling, tasting me with a thousand tiny feet. The rain came harder. I didn't look at the bees. I looked at the slab, and where to lift it, and lifted it the way you'd lift a sleeping child out of a chair, one hand under, one behind, slow. It was heavy with honey at the top and warm with brood in the middle. Bees walked up my arms, into my collar, onto my eyelids and the corners of my mouth. I let them.

Hask's top stone came down to me, level and steady as a step. I stood on it with the slab against my chest and my back against his stack, and Hask rose the way a sunrise rises, all of a piece, until the torn mouth in the wall was in front of my face, hot and loud and smelling of the inside of the world.

The slab had bent in the fall. To get its top edge home I had to lean right into the gap, my face against the inner comb, and the queen cells came in against my neck and one of them burst, a soft cold pop under my collar.

Something ran down inside it. Thick and cool and sour-sweet, like yoghurt and wet paper and lemon rind, and the smell went straight up my nose and filled my head, and all around me the bees went still. Not quiet. Still. As if someone had walked into a crowded room and said a name they all knew.

I held the slab with my palms and my forehead and my whole weight, standing on a stone on a stone in the rain, while the bees came out of the dark and covered the seam in a thick gold rope and began to work it. I felt them chewing. I felt the wax go soft and warm under my hands. My shoulders burned. Water ran into my eyes and out of my sleeves. Once the wind came again and the slab bucked, and Hask leaned his whole stack into the tower and held me against it like a book against a shelf.

"Examiner," he said, very calm. "There are bees in my joint."

"Are they harming the leaves?"

"They are inspecting them," said Hask. "Thoroughly."

The rain eased. The seam set. I took my hands away, one and then the other, and the slab stayed, and through the new thin paper the bees on the brood were fanning, and the sound out of the hive came down through the saw, and down, and under it, the chord.

Hask set me down in the wet violet. I was crawling with bees from hair to belt. They walked over my face, my stung neck, my lips, and one by one lifted off and went home. Not one had stung me the whole time I was up there.

My collar came away on my fingers with a smear of pale cream, and the smell of it rose sour and milky and sweet, and somewhere in the oldest part of my skull it said home.

The guards came out of the doorway. The first walked across the trampled wax and climbed onto my boot, and tasted the leather and the laces and my ankle. Then it lifted its feelers and touched my shin, twice, quite gently, the way you'd tap a friend on the shoulder in a crowd. It turned and went back inside, and the other two followed.

"Examiner," said Hask, after a while. "I am obliged to report that you smell of a hive."

I rubbed at the collar with my thumb. The cream went into the weave and stiffened there, and the smell came up stronger, as if I'd only woken it.

"I know," I said. "I don't think it's coming out."

* * *

At six, with the storm wall standing over the western hills so dark it was nearly green, the Register chimed its double note on the Hiveplains entry I'd followed.

VARRO-TANDEM BIOREFERENCE, FIELD SERVICES. Notice of works. Hiveplains reserve: tower telemetry units, all, scheduled for replacement. Day 19, 06:00. Superseded units to be recovered for disposal.

Twelve hours. The morning after an Examiner pulls nineteen years of a series at eleven at night. I don't believe in coincidences. I believe in field offices that are awake.

"They're going to take the box," I said.

"Is that enumerated?"

"It's maintenance. Nothing personal."

The first thunder came at seven, like furniture being moved in an attic. I counted from the flash. Twenty-four. Eight kilometres; the storm's heart still over the coast. The air had gone thick and warm and smelled of hot stone, the way a room smells just before the stove catches. The towers stood dark and humming, not glowing tonight. I checked what I had: slate, cracked and dark, but my nails would light it; flask, full and scalding, strapped across my back; forty metres of honeyed rope; knife; a pad of paper forms, because paper doesn't need charging.

"Examiner," said Hask. "Item nine hundred and twelve. Harm by weather of the upper air, fire excepted." A long pause. "Lightning is enumerated. I am obliged to prevent it. I cannot reach it."

"Then don't try. Lightning hunts the tallest thing, and tonight that isn't you, and it isn't me." I pointed west at the black tower with its fishhook mast. "That's why it's black. It's been taking the plain's lightning for years. Never be the tallest thing on a hill, Hask. Be the second tallest, next to the tallest."

"Who told you that?"

"A Survey cook, on a world where we lost a radio mast a week."

The guards met me at the doorway in the rain. The first tasted my boot, my shin, and then lifted its feelers to the warm damp air in front of my collar and stood a long moment, tasting.

It stepped aside, and I went into the hive.

* * *

It was dark inside, and warm as a kitchen, and so loud the sound was a hand pressed flat on my whole body. Honey so thick it was nearly a taste; hot wax; the inside of a sheepskin; and under it, faint and sour, my collar. I held up my hands and the blue-green crescents under my nails lit a hand's breadth of the world.

The heart of the tower was hollow.

The bees had built round the old iron mast the way a pearl grows round grit, and left a shaft about it as wide as a well, running straight up into the dark. Its walls were comb, the cells as big as my thumb, and every inch of it was bees, a living wall glittering in my fingernail light. Hot air came up past me. They were fanning it. The whole hive breathed up its own chimney, the way a house breathes up its stairwell. And on the mast, every forty centimetres, the company had bolted iron rungs, and the bees had waxed every one, so they shone like a ladder made of honey.

I climbed up the inside of a living hive, in the dark, with a storm coming, by the light of my own fingernails, and the bees let me. They walked over my hands on the rungs and away. They hung before my face, tasting, and moved aside. When I leaned my shoulder on the wall to rest, they shifted under it like a crowd making room on a bench. Through the thin comb on every side I saw into the hive, the brood, the stores, the slow gold glitter of honey, and dancers, even now, running their little straight lines in the dark.

At ten metres the first lightning came through the walls.

The whole chimney went gold. Every cell, every bee, every rung, lit from outside like a lantern with a hand round it, and for one long breath I stood in a tower of gold glass full of living things, a thousand thousand of them, all turned to the light. Then dark. Twelve, before the thunder. Four kilometres.

I'm not ashamed to say I stopped there a while and simply held on.

Higher up the chimney narrowed and the heat thinned, and I felt the wind leaning on the tower and the whole hive swaying with it, slow, with a long creak of paper. Rain hammered on the comb like gravel on a window. The hum had climbed to a storm-key, high and tight.

At twenty-four metres my head came out of the top of the hive into the storm.

* * *

The tower's crown was a rounded dome of paper with the mast standing out of it, slick and black, and the box on its tip at the height of my face, its green light still blinking in the rain. All round me in the dark lay the whole of the Hiveplains, with the wind coming over it like a sea.

I got an arm round the mast and a turn of rope round it and round my waist, and tied a hitch Rook would have checked twice and passed, and held the slate to the box.

It was an old company box. It wanted contact, and it wanted time. The two of them began to talk at the speed of a protocol that had been old when Teague was eating plums.

Telemetry unit HP-22/14. Local store: 19 yr 4 mo. Transferring.

Four per cent.

Flash. In it, four hundred metres west, a white-violet rope of light stood on the black tower's fishhook mast like a finger on a pin, and the whole plain jumped out of the dark at once, every block, every tower, every silver line of rain. One. Two. Th— The thunder came like a building falling over.

Eleven per cent.

I read the numbers in the gaps, by my fingers, as the slate laid them down. Hive weight: falling, year on year, a long slope with little spring bumps where something still flowered that the bees knew, until the bumps stopped coming. Forage flights: falling, and this spring, near nothing. Hum: climbing, a thin rising line. The kettle in the empty house.

And at the very end, yesterday and the day before: the hum dropping all at once like a fever breaking, and the forage flights going up like a rocket. Somebody had come.
