Forty per cent. Flash, thunder on top of it. Sixty. The wind tried to take me off the mast and I let the rope take it and hung with my cheek on wet iron. Eighty.

Transfer complete. Checksum valid.

I let out a breath I'd been holding since the Corvel reach.

Then I opened a Discrepancy, on top of a hive in a storm, with my arm round a lightning mast and my fingernails for a lamp.

In the matter of the MORIEL BIOREFERENCE INDEX, pollinator lines 18–26. The Index holds the lines STEADY, variance 0.0%, from certified tower telemetry. The towers hold otherwise. Attached: the raw store of unit HP-22/14, recovered in person from the unit, showing nineteen years of decline, the hive starving in full bloom. The certified series repeats one day's readings, unchanged, for the same nineteen years. The Examiner has also dug a hole. Class: Forged. Correction: the certified series struck, the raw series entered, the Index to publish what the hives say. Case open. Unit HP-22/14 and the whole Hiveplains reserve entered as evidence.

I hesitated over one more line, then put it in.

Note: the block-14 lines and some others are now recovering. Cause under investigation. The cause appears to be polite.

The Register thought about it for no time at all.

MORIEL BIOREFERENCE INDEX · POLLINATOR LINES 18–26 · STATUS: UNDER DISCREPANCY (FORGED). Certified series withdrawn. Raw telemetry entered. Hiveplains reserve: evidence under open case. Works suspended.

Under it, the replacement order quietly turned grey.

Nine hours away, over the customs queue at Margin Station, the news crawl was sliding past in six scripts, toll rises, soap operas, and the line that had said the same word every day for nineteen years was, about now, very neatly, the way the Register does everything, saying something else. And somebody in the queue would look up.

I buttoned the slate inside my coat.

My hair stood up.

* * *

It started at the back of my neck, a crawling lift like a hand stroking the wrong way, and then every hair on my head rose off my scalp in the rain. The mast under my arm began, very softly, to sing, a thin buzz like a bee trapped in a glass. On the corners of the box and the tip of the mast, little flames came out: blue, pale, heatless, flickering like a stove jet turned right down.

I looked west.

The black tower was down. Broken off at half its height, lying across its block in a long dark smear, smouldering in the rain. It had been the tallest thing on the hill for years, and it had finally put that down.

Now I was.

I didn't decide anything. I pulled the slip end of the hitch, let go of the iron, and dropped into the chimney. Back against the comb, boots skidding on the rungs, bees going up past my face like sparks up a flue, five metres, eight, my coat burning on the wax. I jammed a boot on a rung and stopped, and folded myself small against the wall.

The bees came over me like a blanket. Thousands. A warm humming quilt of fur and wings over my head and back and hands, the way they'd covered the fallen brood in the rain. I shut my eyes and smelled honey and wax and my own sour-sweet collar, and held on.

The lightning hit the mast.

It was too big to be a sound. The whole hive went white through my eyelids, through the bees on my face, white and then gold, one enormous lantern with a sun inside it. The iron beside me jumped. A smell like a hot iron set down on a damp shirt, scorched wax, and something sharp and clean I've only ever smelled in storms. Then the thunder, up through my boots into my teeth, shaking the honey in the comb.

Then dark, and rain, and the hum: climbing to the saw, holding, holding, and slowly, slowly coming down.

The old company ground cable, still good after all these years, had carried the whole sky down the mast's spine into the earth and left us humming in the dark. The bees lifted off me one by one and went back to the comb. My nails glowed. I was alive, and so was the hive.

I climbed back up. The box was blackened and split, its green light out for good, nineteen years cooked in its shell. The only copy was buttoned inside my coat, warm against my heart.

* * *

The storm had walked on east on its legs of light, and behind it, to the west, the cloud had begun to tear.

Then a long sheet of lightning, cloud to cloud, held for a full breath, and the whole country lit from end to end like a page held up to a lamp.

The Hiveplains, every block silver with rain. The fallen black tower, smoking. And to the north and west, past the last of the blocks, where I'd thought there was only more dark, the land ran down in long dim folds to a line, and the line was the sea. I had never seen this planet's sea. It was grey and white and enormous, and it was moving.

Along its edge lay a long dark band, low and ragged, between the land and the water. Trees. A forest standing in the sea, its crowns a rough dark shelf along the coast as far as the light went.

And out of the trees, in three places, straight up into the clean air behind the storm, rose three thin grey lines.

Smoke.

The flash died. I hung on to the warm wet iron with my hair still standing up, and with my other hand I took out the slate and asked the Register what was on the coast.

COASTAL MARGIN, north-west (tidal forest, flooded orchard stock, origin lapsed). Settlements: none. Population: none. Status: uninhabited.

I looked at the place in the dark where the smoke had been. Somebody out there, in the trees in the sea, where the Register said there was nobody at all, had lit a fire against the storm, and was sitting by it.

Below me the hive hummed its chord. Far below that, a mountain of carved stone with three leaves in his joint was calling up the chimney, very calmly, to ask whether I had been struck, and if so, whether by weather of the upper air.

"Not today, Hask," I called down.

I unscrewed the flask. Steam came out into the rain, and tar, and burnt raisins.

"Item fourteen," came up the chimney, faintly.

I drank it anyway, at the top of the tower in the tail of the storm, with four days left, looking at the place where the smoke had been. For the first time since a boy climbed out of a cleaning hatch, I thought I knew where he was going.


## Chapter Nine: The Third One's for the Sea

> Twice in, twice out, the sea goes round about; once for the fish and once for the tree, and the third one's for the sea.
>
> — skipping rhyme, Highwater; taught to children before they are allowed on the low walks. Nobody at Highwater knows who made it up, and nobody has ever written it down.
>

I walk all night towards the smell.

It gets bigger. That's the only way I can say it. Every hill I come over, the wind off the far side is wetter and saltier, until I can taste it on my teeth, until my lips go stiff with it, until Biscuit puts its head out of my collar, sneezes, and goes back in. The footfall goes on behind me, fifty-one, fifty-one, fainter every hour, like a clock in a house you've left.

Just before dawn I come up the last slope, through grass gone thin and tough and grey, and the ground stops.

And there it is.

I don't know what I thought. Ommery read us the story about the boy on the raft, and she did a voice for the wind, and I suppose I thought the sea would look like the pond, only wider. Like the pond if you stood on Tilly's shoulders.

It isn't wide. It's all. It starts at the bottom of the hill and goes out, flat and grey and moving all over at once like the skin on hot milk, and it doesn't stop. There's no far side. There's no glass. There's a line, a long way off, where the grey of the water meets the grey of the sky, and the line is perfectly straight, and the sky comes down and sits on it.

The sun comes up out of the water.

Not from behind a hill. Out of it, as if it's been down there all night keeping warm. It lays a road across the sea, gold, broken into a thousand pieces that won't keep still, and the road comes all the way to the beach below my feet.

I sit down. My legs decide it.

"More sea," I say. My voice comes out odd. "And then land."

Where? says the voice. It's been quiet all night. It sounds the way I feel. Where's the land?

"Ommery said there is some."

She might be wrong.

The sound comes up the hill in long slow breaths, a hush and a draw. In the back of my jaw, where the Undercroft taught me to listen, it's a deep slow rocking, the whole world turning over in its sleep.

I can't hear the fifty-one any more. The sea's too big for it.

* * *

Below the hill there's a strip of grey sand with dark weed piled on it in lines, like the tidemark round a bath, and past the sand the mud begins. Miles of it, shining, the colour of a slate roof after rain, cut with channels of silver water that wander about as if they've forgotten where they were going.

And out on the mud, a long way off, the trees.

A whole forest, standing in the mud on legs: roots like a spider's, dozens to a tree, arching down so each trunk stands a man's height clear of the ground in a cage of itself. Their roof runs along the coast as far as I can see both ways, a long green cloud on stilts.

The smell coming off it is salt, and rot, and something sweet under the rot, like a windfall apple in long grass.

Fruit, says the voice.

I've had two fistfuls of violet roots since yesterday. I go down onto the mud.

My plated feet spread and grip, and when I go in to the ankle the mud lets me go with a long rude noise. It smells of eggs. In the pools there are things with too many legs that run sideways and wave one big claw at me, outraged, as if I've walked into their kitchen.

I'd like to say I notice the water coming in. I'm looking at the claws.

What I notice is that the channel I stepped over a while back, a trickle I could jump, is now as wide as the lane outside our house. And it's running. The wrong way. Inland, past my ankles, silver and quick and full of turning weed.

I turn round.

There's water between me and the beach, spreading over the mud like milk over a table, coming from everywhere at once. I watch my own footprints fill.

"It's coming in," I say. "Why is it coming in?"

I don't know what it does, says the voice. I've never seen it either.

I run for the trees. They're nearer.

It's at my knees when I reach the first roots. It's at my waist when I'm in among them, grabbing at the arches, slimy, slipping, and it isn't just rising now, it's pulling, a whole river of it pouring sideways into the orchard, and I lose my feet.

I've never been in water over my head.

Nobody in Firstfield has. There's the mill race, which is forbidden, and the pond in the square, which comes to Tilly's knees. You don't learn a thing you'll never need.

It's green and loud and in my nose and mouth, so much salt it burns all the way down, and I come up once for half a breath and a root hits me across the back and I'm under again. My hands close on weed and the weed comes away. Biscuit's gone out of my shirt, a dark shape flicking off through the green, and I think, good, it can swim, good, and then I can't think anything, because my chest is on fire.

Bram. The voice, very close, very fast. Your chest. Tell me what's in your chest. Tell me what it is.

I can't. There isn't a word in me. There's just the burning.

Something has my collar.

Small, and furious, and pulling. An amber glow in the green right by my face, six legs going, teeth in the cloth at the back of my neck. It isn't strong enough. It does it anyway.

My hand hits a root, and this time the root doesn't come away.

* * *

I wake up being sick.

Seawater, a great deal of it, out of my nose and mouth together, onto bark. Everything hurts in the way that means it's still there.

Biscuit is sitting on my back, its heart going like rain on thatch. It smells of river and lamp oil and, very strongly, of fright, like a struck match.

"Thank you," I say into the bark.

Biscuit bites my ear, not hard, and climbs into my shirt.

"It's alive," says somebody above me, and laughs.

I roll over.

There's a girl in the tree.

She's hanging upside down from a branch by the backs of her knees, eating a plum, her hair a dark wet rope dripping beside my head. Her fingers are long. Her toes, hooked over the branch, are nearly as long as fingers. She has freckles across her nose and shoulders, dark ones, in patches, like the bark of a plane tree.

She's looking at me the way Jory looks at a frog.

"I saw that," she says. "You were doing this." She lets go of her elbows and does a sort of windmill with both arms. Then a sort of slap. Then she lets her arms go floppy and sinks them, slowly. "And then this."

"I wasn't—"

"What are you?"

"Bram."

"No, what line." She swings a little, considering me. "You've got feet like a mud-crab, so you're a wader. But you swim like a dropped sack." She frowns, as if I'm a sum that won't come out. "Are you a very bad swimmer of some kind I don't know about? Or a very good one having a bad day?"

"I've never been swimming."

She stares at me. Then she laughs, and the laugh starts before I've finished talking, as if it was already on its way and my words just opened the door. It's a big laugh for someone upside down. She has to grab the branch.

"You came here," she says, "on the tide, and you can't swim?"

"I didn't know about the tide."

That's worse. She laughs so hard the plum falls out of her hand, and Biscuit puts one paw out of my collar and catches it before it hits the water, and eats it.

The laugh stops.

She looks at Biscuit. Biscuit, chewing, looks back, its throat going a slow, satisfied amber.

"That," she says, "was my plum."

"It pulled me out," I say. "It's had a long morning."

* * *

Her name is Sedge.

She tells me while she's climbing down, which she does the way water comes down a wall: no steps, just a long fall that happens to touch every branch on the way. She lands on the root beside me without making it shake.

"Who wrote you?"

"What?"

"Who wrote you." Slowly, as if I'm being dense. "Which company. Which line."

It comes off her then. I've had the nose since the Hiveplains and I can't switch it off. Under the salt and plum and clean wet-wood smell of her there's something thin and alert, like a wire pulled tight. She's laughing at me, and she's also ready to be up that tree before I can blink.

"Nobody wrote me. I'm from Firstfield." It's what you say, so I say the rest. "Bram, son of Wenna, of the house of Hale. Grandson of Ada Hale."

Saying Gran's name out loud is strange. I can't remember the last time I heard anyone call her Ada. She's Gran to the whole of Firstfield. Gran to Ommery. Gran to Jory's da.

Sedge has gone very still. The wire pulls tighter.

"Nobody," she says, "wrote you."

"No."

"Same as us." She looks at my feet, at the green-gold veins at my wrist. "Except somebody did."

* * *

She takes me up.

She makes me climb, and I'm bad at it, and she says so. She goes up round me and over me, showing me where to put things, with Biscuit on her shoulder now, because Biscuit has decided that anyone who drops a plum is worth knowing.

We go through the roof of leaves like going up through the floor of a house, and on the other side of it there's another house.

There's a whole village in the sky.

Platforms lashed across the branches. Houses woven out of living branches, roofed with sailcloth patched with red. Rope bridges from tree to tree, sagging and swaying, some wide enough for two, some only a rope to walk on and a rope to hold. Babies asleep in nets slung between branches. Blue smoke from cookfires in clay pots.

And the noise. Gulls on every high branch, barking like dogs. Creaking: rope, wood, the whole village riding up and down in the wind like the ship in the story. And whistles, from everywhere, short and long, passing over my head and away into the leaves.

"They're telling everyone," says Sedge. "About you."

"What are they saying?"

She listens. One long whistle, two short.

"That you can't swim."

Below us, the mudflat has become a lake with trees in it. Something long and dark slides under the platform without a ripple.

"Snap-eel," says Sedge. "Bites what's quick. Don't be quick."

The houses on the lowest platforms have water in them. A hand's depth, lapping over the doorsills, and people inside lifting things onto shelves without hurrying, the way Ma clears a table. On the biggest trunk in the village, grey and smooth as a wall, somebody has cut notches, dozens of them, from just above the water to just below my feet, each with little marks beside it.

"What are those?"

"High water. The big autumn tide, every year." She points down, near the water. "That one's my gran's gran's. That one's my gran's." Then up, nearly level with my knee. "That one's last year."

It's a long way from the bottom notch to the top. Most of it is near the top.

"It's coming up," I say.

"Every year. The reef's gone soft." She says it the way Gran says the chest's bad. Not news. Weather.

* * *

They don't like me.

I smell it before anyone says a word. It comes off the woman gutting fish, off a boy my size with webbed hands who stops halfway up a ladder to watch me pass: thin and cold and sharp, like the dome on a winter morning. They breathe in as I go by, and their faces change.

By the notched trunk there's a man waiting. Tall, long-fingered like Sedge, grey in his beard, a white scar puckered down from one ear. He has a gutting knife on his belt and doesn't touch it. He doesn't need to.

"Teasel," says Sedge, "this is Bram. He can't swim."

"I heard." He looks at me. Then he leans down, not close, and breathes in through his nose.

His face goes flat.

"He smells of the dome," he says.

"I haven't been in the dome since—"

"Not your skin. Your shirt."

The grey shirt they gave me upstairs. It's been through the Spill and the Undercroft and a meadow of furious bees and the sea. It can't smell of anything but me.

I put my nose to my sleeve.

Under the salt and mud and Biscuit and pollen, faint as the words on a weathered sign: cedar. And cooked ink.

The corridor outside the glass room. The two grey men in the violet.

It's been on me the whole time, in the weave, in whatever they washed it in up there, like a label I didn't have the nose to read.

"We've had that smell here before," says Teasel. "It came in a grey boat. It asked for people by number."

A whistle somewhere up in the leaves, long and falling.

"He's not from them," says Sedge. "Look at his feet. He's running from them."

"Then they'll be following, won't they." Teasel looks up past the canopy. The sky's gone a funny colour while we've been talking, a yellowish brass, and the gulls on the high branches have all turned to face the same way, into the wind. "And it's the wrong night for it." He says to Sedge, not to me: "He goes at first light. On the ebb."

Then he walks off along a one-rope bridge, without holding the other rope.

I take the shirt off.

I don't decide to. My hands do it. They take the reed stem out of the pocket first, with its seventeen knots. Then I throw the shirt as hard as I can out past the edge, and it opens in the air like a grey bird and comes down on the water, and the tide takes it away under the trees.

Sedge watches it go.

"That was your only shirt."

"Yes."

She looks at me a long moment, and the wire loosens, a little, and something comes up under it I don't have a word for yet. Warm. A bit annoyed. Like bread that's caught on the bottom.

"You're a lot of work," she says, and goes to find me another one.

* * *

The new shirt is sailcloth, stiff and pale, patched on one elbow with red, and it smells of smoke and salt and somebody else, and it's too big. Sedge rolls up the sleeves without asking, four hard turns each, like wringing out a fish.

Then she feeds me, because she says I look like a drowned dog and it's putting people off their dinner.

Salt-plums, purple-black, with a white bloom on the skin you could write your name in. I bite one and my whole face folds up, it's so sour, and Sedge laughs at me, and then halfway down it changes its mind and goes sweet, dark and winey, and I eat four in a row with the juice running to my elbows.

Then moonpears.

For those she climbs out along a branch that bends under her like a fishing rod. They're pale yellow-white, the size of my fist, with skin so thin I can see into them the way you see into your hand held over a lamp. In the shade under the leaves, they glow. Just a little. A lamp turned right down.

"We hang them by the beds," Sedge says. "For the little ones."

I bite one. It tastes like cold water with honey in it, and smells like rain.

Oh, says the voice. It hasn't said anything since the water. Oh, more of that.

I'm on my second when I feel Saul's twist in my trouser pocket, soaked through and soft as pulp. I can feel through the paper how few are left. There's a cut-down barrel at the platform's edge, full of dark silt, with something growing in it that smells of aniseed. I press two seeds in beside the aniseed. First knuckle deep and not a hair more.

"What are those?" says Sedge.

"Tomatoes."

"What's a tomato?"

I look at her. She waits.

"It's red," I say. "And when it's warm off the vine and you bite it, it goes everywhere, and it tastes of—" I don't know what it tastes of. It tastes of Saul's east rows in August, and Tilly's joke, and the inside of the summer. "You'll see."

"I will?"

"In case wherever you're going," I tell the seeds, "hasn't got proper ones."

I fold up the twist and put it away. It weighs nothing at all.

Sedge is looking at the barrel the way she looked at me when I said Firstfield.

* * *

In the afternoon the sea goes out again.

It hisses out between the trees in a hundred channels, and the roots come up, dripping, and people come down the ladders with baskets and pick fish off the mud like windfalls.

"Twice a day," says Sedge. "You'd think they'd tell you that, in a dome."

"There isn't a sea in the dome."

"Is it true you can't see the sky?"

"You can see the weather. Not the stars."

"Is it true they watch you eat?"

"Only on Tuesdays."

She thinks I'm joking. I let her.

"Come on," she says. "I'll show you why you're leaving."

We go out along the root-walks, roots bent and tied as they grew into a knotted road above the mud. Sedge doesn't use her hands. I use both hands, both feet and, at one point, my chin.

Then the trees end, and there's the reef.

It's a wall, shoulder-high at low water, along the whole seaward edge of the orchard, and it's alive. Grey roots locked through each other like fingers, and plastered over them, shell, crusted with little blue-black shells packed close as cobbles. On the far side the sea slaps at it white. On this side the water's calm, and in the calm, among the roots, there are thousands of tiny silver fish, hanging like dust in a sunbeam.

"Nursery," says Sedge. "Every fish we eat was born in there."

Where the reef bends out into the sea in a great knotted elbow, the roots are as grey as old stone, and the shell is so thick it's a cliff. No root shows at all, only the blue-black shells, packed tight.

"The Knuckle," she says. "Oldest bit. The rest grew out from it." She works her long fingers in behind one of the shells and pulls. It doesn't come. She braces a foot and pulls harder, and it tears away, and from the back of it hang dozens of fine golden threads, like hair, still stuck to the reef.

"Stitchers. They hang on with those, and grow shell, and the roots feed them, and the shell holds the roots." She holds it out. The threads touch my fingers and stick at once, fine as spider-silk and stronger. "On the big tides their babies come in. Millions, like snow, in the water. They used to land all along the reef and stick. That's how it grew."

"Used to?"

She points along the wall.

Fifty paces from the Knuckle the reef changes colour. The roots go greenish-white, swollen, and the shell on them is thin and flaky, coming away in plates like the crust on old bread. Hardly any stitchers. And a long way further on, the wall has sagged in the middle like the back of an old horse, and the sea is coming over it in white sheets at every wave.

"The Mouth," she says. "It's been opening since before I was born."

"Why don't the snow ones land there?"

"They just don't." She shrugs, too hard. "They come in, and go through, and go out again. Now they only stop at the Knuckle."

Bram, says the voice, quiet. Lick it.

"What?"

"I didn't say anything," says Sedge.

The reef. Here, and then there.

I crouch at the Knuckle and put my tongue on the wet shell.

Salt, of course; nothing here isn't salt. Under it, the slick green taste of the roots. And under that, coming off the roots into the water the way the bees' words came off their skins: something cold and dry and clean. Chalk. The stick of chalk in Master Aldous's hand, squeaking on the board, the dust of it on your tongue when you've been sent to sit in the front row.

"Did you just," says Sedge, "lick the reef?"

I go along to where it turns greenish-white, and lick that.

Salt. Green. And a taste like the chalk, only wrong. Smudged. As if somebody rubbed it out with a sleeve and wrote it again from memory, a bit crooked, and then copied the crooked one.

Drifted, says the voice. Like the flowers. A careful pause. The Knuckle still says it right. The rest is saying it from memory.

"Saying what?"

Here, it says. Stop here. Hold on.

"You're talking to yourself," says Sedge.

"I'm talking to my wrist," I say, which doesn't help.

* * *

She doesn't ask. The wire comes up off her again, and under it something quick and bright, like a green stick cracking.

Then she grins.

"Bet you won't," she says.

I had it ready. I've had it on my tongue since she pulled the stitcher off. She got there first.

"Won't what?"

She points down. On the inside curve of the Knuckle there's a pool, deep and clear, green going to dark, the light coming down into it in long wavering bars and the roots going down and down, crusted with stitchers, to a bottom I can't see.

"Touch the bottom. And stay down as long as me."

She drops off the edge without a splash, like a stone off a wall in a dream, and she's gone.

I count, the way Gran counts before she peeks at bread.

At two hundred I'm on my knees at the edge. At three hundred I've got one foot in the water, when the pool goes bloop and her head comes up, sleek as Biscuit's, breathing slow.

"Your turn."

I go in.

It's warm as a bath that's been standing. I kick the way she kicked and go down, eyes stinging, tiny fish scattering off the stitchers like sparks off a fire.

Ten. Twenty. My chest starts.

Thirty. It's a fist under my ribs, squeezing.

I come up at thirty-four, choking, and hang off a root breathing like Ma's smoker.

Sedge is sitting on the Knuckle with her feet in the water. She doesn't laugh. That's worse. She smiles a small, kind smile, like Ma's when Tilly tries a long word, and says, "Thirty-four's quite good. For a dropped sack."

* * *

I can help with that, says the voice. If you can name it.

Sedge has gone down again, just for fun. I'm on the root with my arms round my knees, dripping.

"My lungs."

That's a word. That's not a shape.

I shut my eyes.

Dr. Ommery, in Gran's room, the winter Gran's chest was bad and wouldn't mend. Gran sitting up in bed with her back bare and her shoulders like a coat-hanger, and Ommery's wooden listening-trumpet with the brass rim, the wide end on Gran's back and the narrow end at my ear. Listen.

It's a tree, Bram, upside down. The trunk comes down your throat and branches, and those branch, smaller and smaller, and at the end of the littlest twigs, instead of leaves, little rooms. Millions, in bunches like grapes, each with a wall as thin as a soap bubble and blood on the other side. The air goes in the rooms and through the walls into the blood. That's all breathing is.

And under the trumpet, Gran's breath, with a crackle in it like paper.

That's the little rooms at the bottom not opening, Ommery said. That's why she's tired.

I say it. All of it. The trunk, the branches, the rooms like grapes, the soap-bubble walls, the blood on the other side.

Yes. The voice is very interested. That I can hold. More rooms. Bigger. But that only holds a longer breath. That's not the sea.

I'm thinking about the Window. The school group from the Changed lands, the day Jory drew the goat, faces pressed to the other side of the glass. The pink slits on their necks, three each side below the ear, fluttering when they got excited, as if they were breathing even while they laughed.

"Gills," I say. I touch my neck where theirs were. "Three each side. The water goes in your mouth and out of them, and inside they've got the same little rooms, only flat, with blood on the other side. And the air's in the water."

The voice is quiet a long moment.

You saw that once, it says. Through glass.

"Can you hold it?"

I can try. A pause. It'll cost. Your chest will want the water. In dry air it'll ache. Gentler: Like hers.

Gran's breath, crackling like paper.

Below me the pool bulges and Sedge comes up, and shakes her hair out, and looks at me sitting there dry and talking to my wrist.

"Yes," I say.

* * *

It starts in my ribs.

A stretch, wide and slow, as if somebody has a hand on each side of me from the inside, pushing. Then a pressure, deep, down where Ommery's trumpet listened, as if the rooms are opening, all of them, room after room, like doors down a long corridor. It doesn't hurt. Then it does. My chest goes huge and tight and I can't get a breath in or out, and I'm on my back on the Knuckle with Sedge's face over me upside down saying what's wrong with you, and I can't answer.

And I'm small, and I'm up high, higher than the bakehouse roof, and the ground's swaying under me, and it isn't ground. It's shoulders. Big ones, warm through wool, a hand round each of my ankles, and under my legs a chest going up and down, humming. Singing. Something low, no words, or words I've lost, and I feel it more than hear it, coming up through his back into my knees, like the dome's own note but warm. We're going down the lane. I've got two fistfuls of his hair. Everything smells of sawdust.

That's all there is. The shoulders. The song through his chest. I don't remember his face.

Then my neck splits open.

Not a cut. Like a yawn, in a place that's never yawned. Three each side, under my ears. Air rushes over them, cold, and they burn and flutter, and when I put my hands up they slam shut under my fingers like little mouths.

The breath comes back. Huge. More breath than I've ever had, going down and down the tree.

And at the bottom, an ache. Dull and dry and scratchy.

I cough. It sounds like Gran.

Sedge is kneeling over me with both hands flat on my chest, as if I might go off somewhere. She's staring at my neck. She smells of fright, the struck match, and under it the green stick, and something new, wide and warm, that I have no word for at all.

"What," she says, "was that."

"I asked for gills."

"You asked—" She sits back on her heels. She opens her mouth. Shuts it.

It's the first time since I washed up that she hasn't got anything to say.

"Bet you won't," I say, "go and touch the bottom again."

* * *

We go in together.

I breathe out on the way down, all of it, the way we do at the Window. Then I open my mouth and let the sea in.

Everything in me says no. My throat closes. My gills slam shut. I'm sinking with a mouthful of salt water and nowhere to put it, and I nearly kick for the top.

Breathe properly, says Ma. Not her bee-yard voice. Her Tuesday one.

I let the water through.

In at my mouth, warm and salt, out at my neck, a soft pushing flutter, three each side, like holding a moth in each hand. And the burning doesn't come.

I'm breathing. Under the sea. With my eyes open.

It's green, and gold where the light comes in, and the fish hang in their thousands and part round me like a curtain and close again behind. The slap and roar of the reef is up there somewhere, a sound in another room. Down here there's a deep slow rocking, and the clicks and pops of a thousand small things eating, and my own heart.

Sedge is beside me, upside down, her hair floating straight up like smoke, her eyes very wide. I go down and touch the bottom. Sand, and something that scuttles off under my palm.

I count. A hundred. Two hundred. The dry ache fades, because it isn't dry down here.

At three hundred, Sedge puts her long hand round my wrist to tug me up.

My gills slam shut.

All six at once, like doors in a wind. I've no idea why. One moment I'm breathing the sea, and the next I'm a boy with a mouthful of salt water at the bottom of a pool, and I go up so fast I crack my head on a root.

I come out choking. She comes up beside me laughing so hard she has to hang onto the Knuckle.

Your heart rate, says the voice, is doing something unusual. It started when she took your wrist. It's still doing it. It sounds puzzled. Shall I fix it?

"No," I say. Out loud. Very loud.

Sedge stops laughing. "No what?"

"Nothing."

"You said no."

"I said it to my wrist."

She treads water and looks at me, her freckles dark and wet, her mouth trying very hard to stay straight and failing at one corner.

"Your neck went shut," she says. "When I touched you."

"It does that."

"When you're scared?"

"When I'm surprised."

"Bram," she says, with tremendous dignity, "you can't swim, you lick the reef, you talk to your arm, and your neck shuts when you're surprised." She pushes off backwards, floating, looking at the sky. "You're the worst fish I ever met."

But what comes off her over the water is the bright one. The green stick, cracking.

I don't say anything. I'm not a fool.

* * *

The sky goes brass, then purple, then a bruised green-black I haven't got a name for, and the gulls stop barking and go somewhere else.

At Highwater everyone is tying things down. Babies come out of the nets and go up the ladders hand to hand. Teasel goes past on the one-rope bridge with a coil over each shoulder and doesn't look at me, and under the cold sharp smell coming off him there's something tight and low that I know from my own skin. Fear.

We're on the high platform, the top of the biggest tree, where they've lit a fire in an iron basket for the people still out on the mud to steer by. The smoke goes straight up a moment, then the wind lays it flat across the canopy like a grey flag.

"Storm tide," says Sedge. "The third one. Big tide, and a storm on top, and the wind piling the water up." She's watching the Mouth, where the sea is already coming over in long white sheets and the water inside is brown and rising. "Last time I was little. It took the bottom two platforms and the school. Teasel's house." She doesn't say what else. "The reef was higher then."

Out past the reef, the lightning comes down.

In the dome we only ever heard it, like furniture being moved upstairs. Out here it cracks down out of the black all at once, a tree made of light, upside down, branching, and for one heartbeat the whole sea is lit white and flat as a page.

Then the sound. It hits me in the new big chest and the jaw and the teeth, and the whole tree shudders.

Biscuit, in my shirt, says something I'm glad I can't translate.

Then the rain, warm and sideways and so heavy I can taste the sea in it, and the wind behind it, and the whole of Highwater starts to creak like a ship.

* * *

It's full dark when I see the snow.

Sedge has sent me down to a low platform, where the water's already lapping over the boards, to hold a rope I don't understand, and I look down into the flood, and it's glowing.

Where it moves, there's a light in it, cold and blue-green as the Undercroft. Every wave that slaps the boards breaks in sparks.

And in the light, falling through the water, there are specks.

Millions. Pale, tiny, turning, drifting in on the tide past the roots and under the platforms and away into the drowned dark of the orchard. Like snow on a still night when they open the dome's top vents in midwinter, falling through the lamplight in the square.

"Sedge."

She's beside me out of nowhere, wet, hair plastered to her face. She looks down.

"Stitchers." Her voice goes funny. "The storm brings them. They'll go straight through."

They turn as they pass my feet, as if they're looking for a word that isn't there.

I've had the word in my mouth all afternoon.

"Could you find the Mouth from here?" I say. "Underwater. In the dark."

Sedge looks at me.

"There's guide-ropes along the inside of the reef," she says slowly. "Knotted. Every knot's different, so you know where you are by feel." She holds up a hand; her long fingertips are calloused hard. "I could find it with my eyes shut. Why?"

"Because the Knuckle still knows what to say to them," I say, "and the Mouth's forgotten. And I can say it."

"You can what?"

"With my skin."

A pause, full of rain.

"That's the stupidest thing anyone's ever told me," says Sedge.

"I know."

"It's a storm tide. In the dark. The water through the Mouth'll pull you through the reef like a cork out of a bottle." She's angry; it comes off her hot as a stove door. And under the anger the bright thing, the green stick, louder than I've ever smelled it. "You've had gills for half a day."

"Bet you won't," I say.

She stares at me.

Then she pulls the knife off her belt, cuts the rope I'm holding, and ties one end round my waist in a knot I've never seen, a knot that takes her one movement and three heartbeats, and the other end round her own.

"Don't let go of that," she says. "And don't be quick."

* * *

We go off the end of the root-walk at the Knuckle.

The surface is a nightmare. Black water coming at us in hills, rain so hard it hurts, the wind screaming in the canopy behind. A wave throws me at the roots, and the rope jerks at my waist, and then Sedge has my wrist and pulls me under.

And it's quiet.

The storm's up there, a roar through a wall. Down here there's the rocking, and the glow. Everywhere we move, we light up: Sedge's kicking legs trail blue-green fire, my hands leave shining smears. And all round us, drifting in through the dark in a great slow storm of their own, the snow.

I breathe the sea in. My gills flutter, and stay open.

The Knuckle rises in front of us, a cliff crusted black with stitchers. I put my face to it and taste.

Chalk. Clean, cold, dry. Here. Stop here. Hold on. And the snow round the Knuckle is coming down onto it, settling on the old shell, sticking. Here, they land. Here, they always have.

I take the taste in and hold it, the way you hold a word you're afraid you'll forget before you get to say it.

Then I tap Sedge's shoulder, and she takes the guide-rope in her long hand, and we go.

She doesn't look. She feels, knot to knot, never hurrying, and I follow her fire through the dark. Something long and slow goes past, close, and turns its head towards my legs, and I make myself go slow, slow as Ma lifting bread out of the oven, and it goes on into the black.

My chest aches. My gills are working so hard they burn.

The roots under my hand go soft. My fingers sink into one like a rotten apple. And the water's running, all one way, through the reef, pulling at my legs, at the rope, at Sedge, until we're not swimming, we're being carried towards a great roaring glowing rush where the whole sea is coming over the wall and down through it at once.

The Mouth.

The snow is pouring through it. Millions, turning over and over in the bright water, on and away into the dark of the orchard, where there's nothing for them to hold.

Sedge grabs a root and wraps her legs round it and hauls on my rope, and I slam into the roots beside her so hard my teeth click.

She puts her face right up to mine. Her hair is streaming sideways in the current like a flag.

She jerks her chin at the Mouth. Well?

I let go of the rope.

I get both hands in among the soft roots, as deep as they'll go, and both plated feet, and I hang there in the throat of the Mouth with the whole sea trying to pull me through, and I do the hardest thing of all.

I keep still.

And I say it.

Not out loud. With everything: the two million coiled springs of me, palms and neck and the backs of my knees. I think of chalk, and the old roots at the Knuckle saying the same thing for longer than Highwater has had a name. Here. Stop here. Hold on.

My wrist lights up.

I see it through my own eyelids. The leaf veins burning green-gold, running up my arm in threads, brighter than they've ever been, until I'm a lamp hung in the Mouth of the reef with the sea pouring past me.

The snow turns.

Not all of it. A drift of it, nearest me, wavers in the current. Turns over. And comes down.

They land on my hands.

Tiny, pale, soft as a breath, a dozen, a hundred, settling on my fingers where they're buried in the roots. And I feel them grab. Each one throws out threads, fine as hair, faster than I can see, and the threads stick and pull tight, and the soft root under my hand tightens with them, one thread at a time, like somebody sewing up a split seam.

I keep saying it. And the snow keeps coming.

Out of the dark, out of the roaring current, the glowing specks peel away by the thousand and swing in to the reef, all along the Mouth, a long bright curtain folding down onto the soft white roots, settling, grabbing, stitching. Every root in the Mouth lights up, crawling with threads, the way frost grew across the dome's glass the hard winter, in feathers, pane by pane.

Something hits me.

A branch, a great dead limb torn loose by the storm, coming through the Mouth end-on like a battering ram. It takes me across the back and rips my left hand out of the roots, and I'm going, turning over in the bright water with the Mouth roaring past.

My gills slam shut.

Breathe properly.

A hand closes on my ankle. Long fingers. Hard as a root.

Sedge, upside down, both legs locked round a root and her whole body stretched flat into the current, has me by the foot. She hauls. I come back into the roots and get my hand in, and she doesn't let go, she just holds on, and holds on, and I open my mouth and let the sea in, and my gills open, and I say it again.

Here. Hold on.

I don't know how long. My wrist burns. My chest burns. I'm so hungry the hunger's got teeth. The snow comes down and down, and under my hands, root by root, a thread at a time, the Mouth stops being soft.

It doesn't close. I won't pretend it does. The sea still comes over the top in a long white roaring sheet. But it comes over the top. Not through. The pull on my legs goes from a river to a stream to a heavy breathing, in and out, the way the sea breathes everywhere else.

And the snow still falling past us on the tide swings in to the reef by itself now, wherever a root has caught the word.

Sedge lets go of my ankle.

She puts her hand flat on the root beside my face, on the stitched place, glowing. Then she puts her hand flat on my chest.

My gills don't close.

* * *

We come up in the lee of the Knuckle in the last of the dark, climb out onto the roots, and lie there.

The storm's gone over. The rain's just rain. Far out, the lightning's still going, silent, too far off for its sound to reach us, over somewhere else. At the top of the biggest tree someone has kept the fire going in its basket, and the smoke goes straight up.

I'm coughing, the dry ache, the paper crackle. Sedge thumps me on the back without being asked, which doesn't help at all.

Then something comes out of my collar.

Not Biscuit; Biscuit is up on the high platform where I left it with three moonpears and a whistle from Sedge that I think meant watch the otter. It's one of the snow. A stitcher the size and colour of my littlest fingernail, that's ridden all the way up out of the sea inside my shirt. It walks along my collarbone. Then it throws out six fine golden threads and sticks itself firmly to my skin, just below my ear, just above my gills.

I look at it, cross-eyed.

"Oh, no," says Sedge, and she's laughing again, flat on her back on the Knuckle with the rain on her face. "Oh, no. It thinks you're the reef."

"Get it off."

"I'm not touching it, it's holding on. That's the whole point."

It's very happy, says the voice. It sounds tired, and pleased, and full. It says here. It says stop here.

"Tell it I'm not a wall."

You were, says the voice. For a bit.

* * *

At dawn the tide goes out, slow and brown and tired, full of branches and torn sailcloth and drowned fruit, and the reef comes up out of it.

I watch from the Knuckle, with the stitcher under my ear like an earring Gran wouldn't approve of. All along the Mouth, where the soft white roots were, there's a crust now. Thin, new, pale gold, dense with tiny shells, each one hung on by its threads. The Mouth's still there, a long dip in the wall like the back of an old horse. But it's a horse that's standing up.

Sedge is asleep sitting up beside me, head against a root, her long toes still curled round another one, in case.

Behind us, people are coming down onto the root-walks to look. The fear's gone off them. There's tiredness, like a wet coat, and something fizzy and disbelieving, the way the lane felt the morning the bread came back; and under it the cold sharp one, but thinner.

Teasel walks the reef with his hands behind his back, looking at the new gold crust all the way to the Mouth and all the way back. When he gets to me he stops, and breathes in.

He doesn't say anything. He takes a moonpear out of his coat, puts it down on the root beside my foot, and walks on.

I eat it. Cold water with honey in it. The best thing I've ever eaten.

Then I take out the reed stem and tie the eighteenth knot, late, with fingers that still taste of chalk.

That's when the voices on the landing go quiet, where the root-walks come in under the trees, and Sedge wakes, and I stand up and turn round.

There's an old woman on the landing.

Small. Not much taller than Tilly. Brown as a nut, white hair cropped short as a field after harvest, one eye milky and clouded and the other black and quick as a bird's. She has both hands on top of a stick and her shoulder against a grey post, and I could swear the side of her that's touching the post has gone grey too, a little, as if the post is coming off on her.

The people on the walk have stopped. They're looking at her the way you look at Elder Marrow when he's about to say something.

She's looking at me.

At the stitcher on my neck. At my plated feet. At my tired, glowing wrist. Then at my face, a long time, head on one side, the good eye narrowing, as if she's reading a sign that's gone silver in the weather.

Then she laughs: short, creaky and loud, like a gate that hasn't been opened in years getting kicked open.

"Well," she says. "You've got Ada Hale's chin."


## Chapter Ten: Population: None

> RECOVERY TENDER RT-6 · CAPACITY: 2 (TWO) ASSOCIATES OR 400 KG RECOVERED MATERIALS · OPERATE IN CHARTED WATERS ONLY · ALL WATERS ARE CHARTED
>
> — instruction plate riveted inside the bow of a Varro-Tandem Bioreference recovery tender; someone has scratched a small question mark after the last line
>

The boy's footprints went out onto the mud and stopped, and for about a minute I believed the sea had him.

I'd found the first sign of him at noon on the nineteenth, on the last hill above the coast, after a night and a morning walking north-west from the Hiveplains. The grass up there was grey and tough as wire, combed inland by the wind, all but one oval at the brow, flattened as if it had been sat on and had not yet decided to get up.

I knelt and put my face to it, because that is the kind of Examiner I have become, and smelled salt, and crushed stems, and under them, so faint I might have been remembering it, warm bread.

"Hask. He sat here."

Hask came up the slope behind me, three leaves in his joint and a skin of Hiveplains gold dust still in the carving, and stopped dead at the brow.

Below us, the world ended.

I had seen the sea once before, in a lightning flash from the top of a hive. That had been a picture. This was the thing itself. It began at the foot of the hill and went out flat and grey and moving everywhere at once, all the way to a straight line where the sky came down to sit on it. It breathed. A long hush up the sand and a long draw back, so slow and so big that after a while you stopped hearing it and started feeling it in the soles of your feet, the way you feel a ship's engine through a bunk.

Between the hill and the sea lay miles of shining mud, slate-grey, cut by wandering silver channels; and out on the mud stood the forest I'd seen from the tower. Trees on legs. Every trunk held up a man's height clear of the ground in a cage of arching roots, dozens to a tree, so that the whole wood seemed to be wading out to sea on tiptoe, holding its skirts up. Its green roof ran along the coast north and south until the haze ate it.

And out of that roof, a long way off, a single thread of grey smoke still went straight up into the clean air.

Hask said nothing for so long that a gull landed on his top stone, looked at me, barked once like a small offended dog, and left.

"Hask?"

"I have crossed between stars eleven times," said Hask, "in the holds of ships. I had not seen this." A long grinding pause. "Is it enumerated?"

"The sea?"

"Drowning is enumerated. For the bearer." Another pause. "Not for the Proxy. I have looked. I cannot drown." "It is very large, Examiner."

"Yes."

"I find I wish to stand in it."

* * *

The prints went down the hill, and I went with them.

On the sand at the bottom they were easy: broad, ridged, toe-knuckled, the plated feet I had first seen at the 40-K stream ten days ago, a boot tread with no boot. And beside them, weaving in and out of them like a dog that cannot walk in a straight line to save its life, a second, smaller track: two little forefeet, then four behind, webbed between the toes, with a drag-mark of tail. The same print I'd found beside a replanted seed under a skylight in the Undercroft.

"His animal's still with him," I said.

"I am glad," said Hask, which surprised us both.

The prints went out onto the mud. The mud took them and kept them, deep and clear, every ridge of plating printed in it like a seal in wax. They went a hundred paces towards the trees, detoured to a pool where something with one enormous claw was still waving it at nobody, and went on.

And then they went into a channel, and did not come out the other side.

I stood at the edge of it. It was as wide as a village lane, and it was full, and it was running: inland, past my boots, quick and silver and full of turning weed, the wrong way for any river I'd ever met. While I watched, the water came up over the toes of my boots. I looked back. There was water between me and the hill, spreading over the mud like milk over a tabletop, coming from everywhere at once, and the last of my own footprints were filling.

"Examiner," said Hask from the sand, in the voice he keeps for things that are about to be enumerated.

I went back fast, with the sea round my shins. By the time I reached him the mud was a lake, and the forest was a forest standing in a lake, and every print I'd followed was under a metre of moving water.

I stood on the sand and dripped and thought: he didn't know. He came down this hill at dawn yesterday and walked out to look at a crab, and the sea came in behind him from everywhere at once, and he had never in his life been in water over his head.

I made myself look again.

Twice, the Survey teaches you. Once for what you're afraid of, and once for what's there. And what was there, on the sand at my feet where I'd come back up, was the smaller track, the six-footed one, coming out of the water. Wet and new, from this morning's low tide, wandering up the beach to a dead fish, and turning round, and going back towards the trees as purposeful as a postman.

An animal doesn't come back to a place where its boy drowned and go foraging. It goes home.

And the smoke, out there on the drowned forest, went straight up.

* * *

That evening I did the thing I'd come to do, which was watch.

I sat on the sand with my back against Hask and my slate on my knee and wrote down the sea. By dusk the tide was going out again, hissing away between the trees in a hundred channels. I counted from the turn: Survey habit. Count, write it down, count again. Twice a day, a little over six hours each way. Tomorrow's high water would come just after dawn.

At the edge of a pool left by the ebb I watched a small fish flip, silver, on the mud. Something came out of the water at it so fast I didn't see it arrive, only the shape after: long, dark, a mouth like a pair of shears. The fish was gone. Beside the same pool, a crab walked slowly along the bottom, right under the long dark shape, and nothing touched it.

Slow was not cross. Fast was. I had learned that from a meadow of bees two days ago, and it seemed the sea had the same opinion. I wrote it down anyway. A rule you've only met once is a rumour.

On every root that the ebb uncovered, at the same height, ran a band of tiny blue-black shells, packed as close as cobbles, and above the shells a line of dried weed, and above the weed, bare bark. A bathtub ring round the whole forest. I thought about a crayfish shell in the fork of a tree on a brown river world, and Teague's tired kind voice, and then I made myself read it instead. The shells were where the water lived. The weed was where it went at the top of an ordinary tide. Where the roots were shell to the knee, the channel ran deep. Where a root was bare to its foot, the mud under that channel sat high, and the ebb would leave it first.

I sketched the nearest channels, marked the bare roots, and felt better than I had all day.

Out over the forest, against the last green of the sky, something small and grey with a blue nose-light moved slowly along the canopy, stopping and starting, like a heron fishing.

"Pell-4," I said.

"He is looking for warmth," said Hask.

"He's looking for a number."

* * *

Night came down warm, smelling of salt and rot-sweet fruit and, very far off, woodsmoke.

I took my boots off and walked into the sea.

I'm not proud of the reason: I was a sandwich of gold dust and sweat and dried mud. But when I stepped into the shallows the water lit up round my ankle.

Blue-green, cold, the colour of the Undercroft and of my own fingernails. Where my foot moved it left a smear of light. I bent and swept my hand through the water and a comet came off my fingers, sparks streaming off every knuckle, falling away behind like embers off a blown coal. Each spark was a living thing too small to see, flashing when disturbed. Every wave broke in green fire along its whole length.

I stood in the sea to my knees, an Examiner of the Inspectorate with my trousers rolled, and wrote my name in light on the water with one finger, and watched it go out. Then I did it again.

Behind me there was a long grinding step, and another, and Hask walked into the sea.

The water lit round him, and he walked out into the dark on a pool of green light, the carved law on his lower stones glowing from beneath as if written in fire. The sea came up to his third stone, his fourth. He stopped with the water lapping at the lichen, and stood there, enormous and dark against the glowing waves, and did not say anything at all.

I didn't either. Some things you let a person have.

Later, from the wrack line where the storm had piled branches and weed and torn red-patched sailcloth, I pulled out a boat.

A punt, flat-bottomed, pegged, not a nail in it. It had a pole lashed inside it and a short frayed bow rope, and on its bow somebody had carved, small and with great care, a heron standing on one leg.

At about midnight a company drone came in low over the sea, a grey shape with its running lights off, and set something down on the sand a few hundred metres north. Something long and grey with a short mast. Then it lifted away.

Two thin figures came limping down the beach to meet it, carrying their shoes. Their voices carried over the sand.

"Twelve. That is my side."

"There are no sides, Seven. It is a boat."

"There are always sides."

* * *

Halloran called at dawn, bacon frying.

"Pastern." Tock. "Your bulletin made the crawl."

I was sitting in the heron punt with my boots on, watching the tide come in. "Which one?"

"You know which one. STEADY to UNDER DISCREPANCY, between a soap opera and a toll rise, and by breakfast the Chancery had sent me a note with a seal on it the size of a dinner plate." Tock. "I've put it in the tin with the shells."

"Am I in trouble, sir?"

"You're always in trouble." A pause, frying. "Varro-Tandem filed a fresh recovery claim at four this morning. Coastal margin, north-west. Materials, various. Not specimen. Various."

"The Register says the coast's uninhabited," I said.

"It does." Tock. "Which means anything they find there is nobody's, and anything that's nobody's is whoever picks it up." He let that sit. "You've got three days, Pastern. Don't spend them all in one place."

"I wasn't planning to spend them anywhere, sir. I was planning to find him."

"Then find him," said Halloran, "before somebody numbers him," and the line closed like a log settling in a grate.

North along the beach, the grey tender had its motor running: a high, quick, eager whine, like a wasp in a jar.

* * *

Hask towed me into the forest along the bottom of the sea.

A punt is a boat for one person and a pole. Hask in it would have gone to the bottom like an anchor with opinions. But Hask cannot drown. He has looked.

So I put the bow rope in his top stone's grip, and he walked into the channel, and kept walking, down the slope of the channel bed, until the water came up past his fourth stone, his fifth, his sixth.

He stopped there. "The leaves," he said.

"Salt?"

"Salt is not enumerated as a harm to leaves. I have checked." A pause. "I do not find this reassuring."

So he took the channels by their margins, where the mud shelved up, and kept his seventh stone above the water. And the bow rope went down into the sea at a slant, and the punt went forward with a little chuckle of water at its bow, steady and slow as a man walking to church, and ahead of me there was nothing in the channel but the upper half of a cairn, wading, with three pale leaves riding a hand above the water like a buttonhole.

Through the clear water I could see the rest of him. The dark stones walking on the bottom, the carved law on them wavering in the green light, tiny silver fish nosing at the lichen on his fourth stone. A crab had got on at the beach and was riding on his shoulder ledge, claws up, like a figurehead.

We passed in under the roof.

It was like going into a church from a hot street. The light went green and soft and dim, coming down through leaves in long slanting bars that lay on the water and wavered. The air was warm and wet and smelled of salt and fruit: windfalls bobbing in the slack water, purple-black plums with a white bloom on them, long knobbled figs hanging in ropes from the branches, and higher up, in the deepest shade, pale things the size of a fist that glowed. Faintly. A lamp turned right down. Roots creaked. Water sucked and clicked round ten thousand arching legs, and everywhere, small and high and quick, came whistles: one from the left, and an answer far off on the right, and another overhead, passing away into the leaves like a rumour going round a market.

"They are talking about us," said Hask, ahead of me.

"How do you know?"

"Everything is," said Hask.

A fish broke the surface ahead, quick and silver, and the shears came up out of the dark and took it, and the long shape slid back under, right past the punt. It turned its blunt head towards the bow, and the bow went on past at a church-going walk, and the eel lost interest and went down among the roots.

I sat very still with my hands in my lap and my heart going like a drum. Twice is a rule.

Behind us, the wasp-in-a-jar whine came into the forest, and the whistles all round us stopped.

* * *

The net came out of the sky.

I didn't see Pell-4 until he'd fired. The little one-seat skiff was hanging in a hole in the canopy where a storm-killed tree had come down, blue nose-light on, and Pell-4 was leaning out of it with the net-launcher's bell mouth pointed at me. There was a soft cough, and the air above the punt filled with something spun and spreading and fine as cobweb.

I had time to get one arm up. The net came down over me and the punt together, light as a breath and tough as wire, and pulled tight; and up above, a small motor whirred, and the line from the launcher to the net went taut and began to reel in.

The punt's bow came up out of the water.

"Good morning, Examiner," called Pell-4. The bee stings had gone down; his face was nearly a face again. "I'm so sorry. Nothing personal." The launcher on its strap chimed. He glanced at its screen. "Oh dear."

I could see it from where I was, tipped on my back in a tilting boat with cobweb over my face. RECOVERED MATERIALS: 1 (one) EXAMINER, grade unknown. 1 (one) BOAT, ownership not established. Entered to company stock 6:41 am.

"I'll get you out at once," Pell-4 said, and pressed something, and the launcher made a sorry little two-note sound and said in a pleasant recorded voice, Release of logged stock requires disposal authorisation. Please contact your line supervisor.

He pressed it again.

Release of logged stock requires—

"Ah," said Pell-4.

The punt came up another hand's breadth. I was going to be reeled up out of the forest like a fish in a bag and filed somewhere in a company warehouse under Boats.

Then Hask let go of the bow rope and turned, and came back down the channel in three long strides with the sea breaking white off his chest, and rose.

He came up all of a piece, the way he rises, the water pouring off every stone in sheets and streaming out of the carving, weed in his joints, the crab still on his shoulder with its claws up. He rose until he towered over the punt, three wet leaves trembling in his middle, and his top stone came down onto the net and held it, and the punt, flat to the water.

The line sang tight. The skiff above dipped its nose.

"Item eighty-eight," said Hask. "Unwelcome hands."

"It's a net," called Pell-4, sounding genuinely interested.

"A net," said Hask, "is a great many hands."

The skiff's motor climbed. The line hummed between the sky and the stone like a bowstring. Pell-4 was trying to lift an Orrun Proxy out of the sea with a craft built for one thin man and his ledger case.

I lay under the cobweb looking up the line, and thought about Rook on a limestone world saying walk up the inside of the world.

I had my knife out of my boot before I'd finished thinking it.

The net gave to the blade one strand at a time with a sound like plucked wire. I cut a slit, wriggled out, and stood up in the tipping punt with Hask's top stone beside me and the taut line going up from the net into the sky.

Ten metres. Maybe twelve. A single grey filament no thicker than a bootlace, humming.

I put the coil of my honeyed rope over my shoulder. I wrapped the launcher line round my right forearm, twice, and put my boots against the trunk of the nearest tree, and walked up it.

Hand over hand. The filament cut into my palms through the dried honey. Below me the green water spread out like a floor; above me the skiff came closer, dipping and straining, and Pell-4's face over the side looking down at me climbing his own line towards him with an expression of the purest courtesy and dismay.

"Examiner," he said, when my head came level with his beetle legs. "You're on my line."

"Yes."

"I really must ask you to—"

I got a boot onto the skiff's left leg and swung, and the whole craft rocked like a cradle, and Pell-4 grabbed the edge of his canopy with both hands. I didn't go for him. I don't hit men who say nothing personal; it only encourages them. I went for the bough of a rope-fig a metre from the skiff's tail, thick as my waist.

I took the end of the launcher line, where it ran into the skiff's belly through a little brass eye, and I took a turn of my own rope round it, and round the bough, and round the line again, and pulled it down tight into the hitch Rook had made me tie fifty times on a cold morning until I could do it in the dark. I cut the line below the hitch. Then I let go and dropped.

Two metres into a crook of branches, which hurt, and three more down a curtain of hanging fig-ropes, which hurt differently, and a short fall into the channel beside Hask, which didn't hurt at all.

I came up spitting salt.

Above us, the skiff hung in its hole in the sky, nose down, motor whining, tied to a fig tree by its own net line like a grey kite on a string. Pell-4 leaned out, looked at the hitch, looked at the launcher's screen, and pressed the release once more, without much hope.

Release of logged stock requires disposal authorisation.

"I'll contact my line supervisor," said Pell-4, with dignity, and took out his ledger.

From the trees all round us, somewhere up in the leaves, a whistle went up. Short, short, long. And then another, from further off. And then, I could have sworn, something that was not a whistle at all, but a child, laughing.

* * *

The tender came round the bend at a speed it had no business doing in a forest.

It was long and grey and low, the colour of a filing cabinet, with a high quick motor at the stern like a wasp in a jar, and two thin men in grey sitting in it, one at the tiller and one in the bow, both very straight, very far apart.

"Examiner Pastern!" called Pell-12. His face was still faintly pudding from the bees. "Good morning! We have a claim."

"I heard."

"Coastal margin. Materials, various." "The coast is listed uninhabited, Examiner. Anything we recover here is, by definition, nobody's." He smiled, pleasantly. "It really is nothing personal."

I thought of the whistles, and the child laughing.

"Hask," I said quietly, "how fast can you walk on the bottom?"

"As fast as I walk on the top," said Hask. "Which is to say, Examiner, with deliberation."

"Good."

I climbed back into the punt, still trailing its cut net like a bridal veil, and Hask took the bow rope, and we went.

Not fast. Never fast. Away from them along the channel at a church-going walk, Hask wading ahead, the punt chuckling. Behind us the wasp-whine rose, and the tender came after us in a wake that slapped the roots and woke the whole channel up.

"Examiner," called Pell-7 from the tiller, in a voice like a door closed very gently on a very cold room. He was wearing company shoes. They were plainly two sizes too large. "I don't wish to be rude, but you are going extremely slowly."

"Yes," I said.

The net-launcher in Pell-12's hands coughed. I ducked, and the cobweb went over my head and wrapped itself, with great efficiency, round the low bough of a fig tree hanging over the channel. The launcher chimed. Pell-12 glanced at it, and his face did something complicated.

"What does it say, Twelve?" said Seven.

"It says we have recovered a tree."

"What kind?"

Twelve read it. "Fig, rope. Ownership not established."

"I shall be filing about that."

"Of course you will."

Ahead of me the channel split round a big tree with roots like a cathedral. To the left it ran on wide and obvious, the channel you'd take in a hurry; to the right, narrow and dark. The roots on the left were bare to the foot, every one, weed and grey bark going down into the water. On the right they were crusted with blue-black shells to the knee, all the way along.

The water round the punt had stopped running upstream. The weed on the roots, a minute ago streaming inland, hung slack now, turning, thinking about it. High water. Just after dawn, as I'd counted.

"Hask. Right, please. Into the dark one."

"Is it enumerated?"

"No. It's arithmetic."

We went right. Slowly.

And the tender, which had been chasing an Examiner in a punt for a quarter of an hour and feeling foolish about it, saw the wide clear water on the left, and a chance to cut round ahead of me, and opened its throttle with a great eager howl.

It was the right decision for a boat in a hurry. The weed on the roots was already turning, beginning to stream the other way, seaward. The ebb had begun. And under that wide clear water, where not a single root carried a single shell, the mud sat high.

I heard it before I saw it. The motor's note went up, and up, a scream, as the little propeller came near the surface and bit into the churned water and spat foam, quick as a fish flipping on mud. And something long and dark came up out of the roots at it the way the shears had come up at the fish.

And another. And three more.

There was a thump and a sound like a wet sack fed into a mangle, and the motor's scream went to a choke, and then to nothing. The tender sat in the middle of the wide channel, stern down, festooned with long dark furious shapes that had their teeth in the propeller and were not letting go. Pell-12 had his compliance wand out, its brass cap fizzing blue, and was leaning out over the stern towards the water.

"Twelve," said Seven, quite calmly. "Salt water."

"I know what water is, Seven."

"Then you know where it conducts to."

Twelve looked at the wand, and the water, and the bottom of the tender, and his own feet in their good shoes, and put the wand away.

The ebb took the water out from under them. You could watch it go. It drew away down the wide channel, quick and shallow, and the tender settled, and tilted, and sat down on a shining bank of mud with a long contented squelch, like an old man lowering himself into a chair. The eels stayed where they were, among the roots round the stranded boat, in the pools, waiting for something to move quickly.

The Associates sat in their tender on the mud and did not move quickly. They are not fools. They are only Associates.

"Examiner!" called Pell-7.

I had Hask stop. We were thirty metres off, in the deep crusted channel, still afloat.

"I should like it noted," said Pell-7, "that I am filing a grievance against the sea."

"Noted."

"And against Twelve, for the route."

"Against Seven," said Twelve, "for the shoes."

From somewhere above, faint and tinny, a third voice drifted down through the canopy from a grey skiff tied to a fig tree. "I should like it noted that I was here first."

I tore a Form 9 off the pad in my coat, wrote on it Assistance available on request. Tide back in about six hours. Move slowly. The eels bite what's quick, folded it into a dart, and threw it. It landed in the mud a metre short of the tender. Neither of them reached for it.

They're learning.

* * *

Highwater did not so much appear as let me see it.

One moment I was gliding under the roof with the ebb running and Hask wading ahead; the next I looked up and the roof was full of people. On platforms lashed across the branches, on sagging rope bridges, in doorways of woven houses roofed with sailcloth patched red: children, mostly, but not only children. Nobody spoke. They watched me the way the guard bees had watched me on the hive doorstep, and every few heartbeats somebody whistled, softly, and somebody else, further off, whistled back.

Their hands were wrong for the Register. A woman gutting fish had fingers as long as my hand. A boy on a ladder had webs between his. An old man in a doorway had forearms scaled fine as a fish's. Pickers. Divers. Climbers. Lines I'd seen in catalogues, under discontinued.

The Register said there was nobody here at all. I half-closed my eyes and did what Fallow taught me, and for one breath walked it: the long hall of the coast in my head, drawer after drawer, every one labelled none. And standing in the aisle between them, in the dark, more people than I could count, with nowhere to put their names. That would want a proper Reading, later, sitting down.

The water had gone out far enough to bare the mud round the foot of the biggest tree in the village, a grey trunk as smooth as a wall with notches cut up its side from the mud to the lowest platform: dozens, each with little marks beside it. Most of them were near the top.

Hask walked up out of the channel onto the mud and stood at the foot of the trunk with the sea running out of his joints, and the whole village leaned back a little, all at once, like a field of barley when the wind changes.

A ladder came down from the low platform. A man came down it, not fast. Tall, long-fingered, grey in his beard, a white scar puckered down from one ear. A gutting knife on his belt that he didn't touch.

He looked at me in the punt, and at Hask, and then he leaned towards me, not close, and breathed in through his nose. I saw his face go puzzled, as if he'd opened a cupboard expecting plates and found a bird's nest.

"You smell," he said slowly, "of a goat. And a hive. And tar."

"Tea."

"That's not tea."

"It's very strong tea." I held out the bow rope. "This came ashore on the beach in the storm. I think it's yours. I'm sorry about the net. That's not mine either."

He looked at the heron on the bow. Something in his face went very still. He took the rope, and turned the punt in the shallow water with one hand, and looked at the knot I'd tied in its bow ring last night, by fingernail-light, in the dark.

"Who tied that?"

"I did."

He looked at me with a sort of tired pity. "Who wrote you?"

I know a form when I hear one. Which company. Which line.

"Nobody," I said. "I'm the plain kind."

"There's no plain kind," said the man, and went up the ladder with his boat's rope over his shoulder, leaving the punt for somebody else to haul, and me standing in a foot of ebbing water, being looked at by a whole village.

I climbed the ladder after him, because nobody had said I couldn't.

* * *

The low platform was wet to the ankle still, the boards dark with the tide,, the houses round it marked a hand high inside their doorsills. It smelled of woodsmoke and fish and, from somewhere, aniseed.

The aniseed came from a cut-down barrel at the platform's edge, full of dark silt, with a feathery herb growing in it.

I'd been looking at the ground since a stream in the Spill, and I knew the shape before I knew I'd seen it.

Two small dimples in the silt, beside the herb. First knuckle deep, and not a hair more.

I crouched by the barrel, and put out my finger, and didn't touch them.

When I stood up, he was there.

He was on a rope bridge at the far side of the platform, coming down it with his arms full of pale glowing fruit, talking over his shoulder to a girl behind him. He stopped dead with one foot on the boards.

I'd been carrying his face in the Register for eleven days: the yearly portrait, taken in a white room under the Upper House the spring he was called, a boy with a village haircut and a clean neck, looking at the lens the way you'd look at a dog you weren't sure of. It was the face. It was not the boy.

He was shorter than me, as I'd known from a bolt-plate in the Undercroft. Thinner than his portrait, brown from three weeks of sky, in a sailcloth shirt far too big for him with the sleeves rolled up in four hard turns and a red patch on one elbow. His feet were bare, and they weren't feet any more, not as the Register would have them: grey-green, ridged like bark, the plating grown up round his heels and over the knuckles of his toes, gripping the bridge's rope the way a hand grips a rail. At his left wrist, under the skin, lay a tracery of fine green-gold lines like the veins of a leaf held up to the sun, and it was faintly lit, as if something under there were awake and paying attention. Under each of his ears, three pink slits, one above another, the edges fine as the edge of a mushroom's gill. And under his right ear, just above the slits, stuck to his skin like an earring nobody's grandmother would allow, a single small shell, blue-black, on threads of gold.

Something on his shoulder lifted its head. Brown-black and sleek, two short legs in front holding on to his collar, four behind, a blunt whiskered face, and a throat that glowed, as I watched, from nothing to a slow, wary amber.

He looked at me. He looked down past me, over the edge, at Hask standing on the mud with the sea running out of his carving.

And all six slits on his neck slammed shut.

I saw it happen. Like little doors closing in a wind, snap, three each side, all at once. I'd never seen a body say I am frightened so plainly, or so far outside anything in the Register.

"Bram," I said. "Bram Hale. My name is Odile Pastern. I'm an Examiner, I'm not with—"

He ran.

Back up the bridge the way he'd come, the moonpears going everywhere, glowing, bouncing off the planks and dropping into the water below like lamps let fall. The girl behind him swung aside without seeming to move to let him past, and stared after him, and then at me.

And halfway across the bridge he stopped.

An old man was there, coming the other way with a basket of plums on his hip, and in getting past him Bram had knocked it, and the basket had tipped, and purple-black plums were rolling all over the planks towards the gaps.

With an Examiner on his trail and a pile of walking stone below him and every reason in the world to keep going, he went down on his knees on a swaying bridge in front of the whole village and picked up the plums. Quickly. One at a time. He put them back in the old man's basket, and set it straight on the old man's hip, and said something to him I couldn't hear.

Then he went up a ladder and into the leaves like a squirrel up a wall, and was gone.

* * *

I didn't chase him.

I could climb; I'd climbed a net line that morning. But nobody ever turned round to answer a question for the person behind them.

My first year at the Inspectorate, on a wet world whose name I've let myself forget, Fallow and I had a witness run from us. A boy of ten who'd seen who set a mill fire went out of the back of his mother's house like a cat off a hot stove the moment we came in at the front. I was twenty-seven and fast and I went after him, and lost him in three streets, and came back an hour later with my knees torn and my temper worse, and found Fallow sitting on the boy's mother's front step in the drizzle, eating an onion pasty out of a paper bag. The boy was sitting next to him. He had half the pasty.

"How," I said.

Fallow wiped his fingers on his coat. His knees were bad that winter; he hadn't moved off the step in an hour. "Nobody," he said, "runs from a man eating lunch."

I sat down on the edge of the low platform at Highwater, with my boots hanging over the mud beside Hask's top stone, and took out my flask.

The steel was warm through my coat where it had ridden all morning. Four dents in it now: three from long before I met any of this, and one from a heat-hunter in the Spill, with two tooth holes next to it. I unscrewed it, and the steam came up into the green light, tar and burnt raisins, and every head on every platform turned to look.

"Item fourteen," said Hask, below me, very softly.

"I know, Hask."

I took out my slate, and laid it on my knee, and opened the case file, and began to write up the morning. 1 (one) Associate, grade 4, tethered. 2 (two) Associates, grades 7 and 12, aground. 1 (one) tree, fig, rope, recovered, ownership not established. My palms stung where the launcher line had cut them. I drank my tea. I wrote. Nobody runs from a woman eating lunch, and I hadn't got any lunch, so it would have to be paperwork.

The village watched me do it.
