After a while, the whistles started up again, low and conversational. After a longer while, the woman gutting fish went back to gutting fish. A baby in a net above me woke, and grizzled, and was handed down a ladder from person to person.

And a small girl came and stood in front of Hask.

She was perhaps five. She had long fingers like the woman with the fish, and a smear of plum on her chin, and she stood on the root at the foot of the big tree and looked up at him, all the way up, at the carving and the crab and the three leaves, and she didn't say a word. She just stared, with her whole face.

Hask looked down at her for a long time. Then his top stone turned, very slightly, the way it had turned once to look at a girl in a kitchen under a dome with her spoon halfway to her mouth.

"Yes," said Hask. "I am from beyond the Shining."

The girl's mouth fell open. "What's the Shining?"

A long grinding pause.

"I do not know," said Hask. "I was asked once. I have been waiting to answer correctly."

She thought about this. Then she reached out one long finger and touched the crab on his shoulder ledge, and the crab raised a claw at her, outraged, and she shrieked with joy and ran up the ladder, and came back with three more children.

* * *

The girl from the bridge came down about an hour later.

She came down out of the leaves like a dropped scarf, touching every branch on the way, and landed on the boards beside me without making them shake. Long fingers, long toes, freckles in dark patches across her nose and shoulders like the bark of a plane tree, hair in a wet dark rope. His age, near enough.

She squatted on her heels an arm's length from me and looked at my flask, and my slate, and my fingernails, which were glowing a little in the shade, and then at my face.

"Are you the grey boat?"

"No," I said. "I'm the thing the grey boat files against."

She considered this. "Teasel says you tied his boat up with a knot like a wet dog."

"Is Teasel the man with the scar?"

"Teasel says," she went on, "you hung one of them in a tree."

"Only by his net."

Something went across her face that might, in a better light, have become a laugh. She shut it down hard.

"You followed him," she said. "All the way from the dome."

"From a stream in the Spill. He planted tomatoes by a pond. And four at the bottom of a hive. And two," I nodded at the barrel, "there."

She looked at the barrel for a long time. Then back at me, and her eyes had gone narrow.

"What happens," she said, "if you catch him?"

Every answer I had was on a form, and none of them was good enough to say out loud to her.

"I don't know yet," I said. "That's the truth. I know what happens if the grey boat catches him." I drank my tea. "So I'd like to get there first."

She looked at me a while longer. Then she reached over, without asking, and took the end of my honeyed rope, and tied it to the platform's rail in a knot I had never seen in six years of Survey and eleven of Inspectorate. One movement. Three heartbeats. It sat on the rail as neat as a bird on a twig.

"Show me that," I said.

"No," she said.

Then, after a moment, very slowly, with her long fingers held where I could see them, she untied it and did it again.

I tried. It came out like a wet dog. She snorted, and untied it, and did it again, slower.

Across the platform, in the shadow of a doorway, an old woman had been watching us. I hadn't seen her until she moved; she'd been leaning against the woven wall and her skin, I would swear under oath, had gone the same brown-grey as the wicker. Small, white hair cropped short, one eye clouded white and the other black and very bright. She looked at me the way a good Examiner reads a document: all of it, and then the signature.

"Sedge," she said. "Fetch the woman some water. She's drinking tar."

The girl, Sedge, rolled her eyes and went. The old woman leaned back against the wicker and let it have her again, slowly, the way a stone takes on the colour of the rain, and looked away over the drowned orchard as if I were already filed.

* * *

He came back in the middle of the afternoon, with the tide on the turn.

I'd seen him a dozen times before that and not let myself look. A pale shirt in the leaves. A glow of amber in a fork of branches where something sat with its throat lit, watching me with great suspicion, and then my flask with great interest. Once, a pair of grey-green feet hanging from a branch right over my head, toes curled round the bark, for the length of one page of slate, before they were pulled up out of sight.

Then a moonpear came down out of the leaves and landed on the boards beside my flask with a soft thump, and rolled, and stopped against my knee, glowing faintly.

I didn't look up. I picked it up and bit it. Cold water with honey in it, and a smell like rain. I hadn't eaten since the Hiveplains.

A ladder creaked. Bare plated feet on the boards, slow. He sat down an arm's length and a half away with his knees up, and the animal came down off his shoulder and walked straight across the boards to my flask and smelled it, sneezed, and smelled it again.

"It likes that," he said. His voice was hoarse, with a dry catch at the bottom like a cough he was keeping in his pocket. "I don't know why. It likes all the worst smells."

"Good," I said. "So do I."

He wasn't looking at me. He was looking down at the mud, where Hask stood at the foot of the big tree with five small children sitting on his bottom stone and a sixth trying to get onto his shoulder ledge with the crab.

The six slits on his neck were open, fluttering a little in the air. As I watched, one on the left side began to close, and then thought better of it.

"At the stream," he said. "In the Spill. When the grazers came down." He swallowed. "There was a pile of stones, and they turned round, before they stood up. Like they were looking at me. And then they were a roof."

"Yes," I said.

He looked at Hask a long time. The child on the shoulder ledge fell off into the soft mud, and laughed, and climbed up again.

Then the boy turned his head and looked at me properly for the first time, and asked the only question he'd come back to ask.

"What is he?"

Below us, Hask's top stone turned, slowly, in the green afternoon light, until it was facing up at the two of us on the edge of the platform, and the children on his feet went quiet.

"I," said Hask, "am a discrepancy."


## Chapter Eleven: Apple, Crock, Gran, Tilly

> Every word in this book, and every book in the meeting house, is made of the same twenty-six letters. Learn them well, and nothing written can be kept from you.
>
> — The Firstfield Heritage Primer, page one, "Our Letters"; copied out on slates by every child in their first week of school
>

"A what?" I say.

"A discrepancy," says the stone-man.

His voice doesn't come out of anywhere. There's no mouth. It comes up through the mud and the big tree's roots and the boards under me, and I hear it in my jaw before I hear it in my ears, low and slow, like the mill wheel turning over once in its sleep.

The children on his bottom stone have gone quiet. The crab on his shoulder lifts one claw, as if it's seconding him.

I've seen him twice now. Once from the sky, swinging between two grazers, when a pile of dark stones turned round to look at me and then stood up and became a roof. And once an hour ago, from a bridge, which is when I dropped the moonpears.

Close to, he's bigger. Eight stones, or nine, each as big as our kitchen table and carved all over in tiny close lines, like the Charter if the Charter went on for ever. There's lichen on one. There are three pale leaves coming out of a crack between two others, like a buttonhole, which is the most surprising thing about him, and he's got a lot of competition.

And I can't smell him.

I've smelled everything since the Hiveplains. I smell the woman beside me, her tea and the dried honey on her hands, and under them, wary and tired and steady, a smell like a lamp turned down low. I smell the children, and the crab, a bit. But the stone-man smells of wet rock, and salt, and the green of his leaves, and nothing else. No mood. Nothing to tell me if he's cross or kind.

He's the first person in a week I've had to just listen to.

Oh, says the voice behind my eyes, very quietly, as if it doesn't want him to hear. He isn't written. Not in anything I can read. He's carved.

"What's a discrepancy?" I say.

"A thing that is there," says the stone-man, "and is not what the record says." His top stone tilts a little, towards the woman. "The Examiner finds them. I keep the Examiner."

"From what?"

"From four thousand one hundred and twelve harms," he says. "The others are her own affair."

The woman drinks her tea.

* * *

I should say how I got here.

After the reef, after the old woman on the landing said Ada Hale's chin, I opened my mouth to ask how she knew, and she put one dry finger on my lips. "Not now. You're grey. Eat something and sleep, and then we'll see whose you are." I lay down in a net to close my eyes for a minute and opened them on the next morning, with the whistles screaming grey boat, grey boat and Sedge shaking me by the foot.

So I spent yesterday up the trees, and watched a woman in a punt hang a man in grey from a fig tree by his own net, and the stones walk out of the sea. When she said my whole name, I ran. Then I watched her drink tea and do sums for three hours while six children climbed her stone-man, and I felt stupid. So I came down.

* * *

"You were the roof," I say to the stone-man. "At the stream. In the Spill."

"I was." The top stone turns back to me. "Three grazers fell. Item two thousand and six."

"What's item two thousand and six?"

"Falling livestock."

The woman makes a small noise into her flask that might be a cough.

"I'm sorry," I say. "That was me. The flock went up because I grabbed two of them, and the weak ones couldn't keep up, and—" I stop. "Did they hurt you?"

"My third stone is cracked." He says it the way Saul says it rained. "It hears better now."

"You turned round," I say. "Before you stood up. You turned round to look at me."

A long grinding pause. A ripple goes out round his base.

"You were falling upwards," says the stone-man. "It is not enumerated. I wished to see it."

Nobody's ever said anything like that to me. I think he means it the way Tilly means it when she wants to see a thing again.

The girl with plum on her chin tugs at his carving. "Ask him. Ask him what the Shining is."

The stone-man goes still. Not stone-still; he's always that. Stiller.

"I was asked," he says to me, "and I could not answer. I was told I am from beyond the Shining. By a child, in a kitchen, under the dome. She had a spoon halfway to her mouth. I have been waiting to answer correctly."

A kitchen. Under the dome. A spoon halfway to her mouth, because she'd forgotten it was there.

"Was she eight?" I say. My voice has gone small.

"She was of that size," says the stone-man.

"That's Tilly," I say. "That's my sister."

I look at the woman. She's put her flask down.

"We came to your house," she says. "On my first morning. Your sister told me you weren't bad. You just wanted to know." A pause. "Your grandmother didn't look up. She was carving a little wooden bird."

Gran carves when she's waiting for something. Something goes up my throat and sticks there, and I have to look at the mud for a while.

When I can, I look up at the stone-man.

"It's the skywalks," I tell him. "The glass walks over the village, where the visitors go. In the morning they light up all along like a necklace. We call them the Shining. So beyond the Shining is everything that isn't Firstfield. It's here."

The stone-man doesn't move for so long that the crab gets bored and climbs down.

"Then I am from beyond the Shining," he says at last. "I was correct."

He turns his top stone down to the little girl, slowly, with enormous care, the way you'd turn a full jug.

"It is a walk made of glass," he tells her, "where the people go who are looking."

She thinks about this. Then she says, "I knew that," and climbs up onto his shoulder ledge where the crab was.

He's happy, says the voice, puzzled. I can't smell it. I can just tell.

So can I. Something in the boards, in my jaw: a tiny change in his hum, like a held note going up a hair.

"Hask," says the woman. "His name's Hask."

* * *

Then she tells me what she is.

She doesn't make a speech of it. She turns her slate round on her knee and talks while the tide comes in under the platform, slow and brown, lifting the weed on the roots.

Her name is Odile Pastern. She's an Examiner of the Inspectorate of Discrepancies: when the great book of everything on Moriel says one thing and the world says another, she goes and looks. Before that she dug ditches on new worlds and got bitten by things.

"I'm not with the men in grey," she says. "I'm not with the Preserve. I can't make you go anywhere. I can't take you back by myself; I'd need a ruling, and I haven't made one. And I can't stop them coming for you by standing in front of you, though I'll do that as well."

"Then what can you do?"

"I can write things down," she says. "And when I write them down properly, they're true. That's all an Examiner is."

It's the silliest thing I've ever heard, and she doesn't say it like it's silly.

I breathe in. My nose does it without asking now. And under the tea and the honey and the salt I catch something that makes my gills flutter.

Hay. Wet wool. Honey gone a bit to vinegar. And under it, very faint, warm bread.

"You smell of my grazers," I say.

She touches her jaw, on the left side. There's nothing to see there. "One of them sat with me, by your pond. It wiped its face on me. I can't get it off."

"Are they all right?"

It's Hask who answers, in the voice that comes up through the boards.

"The Preserve sent eleven people with fire to burn your valley," he says. "The Examiner made your flock evidence. Evidence may not be destroyed, altered or cleared. The people with fire went home."

I look at her.

She's looking out over the water as though none of this has anything to do with her. But it comes off her, faint and warm, under the lamp-turned-down: something embarrassed. Like Ma when you catch her singing.

"You wrote them down," I say.

"Sixty-three grazers," she says. "Under one number. It was quicker."

Biscuit, who has been circling her flask for some time, puts its whole face in the steam, sneezes, and goes on smelling it with an expression of great happiness.

"Thank you," I say.

"Don't." She sounds cross. She doesn't smell cross. "You haven't heard the rest."

* * *

She turns the slate round again.

There's a picture on it of a boy with a village haircut and a clean neck, looking at whoever made the picture like they were a dog he wasn't sure of. The white room under the Upper House, the spring I was called. I remember the man behind the box saying chin up, and me thinking I didn't know it was down.

Under the picture there are words.

> REFERENCE 12 · HALE, BRAM · MORIEL HERITAGE PRESERVE STATUS: RETIRED.
>

And nothing after. A line, and then nothing, like the bottom of a page somebody tore out.

"That went in more than a week ago," says Odile. "Eleven hours after I opened a case on you. Someone upstairs entered it, and the Register agreed with them."

I know that word. I've known it all my life. It's on the eleven brass plaques above the elders' bench, polished like coins, after the second date on every one. Called. Retired. The work is holy and it takes the rest of your life, and when it's done, you're retired.

"But it means honoured," I say. "It means you did the work, and you rest."

"In the Register," says Odile carefully, "it means there's no next line. Nobody's responsible for you. Nobody has to know where you are."

"It means," says a voice behind us, "they put you out where things eat you."

* * *

The old woman is standing in the doorway.

I didn't hear her come. I didn't smell her either, and that frightens me more than anything since the storm, until I see why. She's leaning against the woven wall, and the wall has had her. Her arm and shoulder and the side of her face have gone the grey-brown of the wicker, slow and patchy, like ink soaking up through blotting paper. Even her smell's gone quiet, the way a moth goes quiet on bark.

She steps off the wall, and the colour drains out of her a little at a time, until she's brown as a nut again. She lowers herself onto the boards beside me, one hand on her stick and one on my shoulder, with a creak in both knees I can hear in my jaw.

She smells of woodsmoke, and salt, and, very faint under everything, of something sharp and green that I last smelled on my own wrist.

Oh, says the voice, very softly. There's somebody in her.

"You've met my lodger," the old woman says, though she can't have heard it. Maybe she just saw my face. "Don't mind it. It's nosy, but it means well."

"Who are you?"

She looks at me sideways with her good eye. "You know how to ask that better than that, Bram Hale. Ada would skin you."

My face goes hot.

"Bram," I say. "Son of Wenna, of the house of Hale. Grandson of Ada Hale." I swallow. "Whose are you?"

She smiles. It goes all the way up her face and makes the clouded eye crinkle along with the good one.

"Lenna," she says. "Daughter of Sal, of the house of Brook. Reference Number Five of Firstfield." She tips her head at the slate on Odile's knee as if it's a dog that once bit her. "Retired."

* * *

Number Five.

I know where it is on the wall. Fifth from the left. Two dates. No name; none of them have names. I used to wonder why, during the long prayers.

"You're dead," I say. It comes out stupid.

"I am seventy-seven," says Lenna Brook, "and I have never once been dead. I've been retired for sixty-two years. It's very restful. I recommend it." She pats my knee. "You've got her chin. And her way of looking at a thing as if it owes her money. Is she well?"

"Her chest's bad."

"It always was. She used to wheeze up the meeting house steps and blame the steps." Her good eye goes far away, past the drowned trees. "We were girls together. You couldn't put a knife between us. We stole bread off the bakehouse sill and blamed the starlings. There weren't any starlings."

"There still aren't."

"Good," she says. "Some things ought to keep."

"Do you know Gran's sayings?"

"Do I know—" Lenna laughs, the creaky gate kicked open. "Ada got every one of them off my mam. Go on. Try me."

"Look twice."

"Saul's," she says, without blinking. "His da said Look twice and spit once. Saul dropped the spit when he started courting."

I laugh. I can't help it. Saul, with his stick and his tomatoes, spitting.

"The root doesn't see the fruit," I say slowly, "and the fruit forgets the root, and the tree goes on anyway."

Lenna nods along, as if it's a song, and when I stop, she goes on.

"And the seed remembers both."

I stare at her.

"Ada always left that line off," she says. "Said it was too pleased with itself." She shrugs. "It is, a bit. My mam liked it."

My hand has gone to my pocket without asking. Saul's twist is in there, soft as cloth, weighing nothing. I can feel the last few seeds through the paper, small and hard and quiet.

The tide comes in under the platform, and lifts the weed on the roots, and lets it go.

* * *

She takes me up to see the others.

Odile comes too, because Lenna says, "You'd better. You'll want to write them down, and they'll want to watch you do it." Hask stays at the foot of the big tree, explaining the Shining to a new lot of children who have come specially. As we climb I hear him through the trunk. He's added a bit. It shines in the morning.

The graves are on a tree on its own at the edge of the village, reached by a bridge that's one rope to walk on and one to hold. Lenna crosses with her stick tucked under her arm like an umbrella. Odile crosses like someone who's done worse. I do the middle bit with my eyes shut.

On the other side, a platform open to the sky, the breeze smelling of salt and, up here, something cleaner, like rain that hasn't landed yet. Five long boxes of earth, each the length of a person, planted thick with something low and silver-leaved that smells of thyme when the wind crushes it. At the head of each, a board with letters burned into it.

A woman is kneeling by the nearest, pulling weeds. Her hands are wrong: brown and ridged and hard all over, like the bark of the big tree, and they move slowly, the way old roots would move if roots moved. She closes them round a weed one finger at a time, and only then looks up.

"Hollis," says Lenna. "Ada's grandson. The Twelfth."

The woman wipes her bark hands on her apron, which does nothing at all, and holds one out. It's like shaking hands with a gate.

"Hollis," she says. "Daughter of Ruth, of the house of Fenn. Number Six."

"There's no house of Fenn," I say, before I can stop myself. "In Firstfield. There's not."

"There was," says Hollis Fenn, without any fuss, and goes back to her weeds.

I read the boards. One, Two, Three, Four, Seven. And under each number, burned in a careful slanting hand, a whole name: a first name, and whose, and what house.

I read all five out loud. I've delivered bread to every door in Firstfield, and I've never heard a single one of these names.

"How do you know who they were?" says Odile quietly. She's got her slate out, but she hasn't written anything.

"Firstfield's small," says Lenna. "I counted. And then I went and found them. One to Four I found as bones, where they leave you. I knew them by what they had. A ring. A boot. A tin whistle somebody's da had made." She leans on her stick. "Seven, I found alive. He lasted nine days. I'd been looking for him a year."

"Where they leave you," I say.

She tells me. Not long; she's not a woman who tells things long. When the board upstairs has drawn all it wants from you, you go to sleep in the white room, and you wake in the back of a grey skiff with no windows, and then on your back in the grass, far out past the ridges, where the heat-hunters go and nobody else does. No shoes. Nothing. The skiff lifts off, and the grass leans in towards you, whispering warm here, and you've no idea yet what that means.

"Retired," says Lenna. "Honoured. At rest."

The wind crushes the silver plants, and the platform smells of thyme.

I think of the brass plaques, polished like coins, and the four little holes drilled in the wall for mine. I think of the grass in the Spill, leaning in.

"I ran before they'd finished with me," says Lenna. "Out of the white room with a lodger I never asked for and a coat off a hook. After that, whenever the bell rang a Calling, I'd count off the years they usually keep a Reference, and go and wait where they leave you. You can hear the bell from the ridges, if the wind's right."

"How many did you get?" I say.

"Four," says Lenna Brook. "And one I can't name."

* * *

The others come one at a time.

Mae Cotter comes up the trunk without a ladder, wet from the channels, silver scales from her wrists to her elbows flashing like a fish turning. She taught Sedge to dive. She says Mae, daughter of Jen, house of Cotter, Number Ten with water running off her nose, and when I say there's a Jen Cotter who does the mill's accounts with Ma, her face goes very still and then very bright.

"Does she still sing when she adds up?"

"All the time," I say. "Ma says it's why the books never balance."

Mae laughs and has to sit down.

Pim Ashby doesn't come up. When Lenna lifts the blanket over his door he flinches back from the afternoon as if it's shouting, his eyes huge and gold, the black in the middle gone small as a seed.

"Look twice," Pim says to me from the shadows, when Lenna tells him who I am. And then, as if he's checking: "And once in the dark."

"Saul just says look twice."

"Saul's young," says Pim. He's at least fifty.

And Joss Tanner is in a hammock on the platform below, wrapped in two blankets in all this warmth, with a cough that comes up from the bottom of him like a bucket from a well. He's the youngest. Ten years ago he was called. He hasn't changed much outside, Lenna says. They took the most from him.

He looks at me and his face does a slow, astonished thing.

"You're the Hale boy," he says. "You fell in the flour at the bakehouse. Your gran fished you out by the ankles and you came up white all over like a ghost, and sneezed."

I don't remember that at all. I believe it entirely.

"Does my mam still do the eggs?" says Joss.

"Thursdays," I say. "On the bakehouse step. She keeps the brown ones for Gran."

Joss closes his eyes. Then he coughs, a long one, and I cough too, my dry gill-ache, the paper crackle, and we sit there coughing at each other like two old men outside the meeting house, and Sedge, who has turned up from nowhere as usual, says, "Oh, wonderful, there's two of you," and Joss laughs so hard he has to be sat up.

Odile writes their names down. All of them. Slowly, the way Master Aldous writes the date on the board, so you can see each letter go on. Each of them watches her do it.

* * *

Back on the graves platform, with the sun going down orange behind the orchard and the tide nearly in, Lenna asks me.

"When you went. Did Ada say anything?"

And I'm back in the kitchen.

Three in the morning. My coat on, bare feet on the cold stairs because shoes make noise. The dome's night-blue at the window, soft as the inside of a mussel shell. The stove dead, smelling of old ash and cold iron. The mother crock in its dark corner under the stairs, sour and cool, breathing to itself.

And Gran in her chair, in her shawl, awake.

She wasn't surprised to see me. I thought at the time she'd heard me on the stairs. But she was sitting very straight, the way she sits for a Calling, and her hands were already in her shawl, already holding the twist. Already twisted. Saul's seeds, counted out and folded, waiting. She'd have had to go down the east rows for them in daylight, after the Calling, with everyone still cheering.

In case wherever you're going hasn't got proper ones.

The root doesn't see the fruit, and the fruit forgets the root, and the tree goes on anyway.

And then she closed her eyes and said the last thing in a different voice, younger somehow, the way you say something you learned off by heart a long time ago.

"She said, Go and see, then," I tell Lenna. "Somebody in this family ought to."

Lenna Brook doesn't say anything at all.

She sits down. Not on purpose; her knees just go, and she's sitting on the edge of a grave-box with her stick across her lap, and her skin, all down the side touching the board, goes slowly the colour of the burned wood. Hollis stops weeding.

"I said that," says Lenna at last. "On the stair, the morning I went up. Ada was crying, and I didn't want her to see me cry back, so I said, Go and see, then. Somebody ought to. As if it was an adventure." She laughs, but not like a gate. "I was fifteen. I was being brave."

The sea comes in under the trees, glowing a little now where it moves.

"Sixty-two years," says Lenna, "and she's had it in her mouth the whole time, the old crow."

* * *

"There's the one I can't name," says Lenna, when she's stood up and her skin's come back. "You'd better see him before dark. He doesn't come in."

We go out to the edge of the orchard, where the trees thin and the wind comes straight in off the reef. Odile comes. Sedge comes, which nobody asks her to and nobody tells her not to, with Biscuit on her shoulder, because Biscuit has decided Sedge is the one with the plums. Hask wades the channel below us with his seventh stone above the water.

Lenna talks as we go, quick and low. They found him eleven years ago on the mudflats, eating crabs raw. One of theirs, her lodger said, from the way he was made under the changes. But the wild had been at him for years. Things out there swap words like children swap marbles, and he'd been left in the middle of them with nothing.

"He talks," says Sedge. "A bit."

"He talks," says Lenna. "He doesn't answer. Ask him his name, he says up. Ask him whose he is, he looks at you as if you've asked a tree." She stops at the foot of the tallest tree at the edge. "Pim's Eight, so he's Nine, unless I've counted wrong. I've told him every family name I've got. None of them took."

The tree's roots stand out in the water like the legs of something about to walk off. Up at the very top, where the branches are thin enough to sway, there's a dark bundle wedged in a fork.

"He likes it high," says Lenna.

I climb, better than on the first day. Sedge goes up round me and says so. Lenna takes a rope ladder at the speed of a woman who's climbed this tree for eleven years, and Odile comes behind her. Hask stays at the bottom in the rising tide, like a doorstop for the whole tree.

At the top, the wind smells of the open sea, cold and huge, and the crown sways in a slow circle, and I can see the Knuckle and the long new gold of the Mouth, and beyond it the sea going out to the edge of everything.

He's asleep along a branch as thick as my waist, one arm hanging, his cheek on the bark. Long and lean, not much of him that isn't muscle and bone. His skin is mottled green-brown and grey in patches the size of leaves, and as the branches move and the light comes and goes, the patches shift, very slightly, to match. His nails are thick and dark, like Biscuit's claws. He smells of salt and crab and green bark, and of a hundred things I can't put names to, layered up like the smells in an old coat.

There's somebody under there, says the voice. I can almost taste it from here.

"Can you read him?" I say, very quietly.

If you touch him. If she lets you. If he lets you. A pause. It'll cost. More than the reef. Reading's dearer than speaking.

Lenna's on the branch below, hanging off it by one arm the way Sedge hangs off things, watching me with her good eye very narrow.

"My lodger says yours could do it," she says. "It's been saying so since you came. It says yours is a better reader." A dry little smile. "It says it a bit sulkily."

"Can I?"

Lenna looks at the sleeping man a long time. "I've told him every name I've got," she says. "Go on. Ask him. If he says yes, it's yes."

So I lean out along the branch, and the sleeping man opens his eyes.

They're grey. Ordinary grey, like rain on the dome. The only ordinary thing about him.

He lifts his head the way a dog does and breathes in at my wrist, where the green-gold lines are. Once. Twice.

Then he puts out his long hand, slowly, and lays it in mine, palm up, like a child giving you a stone it found. And closes his eyes again.

That's yes, says the voice.

"That's yes," says Lenna.

His hand is cool and hard-skinned, and under the hard skin there's a pulse, slow and steady as the tide.

Name it, says the voice. Name what you want to see, so I can hold it.

"Him," I say. "All the way in."

* * *

It's like going down into the pond.

The light changes first. The sunset goes, and the wind, and the swaying, and the smell of the sea, and I'm sinking through something warm and lit and thick, like honey with a lamp in it. Then there's a wall in front of me, soft and wet and moving all over like the skin on hot milk, and I go through it, the way you go through a soap bubble if you're careful.

And I'm in a city.

It's huge, and lit, and busy. Long thin roads cross it every way, stretched tight as rope, and things walk along them hauling loads bigger than themselves, step, step, like porters on the long stair. Doors in the walls open for one kind of thing and not another, snap, snap, like the bakehouse shutters on a windy morning. Everything is talking: little puffs of taste, more of this here, less of that there, pass it on, like the whistles at Highwater.

One cell, says the voice, calm, the way Ma sounds in a bee cloud. Only one. He's got more of these than there are stars you saw. They're all like this.

There's a roar.

Off to one side, a whole street of long bean-shaped things with folds inside like the gills of a mushroom, and they're roaring. Hot as the bakehouse oven when the door's open at five in the morning and you have to stand back. Everything that walks past them comes away carrying a little of the heat.

They burn the food, says the voice. That's where his warm comes from. Yours too. They were something else once, a very long time ago, a small thing living on its own. Now they live in here and pay the rent in heat.

Inside each roaring thing there's a tiny loop of something, round as a ring.

Their own writing. They brought it with them. It comes down from mothers. Only mothers.

Somewhere in Firstfield, a long time ago, a woman I don't know gave a baby this. It's still here. It's still roaring.

* * *

In the middle of the city there's a wall inside the wall, and through a door in it, the library.

I don't know what else to call it. It's quiet in here after the oven street, the way the meeting house is quiet after the square. And it's full of thread. Miles of it, wound on spools like Ma winds wool on her bobbins, the spools packed into bundles and the bundles packed into shapes, all of it so fine that if I unwound it I could go from Firstfield to the sea and back.

And it's written on. All along the thread, close as the carving on Hask, there are letters.

Read it, says the voice.

"I can't."

You can. There are only four. It sounds like it's smiling. Name them, and you can hold them.

So I look at the four of them for a long time, the way I looked at the Primer the first week of school, with my slate on my knee and Master Aldous going round behind us.

There's a round one, plump and sweet, that makes me think of the first page. A is for Apple. So that's Apple.

There's one that tastes sour and cool, like something alive breathing in the dark under the stairs. Crock.

There's one that's steady and a bit stern and always holds on to the Crock, wherever the Crock is, across the thread. Gran. Gran's always by the crock.

And there's a quick small one that never lets go of Apple. Not ever. Every Apple on one side, a Tilly on the other, holding hands.

Apple, Crock, Gran, Tilly.

I laugh, there in the library in the middle of a man I don't know, and the sound goes all down the thread.

Good, says the voice. Now read.

It's slow at first, like the Primer was. The letters go in threes, and every three is a word. Small busy machines copy a stretch and carry it out into the city, where other machines read it three at a time and build what it says: long chains, clicking together bead by bead. Then the chains fold. All by themselves, over and under, tucking and pleating, the way Gran folds a sheet off the line, corner to corner, shake it, corner to corner again, until it's a small tight square with a shape all its own. And the shape does something. Carries. Opens a door. Holds a wall up.

The thread doesn't build the man. It builds the things that build the man.

I read faster. Apple, Gran, Crock. Tilly, Tilly, Apple. Words, and sentences, and long passages that build the roads and the doors and the oven-things, the skin, the hair, the grey of the eyes.

And then I see the other writing.

* * *

It's all over.

Across the old letters, in among them, crammed into the margins, there's new writing. The same four letters, but in another hand, a hurried hand, a hand from somewhere else. Here a whole sentence has been copied in from something that lives on bark and changes colour, and that's the leaf-shade on his skin. Here's a passage from something with claws. Here, a stretch about drinking the sea and not dying. Here's one that's been copied over and over, a dozen times, like Tilly writing her name all down the edge of her slate.

Layer on layer. Some of it neat. Some of it smudged and crooked, a copy of a copy, the way the reef's word was crooked at the Mouth.

The wild did that, says the voice. Years of it. Things out there swap words. They got into him and left their writing.

I'm angry. I think of him on his back in the grass with no shoes, and every little thing in the Spill writing on him, because nobody was there to stop it.

But the more I read, the stranger it is.

Because the new writing isn't instead. It's over.

Like the big slate on the schoolroom wall, written on and wiped and written on again for a hundred years, so that when the light's low you can still see the ghost of old lessons under today's.

I tilt my head, the way you do at the slate.

And under all of it, line after line, there's the old text. The first hand. Steady and plain and careful, going on and on.

And I know it.

I know it the way you know your own handwriting. The way you know your own lane in the dark. Nearly all of it, Apple-Gran-Crock-Tilly, word after word, page after page, is the same as mine. Not like mine. The same. As if somebody took the book in me and the book in him off the same shelf.

Firstfield, says the voice, very quiet. This is what you taste like. Under all that, he tastes like you.

The new writing changes his skin, his nails, his eyes, what he can drink. It's very loud.

But it's on top. The book underneath is still the book.

* * *

I want to fix it.

There's a passage deep in where the new writing's gone in crooked right across something that matters, something about the way the city in his head talks to itself, Tilly's name scrawled over the middle of the sentence, and I think: that's why he says up. That's why he can't answer.

"Can you rub that bit out?"

The voice is quiet a long moment.

I can only write in you, it says. You know that. And then, gentler than I've ever heard it: And he didn't give me that. He gave me his hand. He said I could look.

The city goes on round me, hauling, roaring, folding. It's been going on for more than forty years without me.

There, says the voice suddenly. Bram. Smell that.

* * *

It's a passage I haven't read yet. First hand, hardly touched; the new writing has gone round it the way water goes round a stone. And when I read it, Apple, Crock, Crock, Gran, the city builds what it says, and what it says is a smell.

Not a smell for anything. A smell for him. The one under the salt and crab and bark, that says to anyone with the nose to read it: this is who I am, and this is whose.

Kin smell like kin, says the voice. It's written. It runs in families.

I read it.

And I'm on the bell tower.

Not really. It's just that the smell is so exactly the smell that the rest comes with it. The top of the bell tower, the summer Jory said bet you won't, when I went up to see if the sky got closer and then couldn't get down. Pigeon dust. Hot slate. The great bell beside me, cold even in summer, smelling of old iron. A rope coming up over the parapet, rough and tarry, and a big hand on it, and a big slow face with a beard like a hedge, puffing, and arms coming round me, and I'm carried down the inside of the tower one rung at a time with my face in a wool coat.

And the coat smells of this.

Tar, from the rope. Pipe smoke, gone stale. And under those, warm and close and a little sour, like green apples in a bowl: the smell of the man himself.

Elder Marrow.

Who reads the Calling letters in his big slow voice. Who climbs the bell tower once every thaw, on the same day, alone, and rings the bell once, just once, and comes down again, and nobody ever asks him why.

Everyone knows why.

* * *

I come out like being pulled up out of the pond by my collar.

It's dark. The sun's gone, the wind's colder, and the sea below the tree is glowing green everywhere it moves. I've been gone a long time.

And I'm cold. Cold from the inside, as if every oven-thing in me has gone out at once. I'm shaking so hard my teeth sound like Saul's stick on the tomato frames. My gills are clamped shut. My wrist is burning green-gold, so bright the branch round me is lit, and Sedge's face, very close, and Odile's, with her slate held to her chest.

And I'm so hungry I could eat the tree.

"He's back," says Sedge. She's got both arms round me; I don't know when that happened. She smells of fright, the struck match, and she's very warm. "Lenna, he's freezing—"

"I know what he is." Lenna's already climbing up with something in her hand. "Eat."

A moonpear. I eat it in four bites, skin and all, and then another, and a lump of sticky brown stuff that tastes of figs and salt, shaking, with Sedge holding me on the branch. It's not nearly enough.

Sorry, says the voice. It sounds hollow, the way I feel. That was dear.

And under the cold and the hunger there's something else. Something that isn't mine.

A wanting to be higher. An ache in the hands for bark. A flinch, deep down, at the colour grey. A loneliness so big and old it doesn't even hurt any more; it's just weather. None of it comes with a picture. I don't know what happened to him out there, or for how long. The thread doesn't keep that.

It's only that I've been inside him, in the chemistry of him as he is right now, tonight, in his blood and his skin. And it rings in me afterwards, like a bell you've stood too close to.

He's awake. He's sitting a little way along the branch with his knees drawn up and his long hands round them, his skin gone dark grey and blue with the night, watching me with his rain-grey eyes as if I'm a bird that's done something unexpected.

I lean towards him and breathe in.

Salt. Crab. Bark. A hundred smells from a hundred things. I know them now; I've read them. I can take them off him one by one, like coats off the hooks in the meeting house porch.

And under them, warm and close and a little sour, like green apples in a bowl.

"Davin," I say.

He doesn't move.

"Your name's Davin," I say. My voice is shaking as much as the rest of me. "Davin Marrow. Son of Tobias Marrow, who's an elder. Of the house of Marrow, of Firstfield." I have to stop and get my breath. "Your da rings the bell for you. Every thaw. Once. He climbs all the way up to do it, and he's got a bad hip, and he does it anyway. Everyone knows. Nobody says."

The man on the branch looks at me for a long time.

Then he breathes in at his own wrist, slowly, the way he did at mine. As if he's smelling it for the first time.

On the branch below, Lenna Brook makes a noise I don't think she meant to make.

* * *

Odile is writing.

She's writing by the light of her own fingernails, which glow blue-green in the dark the way the sea does. I can see her lips move.

"Reference Nine," she says under her breath. "I had you as a number." And something comes off her into the cold wind that I've never smelled on her before. Fierce. Bright. Like a match that's been struck and held, and not shaken out.

"Lenna. His father's first name. Is it right?"

"Tobias Marrow," says Lenna slowly. "Toby. Three years under me at school. He threw a frog at Ada once." She wipes her good eye with the back of her hand, roughly, as if it's done something to annoy her. "She threw it back."

From the bottom of the tree, far below in the glowing water, a voice comes up the trunk and into my jaw.

"Examiner. Is it a discrepancy?"

"Yes, Hask," says Odile. "It's a good one."

"I am glad," says Hask. A long, thoughtful pause. "The boy is shaking. Chilling of the body is enumerated."

"For the bearer, Hask. Not for him."

"I am aware," says Hask. "I wished it noted."

And Biscuit, who has slept through the whole thing in Sedge's collar, chooses this moment to wake up, smell the fig paste on my chin, and lick it off. Thoroughly. Including the inside of my nose.

Sedge laughs so hard she has to hold on to the branch with her feet.

* * *

I can't climb down yet; my legs have gone to string. So we stay in the crown of the tall tree at the edge of everything. Sedge comes back up with blankets and a pot of something hot that tastes of fish and fennel, and I eat the whole pot.

The shaking goes down to a shiver. The shiver goes down to a hum.

Sedge sits next to me under one of the blankets. Not touching. Very nearly. If I breathe in I can smell her, wet wood and plums and the green-stick smell, and I don't, on purpose, because it seems rude. And then I do anyway.

"What was it like?" she says. "Inside."

"Like the bakehouse at five in the morning," I say. I think. "Like reading."

"I can't read," says Sedge.

"I'll teach you. There's only four letters."

"There's more than four, I've seen writing—"

"Not that kind."

She looks at me sideways under the blanket, and for once she doesn't say anything clever. Her mouth goes up at one corner and stays there.

On the branch below, Lenna and Odile are talking low, the way grown-ups talk once the children are meant to be asleep. I catch third drawer, and the Register can't hold him, and Odile saying I've got three days, and Lenna saying Then you'd better not waste any of them sleeping, girl, and Odile, surprised, laughing.

I tie my knot. The twentieth. My fingers still taste of salt, and something under the salt, like green apples.

Bram, says the voice.

"Mm."

Thank you for that. A pause. I haven't read anybody. Not like that. Not for a long time.

I want to ask what it means, a long time. Who it read before. But I'm warm now, and full, and the tree's swaying, and the hum in my jaw is going down like the dome's note at night.

I'm nearly asleep when I hear him.

"Davin," says Davin Marrow, to nobody. Rough. Slow. Trying it the way you try a new tooth with your tongue.

And then, after a long time, as if he's had to go a long way down to fetch it:

"Marrow's."

Lenna goes absolutely still. Odile's slate stops.

Nobody says anything. Nobody needs to.

I open my eyes to see his face.

And past his face, past the branch and the reef and the long gold line of the Mouth, out on the dark sea where there shouldn't be anything at all, there are lights.

Small ones. Low on the water. White, with a blue at the front, like a nose. One. Then another behind it. Then another.

I stop counting at six.

They're coming in on the tide, in a line, as neat as writing.


## Chapter Twelve: Who Wrote You

> PERSON.MAKERLINE: free text. Records the maker and line of the person entered (e.g. "Varro-Tandem, picker line P-9"). No validation is applied; in practice the field is always completed with a company and a line number. Clerks need not query it.
>
> — Register of Moriel, data dictionary, revision 58 (current); note for clerks, unrevised since it was written
>

The lights came in on the last of the flood, two by two, and I counted them the way Survey taught me: first the number, then the spacing, then what the spacing meant.

Six. White running lights, low on the water, a blue at each bow like a nose, a boat's length apart in a line that bent only where the channel bent. Nobody keeps a line that neat through a drowned forest at night without a chart, and the Register said there was nothing here to chart.

Six tenders. Each, if the plate I'd read that morning was honest, rated for two Associates or four hundred kilograms of recovered materials. Twelve, then. Or about two and a half tonnes of us.

Beside me in the crown of the tall tree, a man who had not known his own name at sunset said, "Marrow's," to nobody, and Lenna Brook, on the branch below, made no sound at all.

The tide had been nearly in at sunset. High water within the hour, then, and after it a little over six hours of ebb, the sea draining out of the orchard down a hundred channels and the mud coming up like a back out of a bath.

"Lenna. When does it turn?"

"Ask Teasel. He'll tell you to the minute and be rude about it." She was already going down the rope ladder at a speed no woman of seventy-seven has any business owning. Over her shoulder: "Sedge. Fire."

Sedge dropped off the branch before the word was finished, bough to bough through the leaves, and a whistle went out from her as she fell, long, short, long, short. It came back from three directions and ran on into the village like a crack across ice.

Bram was trying to stand. He'd been inside a man for an hour and come out shaking, and his legs had been filed under string. All six slits on his neck were shut.

"Stay," I said.

"They're here for me."

"They're here for anything they can lift." I turned his face to the water, not gently, so he could see what I'd seen: twelve small points of colour catching the running lights, one on each grey lapel. "Those pins are numbers. That's not a search party. That's an inventory."

* * *

Highwater put itself out.

It did it in whistles. The iron basket on the top platform, the beacon for the fishing punts, went out with a hiss I heard two trees away. The glow-fungus lamps went into sacks one after another, pinch, pinch, pinch, like someone walking down a row of candles. Babies' nets went up into the high branches on pulleys that creaked once and stopped. Three hundred people in the trees above the sea, and not one of them moving.

When my boots hit the low platform it was the darkest place I had ever stood that was full of people. The flood was at its height, lapping the boards. It smelled of doused smoke and fish and the aniseed in the barrel by the rail.

And the sea was alight.

Everywhere the water moved, it burned green. The tide pushing round the roots drew bright collars on them. A fish turned and left a comet. Riding in on the flood like a scatter of pale seed came the stitchers' young, the snow, each speck lit from inside, pooling in the slack corners in drifts of soft cold light.

And down the main channel came six wakes of green fire, as plain as six lines ruled on a page.

Teasel was at the rail, knife in hand, a coil of rope over his shoulder.

"When does it turn?" I said.

"When the water touches the heron's foot on that root," he said, pointing with the knife at a carved bird I could not see. "Then it stands. Then it goes. You'll have the time between a pot boiling and a pot boiling dry."

"Twenty minutes."

"You'd count a sneeze," he said, with contempt, and I took it as agreement.

"When it goes, which channels go first?"

He showed me with the knife. The wide ones, the pretty ones, where the roots went down bare. I'd learned that by daylight; he knew it in the dark by smell.

"Then for twenty minutes we keep them busy," I said, "and after that we make them hurry. Into those. Anything that moves fast out there lights up. Anything that lights up gets bitten." I looked at the green wakes. "And they don't know that yet."

Teasel put two fingers in his mouth and whistled something long and complicated, and somewhere above us a child giggled and was shushed.

* * *

A voice came across the water, amplified, pleasant, a baritone that smelled of cedar even at forty metres.

"Good evening. This is a recovery operation of Varro-Tandem Bioreference, under claim, coastal margin north-west, materials various. The coast is listed uninhabited. Any materials recovered tonight will be logged, handled with care, and held pending disposal. Please remain where you are." A pause. "Nothing personal."

A second voice, not amplified, carried perfectly over the flat water. "I was not consulted on the formation."

"Seven, you are on an open channel."

"I am aware. I wish it noted."

The lead tender swung its nose-light up into the trees. The beam crossed leaves, a rope bridge, an empty doorway, and the wicker wall of a house where an old woman with one clouded eye was leaning in the full glare: her face, her cropped hair, her arm along the wall, all gone the brown-grey of the weave.

"Nothing," reported the hailer, to itself.

Lenna stepped off the wall when the light had gone, her colour seeping back, and winked her good eye at me across the platform.

* * *

From high above, where Pim Ashby lived behind his blanket and hated noon, came a falling hoot, then another. Mae Cotter, dripping beside me, her scaled forearms dull silver in the sea-glow, translated under her breath.

"Pim's got their eyes. He sees in this like it's afternoon. Two boats at the fig channel. One at the school roots. Three coming here." A pause. "Some are getting out. Up the ladders."

The first came over the end of our platform: tall, thin, silver hair combed flat, a lamp at his belt and a net-launcher on its strap, and on his lapel, catching the glow from the sea below, a small enamel 19.

"Oh. Examiner. Good evening," said Pell-19, finding me with the lamp. "I'm so sorry, you're not on my list."

"I'm not on anybody's."

"We have a form for that." He lifted the launcher. Behind him a 22 came over the ladder top, wand in hand, its brass cap fizzing blue.

I took one step back, which put my heels in the water on the boards, and said conversationally, "Associate. Look down."

Pell-22 looked down. The flood lay ankle-deep across the low platform, as it had every high tide since the reef went soft. His good company shoes were in the sea. So, very nearly, was his wand.

"Salt water," I said. "Ask Seven where it conducts to."

He held the fizzing wand very still at arm's length, like a man who has picked up the wrong end of a snake, and while he was holding it I took it off him. You come at the wrist from below and turn it outward, and the hand opens because hands are built to; a Survey cook who had once been something else taught me that. The wand went over the rail and hit the water with a short angry flash of blue and a smell of hot pennies.

Pell-19 fired. I was already moving, low and sideways, the way you move when a kettle-dog breaks cover, and the net opened like a cobweb over the place I'd been and settled with great efficiency on a stack of crab pots. The launcher chimed.

"Crab pots," I said, from behind him. "Ownership not established."

I put my shoulder into his middle and he went backwards over the rail with a long surprised oh and a splash that went up green as a firework. He came up flailing, which was a mistake. Something long and dark rose out of the roots towards the commotion, quick as shears.

"Float!" I shouted down. "Like a man in a bath. Don't move fast."

He stopped. He lay on his back in the glowing water with his arms out and his silver hair spread round his head like weed, perfectly still, and the eel nosed the light at his heels, lost interest, and slid back down among the roots.

"Thank you, Examiner," said Pell-19 faintly, to the sky.

Pell-22 was on his knees with both hands up. I took his launcher too, and his lamp. "Sit on that ladder and don't get up."

"I'll file something."

"I'm sure you will."

* * *

The tide stood. I felt it rather than saw it: the push of water against the posts went slack, and the weed on the roots hung in the green light like hair in a bath, undecided.

"Now," I said, to nobody in particular, and the trees heard me.

It began with the children. All over Highwater, from platforms and branches and doorways, small long-fingered hands let go of moonpears.

They fell out of the dark canopy into the channels like lamps let fall, dozens of them, and every one that hit the water made a burst of green fire round its own small light, and bobbed, and drifted. In ten heartbeats the orchard was full of bright slow-moving things, and every Pell in every tender swung round to look.

"Movement. Multiple," said the hailer. "Recover."

Launchers coughed. Nets bloomed grey over the water and came down on fruit. Chimes sounded up and down the channels, pleasant and two-noted, like a dinner bell nobody had asked for.

"Fruit," said a voice across the water, in disgust. "Moon. Ownership not established."

"Mine says two."

After the moonpears came the salt-plums, which do not glow and have stones in them the size of a thumb-end, and which arrived from a great height onto grey suits with a noise like hail on a tin roof. Somebody's lamp went into the sea. In the dark between the trees there was a good deal of courteous swearing.

"All tenders," said the hailer, the baritone gone thin at the edges. "Proceed to the central structure. At speed."

Motors rose to their wasp whine. Six green wakes went from lines to plumes, and the sea blazed round them as if they were dragging lanterns.

"There," said Teasel softly, and he didn't mean the boats. He meant the weed on the heron root, which had begun to stream the other way.

* * *

A whistle came down from above, high and frightened, one note held. Mae caught my arm.

"A little one's out. On the school bridge."

The school bridge ran from our tree to the next, three metres above the water, one plank wide with a rope each side. In the middle of it, holding a plum in each fist, stood the small girl with the long fingers who had first asked Hask what the Shining was. Below her a tender had cut its motor and drifted to a stop, and the Pell in its bow was raising a launcher.

I was already running.

The launcher coughed before I'd reached the bridgehead. The net caught the child round the shoulders and swept her onto the planks, and the line went taut and began to reel. She didn't scream. She clung to the plank and the plums rolled away into the dark, and the launcher chimed, and the Pell read its screen aloud in the pleasant voice they all have, as if he were telling her the time.

"Recovered materials. One. Juvenile. Unclassified."

The line drew her, plank by plank, towards the edge.

I was on the bridge. It swayed like a hammock. I had a knife and about four seconds.

From the far end, over my head, a voice came up through the trunk of the tree and into the soles of my feet.

"Sedge. What is in the basket?"

The basket hung from a branch above the channel: a crab-well of woven withy the size of a wine barrel, that Highwater lowered into the sea at night to keep its catch alive. Sedge crouched beside it with her knife at its rope. Hask stood on the mud below with the ebbing water at his fifth stone, his top stone reaching up the trunk like an arm, close enough to take the weight.

"Crabs," said Sedge, very fast. "Drop it on him, Hask."

"Crabs are livestock," said Hask. "They are kept. They are fed."

"Hask—"

"Item two thousand and six," said Hask, "protects the bearer from falling livestock." A pause, stone on stone, in which I reached the child and got my knife under the net. "It is silent on the subject of the Proxy dropping livestock on others. I have looked."

Sedge cut the rope.

Hask's top stone took the crab-well, tilted it out over the channel with the delicacy of a man pouring tea for a duchess, and upended it.

About a hundredweight of live crabs came down out of the dark into the tender. They came down clicking. They came down furious, claws up, the size of saucers, and found, as crabs will, every soft place on two Associates who had dressed that morning for an office. The launcher went one way and the Pell the other. He stood up. He stood up fast, which in that orchard, that night, was the worst thing he could have done; the green fire round the tender blazed as it rocked, and something long and dark rose out of the roots at it, and then another.

The line went slack. I cut the last strands and the child came out of the net into my arms, all elbows and plum juice, and put her face in my neck and said, muffled, "I knew he'd do that."

"So did I," I said. I hadn't.

Below us two Associates stood stock-still in a boat full of crabs, with the eels at the propeller and their arms held up out of reach, and one of them said, with the dignity of the truly wronged, "I wish it noted that I was pinched."

On the mud, Hask's top stone came slowly back down to its place.

"I shall need to check," he said, to nobody, "whether that was a harm."

* * *

Things went quickly after that, and I remember them the way you remember a fight: in pieces, very bright.

A bridge between the fig tree and the school roots, a Pell at each end, me in the middle with the child under one arm. Sedge at the far bridgehead: "Down!" I went flat, and she cut both right-hand ropes, and the bridge rolled over like a dog showing its belly and tipped the Pells into the sea, and the child and I went round with it and hung underneath by hands and knees, upside down, like sloths. Three more children were waiting on the far platform, and the water under them was full of Associates, so I hung there by my knees and caught the rope end Sedge threw me, and tied it.

One movement. Three heartbeats. The knot she'd shown me four times that afternoon, that had come out every time like a wet dog.

It sat on the rope as neat as a bird on a twig.

Sedge looked at it, and at me, upside down with a child clinging to my coat.

"Still a wet dog," she said.

"It's holding."

"Wet dogs hold." The corner of her mouth went up and stayed there, and she ran the three children across one after another. When I came up the right way round my hands were shaking. The knot was not.

A Pell in the fig channel, aiming his launcher up at the place Pim's hoots came from; and down a hanging fig-rope, head first and silent as a shadow crossing a wall, came something long and leaf-mottled that took the launcher off his shoulder by the strap and went back up. The Pell looked at his empty shoulder.

"I've been robbed," he told the next boat, "by a tree."

"File it."

High in the fork, the man who had been Reference Nine for twenty-nine years sniffed the launcher and dropped it, with great care, into the sea.

And Biscuit, which I saw only as a light: a small amber glow, throat-high on the water, swimming purposefully along the lead tender while its occupant hailed the dark. A pause. A sound of enthusiastic chewing. Then the glow went away through the roots, faster, with something flat and pale clamped in its jaws.

"My ledger," said the hailer, unamplified, with a crack down the middle of it. "Return that. That is the original."

* * *

I didn't see Bram go into the water. I saw what he did there.

Sedge pointed. Down past the lead tender, something was moving under the water so slowly that the sea barely lit round it: a faint green line, faint as a pencil mark, unfolding towards the boats. It reached the lead tender's hull and stayed there a long time. Then it moved on to the next.

"He's breathing down there," said Sedge, her hand tight on my sleeve. "He's never been under that long."

Three boats. Four. Then the faint line came back towards our tree, and at the foot of the trunk a head broke the surface, and Bram Hale hung on a root with his gills wide and pale in the glowing water and coughed like a man bringing up a lung.

I went down and pulled him out by the back of his shirt. He was cold as a stone and his teeth were going.

"What did you do?"

"Talked to the boats." He held up his hands. The lines at his left wrist were burning green-gold, and his palms smelled of something sharp and wrong that a month ago I'd have had no word for. Fish. Torn. Frightened. Here. "I put it on the bottoms. All of them. The eels can smell it for a mile. It's like hanging a sign on the butcher's back door."

"And when the ebb grounds them—"

"They'll want to sit very still," said Bram, "for about six hours."

I wrapped my coat round him. He weighed nothing. Somewhere above us Sedge was saying something unrepeatable, in a voice like a stove door, about people who stay under longer than they ever have.

* * *

The ebb did the rest. It had always been going to; that was the point of it.

The tenders came hurrying after moonpears and plum-throwers and hoots, down the wide clear channels where every root went bare into the water, and the water went out from under them as if someone had pulled a plug. You could hear it up and down the orchard: a motor going up to a scream as the propeller neared the surface and spat foam, the long dark shapes coming out of the roots at the scream, a thump, a choke, nothing. Then the squelch. Six times, from six directions, like an old man lowering himself into six chairs.

By the second hour of the ebb, six grey tenders sat on six shining mudbanks with their nose-lights bravely on, ringed by pools of eels that had been told, by a smell on the hull, that something here was hurt and ought to be bitten the moment it moved. Twelve Associates sat in them, very still indeed. Two were still wearing a crab.

"Recovery operation suspended pending tide," said the hailer, flat with fatigue. "All copies remain seated." A pause. "Which of you has the spare ledger?"

A silence across six mudbanks.

"I was not issued a spare," said Pell-7, out of the dark, with enormous satisfaction. "I was not consulted on the formation."

* * *

The lamps came out of their sacks one by one, pinch, pinch, pinch, as if somebody were walking back along the row of candles, and Highwater came down out of the high branches to count itself.

Nobody missing. Nobody taken. A boy with a net graze; Pell-22 with a crab on his ear, asking politely whether anyone had a form for that. The girl with the long fingers was asleep in Hask's shoulder ledge where the crab usually rode, plums in her fists, and Hask stood on the mud beneath her, not moving at all, as carefully as a man holding a full jug.

I sat on the wet boards with my back to a post and read, by the light of my own nails, the log of the launcher the crabs had knocked out of a Pell's hands. Mae had fished it off the mud.

RECOVERED MATERIALS: 1 (one) JUVENILE, unclassified. Entered to company stock 10:52 pm.

As far as the launcher and the Register and every office between here and Margin Station were concerned, a little girl had been stock for about a minute and a half, and the only reason was that nothing anywhere said she was anything else.

My tea had gone cold. That has never happened before or since.

Lenna lowered herself down beside me, both knees creaking, and read the screen over my shoulder.

"That's what we are, to them," she said. "Materials, various."

"Not for long."

"You've got three days."

"Two. As of midnight."

"Then tell me what you need, girl," said Lenna, "before I fall asleep on you."

So I told her, and it was like setting down a pack. If the boy stayed Retired, he had no next line: nobody responsible, no protection, and anyone who found him could log him. If I ruled him a protected specimen of the Preserve, the law would hand him back to the white room by the end of the week, and I would sign it. There was no third drawer. I'd looked.

Lenna listened with her chin on her stick, looking out at the six nose-lights burning patiently on their mudbanks.

"My gran used to say a thing on the stair," she said at last, "when the big ones went off to the meeting house for a Calling. Under her breath, like a bit of prayer you half remember." She shut her eyes. "And at the coming of age, each shall choose: to keep, or to go and see. And the choosing shall be theirs."

I sat very still.

"That's all there was of it by her day. My mam said it was out of the old Charter, before they put it under glass." A dry sound. "Under paint, I expect you'd say."

"Article Fourteen."

"Is that what it was?" She opened her eyes. "Sixty-two years I've been saying go and see to children who never heard of it. Fancy it having a number."

I took out my slate. "I need to sit down for a while. Properly. Can somebody keep the children off me?"

"Hask will," said Lenna. "He's been wanting a job that isn't crabs."

* * *

Fallow taught me the Reading in my first winter, in a back office that smelled of wet wool, with his bad knee up on a chair.

"You can't hold the whole Register in your head, Pastern. Nobody can. So build it, out of something you already know, and walk round inside it, and let it hold itself. Sit down first; people who do it standing fall over." He bit his pasty. "And leave by the door you came in. Or you'll leave by a window, and you won't like it."

I half-closed my eyes, let the sound of the ebb go out of me, and built the door. It is always the same one: oak, iron-bound, smelling of beeswax and cold stone, the door of the Inspectorate's old record office at Margin Station, where I sat my examinations at twenty-seven and could not stop shaking. I put my hand on it. It was cold. I went in.

* * *

The Register is a building, enormous, and every part of it exactly as wide as it needs to be.

Every kind of thing it knows is a hall: a long hall of drawers, oak and brass, floor to vaulted ceiling, going away into lamplit distance. One hall for persons, one for places, one for lines, one for claims. Every drawer in a hall is the same shape, because every entry of a kind has the same parts, the way every house in a terrace has its front door in the same place. A drawer is a row. Its key is on the front, a small brass plate with its true name stamped in, and no two keys in a hall are ever the same.

Some drawers hold the key of a drawer in another hall. Then there is a corridor: a narrow lit passage opening beside the drawer and running straight to the one it names. You can walk it. You can feel in the soles of your feet the Register's certainty that there is something at the other end.

I walked to the hall of References.

It was short. A single bay of twelve drawers off the great hall of the Moriel Heritage Preserve, handles polished like coins, and a cold coming off them the way cold comes off a church wall in summer. Seven slots on each. Number. Name. Born. House. Called. Status. Next line.

I opened Twelve. HALE, BRAM. Status: RETIRED. And beside it, for Next line, a corridor.

I walked it. It went a little way, lit and confident. Then it ended in a wall.

New plaster, pale, with the faint damp smell plaster keeps for weeks. I put my palm on it: cold, a little tacky. Eleven days old. On the other side there should have been a custodian, a guardian, a body responsible. The Register had built a corridor to promise there was, and walled it up, and left the promise standing.

I opened Five. BROOK, LENNA. Status: RETIRED. Corridor to a wall, the plaster old and crazed like the glaze on Fallow's teapot. But under Died there was nothing at all.

Not none. Not a date. An empty drawer: clean wood, smelling of nothing, no label. The Register's way of saying I do not know.

I stood there longer than I should have, because it was the only honest drawer in the bay. In sixty-two years nobody had told the Register that Lenna Brook was dead, and it had never pretended. Up the coast was a drawer in the hall of places that said Population: none: a number, nought, a lie with a value in it. This was the other thing. This was a drawer that admitted it was empty.

Six, Eight, Nine, Ten, Eleven: RETIRED, each a corridor into a wall, and each of them, at that moment, in the trees above my head, asleep or coughing or keeping graves. Absent and Unrecorded in the same drawer at once, which the Manual says cannot happen.

* * *

Twelve had a second corridor. It ran from House across the great hall into the hall of Heritage Residents, two hundred and eleven drawers, every soul in Firstfield. At its end, Bram's other drawer. HALE, BRAM. Son of Wenna Hale. Grandson of Ada Hale. Status: HELD (conservation).

From that drawer more corridors went out, short and warm and close: to his mother's drawer, two along; to his sister's; to his grandmother's. A family is a knot of corridors. You can stand in the middle of one and feel it round you like the walls of a small kitchen.

On the front of every resident's drawer, under the key, was a little brass slide with two words stamped on it and a notch for each. HELD. RETIRED.

I put my thumb on Bram's and pushed. It wasn't stiff. It was simply finished: there was no metal past the second notch for it to move into.

So there it was, plain as a floor plan. Leave him RETIRED: materials for anyone with a net. Push the slide back to HELD: a protected specimen, and the corridor to his drawer ran straight back to the white room under the Upper House. Or take him out of this hall altogether and file him as a free person somewhere else, and to do that I would have to empty this drawer, and every corridor that ran to it, from his mother's and his sister's and his gran's, would end in fresh plaster.

I stood with my thumb on a slide that had two notches, colder than I'd been since the ice world in my fourth Survey year, and did not know what time it was.

Somebody decided how many notches it had, I thought. So I went to the plan-room.

* * *

It is at the centre, where the halls meet: a round room under a dome with no drawers in it at all. On a great table under glass lies the plan, every hall, every slot, every kind of corridor the Register permits, drawn in brown ink on paper gone soft with age. The schema, Fallow called it when he was being Inspectorate about it. It holds nothing. It holds what shape things are allowed to be.

Beside it, chained to a lectern by a brass chain worn bright, is the book of revisions. Every change to the plan since the founding, dated and numbered. It never forgets one. It only stops showing you the old plans, unless you ask.

I turned back the pages, heavy and cold, smelling of dust and iron-gall ink. Fifty-eight. Forty-four, the year they added Maker / line to persons. Thirty-five. Thirty-two.

Thirty-one. About a hundred and forty years ago, in a clerk's tidy upright hand, half a page among a great many half-pages:

Revision 31. Chancery consolidation. Column CHOOSING removed from REFERENCE and from HERITAGE RESIDENT. Superseded by conservation provisions. No data migrated.

A small tick in the margin, the kind you put by a thing on a list of things to do before lunch.

Nobody had signed it with a flourish. Nobody had underlined anything. A clerk on a long afternoon had gone down a list of tables, tidying, come to a column that didn't seem to do anything any more, taken it out, ticked it, and gone on to the next.

Show me thirty, I said to the room, the way Fallow taught me.

The glass clouded and cleared, and the plan beneath was a little different. On every drawer in the hall of Heritage Residents, and on every drawer in the hall of References, there was a third brass plate under the key. CHOOSING. And beside it, in fine brown ink, the default every drawer would hold until someone wrote something else.

Open at coming of age.

I walked the hall of References as it had been, and the twelve drawers were there with their twelve CHOOSING plates, polished, waiting. And empty. Not a key on any of them.

The hall had been built with a Choosing on every drawer before anybody was put in it. Then a clerk took the Choosing off. Then, seventy-odd years later, they began to fill the hall.

I went back into the present, down the great hall, into the bay of twelve, and bent to Bram's drawer with my nails held up for light. Under the key, where a plate had once been, were four small holes drilled in the brass. Neat. Evenly spaced. Filled with a little old dust.

Every drawer in the bay had them.

* * *

I couldn't put the plate back. An Examiner can open a case and walk a hall and move a slide as far as it goes; only a ruling under an open case, before a hearing, can change the plan. I had not made one.

But beside the lectern is a tray of black japanned tin, gone grey at the corners, marked PROPOSED. It is nearly always empty.

I wrote on a paper form, because in the Reading paper is the only thing that weighs what it should. Proposed revision: restore column CHOOSING to REFERENCE and HERITAGE RESIDENT, as at Revision 30. Default: open at coming of age. Grounds: Charter of Firstfield, Article XIV, found under paint; Revision 31, no data migrated; open Discrepancy, Reference 12. Examiner O. Pastern. I put it in the tray. It lay there, white on grey, the only thing in it.

Then I went back through the halls to the oak door, because I wanted very much not to leave by a window, and put my hand on it. It was so cold my palm stuck.

* * *

I came out with a headache behind the eyes like a drawer slammed shut.

It was the grey before day, the sea gone pewter, every channel a wet silver ribbon and every root bare to the knee. I had sat down at midnight. I'd have sworn on the Manual it had been twenty minutes.

I was cold from the bones out, as after a long swim. My legs had set in the shape of sitting. Someone had covered my knees with a blanket that smelled of fish and woodsmoke, and on my slate a line of white text was waiting.

Revision proposal 59-P received. Schema revisions require a ruling under an open case. STATUS: PENDING.

And I was hungrier than I had ever been in my life: a hollow under the ribs, as if something had been taken out and not replaced.

In front of me, filling the platform's edge, was Hask. He had climbed out of the channel in the night onto the low platform, which nobody had been sure would take him, and it had. On his far side, at a respectful distance, sat a row of small children, cross-legged, watching me with the total attention of an audience that has been told it may not climb on the stage.

"Examiner. You have been very still."

"I was working."

"I told them that." A grinding pause. "They asked what work was done by sitting. I said, the most difficult kind. One has been sitting very still since three, to see if it is." The top stone turned slightly towards the row. "It is not, she says. She says it is boring."

The girl with the long fingers got up, came round the end of Hask, and gave me a moonpear without a word.

I ate it in four bites, skin and all. Cold water with honey in it, and a smell like rain.

* * *

Halloran called while I was on my third, bacon frying in my ear.

"Pastern." Tock. "Varro-Tandem has filed twelve complaints against the Inspectorate since two this morning. One per Associate. Three are against each other. One alleges you dropped a crab on him."

"That was the Proxy, sir. It was a hundredweight of crabs, and it was within his mandate. He checked."

A silence, with frying in it.

"I've also got a revision proposal in my tray with your seal on it," said Halloran, "which is a thing I have not seen in thirty-one years in this chair. It wants to change the plan, Pastern. Not a drawer. The plan."

"Yes, sir."

"You know what they'll say upstairs."

"That I can't. Not without a ruling and a hearing."

"That's what they'll say first." Tock. "Then they'll say it's a hundred and forty years old and nobody's complained, and then they'll say I've lost control of my Examiners, and then they'll go to lunch." A pause. "Is it sound?"

Out on the mudbanks the tide was coming back round six grey tenders, inch by inch, and twelve Associates sat very still and waited for it.

"It's sound, sir."

"Then bring it to me. At the Preserve. You've got today and one more, and not an hour of the second I'm not counting." Tock. "And Pastern. Whoever wrote that column out. I'd like to know if they had lunch first."

"There was a tick in the margin, sir."

He made a sound I have heard from him twice in eleven years. In a warmer man it might have been a laugh.

"Of course there was," said Halloran, and the line closed like a log settling in a grate.

* * *

I couldn't give Bram a third drawer. But there was something I could do that morning on my own seal, because the Manual has always allowed it and nobody ever asks: I could enter the Unrecorded.

First a place, because a person needs somewhere to be. HIGHWATER. Settlement, coastal margin, north-west. Status: inhabited. The drawer up the coast that said Population: none got a corridor to it and a correction under an open case, class Unrecorded, and I watched the word none go out like a lamp.

Then the people. They made a line without being told, the way people do at a well, and Lenna was at the front of it, leaning on her stick, her skin gone slowly the colour of the post behind her.

"Name," I said.

"Lenna Brook."

I wrote it slowly, so she could see each letter go on. Place: Highwater. Then I read her the last slot.

"Maker, slash, line."
