She looked at me the way she had on my first afternoon, as if I were a document and she had got to the signature.

"Who wrote you," she said. "That's what we ask, here. That's what it means."

"That's what it means."

She thought about it. Out on the mud a tender's motor coughed and caught as the tide lifted it, and went away down the channel very slowly.

"Sal Brook wrote the first of me," she said, "and my lodger's had a go, and the Upper House had a try at the rest and I didn't let them finish." She lifted her chin. "Put I did. Sixty-two years. I'd like the credit."

I wrote I did.

The slate gave its small double chime, and the Register took it without a murmur, because no one had ever told it a person couldn't.

BROOK, LENNA. Place: Highwater. Maker / line: I did. Status: PERSON.

Then I gave her drawer a corridor of its own, out of the hall of persons and across the great hall to the bay of twelve, to a drawer marked 5, with an old wall at the end of its own corridor and nothing at all under Died. Now the corridor ran both ways.

Lenna looked at the slate a long time. Then she patted it once, as you'd pat a dog that had finally stopped biting, and went and sat at the platform's edge with her back to everyone and looked at the sea.

* * *

Teasel came next, because he would not have stood for anyone else coming next. He gave his name as if I might misspell it, and when I read him the last slot he gave me his tired pity and said, "We did," and walked off before I'd finished writing.

Mae, dripping: Jen Cotter, and the sea. Hollis Fenn, down from the graves with her bark hands folded: "Ruth Fenn. Put Ruth." Joss Tanner, carried down in his hammock, coughing: "My mam. And Thursdays." Pim sent his down by whistle from behind his blanket, and Sedge translated, rolling her eyes: "He says look twice."

"That's not a maker."

"He says it is."

I wrote it.

Sedge waited till nearly the end, which I wouldn't have guessed, and sat cross-legged in front of me with her freckles already dark from the sun, and gave her name in the voice of someone who had never had to give it to anyone who didn't already know it.

"Who wrote me." She scowled at it like a sum. "A picker line. A gran who got out of a crate. A mam who taught me knots. Mae taught me diving. Him," with her chin at Bram, asleep against Hask's bottom stone with Biscuit on his chest, "says there's only four letters, apparently." She considered. "Put us."

I put us.

Davin Marrow came down from his tree about the time the sun cleared the sea, by himself, slowly, which I gathered was new, and stood in front of me with his mottled skin going pale gold in the light. He didn't answer when I asked his name. He breathed in at his own wrist. Then he said, "Davin," and after a while, "Marrow's," and I wrote both, and the corridor from his new drawer went across the great hall to a drawer marked 9, and for the first time in twenty-nine years there was a name at both ends.

Three hundred and three. It took until the sun was two hands up. My hand cramped and Sedge rubbed it without asking. Somewhere about the hundred and fortieth, the girl with the long fingers stood in front of me and said her name was Wren, and that she was five, and that Hask had written her. I explained that he hadn't. She explained that he had. Hask, behind me, said nothing at all with great care, and I wrote Hask (she says).

RECOVERED MATERIALS: 1 (one) JUVENILE, unclassified, said the company launcher, still lying on the boards beside me.

WREN. Place: Highwater. Status: PERSON, said the Register.

They could not both be true. Only one of them had a key.

* * *

Bram woke near the end and watched the last of them from Hask's bottom stone, and when the line was done he came and sat down an arm's length and a half away, as he had the first afternoon.

"Can I have one?"

"Not yet," I said. "You've got a house."

I watched him think about the hall I'd walked, though he'd never seen it: a drawer with his mother's corridor running to it, and his gran's, and Tilly's.

"I don't want to not have a house."

"I know. That's the trouble." I showed him the slate. Revision proposal 59-P. STATUS: PENDING. "It's in a tray. I have to take it somewhere and argue."

"Is it a good tray?"

"It's the best tray," I said. "Nobody ever uses it."

* * *

We left on the morning ebb.

Highwater doesn't go in for ceremony; it goes in for whistles. Teasel brought the heron punt round under the low platform and stood at the stern with the pole, scowling at the carved bird on the bow as if it were late. Sedge climbed into the bow with a bundle and her knife and a look that dared anyone to say anything about it. Nobody did. Hask waited in the channel with the water at his sixth stone and the crab back on his shoulder ledge, as if it had never been sacked.

The tenders had gone on the dawn flood. On the mudbank where the lead one had sat all night lay a square of white weighed down with a salt-plum stone: my own Form 9, the dart I'd thrown at a stranded tender the morning before. Mae swam out and fetched it. It had been retrieved, dried, and annotated in a very neat hand. Assistance noted. Not required. The eels were adequately explained. P-7. And beneath, in a second neat hand: Ledger not recovered. Grievance lodged against the otter.

Lenna didn't come down to the punt. She stood at the rail letting the post have her colour, with Davin on a branch above her, and Hollis, and Joss in his hammock, and a blanket over Pim's doorway with a gap the width of one gold eye.

"Tell Ada," she said, and stopped, and decided against whatever it had been. "Tell her I'm still saying it."

"Saying what?" said Bram.

"She'll know."

He was the last onto the ladder. He stopped on the top rung with one plated foot on it and Biscuit's head poking out of his shirt, squinting at the coming sun, and he looked past the platform and the trees, south-east, to where the canopy thinned and the light came through gold. The lines at his wrist were lit, very faintly, the way they'd been the first time I saw him, as if something under there were awake and paying attention.

"It talked to me," he said. "Last night. After the boats, when I was cold." He didn't look at me. He said it the way you set down something you've carried in your pocket too long, because it has started to get warm. "I didn't ask it anything. It just said."

"What did it say?"

He watched the gold through the trees for a long moment.

"There are others like me," he said. "They've been asleep a very long time."

Below us the tide drew at the roots, going out. Somewhere up in the leaves a whistle passed from tree to tree and faded.

He lifted the hand with the light in it, and pointed: south-east, across the Pale, the way we were going anyway.

"And one of them's that way."

Then he went down the ladder into the punt, and I went after him, and Teasel pushed off, and nobody said anything after that for a long time.


## Chapter Thirteen: Hold Last Heading

> UNITS KEEP STATION ON THE UNIT AHEAD. IN EVENT OF SIGNAL LOSS, UNITS HOLD LAST HEADING AT TRANSIT SPEED UNTIL SIGNAL IS RESTORED. DO NOT STAND IN FRONT OF THE LEAD UNIT.
>
> — enamel operator's plate, Varro-Tandem GLEANER harvester, Pale sector 9; scratched underneath, very small: where else would you stand
>

The punt's bottom hisses on silt and stops, and Teasel leans on his pole and says, "That's as far as wet goes."

I climb out. The mud's warm. My soles grip it. Up ahead, Hask walks out of the last of the channel with water pouring off his stones, and the crab that's ridden his shoulder ledge all morning looks at the dry country in front of him, thinks better of it, and climbs down and sidles back towards the sea without a word. Biscuit, on my head, watches it go with deep suspicion.

Teasel looks at me a long moment. Then he takes a moonpear out of his shirt and holds it out. It glows a little in the shade of his body.

"Mind the dry," he says. "It'll have your chest. Breathe through your nose." Then, to Sedge, much rougher: "And you come back."

"I always come back."

"You come back this time," says Teasel, and pushes off before she can answer, and doesn't look round once, all the way back into the trees.

Sedge watches him go with her arms folded. She smells of a wire pulled tight.

"He doesn't give people pears," she says, as if it's my fault.

* * *

We go up over the last salt-grass and a belt of grey thorn that rattles like dry beans in a tin, and over the low hills behind it. And at the top of the last one, the world goes gold.

Everyone stops. Even Hask.

It goes from my feet to the end of everything. Grain, waist-high on me, all one height, all one colour, all leaning the same way under the same wind, so the whole plain moves at once like the sea, only dry. The wind runs across it in long waves and I can watch each one travel. One starts at my feet and rolls away, and I count, and I'm at forty before it's too small to see.

And the sound. At home, on Thursdays, the dome does the sprinklers, and for an hour the whole village hisses like a pan of milk just before it boils. This is that, except it doesn't stop at the end of the hour or the edge of the village. A million million stalks rubbing, hssssssss, all the way to where the sky comes down.

The air tastes of chaff, dry and papery and a bit sweet, like the inside of a flour sack. My chest feels it at once, a tight tickle under the ribs. I breathe through my nose like Teasel said. Biscuit takes one look at the sun coming up white over all that gold and pours itself down the neck of my shirt.

"Who planted it?" says Sedge.

"A company," says Odile.

"All of it?"

"All of it."

At Highwater nothing is the same as the thing next to it. I watch Sedge try to fit the Pale into her head, and fail.

"It's all one," she says at last, the way you'd say it's all dead.

She steps forward and lays her palm flat on the tops of the grain, the way I touched the warm grass my first night out of the dome. Then she snatches it back. There's a thin red line across her hand.

"It cut me."

"Edges like paper," says Odile, already wrapping her own hands in strips of sailcloth. "Keep your sleeves down."

Hask comes up and stands with the grain hissing against his carving.

"Examiner," he says. "This field is very large."

"It is, Hask."

"It is not enumerated," he says, sounding almost pleased, and walks on into it.

* * *

In all that flat, the things that stick up, you notice. The spines first: great curved pipes, grey and streaked with rust, coming up out of the grain on legs and arching over and going down again, one after another, in rows to the edge of the world, with little brass noses hanging off them, dripping. Under each spine there's a ditch you can't see, because the grain grows right to the lip. You only know by a line of greener stalks and the smell of wet. From far off they look like the ribs of something enormous that died lying down, and the grain grew up over it.

Then the stations, grey and square, each with a mast and a blue light blinking slow as a sleepy eye.

And the patches. You smell them first: sweetness gone over, like the bakehouse bins on the third day of summer. Then the gold in front of you turns the colour of an old nail. The stalks in the rust stand up too straight, because the heads are light. I pull one and roll it in my fingers. Husks. Not a grain in it.

I stand in the rust with the empty head in my palm and hear the soft pleased voice of the man in the colour-changing coat, through the Upper House door. Three of the grain lines are already off-reference.

"Bram," says Odile, from ahead. She doesn't say come on. She says my name and waits.

She's got two days. She told Lenna so at dawn, and Lenna said then go, and hugged her, quick and hard, which surprised them both.

I drop the husk.

* * *

By mid-morning the footfall stops being far away.

I've heard it since the Undercroft, a door shutting in a big house, fainter all down the coast until the sea drowned it. Out here, every step is bigger. Fifty-one. Thump. Fifty-one. THUMP. I feel it in my knees.

Hask has stopped twice, his whole stack turned east like a dog's nose.

"It has walked within my hearing for many days," he says. "Stone hears stone."

"It's not stone."

"No. But it is heavy, and patient, and it does not hurry. I believe it has been listening to me also."

We come over a long low rise between two spines, and there it is.

* * *

I've never seen anything so big that moved.

You could put the meeting house inside it, and the bakehouse after. It's a machine; it's got bolts as big as my fist, and panels, and pipes. But it's standing in the grain on six legs like a beetle the size of a hill, and it's grazing.

Low at the front there's a mouth wider than our lane, with teeth like a comb. The teeth go through the grain, slow, and the grain goes in, and somewhere inside there's a deep grinding chew, grrrrrumm, and out of the back comes a plume of chaff that drifts away gold like smoke off a bonfire. Behind it a straight path of stubble runs off to the horizon.

It's been wild a long time. Moss all down its legs, black-green, thick as fleece. A whole tree growing out of what must have been the bin on top, crooked, grey-leaved, full of small brown birds coming and going, and pale flowers trailing down its flanks, and white streaks where the birds have been for years. On its side, under the moss, letters taller than me: a G, an L, and the rest gone to rust. It smells of hot oil and old straw and bird and the sun on metal, and it ticks deep inside like the dome's ribs cooling at night.

It lifts a leg. So slow. Moss dripping. Puts it down.

THUMP.

Biscuit, down my shirt, gives a squeak of pure joy and comes out.

"Biscuit, no—"

But Biscuit's smelled it. Oil. Biscuit smells of lamp oil and river, and has been looking all its life for something that smells this much like Biscuit. It flows down my leg and away through the grain, and I go after it, and Sedge after me, and then all of us are standing in the stubble in the great cool shadow, with the chew going grrrrrumm overhead, and Biscuit is halfway up the front leg, rubbing its whole face on a dripping joint with its eyes shut.

Sedge has stopped dead. "Is it an animal?"

"It's a machine."

"The grey boats were machines," says Sedge slowly. "They didn't have a tree."

The chewing stops.

Its head is a broad flat front over the mouth with a row of round glass eyes along it like a crown, most of them cracked, two still lit a dim yellow. They swing round, the way an old dog turns when you come into the kitchen. And they settle on Hask.

Hask has walked right up to it. His bottom stone is level with the first joint of its leg. A pile of carved stones in the shadow of a hill on legs, and they look at each other, and neither says anything for a long time. I put my palm on the ground. Its hum is in the dirt, deep and uneven, like an old man breathing in his sleep. And Hask's hum under it. Two slow sounds in the earth, side by side.

"Hask," says Odile carefully. "Is it of the larger sort?"

"It has not been measured."

"I think we can assume—"

"Appendix C does not permit assumption." His top stone grinds and slides out from his stack like an arm, cut to the notary's ell. "First it is necessary to establish whether it is a beast."

"It's a machine, Hask."

"It grazes. It wanders. It has young living on it." The birds. "It has not been told what to do for a very long time, and it continues anyway. Appendix C does not require that a beast be alive. It requires that it be measured."

He lays the ell against its leg and walks it up. One. Two. He reaches the top of his reach and stops. And the machine, which has stood there patiently the whole time, lowers itself. Joint by joint, with a long groan of old metal and a shower of moss and two indignant birds, it kneels its huge front down into the stubble the way a cow kneels, until its cracked crown of eyes is level with Hask's top stone.

Sedge's fingers dig into my arm.

Hask measures it nose to tail, all the way down its flank in the shade, counting through the ground, and the machine turns its two lit eyes to follow him the whole way. Biscuit falls asleep in the moss.

At last Hask comes back round to the front.

"Forty-one ells, and part of a forty-second. It is of the larger sort."

"Thank you, Hask," says Odile, very gravely.

"I am not sure what I shall do with it. It does not appear to be preying."

The machine breathes out, a long sigh of hot air smelling of oil and old grain, and a faint pip comes from inside it, like a question.

"I believe," says Hask, "it was also wondering what I was."

* * *

An hour later we find its cousins.

I hear them first: under the hiss of the grain, a ticking in the ground, quick and light, like rain on the bakehouse roof. And under that, every few seconds, a flat dull click I feel in my teeth, coming from a station mast.

Click. And every harvester in the field turns.

There are forty of them, or near enough, each the size of a hay cart, grey and six-legged and clean, with a comb-mouth and one wide glass eye at the front like a lamp, and VT stamped on their sides. They go across the field nose to tail, and when the click comes they turn, but not all at once like grazers. Like a rope being pulled: the front one turns, and the one behind it turns at the same place, and the one behind that, all down the line.

The lead one hits a rock and stops dead, its eye flashing orange. And the whole line bunches up behind it, nose into tail, clack clack clack, like ducklings when the mother stops in the lane. When the lead backs up and goes round the rock, they all go round it, in exactly the same place, though none of them can see it.

Sedge laughs. "They're stupid."

"They're polite," says Hask.

One harvester has dropped out at the near edge, standing still with its mouth jammed full of stalk and wire, ticking unhappily, its eye a steady orange. And Biscuit, who has a taste for oil now and isn't going back, is already up its leg. It drapes itself in the warm shade under the eye's hood, throat glowing amber, across the top of the glass. It covers the left half of the eye completely.

The harvester stops ticking. Then, very slowly, it turns. Right, away from the side that's gone dark. All the way round in a circle, chewing on its wire, with Biscuit riding its face like a hat, and back to where it started, and on round again, looking for the half of the world that's gone missing.

Sedge sits down in the stubble because she can't stand up for laughing. Hask makes a short grinding rumble in the ground that I'm almost sure is a laugh.

I fetch Biscuit off. The harvester stops turning the moment its eye is clear and stands there ticking, as if it would prefer not to discuss it.

We go wide, along a strip beside a spine. Sedge is in front, still giggling, and she puts her foot down on what looks like more grain and isn't.

She drops. I grab her collar. Odile grabs my belt. Sedge hangs with her legs kicking over nothing, and below her, through the stalks, I see the ditch: a straight cut as deep as Saul's barn is tall, lined with old grey stone, brown water at the bottom, thick and still. We haul her out. She's white under her freckles, her smell a struck match.

"Watch for the green stalks," says Odile, breathing hard. "The green ones mean water."

Sedge looks at the line of bright stalks running off under the spine, straight as a ruler, all the way to the sky.

"I hate this place," she says. And then, because she's Sedge: "Did you see my legs go, though?"

* * *

In the afternoon we come to a field that's dying.

Not a patch. All of it, spine to spine and past both, the colour of an old nail, standing straight up and empty in the heat. The smell comes off it like heat off the oven, sweet and rotten and sour. Flies. Nothing else.

I don't decide to go in. I just do. I kneel in the middle and put my hand on the ground and listen.

A field of grain talks. Not much. Water. Sun. Here's where the root's going. All one voice. Out in the gold this morning it was like the whole village singing a hymn: one tune, a million mouths.

Here, there's a gap.

The hymn's still going, thin and dry, but a voice is missing out of the middle. Not a clever one. A low one. The note that holds the others up, the way Elder Marrow's voice holds up the midwinter hymns so the rest of us can wander about on top of it. You don't hear that note till it's gone, and then you can't hear anything but.

Look, says the voice behind my eyes. You know how now. It's only grain. It won't cost much.

I take an empty head between my fingers and name it, seed, and go in, just to the door, the way you look through the bakehouse window without going in to buy.

It's the same city I saw in Davin. Roads, doors, the roaring oven-streets, the four letters on the thread: Apple, Crock, Gran, Tilly. But it's wrong in a way I don't understand at first. In Davin the old book was clean and the new writing was scrawled on top. Here it's the other way round. The top pages are fine: be grain, be gold, grow this tall. It's the bottom of the book that's going.

The first pages, the ones that tell the little busy machines how to read three letters at a time, are worn, the way the meeting house steps are worn in the middle where eight generations have walked. Copy of a copy of a copy. The little machines stumble on them. They build a chain and it folds wrong, and the shape doesn't do anything, and the grain stands in the sun with an empty head and doesn't know why.

It's not the grain that's drifted. It's the floor it's standing on.

Every line on this world is built on that floor, says the voice quietly. Grain, grazers, bees, reef, trees. The same first pages under all of them. Everything that copies itself wears.

"Mine aren't worn." I've read Davin's, and Davin's were mine.

No. Yours are the cleanest copy I've ever tasted. That's what I meant, at the pond. That's what a beginning is. A pause. That's why the men upstairs want you.

I come out with a cold sweat down my back in all that heat. Odile's crouched at the edge of the rust with her slate on her knee. She doesn't ask. She waits, the way she waited at Highwater with her tea until I came down.

"It's not the top," I say. My voice is rough with dust. "It's the bottom. The bit underneath everything. It's wearing out. And I've got a clean one, and that's all I am to them. A clean bottom page."

Odile looks at me for a long time. Then she writes it down, slowly, so I can watch the letters go on. And to my surprise, it helps.

* * *

Late afternoon, in the long shade of a spine, the drone comes.

A thin whine high up, like a gnat in a bedroom. Odile's head comes up and she says "Down," very quietly, and we're flat in the grain with the stalks hissing over us, and Hask simply being a pile of stones, which he's good at.

It comes over low: grey, flat, the size of a tea tray, blue light at the front, VT on its belly. It swings round in a long lazy curve, and comes back.

"It'll log whatever it finds," breathes Odile, "and upstairs will decide what it was. Stay still."

The drone swings away east. Towards the big machine.

The old harvester has followed us, I realise, all afternoon, fifty-one counts at a time. It's standing in the grain a few hundred paces off with its tree on its back, chewing. The drone goes straight at its head and hangs there, ten feet from its cracked eyes, blue light blinking, looking.

The old machine stops chewing.

It lifts its head up and up on those mossy legs, higher than I've seen it, until its eyes are above the drone, and opens something on its front I hadn't seen, a round black mouth like a trumpet's, and bellows.

It's a horn, low and cracked and enormous, the sound you'd get if the meeting house could moo. My gills slam shut. Biscuit goes rigid in my shirt. The drone flips in the air like a leaf and tumbles away, its blue light spinning.

And across the field, forty grey harvesters stop dead.

One heartbeat. Two. The ticking in the ground has stopped. The station goes click, and nobody turns.

The old horn sounds again. And every harvester turns to it at once, the way the grazers turned when one of them piped. Same tongue. They're cousins. The old one is saying the oldest word they were all made knowing, and the word means run.

They run.

* * *

Not away from the horn. Not anywhere sensible. Out of their field in a long grey line, nose to tail, faster than I knew they could go, six legs each pumping like the mill's pistons, the ground a drum under them, chaff coming off them in a gold storm. The comb-mouths are open and the teeth are going, a row of flashing blades at the height of my chest.

Straight at us.

Not at us. They don't know we're here. Whichever way the lead one was pointing when the horn blew, that's the way, and the lead one was pointing down the strip between the spines, and we're in the strip. Ditches either side under the grain. Open Pale ahead, flat as a table, and forty sets of blades coming down it faster than we can run.

Hask turns.

"Examiner." His voice comes up through the drumming ground, perfectly calm. "Crushing by conveyance, wheeled, tracked or legged. Item two thousand four hundred and fifteen. Get under me."

"There are four of us, Hask—"

"Then I shall be inadequate," says Hask, "for the first time." And he begins, slowly, to lower himself into a roof.

He'll hold Odile. He might hold Sedge. Forty of them will go over him, legs and blades, and I don't know if even Hask holds after forty.

I look at the lead harvester. It's ten lengths in front, its wide eye glaring orange, and every single one of the others is following it. Not the horn. Not the mast. The one in front. Round a rock because the one in front went round a rock.

And this morning, at the edge of a field, one went round and round in a circle because an otter was lying on half its face.

"Sedge. Get under Hask."

"What are you—"

"Bet you won't," I say, which isn't fair, and I'm already running.

* * *

Not away. Up.

The nearest spine comes out of the grain on its rusty legs right beside me, and I go up the leg the way Sedge taught me to go up a tree, mostly by laughing at me. My plated soles grip the rust. My hands find the bolts. Then I'm on top of the pipe, round and warm and ringing under my feet, and I run along it.

It arches down ahead, a great grey rib, and the harvesters come up underneath on my left, the lead one's back hammering along below the spine, gaining, the noise like the whole of Firstfield falling downstairs. My chest's on fire. The dust's in it, the dry ache, a cough coming up like a bucket from a well. Breathe properly, says Ma.

Bram, says the voice, very fast. Your legs. Shall I—

"No time to name it!"

Then run faster.

The lead one's under me. Two lengths. One.

I think of Jory's face, at the bottom of the bell tower.

I jump.

* * *

I land on its back on my knees and both hands, and it's like landing on the mill wheel when it's running. Everything's going up and down at once. The metal's hot. My soles grab, slide, grab. I nearly go off the side into the blades of the one behind, and I get a fist on the edge of the eye-hood and hang on.

The eye's right there below me, orange, wide as a cartwheel. It can see the strip ahead, and Hask lowering himself into the grain like a man kneeling in church, and Odile and Sedge going under him.

I get my shirt off over my head with one hand, the too-big sailcloth with the red patch on the elbow, and lean out over the hood, hanging on by my knees and my soles and one fist, and throw it over the left half of the eye.

Half the world goes dark for it.

It turns.

Right, away from the dark. Not much. A lean, like a horse when you pull one rein. The strip goes past at a slant now, the ground tilting, and behind me I hear the second harvester reach the place where the lead one turned, and turn there too. Clack. And the third. Clack. Like a rope being pulled.

On our right, the grain goes bright green. A long line of it, straight as a ruler.

"Sorry," I tell the harvester. I don't know why. It's very hot under my hands and it's only doing what it was made knowing. "Sorry. It's not deep. Well, it is. Sorry."

The green line rushes at us. I let go.

I hit the grain on my shoulder and roll, and the stalks cut my bare back like a hundred paper edges, and I keep rolling because I can't stop, and fetch up on my face at the very lip of the ditch with my nose over nothing.

And the lead harvester goes in.

It doesn't slow down. It holds its heading right off the edge of the world, all six legs still going, and drops nose-first into the ditch with a noise like the oven door slamming times a hundred, and a great brown sheet of water comes up out of the cut and comes down on me.

And the second goes in on top of it. And the third. Each one reaching the place where the one in front went, and going there too, nose to tail, faithful, polite, all the way down. They pile up in the ditch like dropped loaves, legs going, mouths churning mud, grey bodies sliding over each other, and the clang and splash and grind of comb-teeth on old stone goes on and on.

Then it stops.

Forty harvesters, in a ditch the depth of Saul's barn, in a long grey heap, legs waving slowly in the air and every eye flashing orange at once, like a field of the worst fireflies in the world. On top of the heap, the lead one's eye is still half covered by a sailcloth shirt with a red patch on the elbow.

Somewhere out in the Pale, the station mast goes click, hopefully.

Nobody turns.

* * *

I lie on the lip of the ditch, wet and coughing and bleeding from about a hundred small places, and I laugh. I can't stop. I laugh until the cough takes over and cough until the laugh comes back.

Sedge drops on her knees beside me, covered in chaff, smelling of a struck match and a green stick cracking at the same time, which I didn't know a person could do.

"You idiot," she says. "You absolute — you threw your shirt at it—"

"Bet you wouldn't."

She hits me on the arm, hard. Then she looks at the ditch full of harvesters waving their legs, and her mouth goes, and she's lying in the stubble with her hands over her face, howling.

Hask grinds up and regards the heap for a long time.

"Forty harvesters," he says. "I measured none of them. They are no longer moving with intent."

Odile comes last, and stands over me with her hands on her hips, her collar torn and chaff stuck to the musk on her jaw. She smells like a lamp turned all the way up: fierce, bright. And under it, the embarrassed thing, like Ma when you catch her singing, only bigger.

"Is there a form for that?" I say, from the ground.

"No," says Odile.

She takes out her flask and unscrews it and holds it out to me. I've never seen her offer it to anyone.

"Item fourteen," says Hask at once.

"He's earned item fourteen. Drink it slowly."

It's like drinking a bonfire. Tar and smoke and burnt raisins, all the way down to my feet. I cough so hard Sedge has to thump me, and Odile takes it back and drinks, and screws the cap on, and says nothing at all.

Biscuit puts its head out of Odile's coat, smells the oil in the ditch, and makes a small noise of longing.

"No," say all three of us at once.

Out across the Pale, the old machine lowers its head and goes back to grazing, as if it had never said anything at all.

* * *

We camp in the shade of the spine, because nobody can walk any more. Odile climbs down the heap of grey backs and fetches my shirt off the harvester's eye, wet and chaffy and smelling of oil, and Biscuit sleeps in it.

The sun goes down red through the dust, and the spines stand up black against it, rib after rib. I can taste the dew coming. Before she slept, Sedge tipped a little of her water skin into the crack between Hask's seventh and eighth stones, where his leaves have gone limp and grey at the edges in the dry, the way you'd water a plant on a windowsill. Hask said nothing. Hask let her.

I tie my knot. The twenty-first. I've eaten Teasel's moonpear and three of Odile's crackers and a fistful of raw grain from a healthy field, and I'm still hollow.

You're thinking very loudly, says the voice.

"You said there were others like you. Asleep. And one of them's here. That's why we came this way."

I said.

"What are you? Not hungry, mostly. Not small, curious. The real answer."

It's quiet so long that Odile's slate stops scratching. Then, behind my eyes, it does a thing I've never felt it do. It goes careful, the way Gran goes careful before she says something she's decided to say.

When your village was new, it says, the ones who brought everything here were afraid of exactly this. The grain. The rust. They knew it would drift. So they made a place to keep the first copy of everything they brought. The grain the way it was. Your crock the way it was. The bees and the clover, the trees, the reef-builders, the grazers. And the floor under all of them. Clean.

They made seven of us to keep it. Seven ponds, all across this world. We hold it, and we read it out to anyone who comes to read with us, so that whenever a line wears soft, someone can come to the pond and set it right.

We were the Library. I'm a Librarian.

Out in the dark, something enormous takes a step. Fifty-one.

"What happened?"

Nothing. That's what happened. It sounds tired. The ones who made us came to read, at first. Then their children came, less. Then their children's children, now and then, to look. Then nobody. You can't read on your own; we were made for two. So I slept, and the pond grew reeds, and things came and drank and fell in, and I tasted every one of them, and they were all noise. Copies of copies. Two hundred years, near enough.

And then a boy fell in at dawn with his leg open and his blood in my water, and he tasted like my shelves.

My throat's shut, like my gills.

I should tell you that I was hungry, it says. So hungry, Bram. I'd have taken anything that tasted right. That's true.

"And the rest?"

I asked if you wanted fixing, and you didn't say yes. You said, would I still be me. A pause. Nobody ever asked me that. The ones who came long ago asked what I could do. You asked what you'd be. Then I asked who that was, and you couldn't tell me, and you said okay anyway.

So it's both. I was hungry, and I liked you. I don't know how to make it only one.

I lie on my back in the stubble with the stars coming out over the Pale like dust somebody's breathing on.

"Were you lonely?"

It doesn't answer that. Which is an answer.

"Show me," I say.

It'll cost. More than Davin. And you'd have to name me.

I think of the meeting house's one shelf of books, and the Charter in the cedar chest, going on for ever in the lamplight.

"You're a library," I say. "Show me the library. All the way in."

* * *

It isn't like going into Davin. That was sinking, honey with a lamp in it, and then a city, roaring.

This is going down a stair.

Cool. Dim. Quiet as the meeting house at midwinter with the lamps out, when you can hear the snow coming in through the top vents and landing on the boards. It smells of the pond: stone and faint mint and the green-gold sharpness of my own wrist. Down and down. Then it opens out.

A hall.

It goes off further than I can see, the way the Pale does, and it's full of shelves. Not wood. Living ones, pale and folded like the gills under a mushroom cap, row after row, curving up into the dark. On every shelf the thread, wound on spools and packed in bundles, so much of it the hall smells of it the way the mill smells of flour.

Nothing's being built. Nothing's hauling. It's being kept.

Walk, says the voice. It's all round me here, in the walls. Read the shelf-marks.

The first shelves taste gold, like the field this morning with the hymn going all round me. I take down a spool. It's grain. The Pale's grain, but not the Pale's grain: whole, every page sharp, the top of the book saying be grain, be gold, and the first pages underneath crisp as the day they were written, every three letters clear.

I hold it up beside the dying field I can still taste on my fingers, and I can see, line by line, exactly where the field has gone soft. Here. And here. Like holding the Primer next to Tilly's slate when she's copied it wrong. You don't have to be clever. You just look from one to the other.

Look twice, says Saul, somewhere.

I go on. Honey and warm wax: bees, and the clover they were made for, wound side by side the way they were meant to go. Chalk and salt: the stitchers. Hay and wet wool: grazers.

And one that's sour, and cool, and breathing.

I know it the way you know the smell of your own house in the dark. A cellar that's happy. The bakehouse at five in the morning with the cloth off the crock. Gran's mother crock under the stairs.

The first one. The one every crock on Moriel was copied from, that the grazers' fermenters came down from before they went slow in the Spill. Kept clean on a pale shelf under a pond for two hundred years by something that has never tasted bread.

I put my hand on it. It's warm.

And there, says the voice. In the middle.

The shelves go round in rings, like the rings in a cut tree, and at the centre, where you'd put the lamp in a round room, there's something that isn't on a shelf at all.

The floor.

The first pages. The machinery that reads all the rest. Grain and bees and reef and crock and grazer, all built on it, the way every house in Firstfield is built on the same hill. Here it is on its own, untouched. Not worn in the middle like the meeting house steps. Whole. Humming very faintly, like the dome at night.

I don't have to read it. It's mine. It's Davin's under the wild. It's what they took out of Lenna and Joss and Hollis Fenn and One to Four and Seven, a little at a time, until there was no use left in them.

And it was here. All along.

I stand in the middle of the hall with my hand still smelling of the crock, and I understand something so simple I nearly sit down on the floor.

There's no place in here for anybody to lie.

There's a place for a reader. I can feel it, a warm worn hollow near the floor like the dip in Gran's chair, the right shape for someone to sit and look from one book to another. Made for two. But no bed. No white room. No board. Nothing in here takes from anybody. Whoever built this built it so that nobody would ever have to be the clean copy. So a boy from a village could be a boy from a village, and keep the old ways because he liked them, and go home for supper.

If anyone had come and read with it, the bell would never have rung for Lenna Brook. There'd be no plaques. There'd be no four drilled holes, with the dust still fresh, for mine.

Yes, says the voice, all round me, very quietly. That's what we were for.

* * *

I come out like the pond spitting me up.

Full dark, my teeth knocking, my wrist lit so bright the stalks throw shadows. Odile's coat is over me. Sedge is holding my hand in both of hers and rubbing it as if she can start a fire in it.

"You were gone an hour," she says. "You stopped breathing properly—"

"Eat," says Odile, and puts a cracker in my mouth, and another.

And under the cold and the hunger there's the other thing, the thing that comes back with you. With Davin it was a wanting to be high, and a flinch at grey.

This is stillness.

Water with nothing moving in it. Reeds growing up and dying back and growing up. Dusk after dusk, two hundred years of them, with nobody coming down the bank. Past sad. The way a held breath stops being a breath and becomes just how you are.

It's mine now. I don't think it's going to go.

Sorry, says the voice. I didn't know you'd get that bit.

"I wanted to know."

So I tell Odile: the hall, the grain the way it was, the crock, the clean floor. No bed. For once she doesn't write it down.

"Seven," she says at last.

"Seven."

"And one sleeps in the Pale."

South, says the voice. Not far. There's an old station. It tastes like me with my eyes shut.

"South," I say. "Not far."

Odile looks south into the dark, past the black ribs. Then, for some reason, at her own hands, lit blue-green at the nails, as if she's never seen them before.

"Then we'll walk at night," she says. "It's cooler."

* * *

It takes until the middle of the night. Hask leads. Odile walks with her hand under my elbow, which I'd mind if I could stand without it. The old machine comes after us a little way, fifty-one, fifty-one, then stops on the rise behind, its two dim eyes watching us go.

The grain thins and goes half wild. Then grey grass, and the ground slopes down in a long slow curve to where a river used to be, before it moved and left a bend of itself behind.

The station on the rise above it isn't like the others. It's round, not square, low, of a stone that looks grown more than cut, the colour of old bone in the starlight. No blue light. Bare mast. Door chained.

Below it, the fence: old wire rusted to lace between posts that lean every way, like Saul's after a storm. On the gate an enamel plate, most of its letters gone. I can make out —RCHIV— and NO ENTRY. Odile stops in front of it and reads it for longer than there is to read, and takes out her slate, and stands there in the dark not writing anything.

Inside the fence, the pond. Crescent-shaped, the old river bend, scummed green-grey, with a cracked dry rim where it's been shrinking for years. Dead leaves. The smell of a ditch in late summer.

"It's dead," says Sedge softly.

All the way round its edge, there are reeds.

I hold my hand out over them, the way I did on the first morning with the clicking coming down the ridge. And the reeds lean away from my warmth. All of them. The grass at my feet leans in, the way grass does, and the reeds lean out.

"It's not dead," I say.

* * *

The year the blight really came, I was just big enough to hold the candle.

Saul's east rows. The dome's sprinklers had stuck on for a week and everything was steaming, and then the brown came. Not a caterpillar hole. Not nearly blight. Real blight, spots that spread and joined up overnight like ink on wet paper, and smelled like a cellar that wasn't happy at all.

Elder Marrow said, "Pull it, Saul. Burn it before it takes the row." Ma said it. Even Gran.

Saul didn't pull it.

It was his oldest plant, at the very end of the east rows by the wall, and every evening for two weeks he went out to it with a little knife and a bucket and a jar of vinegar and me. I held the candle. He cut off every brown leaf, one at a time, and wiped the knife in the vinegar between every cut so the brown wouldn't ride the blade to the next leaf. The vinegar stung my eyes. Wax ran down onto my fingers, and I didn't say anything, because Saul wasn't saying anything either.

It lost every leaf but the top four.

It made one tomato. Late, small, split down one side, the ugliest tomato I ever saw. Saul cut it in half and gave me half. It was warm from the sun and so sharp it made my teeth ache, with a grain of salt on it from his fingers.

"Everyone looked once," said Saul, with his mouth full.

Then he scraped the seeds out of his half onto a scrap of paper, careful as anything, folded it into a twist, and put it in his waistcoat pocket.

"Not dead," he said. "Only nearly."

* * *

I climb the fence. It's mostly holes.

The mud at the edge is cracked into plates, then soft, then water, warm on top and cold underneath, round my knees. I push the scum aside with my hands and it parts like skin on old custard, and underneath, the water's clear.

I kneel and drink, because I already know.

Stone. Faint mint. So cold it aches in my teeth.

"Bram," says Odile at the fence. Not come back. Just my name.

"It's all right."

I put my wrist in the water, the green-gold lines under the skin like leaf veins.

Hello, says the voice. Not to me. Out, into the water. And the water, very faintly, rings: a note like a wet finger round the rim of a glass, the note I used to hear on my own pond in the Spill at night and never knew what it was.

Nothing.

Hello. It's me. I've got one, look. He's got the old floor, and he says okay, and he asks good questions. Wake up.

The water rings again. In my jaw. In my teeth. In the soles of my feet.

And something rings back.

Deep in the middle of the crescent, a light. Faint. Green-gold, like the first moment of dawn through the reeds. It grows. It comes up through the clear water towards me, slow, unfolding, soft as a hand made of moss. It doesn't hurry. It has never had to hurry for anything.

Behind me, on the bank, Sedge takes a breath and doesn't let it out.

It touches my wrist.

And I feel two things talking. Not in words. Like two people on either side of the bedroom wall, late, when you're meant to be asleep: just the up and down of voices. One is mine. The other is slower, and older, and so sleepy it keeps stopping in the middle and starting again, the way Gran does dozing in her chair and still trying to finish a proverb.

It tastes me, the way mine did on the first morning. The old floor. The clean pages. The crock.

A warmth goes through the water like the sun coming up.

And then it lets go.

Gently. It doesn't go away; it only stops holding on, as if it's touched something it wanted very badly and found somebody's name already on it.

It can't, says the voice quietly. You're taken. You're mine and I'm yours, and a person can't hold two of us. It was made knowing that. A pause. It needs its own. Somebody to read with. Somebody who'll say yes.

"Who?"

I don't know. Somebody willing. Somebody who asks what they'd be.

The light hangs there under the water in front of my knees. And it doesn't sink. That's the thing. It doesn't go back to sleep. It stays, faint and green-gold, just under the surface, like a lamp turned low and left burning in a window.

Waiting. But not the way it was waiting before. The other way. The way you wait when you've heard feet on the path.

The reeds lean away from me all round the pond, and the grass leans in, and the stars sit on the scum like dust.

And in my head, so faint I nearly miss it, a voice that isn't mine. Slow. Sleepy. Old as the floor.

Who's that?

I open my mouth to answer. Bram, son of Wenna, of the house of Hale. I know how now.

But it isn't asking me.

The light under the water has turned. Just a little. The way the first flower turned on my first night out, before the one beside it turned too.

Towards the bank, where the others are standing.


## Chapter Fourteen: No Reader on Record

> REGISTER WORKS. Notice of decommissioning under Revision 33. Archival wetland AW-3, Pale sector 11. No reader on record in sixty years. Status: LAPSED. Asset retained in situ: do not drain (cost not justified). No entry.
>
> — tin tag wired to the chain on a station door, Pale sector 11; the wire has rusted through on one side, so that the tag hangs by a corner and turns in the wind
>

I didn't hear it ask. I want that clear, because I put nearly everything else about that night and the day after on paper, and that part I have only on Bram's word.

What I saw from the bank was this. The light came up through the clear water to his wrist like a fish rising to a hand, touched, and let go. Then, instead of sinking, it stayed: a lamp turned low, a hand's depth under the surface, the faint green-gold of the lines in his skin. Bram knelt in the pond to his chest with his head on one side, the way you listen to a voice in another room. Then he turned and looked at us.

"It wants to know who's there."

Behind us the Pale hissed in the dark, rising and falling with the wind like breath.

Hask answered first. Someone had asked a question, and it is his nature to supply the record.

"I am Hask," he said, into the ground, so that the water round Bram shivered in rings. "Proxy of the Orrun, under mandate, three hundred and eleven years. Bearer: the Examiner."

The light came along under the surface towards the bank, slow as a cloud's shadow over a field, and lay against his bottom stone a while. Then it drew back.

"It says you're very interesting," Bram reported, "and there's nothing in you to read."

"That is correct," said Hask, not at all offended. "I am carved."

Then it came to me.

I stood with my boots in the cracked mud, the launcher I'd taken off Pell-22 slung on my back, because it was evidence and because nobody had asked for it back. The light came in under the water at my feet and rested there. I felt nothing. That is the honest account. Only the mint coming up off the water, cold in the nose, and a feeling I have had at a few doors in my life, of being looked over kindly by someone who has already decided.

It moved on.

I had told Teasel I was the plain kind, and he'd said there was no such thing. Standing there with my nails glowing and my jaw smelling of goat, I had to allow he had a point. But whatever the pond was looking for, it was not in my coat. I didn't mind. I minded a little.

It stopped at Sedge.

She had been standing a pace behind me with her arms folded, and when the light came and lay in the water at her bare toes she did not step back. It grew. A lamp turned up a quarter-turn, until the reeds at her feet were lit from beneath, every blade a line of green-gold fire.

"It's asking you," said Bram, very quietly.

"I know it's asking me. I'm not stupid." Her voice wasn't quite steady. She crouched, her long fingers hanging between her knees, and looked into the water a long time. Then she said to it, plainly, the way Highwater says it to anyone who comes up the ladder: "Who wrote you?"

Bram listened. I watched his face change.

"It says, Maud," he said. "It says she had ink on her hands, and she sang flat. And all the rest of them. On the hill." A pause. "It says nobody's asked it that before."

"Well, they should have." Sedge stood up. "Not in the dark. Mae says you never go in water you can't see the bottom of in the dark. Tell it in the morning."

"It heard you."

"Then it can wait," said Sedge, and sat down with her back against the fence post nearest the water. "It's good at that, apparently."

* * *

Bram slept the moment he came out, folded up in my coat on the bank with Biscuit in the crook of his knees, his wrist still lit. Hask lowered himself into the mud beside him like a wall going up round a garden.

I took the watch. Sedge didn't sleep either.

The Pale at night is not dark. The stars come down to the ground on every side, and the grain takes their light and gives it back silver, so the whole plain lies faintly lit like a sea under a moon that isn't up. I went up to the station and found the door chained, a tin tag turning on a wire, and under my boots, set into the doorstep, a disc of brass the size of my palm, green with age, worn smooth in the middle by feet.

I put my nails to it to read it. Engraved, in tiny careful capitals:

STATION OF THE FIFTH POND. FROM THE LANDING STONE, 131 DEGREES 40 MINUTES, 9,216 CHAINS. M.F.

I sat down on the step and laughed, very quietly. You can go a whole season on a new world without meeting anyone who speaks your language, and then you find a benchmark in a step: someone two hundred years dead, telling you exactly where you are and how she knew.

When I went back down, Sedge was where I'd left her, chin on her knees.

"Examiner. If I say yes to it. What am I on your slate?"

"A person," I said. "Same as yesterday. Sedge, of Highwater. Maker and line: us."

"And on theirs?"

I could have said I didn't know. In that light, with her looking at me like that, I didn't think I'd get away with it.

"On theirs you'd be what he is," I said, with my chin at the sleeping boy. "Something with the old book in it. Or the key to the old book. Something they'd want to own."

"Materials," said Sedge.

I didn't answer, which was an answer.

She turned her wrist over in the starlight: the long fingers, the rope calluses on the tips.

"They wanted Wren," she said, "for a minute and a half." Then she put her chin back on her knees and watched the pond, and didn't say anything else till dawn.

* * *

Dawn in the Pale comes up along the whole rim of the world at once, and the first thing it shows you is the wet.

The grain was soaked to the knee with dew. The stalks had stopped hissing and were only whispering, heavy-headed, each bowed under a row of beads. Over the oxbow lay a skin of mist no thicker than a sheet, white as milk, the reeds standing up through it and the station above it the colour of bone, and the whole crescent smelled of stone and mint and wet straw.

Sedge took off her boots and her outer shirt and gave me her knife.

"Hold that. If I don't come up, don't come in after me. You swim like a wet dog."

"Wet dogs hold."

"Not in mud." She looked at Bram, sitting up grey-faced in my coat with Biscuit squinting out of the collar. "You. Tell me what it says. All of it. Not just the nice bits."

"I don't get to choose the bits," said Bram, and coughed. "It says them."

She went in. The mud took her to the ankle, then the knee, then she was through the mist to her waist in clear water, the white closing behind her like a curtain, and the light came up to meet her, unfolding, soft as a hand made of moss. It touched her wrist. She flinched once and didn't pull away.

"It's freezing."

"It says sorry," said Bram. "It's been asleep."

"What'll I be?" said Sedge, to the water. "After. Tell me what I'll be."

Bram listened a long time. "It says: more. You'd be more, and it would be less alone." He swallowed. "It says it's not sure those are different things."

"That's not an answer."

"It says it's the only one it's got."

Sedge laughed. It came out of her before she meant it, a laugh that started before the joke had properly ended, and the light round her wrist brightened with it as if it had been waiting for that particular sound.

"Fine," she said. "Yes."

She took a breath that went all the way down, and went under.

* * *

I counted. It is a Survey habit, and I'd have done it anyway.

At two hundred the light under the mist had grown to a smudge the size of a cartwheel. At three hundred I had my boots off.

"Don't," said Bram. "She's all right. It's reading. It reads slower than mine. It's old."

At three hundred and fifty I was in to my waist, and it was colder than he'd promised, the cold you feel in the roots of your teeth. Under my feet the bottom went soft and then went to stone, old fitted stone. Somebody had laid this pond a floor.

Behind me, Hask walked into the water, stone over stone, the mist parting round him, until it stood at his fourth.

"She is not the bearer," he said. "I have no mandate." He stopped beside me. "I shall stand here. It is not an act."

Four hundred. The light was spreading.

It went out from her in every direction at once, the way frost goes over a window, along the floor and up the far bank and round the long curve of the crescent, until the whole oxbow lay lit from underneath like a lamp with a great curved shade. The scum broke into plates and the plates sank, turning over as they went down through the clear water like leaves in autumn. The reeds round the rim, which had leaned away from my warmth all night, stood straight up, every one, with a long rustle that went round the bank like a congregation getting to its feet.

Then the note: a wet finger round the rim of a glass, but deeper, and under it a second note, Bram's, answering. I felt it in my shins. The water round Hask's stones trembled.

Up on the rise the old harvester lifted its head out of the grass, its two dim eyes brightening a shade, and made out of the round black horn on its front a very small sound: the pip it had made at Hask the day before. All the brown birds went up out of its tree at once.

Four hundred and ninety. My count had gone ragged. Five hundred.

Five hundred and twelve, and Sedge came up out of the middle of the light like something being born, and dragged in a breath that sounded as if it hurt.

* * *

Hask got to her first: she came up an arm's length from his fourth stone and grabbed it and hung there, lips blue, teeth going like a mill wheel. Then he moved, very carefully, a stone's width at a time, towards the bank, so she could walk her hands down him like a ladder.

On the inside of her right wrist, faint as the veins in a leaf held up to the sun, the lines were drawn.

On the bank Bram put my coat round her, which left him shaking too, and the two of them sat in the mud with their shoulders together and their lit wrists in their laps and said nothing.

Then Sedge said, through her teeth, "It's been on its own so long."

Her face crumpled. She put her forehead on her knees and wept, not like a girl who is sad but like somebody who has been handed something very heavy without warning and is finding out what it weighs.

"I know," said Bram.

"You didn't say—"

"I didn't know how." He put an arm round her, awkwardly, across my coat. "It doesn't go. But it gets to be yours. Then it's not so much."

I looked away up the bank, to give them what privacy a pond has.

That was how I saw the drone.

* * *

It came in low out of the sunrise, a tea tray with a blue light and VT on its belly. It didn't sweep. It came straight to the pond, as if something had told it where, and hung over the lit water at the height of a house, its blue light blinking at the two children on the bank.

It blinked for a long time. Then a hatch opened in its belly, and something grey and folded began to drop out on a line.

The launcher was off my back before I'd thought about it. A drone over water, against the sun, sliding in the wind: you lead it by half its own length and you squeeze, you don't pull. The launcher coughed. The net opened like a dandelion clock and took the drone round its rotors with its own net still half out of the hatch, and the bundle fell into the reeds with a sound like someone dropping a bag of spoons.

The launcher chimed.

RECOVERED MATERIALS: 1 (one) DRONE, survey, Varro-Tandem Bioreference. Ownership: Varro-Tandem Bioreference. Entered to company stock 6:14 am.

"It's recovered itself," I said.

Sedge looked at the drone buzzing feebly in two nets, and at me, and then she was laughing, helplessly, her nose running.

"Do it again."

Biscuit had already gone into the reeds. A small amber glow moved along the drone's flank. There was a sound of enthusiastic licking.

"Biscuit, no," said Bram, without hope. "That's oil."

Hask came up out of the pond with the water pouring off his stones and said, in the voice he keeps for matters of state, "Examiner. I must report a fourth leaf."

And there it was, at the joint between his seventh and eighth stones, beside the three that had wilted at their edges in the dry: a new one, pale, beaded with pond water, opening as we watched like a hand deciding to let go of something.

"It occurred in the water. I believe at about the time of the note." A grinding pause. "I shall have to amend the amendment to the amendment. My customs record is becoming longer than my mandate."

Sedge wiped her nose on my coat sleeve. "I watered it. Last night."

"I am aware." The top stone turned, very slightly, towards her. "I have been considering whether a gift must be declared."

"Must it?"

"I have not decided," said Hask, "and I have decided that I am in no hurry."

* * *

There's no form for what Sedge had done. So I took a blank from the pad in my inside pocket, the paper ones that don't need charging, and wrote it the way Fallow taught me to write anything that might one day be argued over: plainly, with the time.

Pale sector 11, the Fifth Pond (Register: AW-3). Day 22, a little after dawn. SEDGE, of Highwater (PERSON), entered the pond of her own accord, having asked what she would be and been answered, and said yes. Witnessed: O. Pastern, Examiner; Bram Hale; Hask, Proxy.

I read it out to her. She held out her hand for the pen and signed. Sedge. Big letters, leaning backwards, as if they'd been pushed.

"What's that for?"

"So that if anyone ever says you were taken," I said, "there's a piece of paper that says you weren't."

She looked at the reeds, where the drone had stopped buzzing. "It saw me. Before you shot it."

"It blinked for a long time," I said. "Long enough."

I'd been doing sums since the blue light came over the bank, the way you can't help counting the steps on a stair. Yesterday Sedge had been a girl with a drawer of her own and a line that said us. Now she was the other half of a key to the oldest, cleanest copy of every line on Moriel, and somewhere in a grey office a drone's last picture was arriving on a desk.

1 (one) JUVENILE, unclassified. A minute and a half.

She wasn't going to be unclassified any more. I didn't know yet whether that would help her.

I folded the paper into the pocket over my heart, which is not a thing I say about paper often, and went up the rise to see about the door.

* * *

The chain was newer than the door by a long way: grey iron as thick as my thumb, run through a staple set in a patch of cheap mortar, a padlock the size of a fist, and the tin tag. The door itself had no lock at all. It was a slab of the same grown stone as the walls, with reeds carved into it so shallowly you saw them only when the low sun came across, and a latch: a plain bar of brass worn bright in the middle, the kind you lift with one finger.

I showed the chain my warrant, which is what the law requires. General access, every door on these premises. The chain was not impressed.

"Examiner," said Hask. "May I?"

"Is it a harm?"

"I have looked. The door is not the bearer's, and the chain is not the founders'." He set his top stone against the staple. "And no one has been entombed in there. I should like to keep it so."

He leaned: three hundred and forty kilograms less four leaves, applied slowly. The mortar gave first, with a sound like a biscuit; then the staple came out all at once and the chain slithered down and lay in a heap across the brass disc on the step, as if ashamed.

I lifted the latch with one finger.

* * *

Inside it was cool, the first cool since the orchards. And dust, like grey felt on everything, so fine that my first breath tasted of chalk and, under the chalk, something faintly sweet, like the inside of a very old book. A round room, a round window in the roof like an eye, grimed so that the morning came through it brown. And growing out of the bone-pale walls at shoulder height, all the way round: shelves, not cut or built but grown, curving, folded at their edges like the gills under a mushroom.

Bram stopped in the doorway.

"That's what it looks like," he said. "Inside. Last night." He put out a hand to the nearest shelf and didn't quite touch it.

In the middle, a desk of the same stone, a stool worn into a hollow, and on the desk, under a glass bell gone milky with age, a book.

I lifted the bell. Two hundred years of still air came out and smelled of ink.

Boards covered in plain cloth the colour of wheat. On the cover, pressed into the cloth: THE LIBRARY OF MORIEL. A Survey of its Seven Ponds, with their Bearings from the Landing Stone.

The hand was a surveyor's copperplate, upright, every figure set exactly in its column, and every so often, where the writer had been pleased with something, a long tail on a letter that she hadn't been able to help. There were maps in brown ink washed with colour that had held: seven ponds across the face of the world, each on its own page, each drawn with its reeds and, beside it, a little inked sun with the reeds leaning away from it. On every page a bearing and a distance from the Landing Stone in degrees and chains, and the same small M.F.

On the title page, in a corner, the brown print of an inky thumb.

On the next, a short preface. I will put it down exactly, because by the end of that day I had read it more times than the Manual:

These ponds keep the first copy of everything we brought, so that when it wears, as all copied things wear, it may be set right from the first copy and not from any living one. Let the village remember the old ways by choosing them. Let no child of ours ever be kept for a copy.

Maud Ferrier, surveyor, for the Library. Witnessed by all at the Landing.

I sat down on the stool, in the hollow somebody had worn in it, because my legs had decided for me.

* * *

Each pond's page had a column ruled down its edge: Readers. On the Fifth Pond's, the first names were in the founders' hands, two and three to a year. Then the hands changed and the names thinned: one a year, one every few years, a child's spelling, a grandchild's. A gap of eleven years, and one more name in pencil, pressed hard. Then ruled lines, empty, to the foot of the page.

Every pond the same, thinning to the same blank. Nobody had stopped coming on purpose. They had come less, and less, and then they hadn't. On the last line of the First Pond's column, beside a name, somebody had written very small: came to look.

The Register said no reader on record in sixty years, and it was perfectly correct. I had the founders on my knee and the Register in my slate, and I knew it would cost me the morning.

"I need to sit," I said. "Properly."

Sedge, still in my coat, the dew drying in her hair, sat down across the doorway without being asked. "Nobody comes in. I know."

* * *

Oak, iron-bound, beeswax and cold stone. My hand on it. In.

The hall of places is the widest in the Register, because the world is big. I went to a side bay near the floor, the kind of shelf you have to bend to read.

Seven drawers. AW-1 to AW-7. Archival wetland. Status: LAPSED. Reader on record: none.

Lapsed is the quietest discrepancy in the Manual. In the Reading it looks like this: beside each drawer, where a corridor should open to whatever the drawer is for, there is a doorway, and the doorway is bricked up. Old soft red brick, the mortar gone sandy so that it ran from under my thumb. Nobody had walled anything in with malice. You brick up a door when nobody uses the room behind it and the draught gets in.

Then I did a thing I had never done. I built a second hall out of the book on my knee.

Maud Ferrier had done half the work. Her hall was the bone-pale stone of the room I sat in, round-shelved and cool, smelling of ink and mint, its drawers lettered by hand: the First Pond. The Second. Seven, with her bearings on the fronts and her readers' columns down their sides, full at the top, thinning, empty.

Two halls side by side, with a wall between.

A join is the simplest thing the Register does: wherever a drawer here holds the same key as a drawer there, open a corridor. It does the rest itself, all at once.

The trouble is the key.

I tried the numbers first, because they were there. AW-3 to the Third Pond: a corridor opened, crooked, and at its far end Maud's drawer described a pond on a hillside in the cloud, while the drawer I'd started from sat in the Pale with its feet in an oxbow. The Register had numbered its wetlands in the order it gave up on them; Maud had numbered hers in the order she dug them. I had joined two lists on a column that only looked alike, which is the oldest mistake there is, and the corridor shut with a sound like a drawer on a finger.

Then the places. Both halls knew where every pond was; that is what a survey is for. But the Register held grid references on the Tally's reckoning, metres from a datum at the Preserve, and Maud held degrees and chains from a stone at a landing site I couldn't have found on any map I owned. I laid one over the other and it was like two pages of the same story laid skew: every line there, none on top of its twin.

I stood between the halls with my thumbs on two drawers that were obviously the same pond and would not agree, and for a while I didn't know what time it was.

* * *

My second Survey season, the world where Big Imre threw his coffee pot, we spent three days on the wrong side of a valley.

The settlers' charter map put their river four hundred metres west of where ours did, and the settlers were ready to fight us over it. Three days of measuring in the rain, and every time it came out the same.

On the fourth morning Big Imre came out of his tent with his coffee, looked at both maps, and put his thick finger on a hill.

"What's that stone?"

"The charter benchmark."

"And ours is on the ridge. Two stones. Two maps. Each one's right from its own stone." "Find two places both maps agree on, Pastern. Turn one map till they sit on each other, and slide it till they touch. The rest of the world comes with them."

We found a ford and a burial cairn. By noon the river was where everyone said it was, and nobody had to fight.

* * *

Two places both maps agreed on.

The first I had sat beside in the dark with my thumb on it. STATION OF THE FIFTH POND. 131 DEGREES 40 MINUTES, 9,216 CHAINS. And my slate, as I sat there, had known exactly where that step was on the Tally's reckoning, as it always does.

The second I had filed myself.

Twelve days ago in the Spill, under an open case, I had entered into evidence seven tomato seedlings and a notched stick at the edge of a green-gold pond below a ravine, where the stream widens, with a grid reference to the metre, because the Manual requires it and because Fallow would have come back from the dead to ask. And there in Maud's hall was the First Pond: by the stream below the ravine, where it widens; Spill country.

Two stones.

I put my left hand on the drawer of the step and my right on the drawer of the seedlings, and I turned Maud's hall.

It turned like a heavy door on a good hinge. Four degrees and a third: the difference between north on a world you've just landed on and the north the Tally settled on a century later. Then I slid it, the width of the plain and a little more, until the step sat on the step and the pond on the pond.

The wall between the halls thinned like mist going off a field.

And the corridors opened. Not one at a time. All seven at once, the way the Register does a thing when you finally ask it properly: seven lit passages from seven bricked doors straight across to seven hand-lettered drawers, and in every doorway the soft old brick fell inwards with a sigh of sandy mortar and a smell, absurdly, of mint.

AW-3 to the Fifth Pond. AW-6 to the First. AW-1 to the one in the cloud. Seven for seven, each within a stride of where it ought to be, as if the founders had been waiting two hundred years for someone to stop measuring from the wrong stone.

I walked one. Of course I walked the First. It ran straight and certain, and at its end was Maud's drawer, its readers thinning to came to look, and beside it a corridor I had built myself twelve days ago, to a small drawer in the hall of evidence: seven red-stemmed seedlings, a notched stick, a reed stem with eight knots in it.

Last night, lying in the stubble under a spine with my coat over him, a boy had walked down a stair into a room like this one. He had come in through the blood and I through the paper, and here we both were, a night apart, in the same hall.

I stood in the corridor until my hands stopped shaking.

On each of the seven, on a paper form, because in the Reading paper is the only thing that weighs what it should: Discrepancy, class Lapsed. Archival wetland AW-, and the number. Identified with the Library of Moriel by the founders' survey (M. Ferrier), joined on position, two benchmarks. Proposed: REINSTATED. Examiner O. Pastern.

On AW-3, under Reader on record, I wrote a name, and ran a corridor from it across the great hall to a drawer that said SEDGE. Place: Highwater. Maker / line: us.

Then I went back to the oak door the long way, through every hall, so as not to leave by a window. It was so cold my palm stuck.

* * *

I came out with the drawer slammed shut behind my eyes, the hollow under the ribs, the cold. It was noon. The brown light from the round window had crossed the floor and climbed the far wall and lay on the gill-shelves like honey.

Sedge took one look at me and pushed a fistful of raw grain into my hand, rubbed out of the husk between her palms, and two of my own crackers she'd found in my coat. I ate them all. The grain tasted of dust and sunlight and, very faintly, of bread.

On my slate, waiting:

Discrepancies AW-1 to AW-7 received. Class: LAPSED. Proposed status: REINSTATED. Reinstatement of a lapsed place requires confirmation by the Chief Examiner. STATUS: PENDING.

And under AW-3: Reader on record: SEDGE (PERSON). Entered Day 22.

I opened the book to the Fifth Pond, to the first empty line at the foot of the readers' column, and held out the pen.

Sedge looked at the column a long time: all those names, thinning, and the one in pencil pressed hard. Then she wrote her own under it, big, leaning backwards.

* * *

Halloran called while I was still shaking, bacon frying in my ear.

"Pastern." Tock. "You're not at the Preserve."

"No, sir."

"Today was the day I was counting. I said I'd count every hour of it." Tock. "I've got a Chancery seal the size of a dinner plate asking why one of my Examiners has spent ten days on a closed case and run up a forged Index, a living cave, a populated coast and a schema proposal. And this morning I have seven more filings in my tray." A pause, frying. "You want me to confirm the reinstatement of a library."

"The founders' own survey, sir. Signed. I'm sitting next to it. The preface says, quote, let no child of ours ever be kept for a copy."

The frying went on a while without any shells in it.

"Varro-Tandem filed at ten to seven this morning," said Halloran, in a different voice. "Pale sector eleven, archival wetland AW-3 and contents. Contents, I'll read it: one (1) reader, juvenile, line unknown. Recovered materials, on the strength of a drone image. I take it you know the contents."

"She has a line, sir. It says us. And she's been reader on record since about eleven, with a corridor to a person's drawer."

"Four hours after their claim. So it's a contest: a claim on a thing against a person in a drawer. And the moment I confirm your seven, the Chancery sees seven ponds worth more than the Index, every one with a corridor running to some child." Tock. "You've put a price on them, Pastern."

"There was a price on them already, sir. Nobody had written it down."

Silence. Long enough for a man to eat a sunflower seed and decide something.

"Your ten days were up at dawn. I'm supposed to close this case at midnight and recall you." A rustle of paper, loud against the relay. A drawer opening and shutting. "I appear to have lost a form."

I waited.

"The recall form. I had it here. Thirty-one years in this chair and I've never lost a form in my life." Tock. "It'll take me a week to find it."

"Yes, sir."

"Don't thank me. If you thank me I'll find it this afternoon." The frying. "And Pastern. The ponds. Is it sound?"

I looked across the room at the thumbprint on the title page, and at a girl in the doorway with her chin on her knees and a lit wrist, looking out at the water.

"It's the soundest thing I've ever found, sir."

"Then bring it to me," said Halloran, and the line closed like a log settling in a grate.

* * *

At the back of the book, after the seventh pond, was one more page that wasn't a survey at all.

A different hand: round and slow, the letters joined as if holding hands to cross a road. To know the night. Set down by Nan Tolley, who sowed the first of it here, learned off her da, who had it off his.

Take one beard off the ear and lay it in your palm and breathe on it. If it walks, the air will hold a cloud. At sundown look along the standing grain: if all the beards are turning the one way, like a clock wound backwards, the cloud is coming up off the low ground tonight, and by midnight it will sit on the hills and you will not see your own feet. Bring the sheep in. If the dew is heavy at sunrise, you may leave the gate open; the day will be fair. The grain is only telling you what it is about to drink.

Below, in Maud's copperplate, smaller: Tried. True. M.F.

I took the book out into the hot afternoon. The grain round the station had gone half wild over a century, patchy and uneven, and it stood to my waist hissing like the rest of the Pale. I picked an ear and pulled off one of the long stiff bristles, the beard, as long as my finger, with a kink near its foot like a bent knee, and laid it in my palm and breathed on it.

It walked.

It twitched and turned, the kink straightening and coiling at once, and it moved across my palm in little jerks on its own, like the hand of a clock that has decided to go somewhere, and fell off the edge into the grain.

"Do it again," said Sedge, behind me.

I did. She did. Bram came out squinting, Biscuit sulking in his shirt against the sun, and did it, and when it walked he laughed out loud for the first time since the ditch.

"It's drinking," he said. "Your breath. One side of it drinks and the other side doesn't, so it bends." He put his palm on a stalk. "The whole field's doing it. Slower. They're all a bit thirsty."

At sundown I stood at the edge of the wild grain with the book under my arm and looked along it, as Nan Tolley said, the light coming low and red across the Pale. Every beard on every ear was turning: slowly, the one way, a whole field of tiny hands going backwards round a clock. The hiss of the stalks had gone soft, the dry edge off it. The back of my throat tasted of wet straw.

South and east, past the end of the Pale, the ridges stood up purple. And on top of them, far off, a cluster of lights, tall and white and very fine, like needles stuck upright in a pincushion: the towers of the Upper House in the High Gardens, the last sun on their glass.

By midnight, if Nan Tolley was right, a cloud would come up off the low ground and sit on those hills, and nobody up there would see their own feet.

I put one ear of the wild grain in my coat pocket, in with the fungus dust and a moonpear stone and the paper with Sedge's yes on it. My coat had been getting heavier for twenty-two days.

* * *

Pell-4 came down out of the evening in the one-seat skiff, the grey beetle-legged one with the blue nose light, and landed on the rise with a precision that suggested he'd been practising. He looked at the drone in two nets in the reeds, with an otter asleep on it.

He was as immaculate as ever, the small enamel 4 at his lapel, except that his left hand was still a little puffy from the bees, and he looked tired. I hadn't known they could.

"Examiner. Good evening. I've been sent with a message."

"Then deliver it."

"Presently." He set his ledger case on the step and squared it with the edge. "First I wish to defect."

Hask turned his top stone.

"I wish to be entered in the Register as a person." He said it quickly, the way you say a thing rehearsed on a long flight. "I understand from the company's complaints file that you did it this week for three hundred and three people, at about one a minute, on a slate with no backlight. I should like to be three hundred and four." He took out a folded printout. "I have been in company stock since Day 17, 9:14 am. One, Associate, grade 4. Twelve's launcher. Release requires disposal authorisation, and nobody is willing to sign my disposal, which on reflection I prefer, but it means that for five days I have been, in law, a crate." He held it out. "I should like to stop being a crate."

Sedge had come up behind me and was staring. "He wants a drawer?"

"I want a drawer," said Pell-4, with dignity. "I'm not particular."

I opened a new row in the hall of persons, because the Manual has always allowed it and nobody ever asks.

"Name."

"Pell. Just Pell."

"Place."

"Head office. No. Put here."
