I put Pale sector 11. Then I read him the last slot, as I had to three hundred and three people the day before.

"Maker, slash, line. At Highwater they ask it another way. Who wrote you?"

"Oh, that's simple. The original licence was written from the original Associate, whose genome and memory were—" He stopped.

"Yes?"

"From the original," he said again, more slowly. "Whom I am. Obviously. Except that Seven would say he is, and Twelve would say he is, and Nineteen..." His stylus had come out of its own accord and hovered over his ledger. "If I write myself, I concede I was written from somebody, who must therefore be another copy, with a number, which I would have to enter, which would be Seven's, or Twelve's. And if I write one of theirs..." He looked at the stylus. "I can't write Seven's. I'd sooner be a crate."

"Put us," Sedge offered.

"Absolutely not."

"It works."

"You," said Pell-4, "don't have eleven of yourself filing against you over the coffee rota." He put the stylus away with great care, and I watched the company come back into him like water into a footprint. "I withdraw the application, Examiner. I'm so sorry to have taken your time. I'll remain in stock."

"The row's open," I said. "It'll stay open. It doesn't expire."

"That's very irritating of it," said Pell-4, and I thought for a moment he might say something else. Instead he looked past me at Sedge's right wrist, lit in the dusk, and the stylus came out again by itself and wrote one word on his ledger before he could stop it. I read it upside down. Materials.

He looked at it. He didn't cross it out. But he closed the ledger over it as if it had been rude.

"Nothing personal," he said, and it didn't sound like the motto. It sounded like an apology from a man who had never been taught one. "Everything documented. The message."

He set a small grey disc on the step beside the brass benchmark, touched it, and stepped back as if from a stove.

* * *

The voice that came out of it was soft and pleased, the voice of a man who has never in his life had to raise it. I had never heard it before. I knew it at once, because behind me Bram went entirely still, and the six slits on his neck slammed shut.

"Examiner Pastern. Lisle Varro. Good evening.

"I've been reading you for ten days. You've cost my company a forged Index, a mineral claim, a coast, forty harvesters and, I'm told, the lead copy's ledger, to an otter. I'm not complaining. I've waited twenty years for someone to cost me something I could learn from.

"This morning one of my drones saw a pond light up in Pale sector eleven, and a child come out of it. This afternoon your Chief Examiner's tray filled with seven wetlands, and I have read the preface you quoted, and I should very much like to read the rest.

"Come to dinner. The Upper House, tonight, in the garden towers. A skiff will be at your station at moonrise. Bring the young lady from the pond, if she'll come; I'd like to meet the reader on record. The boy and the Proxy will be quite safe below the cloud. I give you my word on that, which you have no reason to value, and which I keep.

"I'm told I have a terrible sum. I've never pretended it was pleasant. But it balances, Examiner, and nobody has ever shown me another that does.

"If you have a better sum, I will fund it."

The disc clicked, and was quiet. On the far ridges the needle-fine lights of the towers had come on, one after another, very high and very clean.

"He means that," said Pell-4, picking up the disc. "That's the trouble with him." He went back to his skiff and sat in it with the canopy open. "I'm to wait and observe. I'm sorry. I'm told it's not personal."

* * *

"No," said Bram.

He'd gone white under the dust, looking at the towers.

"He's the one. Through the door. In the coat. Draw from him. He said it like ordering lunch." He turned to Sedge. "You're not going up there. He asked for you. That's why—"

"I know why he asked for me," said Sedge.

She stood at the edge of the rise with her arms folded, the lit wrist tucked under her elbow.

"He asked for the pond. I'm the pond now. I said yes to it in front of you all, and she wrote it down." She looked at me. "If you go up there with a book and no pond, he'll say it's a nice book."

She was right. That was the worst of it: I'd been working it out since the disc clicked, the way you work a sum you don't like the answer to, and she'd got there first and said it plainer.

"You'd be a witness," I said. "Not evidence. You stand beside me and say what you said at dawn, and you don't say anything else."

"I can do that."

"You've never done it once in your life."

"I can start," said Sedge.

Bram wanted to argue; I watched him want it. Then he looked at her wrist and stopped, because he knew better than anyone alive what she'd said yes to, and that it had been hers to say.

"Hask and I wait below the cloud," he said at last. His voice was rough.

"Hask and you wait below the cloud. Hask, if it comes up thick tonight—"

"It will," said Hask. He had turned his whole stack to the low ground. "I can hear the wet in the earth, rising. The grain has been saying so since sundown, I think. I did not know grain could say anything." A grinding pause. "If it comes up thick, Examiner, I shall go up into it as far as stone can go, and wait at the top of that. And then I shall consider whether I may go further."

"That isn't in the Schedule, Hask."

"No," he said. "I have noticed a great many things are not."

* * *

The moon came up out of the Pale like a lamp out of a well, and with it, out of the south, a long grey skiff with a blue nose light, bigger than any I'd seen and quieter, gliding in low over the grain.

I washed my face in the pond; the light under the water came a little towards my hands, curious, and went away again, polite. I checked my flask (full, scalding), my slate (cracked, dark), my warrant, my pad of paper forms, the survey wrapped in a square of sailcloth, and in the pocket over my heart, the paper with Sedge on it, leaning backwards.

Every beard on the wild grain was turned now, wound back as far as it would go. Down at the river's old bend beyond the pond, the first white was lifting off the earth, thin as breath on a glass.

Sedge came and stood beside me, her knife at her belt where I couldn't persuade her to leave it, her chin up.

"What do I call him?"

"Whatever he asks you to," I said. "Then watch what he does with his hands."

Behind us Bram stood with Hask, Biscuit on his head, his own wrist lit in the dark like a lamp left in a window. He didn't say goodbye. He said, "Look twice," to both of us, as if it were something he'd been given and had decided to lend.

The skiff settled on the rise, and its door opened on a little lit room of grey leather, smelling of cedar.

I had a better sum. It was under my arm, in a dead woman's beautiful hand, with her thumbprint on the front. And it was standing beside me in a borrowed coat, still dripping a little, with a light in its wrist and a knife at its belt.

We went up.


## Chapter Fifteen: Whose

> SETTLING AGENT SA-3 (draw-room use). For reference-line subjects only. Acts on unmodified receptor types; staff of standard modification are unaffected. Dose by body weight. Subject should be seated. Do not exceed one dose. Keep cool.
>
> — label on a grey case in the clinical stores, Varro-Tandem Bioreference, High Gardens towers, level 9
>

The cloud starts about as high up the ridge as the meeting-house bell. Below it there's the ordinary world: wet grass, a few fur-trees, a scree slope, Hask. Above it there's nothing, a white wall leaning on the hill. And the trees go up into it and don't come out.

I lie on my back in the moss at the very edge and look up into it. It's the most beautiful thing I've ever seen, and I've seen a harvester kneel.

The trees here are taller than the dome. Their trunks go up grey and slick and furred with moss, ropes and beards and cushions of it, every colour of green there is and some that are nearly gold. Halfway up, the white takes them, and somewhere up there, inside the cloud, their crowns are living a whole life we can't see. Things hang down out of the white on threads: little gardens, roots and flowers in balls of moss the size of Tilly, turning slowly, dripping. The whole forest drips. Tik. Tik. Tikatik. Like the dome after a sprinkler day, only for ever.

On the left, a waterfall comes out of the white. It's falling already when you first see it, a long rope of water, and it falls, and falls, and about the height of Saul's barn above the ground it just stops being water. It thins and frays and turns into mist and drifts away sideways. It never lands. Nothing under it is even wet.

"Where does it go?" I ask Hask.

"Up," says Hask, after some thought. "It appears to be going up."

He's right. The whole cloud's climbing, the way steam climbs off Gran's pan: warm air off the Preserve's flat land, reaching the ridge and going up it, and getting cold, and turning white. I can feel it pass over my face going uphill. It tastes of cloud: cold, and a bit like licking a spoon.

And I can't smell anything from up there. With the air going up, anything up the hill blows away from me, and all I get is what's behind me. Wet grass. Hask's lichen. Biscuit, on my chest, asleep, river and lamp oil.

I put my palm flat on the moss and listen. The forest's talking, the way the Undercroft talked: Wet. Wet. Light up here. Wet. And over it, high and steady, a hum that isn't alive. Fans.

The towers. You see them when the cloud thins: white, smooth, windowless at the bottom, three or four of them standing up out of the forest like the bones of a hand. And at the very top of the nearest, above the cloud, where the evening sun still reaches, something glittering. Glass.

That's where they've gone.

* * *

Odile and Sedge went up at dusk, with the moon coming up full out of the Pale behind us, in a long grey skiff with a blue nose light that came in low over the grain and sat on the rise by the bone-coloured station as politely as a cat. Odile in her coat, brushed, which I've never seen her bother with. Sedge in a borrowed shirt that was too long in the arms, her sleeves rolled four turns like mine. On the inside of her wrist, very faint, a tracery of green-gold, like leaf veins, that wasn't there yesterday.

She kept looking at it. She kept putting her other hand over it and taking it off again.

"You said there'd be pudding," she said to Odile.

"I said there might be."

"If there isn't pudding, I'm coming straight back down."

Odile looked at me. She didn't say stay here. She'd already said it four times, and Hask had said it twice, and I'd said yes every time.

"Below the cloud line," she said instead.

"Below the cloud line."

Sedge turned in the skiff's lit doorway and did a stupid wave, all her fingers going separately, and then the door shut, and the skiff lifted, and went up the slope over the trees, and the white took it.

Hask and I walked up to where the grass gives out and the cloud begins, which isn't far: the station's rise sits right at the foot of the ridge. Below the cloud line. Like we said.

That was two hours ago. I've tied my knot, the twenty-second. I've eaten the last of Odile's crackers, which she left me in a paper twist, the way Gran does when she goes out.

The sky-grazers are here.

Their smell was in the moss as soon as we got near: hay and wet wool and honey gone to vinegar, and bread, faint, under it.

They come down out of the white at dusk, slow and pale, mouths open, grazing the low seed. Only here there are little ones. Half-size, fat, pale as milk, their back ridges round as rolls, piping so high it's nearly not sound, blundering about beside their mothers on nothing.

This is where they come to have them. Up in the crowns.

"Hask," I say. "Look."

"I am looking," says Hask. "Item two thousand and six is very much on my mind."

One of the big ones drops lower than the others, piping a soft question, low enough that I can see its eyes, big and dark and surprised, and the edge of its left wing.

There's a nick out of it.

It hangs a hand's breadth off the moss in front of my face, smelling of the strongest bread on the slope, the way it did on the shelf above the stream with the heat-hunter coming.

Then it bumps its soft nose on my forehead. Exactly where it did, the morning it left me.

I can't say anything. I put my hand on its neck, and it's warm, and the float in its back is full and tight as a drum.

It brought something. Behind it, lower and more anxious, comes a little one. Fat, pale, new. It hangs behind its mother and looks at me past her side, and pipes.

"You had a baby," I say. My voice goes stupid. "You had a baby."

The little one burps. It's very small. It smells of bread.

Hask makes the grinding noise in the ground.

* * *

But they don't go back up.

When they've grazed, the flock rises into the white towards the crowns, the way they always do at dark, and then they come back down. Piping, worried. They try again. They come back down.

I listen through the ground a long time before I hear it. Up in the crowns, among the drips, a long faint creak, like a rope on a boat when the tide pulls. Lots of them. Strung between the trees.

Net.

"They've netted the crowns," I say.

"Who has?"

"Someone with fans." I think of thirty-one roof claims. "To keep them off the glass."

The nicked-wing grazer settles on the moss beside me with its little one tucked against its side, both of them breathing slow. Above us, in the white, the creaking goes on.

* * *

The wind turns.

I feel it happen. For hours the air's been going up past my face, into the hill. Then it stops. Everything goes very still, and the drips sound louder, and the moss stops breathing upwards. And then, slowly, cold, the air starts coming down.

Out of the cloud. Down the slope. Over me.

The cold air off the top, Hask says later, sliding down at night like water down a roof, because cold is heavier than warm. I don't know that then. I only know that for the first time since we got here, I can smell the top of the hill.

Wet stone. Moss. Cedar.

Cedar and cooked ink.

My stomach goes over. It's the smell of the corridor outside the glass room, upstairs, under the dome. The smell of the grey shirt I threw in the sea.

And then a whistle.

High, thin, from up in the white. Highwater's whistle, the one Sedge does through her fingers. Grey boat. Grey boat. It comes from the left. Then the right. Then from all round, from everywhere at once, bouncing off the trunks and the hanging gardens and the cloud itself, so I can't tell where it is.

"Hask. Where?"

"Above," says Hask. His whole stack has turned up the hill. "The ground does not carry a whistle. I cannot say where above."

The whistle stops. Halfway through.

And on the cold air, so strong I taste it, a struck match. Sedge, frightened. And next to it, a lamp turned all the way up. Odile, furious.

And round both of them, all through them, cedar and copier toner.

Pell.

Not one. Lots of them, like coats on the hooks in the meeting-house porch. All the same coat.

It's coming from the right. Not from where the whistle seemed to be. From the nearest tower.

The whistle lied. The smell doesn't.

* * *

I'm on my feet. I don't remember getting up.

Hask has come right up to the edge of the white, his top stone tipped back as if he's looking up the slope.

"Hask, can you—"

"No." Very quiet. "I weigh three hundred and forty kilograms, less three leaves. I am not built for ascent. I have never needed to be." He turns his stack to me. "The Examiner is my bearer. For three hundred and eleven years I have been, every time, between the bearer and the harm. It is what I am for."

"I know."

"I cannot be between her and this," says Hask. "You can." A pause, as long as a breath. "This is not a thing I have ever had to ask. I do not know the form."

"You don't need a form."

"I am told that frequently," says Hask. "Go."

* * *

I go up the scree on my soles and my hands, fast, and at the top of it the cloud takes me.

It's like putting your head in a sack. White. Wet. Cold that gets straight into the soft little rooms in my chest, so the ache starts at once, and the cough with it, like Gran's. I can't see my hands. I go up the first trunk by feel, and it's moss over bark over wet, and my fingers can't find anything, and I come down hard on my back in the roots.

Biscuit puts its head out of my shirt, takes one look at the cloud, and goes back in.

I lie there. Somewhere above me, a long way up, Sedge has stopped whistling.

Bram, says the voice behind my eyes. Careful.

"I can't climb it."

No.

"I can't see. I'm freezing. My hands won't hold."

No. They won't.

"Then fix it."

I've never told it that before. Every other time, it's asked.

Which?

"All of it."

Bram. Name it.

There's no time to name anything. So I grab what I've got.

Hands first.

Gerald. Master Aldous's jar beast, with the hooked feet, who walked up the inside of the glass every lesson and hung from the lid looking smug. Tilly said he had sticky feet. Master Aldous said no, not sticky: hairs, too small to see, thousands on every foot, so fine they caught on the glass the way a burr catches on wool. "Look closer, Tilly."

"Gerald's feet," I say. "On my hands. And Sedge's. The way she goes up a tree like breathing. The long bit at the ends of her fingers."

That's a beetle and a girl.

"It's what I've got."

Cold next. And I'm in the Hale kitchen with the lamp lit at the wrong time of night, and Ma in the bed by the stove, white and done in, and Dr. Ommery sitting with something red and furious and new on her knee. Tilly. Born an hour. Ommery turned her over, gentle, and put my hand flat on her back, between the shoulders, and it was hot. Hotter than the rest of her. Like a little stove.

"Brown fat," said Ommery. "Babies have it. It burns to keep them warm without shivering, because they're too small to shiver properly. We lose most of it when we grow." She looked at me over Tilly's head. "What did you learn today, Bram?"

"Tilly's back," I tell the voice. "The stove between her shoulders. All over me."

That's not a name, that's a baby.

"It's what I've got."

Eyes last. A knee-deer at my pond, my first week, turning its head, and two green lamps coming on in the dark. Pim at Highwater, owl-wide, looking at me across a black platform as if it was noon. The Primer's page with an eye cut in half like an onion, and the bit at the back that catches the light.

"The shine," I say. "At the back. Like the knee-deer. Like Pim."

The voice doesn't answer for a moment.

That's four, it says. Four at once. And not one of them named properly. A beetle and a girl and a baby and a deer. It's not cross. It's frightened. I've never heard it frightened. I hold you by your names, Bram. Everything you've named, I can keep the shape of while I work. Everything you haven't, I'm holding by habit. If I do four, on these names, all at once, I'll need all of me to keep them straight. Something will slip.

"What?"

I don't know. Something you never named. I won't know till it's gone.

Up in the white, nobody's whistling.

"Yes," I say.

Bram—

"Yes."

* * *

It's like being a loaf when the oven door shuts.

The heat goes through me first, from between my shoulders, out, down my arms and legs and into my fingers and toes, and the cold I've been shaking with is just gone, as if someone took a wet coat off me. I lie in the roots and steam. Actual steam, coming off my skin into the cloud.

Then my hands.

They itch, then burn, then ache like after carrying the milk pails up from the gate. I hold them up and I can see them, which means my eyes are going too, and they're wrong. The fingers are longer. Not much. A knuckle. The last joints bend back too far, like Sedge's. And the pads have gone a dull grey, fine-ridged, like the inside of a mushroom cap, and when I touch my own thumb to my finger, they catch. Not sticky. Catch.

And the cloud goes from white to grey to silver, and then I can see.

Not like day. Like a drawing of the forest in silver pencil, every drop, every thread of moss. The colours have gone. But it goes on as far as I want to look.

I put my hand flat on the nearest trunk and it holds.

And I'm so hungry I could eat the hill.

Climb, says the voice, very small. I'm busy.

* * *

I climb.

My fingers go on the wet bark and catch, and peel off when I lift them, tk, tk, like a cat's paw off a rug. I go up faster than I've ever gone up anything, faster than the bell tower with Jory watching, with Biscuit clamped to my chest under my shirt like a hot water bottle with opinions.

And the forest opens up round me, silver, inside the cloud.

The crowns.

Up here the trunks open out and go everywhere, sideways, branches as wide as the lane, with whole gardens on them, ferns and flowers and little trees growing out of other trees, things I've no names for. Water runs down every branch in silver threads. There are pools in the forks. A frog the size of my thumbnail sits in one and looks at me and goes pip. The sound up here's all drips and wind in moss, and over it, very soft, all round, the grazers, piping to each other in the white, soft as owls.

And the nests.

Baskets, woven of moss and pale seed-down and grazer hair, big enough to sleep a family. They're not on the branches. They float, each one tied to a branch by a few twisted ropes of moss, lifting a little, like a boat at a mooring. I can smell the bread off them. I can smell milk, faint, and hay, and something warm and sleepy, like Tilly's hair when she's been in bed all day with a cold.

Every one is empty.

They float under the net.

Grey company mesh, strung through the crowns from tower to tower, a great sagging ceiling just above the nests. They're pushed down under it, squashed and tilted, nudging up against it like fish under ice.

Biscuit puts its head out and looks at the net with great interest. Biscuit likes anything that holds.

"Later," I tell it. "I promise."

Through the white, off to the right, the hum of the fans gets louder.

On the way past I see the fence. Down in a fork of the ridge, a pond, black and still, with reeds round it, and round the reeds old rusted wire. The reeds lean away from me as I go over. Nobody's home in it.

The one that left, says the voice, so faint I nearly miss it. She's at Highwater. In Lenna.

I don't stop.

* * *

The tower comes out of the cloud like a wall coming out of fog. Which is what it is.

White. Smooth. Cold and faintly wet, humming through my palms. No window, no door, no handhold.

Gerald walked up the inside of a jar.

I put my hands on it. They catch.

I climb it.

The fans get louder. The cloud thins. And then I put my head up out of the top of it, and I'm above the cloud.

A white sea in the moonlight, flat and silver to the end of everything, with the other towers sticking out of it and the tallest crowns breaking through like black islands. And stars like dust, all the way down to the white. The cold up here is clean and sharp and huge, and the stove between my shoulders burns on and I don't feel it.

Round the top runs a narrow gallery with a rail, and fans in round housings breathing out warm air that smells of cedar. And inside the rail, the glass room, lit gold, sitting on the tower like a lantern on a post.

I come up under the edge of the gallery and hang there by my fingers, with nothing under me but cloud, and look in.

Sedge is sitting on the floor with her back against the glass. Right there, a hand's width from me through it. Her knees up. Her borrowed shirt. Her wrist in her lap, glowing faintly green. Her face is wet and furious.

Odile's standing. Her back's to me. She's talking to somebody I can't see, very calm, with one hand flat on a long table, and on the table there's a white cloth and the remains of a dinner and, I notice, a pudding. Untouched.

And by the door there are three of them. Tall, thin, very neat, silver hair combed flat, company grey. Each with a little enamel pin.

I tap on the glass. Just with one fingernail. Tk.

Sedge turns her head.

She sees a face come up at the bottom of the glass, above a drop into the cloud, with eyes that shine silver in the lamplight like a knee-deer's at the pond. And she doesn't scream.

She grins.

It's the best thing anyone's ever done.

Then, on the gallery right above my head, the bootsteps stop.

"Good evening," says a pleasant baritone. "Nothing personal."

* * *

It doesn't hurt much. A slap on the side of my neck, like a horsefly.

He's standing on the gallery with his feet neatly together and a grey pistol with a brass cap in his hand, looking down at me with great interest. The pin on his lapel says 26. He smells of cedar and toner and, very faintly, of satisfaction, like a pan that's just been wiped.

"Settling agent," says Pell-26, and makes a note. "Draw-room strength. It's formulated for reference-line physiology, so you'll find the staff are quite unaffected. You should really be seated."

And then the smell reaches me, coming up off the place on my neck, and I know it.

In the white room under the Upper House, the spring I was called. The chair. A woman in green with cold hands, very kind, saying just a little something to settle you, sweetheart, and the sweetness. Sweet like violets. Sweet like a struck coin. And then everything slow and soft and far away, and the three vials, and nobody looking at me once.

Subject should be seated.

Everything goes slow.

* * *

My hands hold on.

Everything else goes. My arms go soft. My legs swing out over the cloud. The stars swim. But my hands stay on the edge of the gallery, flat, grey, caught, because they don't need me to hold. Gerald doesn't think about it. Gerald just hangs.

I named them that way.

I can hear my heart. Slow. Too slow. Thum. And a long gap. Thum.

And my breath. Going out. And not coming back in for a long time. And going out.

Bram, says the voice, and it's a long way off, it's in your blood. It's going everywhere. It's fitting into things.

"What things?"

I'll show you. There isn't time. There isn't time to show you. Come in.

* * *

The blood is a river at night.

Like the mill race in spring, too fast, too full: red loaves, thousands of them, soft and round with the middles pressed in, tumbling past each other down the dark like bread down the bakehouse chute on a feast morning. They're carrying the breath. I can see it in them, a brightness, and I can see it going dim.

And among them, the poison.

Little things. So small. Sharp, bent, all the same shape, like a key that only opens one kind of lock. Thousands of them, riding the river with the loaves.

The river goes past the houses. That's what the nerves are, here: long thin houses along the banks, lights going on-off, on-off down the street, passing messages. Every house has doors, and some of the doors are brakes. When they open, the house goes quiet for a moment. A house that's always talking is no good either.

The keys go to those doors.

They fit. They turn. And they hold the doors open. Not shut: open. And the houses go quiet, and stay quiet. One window goes dark. Then the next. Then a whole street.

That's the settling, says the voice. That's how they make you sit still in the chair. Just a little, and the houses go quiet and you go soft. They gave you the dose for a boy in a chair. Not a boy on a wall.

Down at the end of the river, there's a street I know without being told. It's the one that says breathe. Every few moments, its lights go on all at once, and a long way off, something enormous, my chest, pulls air.

Its lights are going on less.

Thum, says my heart, from somewhere above the river. A long gap. Thum.

"Take them out."

I can't take them out. I can only write. Things are falling out of it, it's going so fast. I can't change the doors, they're the old doors, the clean ones, and it'd take days. I can't make a new thing out of nothing. It has to be something that's already there.

The breathe-street flickers.

Breathe properly, says Ma.

But I can't. That's the whole—

And I remember the reef. The green dark under the Knuckle. Three hundred counts with no breath, and my heart slow as a church bell, and my body pulling all its warm into the middle and waiting. Not drowning. Waiting. My lungs know how to go without. I had that written into me at Highwater, and I named it properly, with Ommery's upside-down tree and Gran's paper chest.

"The sea," I say. "I can go without. I've got three hundred counts. More. Use them."

The voice stops.

Yes, it says. Yes. That's time. That's not much time.

My heart goes slower. Everything pulls in to the middle, the way it did under the reef. The breathe-street flickers, and holds.

One, I count. Two.

"Where's the dirty water go?"

What?

"Ommery. She said everything bad that gets into you goes to one place to be washed. The washhouse. Under the ribs, on the right." I can hear her, and Ma, and Gran, all in one voice. "The washhouse. Go to the washhouse."

Fourteen. Fifteen.

And the river turns, and we go with it.

It's a great dark wet place full of work, like the washhouse at home on a Monday, only instead of women up to their elbows it's the little busy machines, folded things of every shape, and each one, when something comes past that it fits, takes hold of it and cuts. Snip. In half. And lets go, and the halves go on down the river, harmless, and the cutter's ready for the next. All day. All my life. I never knew.

There are hundreds of kinds. None of them fits the keys.

Forty. Forty-one.

There, says the voice. That one. Nearly.

It's a cutter shaped almost right. Almost. It takes hold of a key as it goes past, and fumbles it, and lets go. The way you try a key in a door that's nearly the same as yours.

It needs to fold different. Just there. A few letters. And then it'll fit.

"Then change it."

You have to name it. I can't hold a new fold without a name for it. Bram, there isn't—

Seventy. Seventy-one.

The breathe-street, a long way off, goes dark. And stays dark. And then, slow, lights again.

Nobody in Firstfield has a name for a cutter in the washhouse under your ribs. But I've watched someone cut, and wipe, and cut again.

Saul, in the dark at the end of the east rows, with the candle wax running onto my fingers. One brown leaf. Cut. And the knife into the vinegar, and wiped, clean, ready. And the next leaf. Cut. Wiped. Never blunt. Never used up. Two weeks of evenings. Every cut the same.

"Saul's knife," I say. "Cut one, wipe, cut the next. It doesn't get used up."

And it's Gran's laundry. The cutter is a long chain of the little shapes, folded up on itself the way Gran folds a sheet off the line, corner to corner, in half, in half again, so it ends up a square that fits in the press. Change three letters in the chain and it folds a different way. A different square. The same sheet, folded to fit a different shelf.

I can see it. Here. And here. And here. Three letters. Apple for Crock. Gran for Tilly. Crock for Apple.

Yes, says the voice. I see it. I've got it. I'm writing.

Hundred and twelve. Hundred and thirteen.

And then it's the bakehouse. The little ovens in every cell roar up at once, the way the bakehouse oven roars at five in the morning, and the new chain is made, and folds, corner to corner, in half, in half again, into a square I've never seen.

It takes hold of a key.

Snip.

In half. Two bits of nothing, harmless, washing away down the river.

And lets go. And it's ready for the next.

"More," I say.

And the washhouse makes them, Saul's knives by the thousand, cut and wipe and cut, the ovens burning everything I've eaten for a week, and the river goes in full of keys and comes out full of pieces.

Two hundred. Two hundred and one.

But it's costing something else. The voice has to hold every new fold straight while it's written, and it's already holding Gerald's hands, and the baby's stove, and the knee-deer's eyes, and the sea in my lungs, all on bad names. It hasn't got enough hands.

Bram, it says. I'm going to drop something.

"What?"

I don't know. Something that's only held by habit. Something you never needed to name.

Two hundred and sixty.

The breathe-street goes dark.

"Drop it," I say.

* * *

My chest pulls.

Air. Huge, cold, with stars in it. I'm hanging off the gallery with my head back, hauling breaths like the mill bellows, my heart going like Biscuit's in the storm, and every window in every house in me lit.

And I'm so hungry I could eat Pell-26.

He's crouched on the gallery above me, writing. He's got a hook out on a pole, the kind you'd use to bring in a boat, and he's reaching it down for my collar.

"Ah," he says, pleasantly. "You're still conscious. That's very unusual. I'll make a note."

"Do," I say.

Except it's not my voice.

It comes out low. Too low. Rough and breathy, with a hiss on the edges of it like wind in a gill-slit, like someone talking from inside a different room. I hear it come out of my mouth and I don't know it.

Pell-26 stops writing.

"That is not the subject's voice," he says, to his ledger.

I don't let myself think about it. I swing my legs up, catch the rail with my soles, and come over the edge in one go the way Sedge comes up into a tree house, and I'm crouched on the walkway in front of him, steaming. Biscuit comes out of my shirt like a cork.

He looks at me.

He looks at me for a long time, and whatever he sees, he doesn't write it down.

Then he lifts the dart pistol again.

* * *

Biscuit bites him.

Not on the hand. On the ankle, through the company sock, hard, the way it bit the binder in the Undercroft, and Pell-26 says something that isn't polite at all and the dart goes into the fan housing with a tink. And I'm past him, along the gallery, round the curve of the glass, to where the net is tied off.

An iron cleat bolted to the rail, and the net's main rope round it in a knot as big as my head, wound and wound and set hard with wet. I can feel the drag through the rail: the long faint creak, all those empty nests pushing up from underneath.

"Biscuit."

Biscuit likes anything that holds.

It goes up the rope like a thrown stocking, throat lit amber, and chews through it like a pear. One strand goes, thung, like a harp string. Two. Three.

The fourth goes on its own.

And the rope jumps off the cleat and whips away over the rail into the cloud, and below us, a long way down in the white, there's a sound like a great sheet being pulled off a bed. A huge soft rushing tear, going away and away through the crowns, from tree to tree, from tower to tower, as the whole grey ceiling sags and slides and falls off the forest into the dark.

And then, under the cloud, everywhere at once, the flock pipes.

* * *

They come up through the white like bubbles in a pan.

First one, rising past the gallery so close I could touch it, the cloud coming off its back in threads. Then twenty. Then all of them, every one I ever chewed seed for and every one they've had since, rising into the stars, piping, and the babies blundering up beside their mothers. And the bread.

The bread.

It comes up off them all at once, warm, into the freezing clean air, and the whole top of the High Gardens smells like the bakehouse at six on a winter morning with the frost on the glass. I stand on the gallery with my hands on the rail and laugh. My voice is wrong. It's still laughing.

Pell-26 comes round the curve of the glass with the pistol up, limping. And a grazer rises up through the gap between the gallery and the glass, slow, enormous, all goat-weight and float and surprise, and takes him with it. Just lifts him. His feet go off the boards. He's sitting on a grazer's back with his ledger in both hands and his pin catching the starlight, going up into the night, saying, "This is— I must— This is irregular—"

The grazer reaches the top of its rise, considers, and drops him on the roof of the glass room from about the height of a hay cart.

Item two thousand and six, I think, and I'm not even sorry.

* * *

Inside the glass, Odile moves.

I've seen her move before, at Highwater. You don't see the middle of it. There's a Pell by the door, standing, and then there's a Pell by the door, lying down, and Odile's got his wand in her hand, and she's done something to the door, and it's open, and the cold's rushing in, and she says one word over her shoulder, and Sedge is up off the floor and out on the gallery beside me before I've finished turning round.

She stops.

She's looking at my face in the light from the glass room. And what comes off her isn't a struck match. Colder. A wire pulled so tight it's singing.

"Later," I say, in the voice that isn't mine. "Two each. The bread ones. Hold the strong ones."

Odile comes out of the door behind her. She looks at me once, the way she looks at a drawer that doesn't match its label. Then at the flock, hanging in the stars all round the tower, piping.

"I am not—" she says.

"Two each."

The nicked-wing grazer comes down to the rail with its baby tucked under its side. Behind it come the strongest, the high riders, the ones that smell most like home.

Odile takes a breath. Then she takes hold of two grazers by the loose skin at the base of their necks, very firmly, as if they're a form she's about to file, and steps off the gallery into nothing.

Sedge goes after her with a yell like a gull.

Biscuit pours down my collar. I take the nicked-wing one and its neighbour, and look back once.

Up on the roof of the glass room, Pell-26 is sitting up, still writing. And past him, on a terrace at the very top of the tower, in the gold light from a door, a man in a long coat is standing. He's not shouting. He's just watching us go. And his coat, while I look, goes slowly from gold to the silver-white of the cloud, as if it's trying to match where we've gone.

He doesn't raise his hand.

I step off.

* * *

We go down into the cloud with the whole flock round us.

The stars go out, and my eyes turn the white to silver. The grazers sink slow, heavy with us, floats tight, piping to each other the whole way down like a hymn in the next room. The crowns go past, and the empty nests rising free on their moss ropes like boats when the tide comes in.

A waterfall comes out of the white beside us, falling and falling, and we sink beside it and it turns to mist on my face before it ever gets anywhere.

Left, I hear Sedge shout. Bram, it's left—

"It's right," I say. "Sound lies up here. Smell."

And I smell for it. Down there, under everything, through the falling cold air: wet stone, lichen, a mite or two, and a leaf. Green and new and unlikely, the smell of the joint between a seventh and an eighth stone.

We come out of the bottom of the cloud like coming out of a sack, into the ordinary dark, and the scree and the moss are right there under us, and Hask's right there on the moss, with his top stone tipped back, looking up.

The grazers let us down slow. My feet touch. My knees go.

Somewhere above, in the white, a dart pistol goes tik. Tik.

Hask comes down over the three of us like a roof falling in slow, stone by stone, with a sound like the meeting-house door closing. It's dark under him and smells of lichen and old rain. Something bounces off his top stone. Then something else. Then nothing.

"I have checked," says Hask, over our heads, "and only one of you is my bearer." A pause. "I have decided not to mind."

* * *

There's a pool at the bottom of the waterfall.

A lower one, that does land, in a black round pool in the ferns. I don't remember getting there. I remember Sedge's hand on my arm, and being so hungry I ate moss, a fistful, and it tasted of nothing but wet.

The grazers settle round us on the slope, folding their legs. The babies sleep in a heap. The sky's going grey behind the ridge, and the towers' lights are going out one by one.

I kneel at the edge of the pool to drink.

And the water's still, and it's getting light, and my eyes still see in silver, and there's a face.

* * *

When I was small, Gran washed my face at the pump every morning before school. Cold water, and her thumb, and no nonsense. She'd hold me by the chin to keep me still. Her fingers smelled of woodsmoke and old wool.

"That's a Hale chin," she said, one morning, holding it. Turning my face to the light like a jug she was thinking of buying. "That's your da's. That's mine. My mother's before me. Stubborn as a gatepost." She gave it a little shake. "You'll not lose that."

* * *

I look for it.

The face in the water is long, and narrow, and wrong. The cheekbones have come up high and sharp, like Davin's. The skin's gone pale and mottled, grey and green, like the moss on the trunks. The eyes are too big and there's a shine at the back of them, silver, like a knee-deer's at the pond. The mouth's the right size. The nose isn't.

The chin is narrow. Pointed. Somebody else's.

I touch it. In the water, a long grey hand with fingers a knuckle too long comes up and touches a stranger.

I'm sorry, says the voice behind my eyes. Very small. You never named it. You never needed to. It was just there, like a floor. When I let go, it went where the new names pulled it.

I don't say anything. I can't.

Behind me, Sedge says, "Bram?"

I turn round.

She's standing a few steps up the bank with her arms wrapped round herself. Odile's beside her, and Hask behind, and Biscuit on Hask's ledge with its throat dark. The grey light's on all of them. And Sedge is looking at me the way you look at someone at the door at night when you were expecting someone else.

"Bram," she says again. Not quite a question. Then it is one. Very quiet. "Who are you?"

* * *

You say whose you are.

That's how you say it in Firstfield. Son of. Brother of. Of the house of. I said it to Davin. I said it on the bank of a dying pond in the Pale.

I open my mouth.

Bram, I think. Son of—

And the voices come, the way they come, when I need them.

They come wrong.

Hands first, says Ma, in Gran's voice. Look twice, says Jory. Bet you won't, says Saul. Name it and you can hold it, what did you learn today, count to five hundred, not dead, only nearly, breathe properly, all on top of each other, all in the wrong mouths, like a meeting house saying the hymn at different speeds. I reach for Ma and get Ommery. I reach for Gran and there's only the words, no face, no woodsmoke, nobody holding my chin.

They're all there. I can't sort them into people.

I can't sort them into mine.

"I don't know," I say, in a voice that isn't mine.

Sedge's face does something I never want to see again.

I shut my eyes.

* * *

And then, in the middle of it, a voice that doesn't belong.

It's not from the village. It comes up through the ground, through my knees, slow and very serious, as if it's thought about this for three hundred years.

This is not enumerated in the Third Schedule.

The meeting house goes quiet.

And another one. Dry. Tired. Patient, the way you're patient with a drawer that's stuck. I've never once heard her say it out loud. I've only watched her do it, in the Pale with her slate on her knee, at Highwater by the light of her own nails. I hear it now.

Write it down.

They don't push the others out. They come in among them, and the others make room. Ma. Gran. Saul and Jory and Master Aldous and Ommery and Tilly's terrible joke. And a gate-creak laugh off a landing saying Ada Hale's chin, and a man with a gutting knife saying breathe through your nose, and Sedge saying bet you won't to me first.

Not fewer voices. More.

I open my eyes.

Odile's kneeling in the ferns in front of me. I didn't hear her come down the bank. She's holding out her pad of blank paper forms, the ones that don't need charging, and a pencil. She doesn't say anything at all.

I take the pencil. My new fingers catch on it, and peel off, tk, and catch.

I write.

It's my writing. The hand's wrong, but the letters come out the way Master Aldous taught us with his watch ticking, the B with the bottom loop too big, the way he always told me off for.

Bram.

And under it, whose.

Wenna's. Ada's. Tilly's.

Saul's. Jory's. Master Aldous's. Dr. Ommery's.

Lenna's. Teasel's, he gave me a pear.

Hask's.

Odile's.

Sedge's.

I stop. Biscuit's watching me from Hask's ledge.

Biscuit's, I write, probably.

I read it all the way down, twice, the way Saul says, and the second time it's the same. It doesn't fit on the form. The last ones go up the side.

"That's whose," I say. It's still the wrong voice. "That's who."

Odile takes the form back. She reads it once, all the way down and up the side. Her face doesn't do anything. Then she folds it in half, and in half again, corner to corner, like a sheet off the line, and puts it in the inside pocket of her coat, the one over her heart, where she keeps the things that are evidence.

"Filed," she says.

* * *

The nicked-wing grazer comes down the slope.

It drifts down to the pool with its baby against its side, and hangs in front of me, and looks into my new face with its big surprised dark eyes for a long time.

I wait for it to pipe and go. Everything's been going.

It bumps its soft nose on my forehead.

And then it burps.

Right in my face. Enormous. Like the mill sluice opening in spring, warm, fresh bread, and the baby, not to be outdone, does a little one too, hup, and it's so exactly home and so exactly ridiculous that it gets out of me before I can stop it.

I laugh.

It comes up out of the bottom of me like it did on the lip of the ditch in the Pale, helpless, wheezing, with a hiccup in the middle and a stupid high bit at the end that Jory always said sounded like a donkey being surprised.

The voice is wrong. The laugh isn't.

Sedge makes a noise. Then she's down the bank and on her knees in front of me in the ferns with both her hands on my wrong face, holding it the way Gran held my chin at the pump, turning it to the light.

"There you are," she says. Her face is wet again. She doesn't seem to mind. "That's you. That stupid donkey bit. That's you."

Biscuit comes down off Hask's ledge and sniffs me, all over, slowly, my hands and my neck and my new chin. Its throat stays dark a long time.

Then it goes amber, and Biscuit climbs into my shirt and goes to sleep.

Hask makes the grinding sound in the ground. It goes on for quite a while.

"I have heard that laugh through the ground," he says, "since the orchards. Stone hears stone. It is the same."

* * *

The sun comes up behind the towers and lights the top of the cloud gold. Up in the crowns, the nests are rising on their moss ropes, and the grazers going up to them in twos and threes, piping.

I'm sitting on a rock by the pool with Odile's coat round me and a mouthful of chewed seed, which tastes, through the noise, of bread.

I can try to give it back, says the voice. Your face. Some of it. Later. If you name it.

"I don't know what it's called."

No, it says. Nobody ever does.

Sedge sits down on the rock next to me, so her shoulder's against mine. She looks at my face sideways for a while.

"It's horrible," she says.

"Thanks."

"I'll get used to it."

I look at her.

"Bet you won't," I say.

She knocks her shoulder into mine, hard enough to nearly put me in the pool. "Bet I will."

Out on the slope, the nicked-wing grazer burps again, softly, in its sleep.

I laugh. It's mine.


## Chapter Sixteen: The Better Sum

> THE UPPER HOUSE · HIGH GARDENS · A dinner for the Examiner. Roast white root of the Spill, brown butter · Clear broth of the Undercroft · Salt-plums of the north-west coast, with river trout · Bread of the Pale, sector 9 · Clover honey of the Hiveplains, set as a custard, with moonpear.
>
> — menu card, cream board, printed in grey; beneath it in ink, in a small upright hand: Every course is from a place you have cost me. I thought you should taste what you've been defending. L.V.
>

The skiff went up the ridge into the cloud and the world shut like a book.

For a long minute there was nothing in the windows but white, with enormous trees going past in it like people in a corridor. Sedge sat with her knees together and her right wrist pressed flat under her left hand, the way you'd hold down a bird.

"It's warm," she said. "In here. It shouldn't be warm."

"They heat the cabins."

"No. Her." She lifted her hand off her wrist and put it back. "She's never been up this high. She wants to look."

Then we came out of the top.

I have worked on a good many worlds and I had never seen a cloud from above at night. The full moon stood clear of the Pale behind us, and under it the cloud lay over the whole ridge as flat and white as milk in a dish, so still that the tallest crowns stuck up through it like black rocks out of a sea. And out of that sea, white and smooth and much bigger than they had looked from the grain, stood the towers: four of them, slender, slightly curved, like the bones of a hand somebody had buried up to the knuckles. At the top of the nearest a room of glass was lit gold, like a lantern set on a post.

Sedge put her face to the window and forgot to hold her wrist.

"You can see the stars to the bottom," she said. "Down to the white. There's nothing in the way."

"Never be the tallest thing on a hill," I said, mostly to myself.

"What?"

"A Survey cook. Be the second tallest, next to the tallest." I looked at the gold room. "He's built himself the tallest thing on the tallest hill on Moriel and put the dinner table in it."

"Is that bad?"

"It's lightning-shaped," I said, and she laughed, which was what I'd wanted.

* * *

There was a narrow gallery round the glass room, railed, with round fan housings breathing out warm air that smelled of cedar, and a door, and three Associates beside the door with their hands folded in front of them like ushers at a wedding. Company grey, silver hair combed flat, the small enamel numbers at their lapels: 15, 17, and 22.

"Examiner," said Pell-22. "Good evening. You have my launcher."

"It's evidence."

"Of what?"

"Of you," I said, and went in.

The glass room was warm and gold and smelled of cedar and cooked ink, the smell of every office that has ever kept a file on somebody. A long table with a white cloth, set for three; on every side, the sea of cloud.

And at the far end of the table, getting up, a man in a long coat.

I had built him in my head for days, the way you build a suspect, out of what he leaves behind, and I had built him taller. Lisle Varro was perhaps sixty, not much bigger than me, with grey hair cut close to a round skull and a plain, pleasant, considering face, the face of the clerk you'd trust with your mother's pension. His voice was the one off the disc: soft, pleased, unhurried. His hands were square and very clean, with a pen callus on the middle finger of the right, and when he came round the table to us he held them a little away from his sides, open, the way you approach a horse.

The coat was the thing. It was long and high-collared and plain in cut, and it was the colour of good honey, and as he looked at Sedge it went a shade warmer, the gold coming up through it like light through a held hand.

Sedge stared at it.

"It's a company line," said Varro, as if she'd asked. "Bred for beekeepers in the Hiveplains, originally, to read the skin and show the hive whether you were frightened. It reads mine." He smiled at her. "I've never been able to hide anything from it. I stopped trying in my thirties. It's been very good for my negotiating."

"Who wrote you?" said Sedge.

He didn't miss a beat. He'd read every file I'd filed, which meant he had read three hundred and three answers to that question in Highwater's own words.

"My mother," said Varro. "And twenty years of the Moriel Bioreference Index. Will you sit down? There's pudding."

Sedge looked at me. I could see her decide that a man who knew about the pudding was more dangerous than she'd planned for.

She sat down.

* * *

He fed us our route.

That was the joke of the menu, and he watched us find it out course by course with the coat going faintly gold at the cuffs. The fat white roots of the Spill in brown butter, tasting of wet bread and pennies. A broth as clear as window glass that tasted of cold stone and the Undercroft's dark, so that my nails, under the cloth, glowed a little in sympathy. Salt-plums and trout. Then the bread.

Sedge had eaten everything as if someone might come and take it. She stopped at the bread.

It was a good loaf to look at, brown, the crust split, from Pale grain, sector 9, where the harvesters had gone into the ditch. She broke it. She smelled it. Her right wrist, on the white cloth, came up faintly green-gold, like a lamp turned a quarter-turn.

Varro saw it. His hands went still on the cloth.

"I'll come to that," I said.

"Please do," said Varro. "I've been waiting all evening." He poured himself water. I had my flask beside my plate, and when I unscrewed the cap the smell of tar and woodsmoke and burnt raisins went down the table and met the cedar coming the other way, and he breathed it in with an odd, appreciative expression, like a man smelling a bonfire from a train.

"Tell me about the grazers," he said instead. "My drones lose them in the cloud."

So Sedge told him, because he asked her and not me: the boy feeding seed mash into a downed grazer's mouth like a mother bird, and the burp. His face did nothing at all while she told it. His coat went the colour of a fire's heart.

"Thirty-one roof claims," he said, when she'd done. "Do you know how the farms file them? I looked it up this morning. Damage by falling livestock. The Heritage Service pays out on the grazers as if somebody owned them." He turned his glass a little on the cloth. "Nobody does. They're the only thing on this ridge I haven't got a claim on. I find I rather like them for it."

"Livestock," said Sedge, with deep scorn.

"Legally," said Varro. "On a roof."

* * *

He waited until the plates had gone. A Pell took them, without a word, and stood back by the door with the others.

Then Varro put his clean square hands flat on the white cloth either side of the place where his plate had been, and looked at me, and the coat cooled, slowly, from gold to the dull dark of old brass.

"You think I'm a villain because I put a number on a child," he said. "Fine. Let me put the number on the table for you, because someone has to.

"Nine million, one hundred and six thousand, four hundred and twelve. That was this morning's count of engineered lives on this planet: grain and bees and trees and fish and grazers and the people who were made to work among them. None of them asked to be made. Every one of them is drifting, a little each generation, a copy of a copy, because nothing living holds still without a reference to hold it to. I have watched it happen. My mother kept bees in the Hiveplains, line twenty. When I was nine I watched them forget what the flowers smelled like. You've seen what that looks like now. I saw it start."

His hands didn't move.

"Your village, sweet as it is, is the reference. Eleven young people in eight generations, a twelfth this year, and in return I can stop the drift for everybody else. You will tell me the old way was a covenant and that it was honoured. I'll tell you it was honoured with a plaque. I'm not asking you to like the sum. I'm asking you to say which of the nine million you will tell, to their faces, that the calibration will not be done because you could not bear the arithmetic."

The fans hummed. Somewhere below us in the white, too faint to be sure of, a grazer piped.

"If you have a better sum," said Lisle Varro, "I'll fund it. If you have only a better feeling, you have not answered me."

He hadn't raised his voice once. And I sat there with the taste of the broth still in my mouth and found that my hands, in my lap, had gone cold.

Not because he was wrong. Because I had heard it before.

Someone has to put the number on the table. A different table. A tired man at the end of a long day, saying it kindly. It went through me the way cold water goes through a dry field, straight down to the old low place, and I felt everything else I knew start to run that way after it.

Fallow taught me to sit down first. I was sitting. I put my hand on the sailcloth bundle beside my chair and held on to it until the cold stopped.

* * *

"I'll answer you in arithmetic," I said. "Since you ask."

I unwrapped the survey on the white cloth. Wheat-coloured boards; the title pressed into them; the inky thumb on the first page. I turned it round so that it faced him and opened it to the preface, and I didn't read it to him. I'd learned that from him, in a way, off a grey disc on a doorstep: you let a good sentence do its own work.

He read it. Let no child of ours ever be kept for a copy. I watched his eyes go back to the start and read it again.

"Your sum has two terms," I said. "The drift, and the reference. The drift's real. I've walked it for twenty-two days and I won't insult you by pretending otherwise. Your mistake is the second term. You've assumed the reference has to be a living child, because a living child is the cheapest clean copy you could find. It isn't the only one. It was never meant to be any. And the tank in your annex doesn't change the sum, Mr Varro. It only takes the asking out of it."

I turned to the maps. Seven ponds across the face of the world, each with its reeds leaning away from a little inked sun.

"The founders built seven archives. In each lives a partner: a Librarian, they called it, built to hold the first copy of everything they brought. The grain. The crock. Your mother's bees and the flowers they forgot. The village was only ever meant to remember the old ways by choosing them. Not to be the copy." I put my finger on the bearings. "Your Register filed the ponds as lapsed wetlands, because nobody went to read them any more. This morning I joined its seven to her seven. They match to a stride. It's in my Chief Examiner's tray. The reinstatement's pending."

"A book," said Varro gently, "is a lovely thing. I have a great many."

"Yes," I said. "So I brought the pond."

Sedge, who had promised me on a rise in the Pale that she would say what she said at dawn and nothing else, and who had never once done such a thing in her life, put the broken bread down on the cloth in front of him.

"It's tired," she said.

He looked at the bread. Then at her lit wrist.

"Right at the bottom," said Sedge. "The bottom page. All the bread on this table's gone tired at the bottom, like the grazers' bellies were, before Bram." She frowned at it, listening to something none of us could hear. "She's got the first one. The first crock. She says it's warm." She looked up at him with the frank contempt of the young for an adult who has missed something obvious. "It's in a pond. It's been in a pond for two hundred years. You could've just asked."

The coat went a colour I hadn't got a name for. Something between slate and the blue at the bottom of a flame.

"What did it cost you?" he asked her. Not me. "Saying yes."

"It was freezing," said Sedge. "And I missed breakfast. And I'm not on my own any more." She considered. "Neither's she."

He was quiet for a long time. When he spoke again it was to me, and it was the voice of a man going down a column with his finger.

"Seven partners. You have one. Where do the other six come from?"

"From six people saying yes."

"And if they don't?"

"Then you'll have to ask nicely. You found eleven children in eight generations without asking any of them. I expect you can find six who'll be asked."

"Calibration from a pond is slower than a draw."

"Yes."

"How much slower?"

"I don't know yet. Slower. You get to keep the children."

"The drift doesn't wait for your yet, Examiner."

"No. It waited two hundred years for somebody to read the first page." I put my hand flat on the open book, beside his. "You asked which of the nine million I'll tell, to their faces. I'll tell all of them. I'll tell them the founders did the sum before any of us were born and wrote the answer in the front of the book. And signed it." I turned the page back to the preface, to Maud Ferrier, surveyor, for the Library. Witnessed by all at the Landing. "That isn't a feeling, Mr Varro. That's a better sum. Signed. You said you'd fund it."

He looked at the signature for a long time. His hands lay either side of the book, and the pen callus on the right one rubbed, once, slowly, against his thumb.

Then he laughed. It was small and dry and entirely unhappy.

"I did say that," said Lisle Varro. "I put it on a disc. I'm told I keep my word." The coat had gone to an ash grey with a faint warmth somewhere deep in it, like a banked fire. "It would be very convenient for me just now not to."

He held out his hand, palm up.

I took a blank from the pad in my inside pocket, the paper kind that doesn't need charging, and wrote it for him plainly, with the time, the way Fallow taught me to write anything that might one day be argued over. I undertake to fund the waking of the seven ponds of the Library of Moriel, with willing partners, in place of the draw. I turned it round and laid my pencil on it.

He read it twice. He changed nothing. He signed it, L. Varro, small and upright, and pushed it back across the cloth with one finger as if it had bitten him.

"There," he said. "You've cost me something I can learn from. I hope you're pleased."

"Not yet," I said. "Ask me when it's done."

I folded the undertaking into the pocket over my heart, with Sedge's yes.

* * *

The chime came while the Pells were bringing in the pudding.

Two notes, from a grey disc set into the cloth by Varro's right hand, and the pudding stopped in the doorway on its silver tray: a pale custard trembling in a dish, with slices of moonpear round it glowing faintly in the shadow of the rim. Sedge's whole face changed at the sight of it.

Varro touched the disc and read what came up in it. I watched his hands, because I had told Sedge to.

They didn't move at all.

The coat went white. Not silver, not cloud: the flat chalk white of a wall, from the collar down, as if something had been drained out of it.

"Well," he said. He read it aloud, in the same soft voice, as if it were a menu. "Board of Varro-Tandem Bioreference. Resolution, nine fifty-two pm. The executive's undertaking of this evening exceeds his delegated authority and is set aside. Recovery of materials to proceed: archival reader, juvenile, with contents. The Examiner to be detained as a witness to the recovery pending counsel." He put the disc face down. "I have waited twenty years for someone to cost me something. They waited eleven minutes."

"They can't set aside your signature," I said. "Only your authority."

"Yes. You'll find that's a distinction they've been paying lawyers to ignore since before you were born." He stood. He took the survey off the cloth, closed it, wrapped it in its sailcloth with his clean hands, and gave it back to me. "This is yours. Evidence, I believe."

The Pells had come away from the door.

Sedge was on her feet. The pudding stood in the doorway on its tray, abandoned, wobbling gently.

"I gave you my word," said Varro, "that the boy and the Proxy would be safe below the cloud. That one's still mine. I'll keep it." He looked at Sedge, and at her wrist, and something went across his face that his coat didn't have time to show. "I'm sorry, my dear. It isn't personal."

"That's theirs," said Sedge furiously, with her chin at the Pells. "Get your own."

He went out through the inner door and up a stair, towards the terrace above us, and didn't look back.

Pell-17 reached for Sedge's right wrist.

She had two fingers in her mouth before he got there, and the whistle went out of her, Highwater's whistle, the one for the grey boat, long-short-long, so high and hard it rang off the glass on every side and went out into the night through the fan housings and came back off the other towers from four directions at once.

Pell-17 took her wrist. The whistle stopped halfway.

And I stood there with the survey in my arms and three Associates between me and the door, and I was so angry I could taste it, like a coin under the tongue.

* * *

They were very polite about it. They always are.

They took Sedge's knife and logged it. They took my warrant and gave it back again, because Pell-22 read it twice and could find nothing in it that said they had to stand aside from any of the doors it opened. My slate said NO RELAY: the towers were a private station above the cloud, and the Register, which reaches every drawer on Moriel, did not reach the room I was standing in. They left me my flask. Pell-15 sniffed it and decided it was a beverage.

"Transfer is at first light," said Pell-22. "Level nine. The young lady will be quite comfortable. The draw-room chairs are very good."

Sedge sat down on the floor with her back against the glass and her knees up, and began, silently, to cry with rage.

I looked at the door to the gallery. Glass, in a white frame, with a small dark plate beside it at hand height and a hum under it: a magnetic latch, the kind that holds as long as the current does. I'd fitted a hundred of them on Survey stores. Every one of them is built to fail open, because a door that stays locked in a fire is a coffin, and even companies have to sign the fire code.

Two hundred volts would do it. Or a short. A compliance wand has a brass cap.

I filed that.

Then I looked at three Associates, the door, the drop beyond the rail, the girl on the floor, the pudding where somebody had finally set it down on the end of the table, and I did the sum, and every move I had ended with me on the floor and Sedge on level nine. My hands were still cold. And under the cold, under all of it, the old low place in me was still filling, someone has to, Pastern, the whole evening running downhill into it the way rain finds a ditch.

You can't do a sum with a head like that. You can't see the board.

I pulled out a chair from the table, and turned it round, and sat down on it, slowly, the way you sit on any chair you don't know.

"What are you doing?" said Sedge, through her teeth.

"Sitting down first," I said, and shut my eyes.

* * *

I had never done the Reading on myself. You aren't supposed to. The Manual has a line about it, in the chapter nobody reads, to the effect that an Examiner who turns the discipline on her own recollection will find no drawers there, only weather, and is advised to stop.

I went in by the oak door anyway, because it's the only door I have. Iron-bound. Beeswax and cold stone. My palm on it.

There was no hall behind it.

There was a moor.

Open ground under a low sky, grey-green to every horizon, smelling of every first morning on every world I've surveyed: wet earth, crushed stems, somebody's cook fire. And crossed with paths. Thousands, from a faint parting a fox might have made once to tracks worn to bare earth by years of feet.

I knew what they were before I'd taken a step. Fallow had said it to me once in the back office, years ago, over a pasty, and I hadn't understood him: Records are drawers, Pastern. Heads aren't. In a head, the thing you remember is the way you went to it.

A path is what's left where something has been walked. Walk it twice and it's easier the third time. Walk two together often enough and they wear into one. Nobody decides any of it. The feet decide.

Here was the tea: a short, bright, scalding track, and running hard beside it, worn into the same rut, Big Imre's coffee pot going end over end into the dark. Here was chair first, which went everywhere; half the paths on the moor had a stretch of it in them somewhere. Here was a thin new track, the grass only bruised, from a red-stemmed seedling to a notched stick to a reed with eight knots.

And then I felt the ground tilt.

Everything on that moor ran one way. Not the paths: the water. It had started to rain in the Reading, a fine grey drizzle, and every drop that fell ran off the grass into the nearest path, and every path ran downhill into the next, and all of them, every one, ended up going the same way, down and down, toward the low place in the middle of the moor that I had felt open at the dinner table.

I walked down to it, because that is what you do with a Discrepancy.

It wasn't a path any more. It was a gully. A sunken lane worn shoulder-deep into the moor, steep-sided, raw at the edges, with water running in the bottom of it, brown, from every direction at once. You couldn't stand on that moor and not feel it pulling. Varro's voice ran into it. So did Halloran's, I've read your file. So did a kind, tired surveyor on a brown river world signing a terrace sound. So did every closed case I had ever looked at sideways. So did my own name, said by anyone, in a certain tone.

At the bottom of the gully stood a room. A plain office at the end of a long day, the lamp lit, the rain on the window. A desk. A file on the desk, mine, thick, and across the front of it in another hand, No discrepancy. And Fallow, getting up from the desk on his bad knees, who looked at me with great kindness and great tiredness and said one word.

Pastern.

That's all. That's the shape of it. A report I was right about; a teacher I trusted, who signed it closed over me. In the third spring the lower town flooded, and nobody drowned, because the settlers had read their own trees and moved; and four hundred families lost their homes. I asked for the hardest posting there was. I have not spoken to him since. He taught me everything I was using to stand there.

I knew the room very well. I had been coming here for seven years.

What I had not seen, until I stood in it with the Reading on, was the ledger.

It lay open on the windowsill, a fat account book, every page in my hand. Day such-and-such: he didn't look at me when he said it. Day such-and-such: he'd have known about the river. Day such-and-such: Halloran said Pastern in the same voice, and it was the same. Each time I had come down the gully, I had added a line. The principal was a few hours on a wet evening seven years ago. The rest was interest, compounding, and the gully was as deep as it was because I'd been digging it.

The rain came down. The water ran past my boots, brown, carrying everything on the moor with it.

* * *

This part Fallow didn't teach me. I had it later out of a book, the way you learn anything after the person who should have told you is no longer someone you speak to. When you walk a memory, really walk it, with your eyes open, it goes soft under you for a while, the way a cut bank does in the wet. While it's soft, it can be laid differently. Then it sets again, and what sets is what you walked last.

On the Survey we re-cut ditches in the wet, never the dry. In the dry the bank's like iron and you'll break your spade. You wait for the rain, when the water's already moving, and you open a new channel with one stroke, and the water does the rest.

I was standing in the rain, at the bottom of my own ditch, with the bank going soft.

I didn't fill it in. You can't; I'd have had to take out every path that ran through it, and every one of them was something he had given me. Chair first. Nobody runs from a man eating lunch. Paper doesn't need charging. The oak door I had come in by. The moor itself.

So I stood still and let the room be what it was. A tired man, wrong. Not a wicked one. The lamp, the rain, the file. Four hundred families, which was true, and which I would carry, and which would never be smaller than it was. And nobody drowned, which was also true, and which I had stopped putting in when I told it.

And from somewhere up on the moor, not loud, in the wrong place entirely, a voice came down the gully on the wind. Hoarse, a village voice, a boy's, with a cough kept in its pocket: on the bank of the Fifth Pond that dawn, with his arm round a crying girl, a whole life ago.

It doesn't go. But it gets to be yours. Then it's not so much.

I went to the windowsill.

I didn't close the account. I'm not there yet, and I won't pretend I am, not even on paper, not even to myself in the Reading, where paper weighs what it should. I took the pencil and I wrote one more line at the foot of the last page, plainly, with the time, the way he taught me:

Day 22, late. Interest: none further. O.P.

Then I walked back up the gully, slowly, the whole length of it, so that the last thing set in that soft ground was me walking up and not down.

At the top I looked back. It was still there. It will always be there. But the rain was running a little differently on the moor already, finding its own ways across, some of it down that new bright track toward the seedlings and the knotted reed, some of it toward a doorstep with a brass disc in it, some of it nowhere in particular, the way rain does on open ground when nothing is pulling it.

I left by the door I'd come in by. My palm stuck to it with the cold.

* * *

When I opened my eyes I was shaking so hard the chair legs tapped on the floor, and I had a headache like a drawer slammed on my fingers, and I was hungrier than I had been since the Pale. The moon had moved a hand's width over the cloud.

Sedge was staring at me.

"You were gone," she said. "Your nails were going. You said something."

"What did I say?"

"Interest." She wiped her nose on her borrowed sleeve. "I thought you'd gone mad from the dinner."

There was a basket of the Pale bread still on the table. Tired at the bottom, every loaf. I ate two rolls, one after the other, and drank a capful of tea so hot it took the roof off my mouth, and then a third roll, and the cold went out of my hands.

The pudding sat at the end of the table, untouched, a skin forming on it. Sedge looked at it with an expression of deep personal betrayal.

"Don't," I said. "It's the only thing in here they haven't logged."

Then I stood up and went down the room to the door, where the three of them stood, because the board was suddenly very clear, and I needed all three of them looking at me.

I put one hand flat on the end of the long table and talked to him about the law, which I can do for a very long time. Pell-22 answered every point courteously and wrote it all down; the other two listened with the glaze of men who have been in a great many meetings. I wasn't trying to win. I was trying to be the most interesting thing in the room.

Behind me, very faintly, something tapped on the glass. Tk.

I didn't turn round. I watched Sedge, in the dark of the window over Pell-22's shoulder, because the glass gives you back the room at night if you know how to look.

She turned her head. And her face, which had been wet and furious for an hour, opened into a grin.

* * *

I let my eyes go to the reflection, slowly, the way you look at a bird you don't want to frighten.

Down at the foot of the glass, by Sedge's shoulder, where the gallery floor met the edge of the dark, a pair of hands held on to the edge. Long hands. Grey, the pads dull and ridged. And between them, coming up out of the cloud, a face with eyes that caught the gold of the room and gave it back silver, like a beast's at the edge of a fire.

It took me a heartbeat to know him. I knew the way he held his head.

Then a pair of polished company shoes came round the curve of the gallery above him and stopped. A hand with a grey pistol in it reached down. I saw the boy's face jerk, once, and go slack.

I turned round. I couldn't help it.

He was hanging there. That's what I remember. Everything in him had gone soft at once, arms and shoulders and neck, his head tipped back, his legs swinging out over the drop, and his hands had not let go. They stayed on the gallery's edge, flat and grey and caught, as if they belonged to someone else who was still awake.

His chest didn't move.

I counted. It is a Survey habit, and I'd have done it anyway.

At forty, Pell-15 had a hand on my arm. At ninety, the Pell on the gallery crouched with his ledger on his knee and began, with great interest, to write. Sedge had her palm flat on the glass and was saying his name without any sound. Two hundred. Two hundred and forty. I had stopped breathing myself.

At two hundred and sixty, a hand's width from Sedge's shoulder, Bram's face began to change. The cheekbones lifted, slowly, like something rising under a sheet. The skin greyed and went mottled, green over grey, the colour of the moss on the trunks below. The jaw narrowed. The chin, the square stubborn chin he'd come out of the orchards with, drew in to a point, the way wax goes when you hold it near a flame, until it was nobody's chin I had ever seen.

Then his chest heaved.

He hauled in air like a man come up from the bottom of a pond, and his head came up, and he looked straight through the glass at the room with a stranger's face and his own expression on it.

The Pell on the gallery said something to him. He said something back. I couldn't hear either through the glass, but I saw the Pell stop writing, and look at him, and not write it down.

Then the boy came up over the rail in one movement, the way Sedge goes into a tree house, and something small and brown came out of his shirt like a cork, and they were both gone round the curve of the glass.

* * *

First, below us, a long way down in the white, a sound like a great sheet being pulled off a bed: a huge soft tearing that went away and away through the forest from tree to tree. Pell-17 went to the glass and looked down and said, "Oh dear."

Then the cloud began to give up grazers: one, a dozen, more than I could count, rising round the tower like bubbles in a pan with the white coming off their backs in threads, piping soft as owls, the babies blundering up beside their mothers. The glass room was suddenly a lantern in a flock. And even through the glass, even over the cedar, I could smell it: warm, yeasty, ridiculous. The whole top of the High Gardens smelled of a bakehouse at dawn.

Then something came up very close past the window, enormous and slow, with a man sitting on its back.

He had his ledger in both hands. His pin said 26. His mouth was open. He went up past the gold glass with an expression of profound administrative concern, and out of sight above us, and there was a pause in which all three Pells in the room and Sedge and I looked at the ceiling.

Then the Pell came down on the glass roof from about the height of a hay cart, and lay there, flat, directly above the dinner table, looking down at us through the glass with his nose pressed sideways.

After a moment he sat up, opened his ledger, and began to write.

"That," said Pell-15, in a voice of real outrage, "is Twenty-six."

He and Pell-17 went for the inner door and the stair to the terrace at a run. Pell-22 turned his face up to the roof, to see what his colleague was writing about him.

He had turned his face up for slightly under a second. That's plenty.

There was a Survey cook on an ice world in my fourth season who had once been something else, and she had taught me how to put a large man down without hurting either of us much: his weight, his own knees, the floor. Pell-22 went down on the dinner-room carpet very neatly, which I think he would have appreciated, and his compliance wand came away in my hand.

I turned it, put the brass cap against the dark plate by the gallery door, and pressed the stud.

There was a crack and a smell of hot metal, and the hum under the plate stopped, and the door, as the fire code requires of every door on every world, fell open.

The cold came in like a slap, and the smell of bread with it, and the sound of the flock: hundreds of them, piping, all round us in the stars.
