Biscuit, who had been living in the heating ducts since we arrived and coming out only for meals and outrage, learned the sound of that lid in a single evening.

Halloran gave Bram a box of dead files from the bottom drawer of the record office, cases nobody had wanted for forty years. On the seventh day Bram gave two of them back with a note on each, in his big careful hand with the too-big B. Halloran read them standing up in the corridor, with his coat still on. Then he took the box away and gave him a heavier one.

In the back of Master Aldous's green book, on the blank pages after the poem, Bram had started a list. I saw it once, open on the galley table, and didn't read it, but the heading was at the top in capitals and I couldn't help that. THINGS TO TELL THIRTEEN. The first one was the joke.

The relay from Moriel came on the sixth evening. Three flat notes, like bacon frying. Bram held my slate in both hands as if it might spill.

"Did you tell it?" said Tilly Hale, from a whole fold away, before he could speak. "Did you say it's mine?"

"I told the Chief Examiner. He laughed."

A pause the length of a planet.

"Properly?"

"He said it was very bad."

"It is very bad," said Tilly, in a voice of pure triumph, and then, behind her, faint, crackling like paper in a stove, another voice said, Tell him to take a coat, and Bram laughed so hard he had to sit down on the galley floor.

When the relay closed he sat on for a while with the slate dark in his lap, turning the brown cord on his wrist round and round with one finger. He didn't say anything about it. I didn't ask.

And on the second day, before any of that, he'd gone up to ring three through the fog, and come back wet to the knees and smelling of cress, with a fistful of grit from the farm-deck beds. He took the cup I don't use. I have two. I'd never had a reason for the second one. He put the grit in it, and pressed one of Saul's seeds into it, first knuckle deep and not a hair more, and watered it, against the fog advisory, and set it on the ledge of the viewport at the end of our corridor, under the day-strip, where the light was best.

Then he stood back and looked at it.

"Somewhere that's never heard of a tomato," he said. "Then look twice."

"Who says that?"

"Saul." He looked out past the cup, at the dark. "And me, now, I suppose."

* * *

On the eighth evening I walked home from ring one the short way.

You'll want to know why that matters, and there isn't much to tell. Ring two is a circle. My door is a little spinward of the viewport. For seven years I had gone home the long way round, anti-spinward, past the laundries and the school and a chapel nobody uses, which adds twenty minutes to the walk and is very good for the legs. I'd stopped noticing I did it. That's how a path wears in.

That evening I turned left out of the tram instead of right.

Hask came with me, a stone's length behind, as he always is. He did not remark on it. He is very good at not remarking on things, for a mind that has never in three hundred and eleven years been able to stop itself enumerating them.

The door was where it had always been. A residential door like all the others, grey laminate, a number, and a card in a little brass holder at head height, written in a square, upright hand I'd have known anywhere, because I'd learned to sit down first by watching it fill in forms.

FALLOW.

There was a smell coming from under it. Onion, fried slow in butter, and pastry browning. And a sound: some programme with a great deal of music in it, which I would bet my flask was the second episode of the three hundred and forty-first season, and over it, close to the door, the creak of a chair taking a man's weight, and the slow, careful, two-handed shove of someone getting up from a table with bad knees.

I stopped.

I put my hand flat on the door. Not oak. It wasn't warm and it wasn't cold. It was a door.

There's a gully in my head that everything drains toward, and I felt the water start to run, the way it does. I let it. And this time other things came down it too. A woman with a chest like gravel in a pail, standing up in a meeting house and taking a doctor's hand off a stack of notebooks: It'll want doing again tomorrow. I'll do it again tomorrow. A small brown woman, gone the colour of a shawl: you great fool. A boy on the bank of a pond in the Pale, before any of it, his voice still his own: It doesn't go. But it gets to be yours. And the man behind this door, years before he was the man behind this door, on his bad knees on a receiving-office floor, holding up a finger grey with dust.

Chair first, Pastern.

Inside, the chair creaked again. The music swelled. Somebody, on screen, was being told something very important in a corridor.

I didn't knock.

I stood there long enough for the onions to catch a little, and somebody inside to say a word I wasn't meant to hear and get up again to see to them. Then I took my hand off the door.

"Shall I file?" said Hask, behind me, very quietly.

I thought of a kitchen under a dome, the first morning, and a bowl set out clean and empty in front of a chair, and the same two words.

"Not yet," I said.

I took out my ledger and wrote it down, under the line from the High Gardens. Day 35. The short way round. O.P. Then I put the ledger back in my coat, and walked on, spinward, the way I'd come, toward the viewport and the boy and the noise.

* * *

He was at the glass.

Viewport 9 is the big one at the end of our corridor: a long, curved panel taller than Hask, with a bench in front of it that nobody sits on and the enamel placard on the frame that nobody reads. Through it, the whole of the dark. Not black. I had thought it was black for eleven years. It's every colour of black there is, and in it the stars, hard and steady and unblinking, and the long pale smear of the rest of them tipped across the middle like flour on a table. The ships came and went at the docking ring below us, small and lit, the way bees come and go at a hive mouth.

Bram had his nose about a finger's width from the glass, and his breath held, and one hand raised. He was waving.

Outside, on the far side of the panel, a hull drone was coming round the ring. A fat silver thing on suction feet, the size of a cart wheel, cleaning the dust of a thousand years of nothing off the viewports one by one, with a little lamp on its front.

"It's the same make," Bram said, without turning round. "Near enough. Different feet."

"As what?"

"As the one in the dome." He waved again. The drone, which had no idea it was being waved at, went on cleaning. "Two hours and six minutes round the ring. I timed it. It docks at a hatch by the farm-deck lock for three minutes and empties its bag."

"Are you planning to leave through it?"

"No," said Bram. "I just like to know where the doors are."

On the ledge by his elbow, under the day-strip, the cup. I'd been watching it for a week, the way you watch a pot. That morning it had nothing in it. Now, out of the grit, a red hook of stem had come up, bent double like an old man tying his boot, with the two seed-leaves still folded together at the end of it, pale, in a scrap of husk.

Small. Red-stemmed. Just sitting there.

I thought of seven of them by a pond on another world, with a notched stick beside them, the first evidence I ever had that he was the kind of boy who stops.

Behind him, on the bench nobody sits on, the dinner argument had been going for some time.

"The Weighbridge," Halloran was saying, with the lighthouse tin on one knee and LUGGAGE on the other, and Biscuit's whole front end inside LUGGAGE. "Pie. It's the eighth night. I've had the pie on the eighth night for thirty-one years."

"I concur," said Hask, "with the Weighbridge."

"You don't eat," I said.

"I do not eat. The Weighbridge has the large screen. Doctor Vell has been accused." He considered. "I object only to the plate. It rings every time. I find I am tired of being a discrepancy in front of people who are eating pies."

"There's bread," said Bram, to the glass. "There's two loaves proving in the galley. They'll be ready at the eighth bell."

"Two?"

"The first one was Biscuit's," said Bram. "It didn't ask."

From inside LUGGAGE, Biscuit's throat glowed amber, unrepentant.

"The noodle stall on the spinward concourse," I said, and sat down at the other end of the bench, and unscrewed the flask. "The Lisk one. The broth."

"Item fourteen," said Hask at once. "The broth at the Lisk stall is served at a temperature I have been unable to find enumerated anywhere but in my own Schedule."

"Then it's enumerated."

"Under scalding. I have stood beside you while you drank it on three hundred and twelve occasions."

"And I've lived."

"You have lived," said Hask, "despite."

The tea was very hot. It always is. The steam smelled of tar and smoke and burnt raisins and the inside of a steel flask with four dents in it, and it took the skin off the roof of my mouth, and Hask said "Item fourteen" again, for the record, and nobody moved toward any dinner at all.

That was when the slate chimed.

The Register's double note, the one for a followed record. I knew before I looked. Halloran's face, over the tin, did nothing at all, very carefully.

"Came in this afternoon," he said. "Nobody else wanted it."

I opened it. And Bram, who had been standing at the glass with his back to me the whole time, was suddenly standing at my shoulder, reading it, his chin nearly on my collar, his breath smelling of crust.

"Nobody reads over my shoulder," I said.

"I'm reading over your shoulder."

"I know. It's very annoying."

INSPECTORATE OF DISCREPANCIES, MARGIN STATION. Assigned: Examiner O. Pastern. Consultant: B. Hale (living records). TARROW, the Vessany lanes. Harbour Board reports that the Old Customs House on Tarrow Quay (stone; three storeys; Listed; built 212 years ago) has moved forty-one metres down the quay since spring and now stands partly in the harbour. Register shows the Customs House as a building. Status: stationary.

And under it, in a harbourmaster's furious capitals, a note somebody had seen fit to forward:

IT HAS STARTED TAKING IN THE TIDE. ON TUESDAY IT SNEEZED.

Two true things that could not both be true. In my experience, that is where somebody is hiding something. Or where something is hiding.

"Buildings don't sneeze," said Bram.

"This one's filed as one."

"Is there a picture?"

There was a picture. A long grey building on a stone quay, slate-roofed, three rows of tall windows, with green water slapping round the foot of its seaward wall and a crowd of small dark birds sitting along its ridge as if they'd been told to wait. An ordinary building. A customs house. I've been through a hundred like it.

Bram looked at it for a long time. Then he put one long grey finger on the picture, very gently, the way he'd touch a seed.

"Look twice," he said.

I looked twice. Every window on the seaward side of the building was open. Every window on the landward side was shut.

"Nobody opens all the windows on one side of a building," said Bram.

I scrolled down. The harbourmaster again, a line further on, in calmer capitals, as if he'd had a cup of tea first: NOBODY HAS OPENED ANY WINDOWS.

"Then it's breathing," said Bram.

Behind us, Hask's stack ground, long and low, like a landslide considering a hillside. His top stone had tipped toward the slate.

"Stone hears stone," he said, slowly. "I should like to be introduced."

Halloran cracked a seed. Tock.

"Tomorrow," he said. "The Tarrow packet's at noon. Nine hours."

"Is there a fold?" said Bram.

"There's always a fold."

Bram's face did something complicated. Inside LUGGAGE, Biscuit went abruptly dark.

"Can we read it?" said Bram, to me. "All of it? The whole building?"

"One at a time," I said.

He looked at me very oddly then, and laughed, and I have no idea why, and he wouldn't tell me. I've written it down. It's an open question.

"Tomorrow," Halloran said again. "Tonight is dinner. The Weighbridge."

"Bread," said Bram.

"Noodles," I said.

"Item fourteen," said Hask.

And it started all over again, the way it had on Moriel and in the hop and on the bench every evening for a week, pie and bread and broth and the plate that rings, round and round, and none of us minded, and nobody got up.

Until Hask said, in a different voice:

"Item four thousand one hundred and twelve."

We stopped.

I had never heard him cite it. In eleven years I had heard him cite perhaps nine hundred items, on stairs and in queues and under falling livestock. I had never heard that number, and I knew why. It's the last one.

"Solitude of the bearer," said Hask, "where company is to be had."

Nobody said anything. On the other side of the glass, the drone went on cleaning.

"It is the last item in the Schedule," said Hask. "The notary added it in a different ink, in the year he died. I had always taken it for a flourish. He was alone a great deal, at the end." His top stone turned, slowly, the way it does when he is looking at you, though he has no eyes. "I have carried it for three hundred and eleven years. I have never been able to act under it. No bearer of mine has ever given me grounds." A pause like weather. "Until lately."

The tea had stopped being hot in my hands. I don't know when.

"It does not specify a place," said Hask. "Only that the company be had. I find I am content with any of them. I shall stand on the plate."

Halloran looked at the tin on his knee for a long moment. Then he put a seed in his mouth and didn't crack it.

"Well," he said. "Well."

Bram had gone back to the glass.

He stood with his toes against the bottom of the frame, in his da's coat, with the cuffs turned back twice, and the seedling at his elbow, and the brown cord on his wrist, and an otter's tail hanging out of a tin on a bench behind him, and he looked out at all of it: the black that wasn't black, and the stars like nail heads, and the ships going in and out of the ring like bees. And then, in a voice I knew from a skywalk, warm and pleasant and not dead at all, he began to narrate.

"Here we see the Baseline Human," said Bram, to the glass, "at the window."

Halloran snorted.

"Note," said Bram, "the single set of lungs."

"And the gills," said Halloran, not looking up.

"Note the gills." Bram turned his head a little, so that the slits under his ears caught the day-strip, and fluttered them, all six, on purpose. "Note the traditional wooden implements." He held up the pencil he stirs the crock with.

"Note the flower," said Hask. "Colour not enumerated."

"Note the otter," I said. "It's luggage."

From inside LUGGAGE, Biscuit sneezed.

"Note," said Bram, and stopped, and I saw him look at his own reflection in the glass, laid over the stars, and over our three reflections behind it, on the bench, a big grey man and a cairn with a flower in it and a woman with a flask. He looked at us in the glass for a long time.

"Here we see the Baseline Human," he said again, quieter, "at — at —"

He looked back over his shoulder at us.

"Dinner," said Hask, who would not have a thing go into the record unfinished.

"Home," said Bram, and turned back to the stars.

Then he leaned in and breathed on the glass. A big, foggy breath, all the way from the bottom of the one good set of lungs he was born with, onto the inside of a panel with nothing on the other side of it. The fog bloomed on the cold pane, silver against the dark. And in it, with one long grey finger, he drew, the way they draw at the Window on Tuesdays. A sun. A tomato. A face.

And a door, standing open.

Outside, the drone came round the ring on its suction feet and reached his panel, and stopped. Its little lamp came up and shone on the fog and the drawing and the boy behind them, for a long moment, the way you look at something you have never seen before and have no category for.

Bram waved.

Behind him, we went on arguing about dinner.


## Epilogue: Thursdays

> PERSON · HALE, BRAM. Son of Wenna Hale. Grandson of Ada Hale. REFERENCE No. 12 (hall closed). CHOOSING: EXERCISED. Care of: O. Pastern, Examiner.
>
> [in pencil, on the printout pinned over the Hale kitchen table, in a hand that presses very hard:] and Tillys
>
> — Register of Moriel, schema revision 59; copy brought down the long stair by the keeper
>

The crock is used to me now. That's why I still feed it.

Gran says it'd be used to anybody by this time, a crock isn't fussy, but Gran is wrong, because when Mistress Thorne fed it the week I had the spots it went flat and grey and sulked for two days, and when I came back it bit me. Not really bit. You lift the cloth and it goes hah at you, sour and appley and so strong it gets you in the back of the nose, and that's how you know it's happy. If it doesn't bite you back, it's sulking. Gran says that too. Gran says most things first.

So every morning I stand on the stool and give it its flour and its water and stir it with the long spoon, because Bram took the pencil. He stirred it with a pencil the whole time, which is disgusting, and now he stirs crocks in space with it, probably. I asked. He said there's no crock on the ship. He said Odile's Hask won't eat bread anyway, being stones, and Odile only drinks tea out of a flask, and Biscuit eats everything including a whole bag of something called Tally crackers and then was sick in the Chief Examiner's tin.

That's the best thing he's told us so far.

* * *

Thursday is tomatoes.

Saul shakes his tomatoes on Thursdays, every truss, tap tap with the end of his stick, so the flowers let go of their dust and make fruit. He's done it since before Ma was born. This summer his knee's bad, so he sits on an upturned bucket at the end of the row with his stick across his lap like a lord, and he says that one and that one and mind the leaf, and I do the tapping.

The three mounds in the end bed came up after all. They're my shoulder now. Bram put his hand on the dirt before he went and said they weren't dead, they were swelling, and Saul said not up, and Bram said only nearly. Saul tells everybody that. He tells it like it was his idea.

They smell green when you touch them. Not grass green. A sharp, furry, bitter green that stays on your fingers all day so you can smell your own hands at dinner and know where you've been. There's one tomato going red on the middle plant. It's been going red for a week. It's the colour of the inside of your eyelids when you look at the sun.

"Not yet," says Saul, before I've even asked.

"When?"

"When it's ready. Not when you are."

I tap the next truss. And then, because Bram's always going on about it now, in every relay, like he invented it, I look twice.

Under a leaf, at the bottom, nearly on the ground where nobody would look the first time, there's another one. Redder. All the way red. Warm when I put my palm round it, like a hen's egg.

"Saul," I say.

He leans and looks. He looks for a long time. Then he grunts, which is Saul for well I never.

"That one's ready," he says. "That one's been ready since Tuesday, I'd say." He sits back on his bucket. "Go on, then."

It comes off with a little snap and a puff of the green smell. I don't eat it. You'd think I would, but I don't. I hold it in two hands all the way up the lane like Bram holding the slate when the relay comes, as if it might spill.

* * *

The far door's open. It's always open now. Nobody's shut it since the hearing, not even when it rained sideways and the meeting house got a puddle in it the shape of a goat. Bet says it's a goat. It's a puddle.

People come through it. That's new as well. They walk down the long path from the towers and through Saul's east rows, careful, in their soft shining shoes, and stand in the square and look at us the way we used to look at them through the Window, except now there's no glass, so they don't know where to put their faces. There's a museum in the stone, now, about what retired meant. They read it and go quiet. Then they come out and buy Mistress Thorne's bread, which is the right order to do it in.

Some of them come from much further than the towers. Yesterday there was a man with skin like a plum and four eyes, two for looking near and two for far, and he looked at our bees with all four for an hour.

I do the guide for them, if they want. I do it in the voice. The one that used to wake up at nine o'clock and tell everyone about us.

"Here we see the Baseline Human," I say, "at the pump. Note the single stool. Note the traditional wooden spoon." And then I do my ordinary voice and tell them which bits were true and which bits were lies, because Odile said that's what the job is, mostly, and also about the time the sky-grazers fell on her.

And then I tell them the joke. What do you call a tomato that won't sing? And they never know, and I tell them (a tomato), and they laugh or they don't, and I say, "It's mine. My brother's telling it to the whole galaxy. So you have to say it's mine." The man with four eyes laughed with all of his face at once, which is a lot of face. He said he'd tell it on his world. He said it's very cold there and they'd never heard of a tomato.

"Look twice," I told him. "There's always one more under a leaf."

* * *

Tuesdays I go up the long stair with Dr. Ommery.

She's the keeper, so she always went up, but she never used to take anybody. Now she takes me, because of Thirteen, and because I asked her every single day for eleven days until she said, "Tilly Hale, you are your grandmother made small," which I think was yes.

The amber room is warm and smells like the inside of an egg. Most of the tanks are empty now. They're being taken out. There's only his.

He's bigger. He was the size of a loaf when Bram talked to him, and now he's the size of two loaves, or a cat, and his fingers are less webbed, and once, while I was talking, he turned all the way over in the water like Biscuit in the mill race and stuck his foot up against the glass. A whole foot. Five toes. I counted, the way Ma counts.

There's a little round port in the side with a brass rim, warm, where the nurses take their samples, and if you put your mouth right up to it and talk, he can hear you. Dr. Ommery says sound goes better through water than through air, and better still through glass and bone. That's how Bram heard me through the Window that time, with his head against it, without the glass even being open. I tried to get her to explain it and she said, "What did you learn today?" which is what she says instead of explaining things, so I have to think of it myself.

I talk to him because Bram promised he'd keep talking to him, and Bram's away, so I'm doing Bram's share. Bram owes me. I've told him. He says he knows.

Today I tell Thirteen about the tomato under the leaf. And about the bees, how Ma says lemon balm is come home and clover is food and pear drops is cross, and that Ma doesn't wear gloves because they make her hands stupid. And about the stars, because nobody in Firstfield ever saw a star till this spring, and now we see them every night that isn't cloudy, real clouds, ones nobody chose, and Master Aldous takes the whole school out on the green with blankets and says the names, and some of them he doesn't know, so we make them up, and he writes the made-up ones in a book. I tell Thirteen that one's called the Crock, and one's called Saul's Stick, and one little fuzzy one is called Gerald, after the jar creature, because it looks like it has legs.

"And when you come out," I tell him, "you'll know my voice already. And you'll go, oh, her."

His foot comes up against the glass again.

On the way down the long stair Dr. Ommery says, "Well? What did you learn today?"

I think about it all the way to the bottom. It's a long stair.

"That if you think something's already ready," I say, "you look anyway. And if you think it isn't, you look again."

Dr. Ommery stops on the bottom step and looks at me over her spectacles on their chain.

"That's two things," she says.

"It's one thing," I say. "Twice."

* * *

The relay comes after supper. Three flat notes, like bacon frying, and everybody runs for the meeting house, where the keeper's set it up on the elders' bench under the twelve plaques.

His voice comes out of it. His real one, his own, a bit rough at the edges because the relay crackles like paper in a stove, but his. Not the hissing one. I still do the hissing one for Jory sometimes when Ma isn't listening. Ma doesn't like it. Jory loves it.

"Tilly?" he says. "Is Tilly there?"

"I found a tomato under a leaf," I say, before anybody else can say anything. "Saul didn't see it. I looked twice."

There's a pause the length of a fold. Then he laughs. The donkey one. The surprised one. It's the only bit of him that never changed, not even when his whole face did, and everyone on the benches laughs as well, because you can't not.

He tells us he's on a world that's mostly ocean, with islands that walk. Not walk. Drift. But slowly enough you can walk with them. He says the people there sing to their fish, and the fish sing back, and he listened through the hull with his hand flat, and it was arguing. He says he found somewhere that's never heard of a tomato.

"Did you plant one?" says Saul, from the back, loud.

"Three," says Bram. "First knuckle deep and not a hair more."

Saul grunts. Saul for well I never, and also for something else that makes him wipe his nose.

Behind Bram, very small and far, I hear Odile say, "Tell them the case is still open," and another voice like a cartload of gravel saying, "It does not specify a place," which is Hask, and I don't know what it means, but Bram says, "He means he's happy," so that's all right.

Gran says, "Tell him to take a coat."

"I've got a coat, Gran."

"Take another."

* * *

Saul showed me how you save tomato seeds, after. You'd think you just dry them, but you don't. You squeeze the wet bit into a jar, seeds and all, and add water, and leave it on the sill with a cloth over, and it goes mouldy and smells so bad Gran made me put it outside. Saul says the seeds come in little coats of jelly, to stop them growing inside the tomato, and the rot eats the coats off. Then the good seeds sink, and the no-good ones float, and you tip the floaters away and keep the sinkers and dry them on paper by the stove.

"Nothing grows," says Saul, "that hasn't had a bit of a rot first."

Mine have been three days. They've sunk. They're drying on the paper now, pale and furry, like tiny flat moths.

I'm going to fold them up in a paper twist, tight, the way Saul does, so it rattles.

Jory's had a bag under his bed since spring. Packed. Bet told everyone, because Bet tells everyone everything, that's what she's for. He says it's just to see if it fits. He's said that all summer. It's a very small bag and it must fit by now.

Nobody knows when his Choosing comes, and you're not allowed to ask. It's his. That's the whole point of it, Gran says. That's what they painted over, and that's what Odile scraped back, and now it's in the Register as well, in letters a bit brighter than the rest. Keep, or go and see.

But I know which way he'll go. He stands at the far door every night with his hands in his pockets, kicking a stone, looking at the stars we made up names for. Bet you he goes.

So when the seeds are dry, I'm going to put the twist in the bottom of his bag, under his other shirt, where he won't find it till he gets there. Wherever there is. And when he finds it, he'll have to find somewhere that's never heard of a tomato.

And then look twice.

And tell everybody they're mine.


## Appendix: A Map of the Going and Seeing

> Drawn fair for the schoolroom, after the fashion of the old road scrolls, which are read from right to left. Annotated in crayon by a member of the class, without permission.
>
> — note pinned beside the map, Firstfield schoolhouse
>

The map is one long scroll. The first picture shows all of it; the four after it unroll it an arm's width at a time, from the right-hand end, where the road begins.

* * *

#### Firstfield

Heritage village of the Moriel Preserve, under a dome of glass. About two hundred people (the Register says 211); forty-one houses; eight generations.

A village kept the old way, so that the people of Moriel can watch the past do its chores from the skywalks above, which the villagers call the Shining. There is a bakehouse with a crock older than anyone, a schoolhouse with one room, a meeting house with a long stair behind it, Saul's rows at the east end, and the Window, where the children breathe on the glass on Tuesdays and draw. Nobody taps the glass.

> [in crayon:] we live here. it is the best one
>

#### The Spill

Wild country beyond the farm cut, forty kilometres north of the Preserve. Ridges that go purple at dusk.

Where the gold grain stops at a line as straight as a knife, the wild begins: fur-bark trees, a dark quick stream, a ravine, and a green-gold pond whose reeds lean away from warmth. Sky-grazers drift over it at dusk, long and pale, floating on their own gas. Lately they have been falling.

> [in crayon:] the goats FLOAT. bram says
>

#### The Undercroft

Fungal caverns under the ridges. Cold, dripping and very large.

A dark country under the ground, lit blue-green by gills of fungus taller than the meeting house, where a network of threads carries messages for miles. Lantern-otters live here, six-legged, with glowing throats, and they will steal your lunch.

> [in crayon:] Biscuit came from here
>

#### The Hiveplains

Former pollination plantations. Owner lapsed.

Rolling meadows of engineered flowers, hives the size of barns, bees the length of a thumb, and air thick with gold dust and humming. The Register says everything here is steady.

#### The Drowned Orchards

Tidal forest of flooded orchard stock on the north-west coast. The Register lists it as uninhabited.

Fruit trees standing on stilt roots in water that rises and falls twice a day. In the canopy, on platforms of woven branch and sailcloth joined by rope bridges, is the village of Highwater, which the Register did not know about. Its people climb like breathing and dive for minutes at a time, and they do not trust anyone who smells of the dome.

> [in crayon:] SEDGE (!!)
>

#### The Pale

The gold grain plains. Company country: stations, drones and irrigation spines to the horizon.

One crop from edge to edge, and the place where the drift began. There are rust-coloured patches now, and seed that will not set. Out in it stands an old station the colour of bone, with a pond beside it that nobody has looked at in sixty years.

#### The High Gardens

Cloud forest on the ridges above the Preserve. Where the sky-grazers breed.

The most beautiful place on the map and the most dangerous. By day the cloud climbs the ridge, and at dusk the cold air pours back down. In the mist, sound lies about direction and smell does not. A waterfall turns to mist before it lands. The white towers of the Upper House stand up out of it like the bones of a hand.

#### Margin Station

Off the edge of this map.

A station of about eleven thousand people, nine hours away by hop, and home of the Inspectorate of Discrepancies. Ring Two has a viewport with a sign on it.

> [in crayon:] and then UP
>


## Appendix: Persons of Record

> PERSON.MAKERLINE: free text. What the person answers to "Who wrote you?" No validation is applied. Clerks need not query it.
>
> — Register of Moriel, data dictionary
>

Entries are given as they stood when each person first came to the Examiner's notice. Later corrections are in the book.

* * *

#### HALE, BRAM

Firstfield. Reference No. 12. Maker, line: not yet asked.

A boy of Firstfield who was called up the long stair and did not stay. Quick, hungry, and in trouble most of the time; his laugh, according to his best friend, sounds like a donkey being surprised. He carries a paper twist of Saul's tomato seeds and plants a few wherever he goes, in case wherever he's going hasn't got proper ones.

> [in crayon:] my brother. he spells my name right
>

#### PASTERN, ODILE

Examiner, Inspectorate of Discrepancies, Margin Station. Eleven years' service; before that, six years with the Survey. Maker, line: not yet asked.

Her job is the places where the record and the world disagree. Small, fast and routinely underestimated, she can put a man on the floor and finish citing the regulation before he finishes falling. She carries a battered steel flask of black tea, very strong and always too hot, and a pad of blank paper forms, because paper doesn't need charging.

> "Nobody. I'm the plain kind." "There's no plain kind." — overheard at Highwater
>

#### HASK

Orrun. Proxy, on mandate from a notary now deceased. Three hundred and eleven years in service. Maker, line: not enumerated.

A cairn of carved stones, taller than a door, who walks. He is the bearer of the Third Schedule, a list of four thousand one hundred and twelve harms, and he may act only to prevent a harm that is on it. His stones are counted from the bottom. There is lichen on the fourth, and between the seventh and the eighth, a seed about ninety years old.

> [pressed into the paper, without ink:] ITEM 14: SCALDING BY BEVERAGE.
>

#### BISCUIT

Lantern-otter. Undercroft-born. Maker, line: declined to answer; ate the form.

Sleek, six-legged, with a throat-sac that glows when it is pleased or about to steal something. Wild, nonverbal and motivated almost entirely by food. Translated, with low confidence: "It says you smell like a disappointing fruit. It also says it will die for you."

#### THE SYMBIONT

Of the Spill. Resident, at present, in Bram Hale. Maker, line: unknown.

A voice from just behind Bram's eyes, and a faint green-gold tracery under the skin of his wrist, like the veins of a leaf. It can change his body, but only the parts he can name. It is curious, hungry and honest, and it has been alone for a long time.

#### HALE, TILLY

Firstfield. Bram's sister. Maker, line: not yet asked.

Small, sharp and proprietary. She feeds the bakehouse crock every morning, standing on a stool, and owns a joke about a tomato that she will tell you whether you like it or not.

> [in crayon:] THIS IS ME. the joke is MINE
>

#### HALE, WENNA

Firstfield. Bram and Tilly's mother. Keeps the bees and the mill's books.

Never wears gloves at the hives, because they make her hands stupid. Talks to the bees in smells. "Hands first. Worry after."

#### HALE, ADA

Firstfield. Gran.

Bram's father's mother, keeper of the mother crock and of more proverbs than anyone has counted. Her chest is bad. Count to five hundred before you peek at the bread. "If it doesn't bite you back, it's sulking."

#### SAUL

Firstfield. Tomatoes.

Old, slow and right. He taps every truss with his stick on Thursdays and plants first knuckle deep and not a hair more. Says one thing, and says it often: "Look twice."

#### JORY

Firstfield. Bram's best friend.

Grins like a fence with planks out. Has never once said anything to Bram that wasn't a dare. "Bet you won't."

#### ALDOUS, MASTER

Firstfield. Schoolmaster.

Owns the only watch in Firstfield and winds it every morning while the children say their names. Smells of chalk and lamp oil and keeps pear drops in a drawer for the brave. "Name it and you can hold it."

#### GERALD

Firstfield schoolhouse. Resident, in a jar.

Many legs, feelers, back plates and hooked feet. Named by Tilly. Walks up the inside of his jar during lessons.

#### OMMERY, DR. MARGIT

Firstfield. Village keeper, doctor and teacher of medicine. Twenty-two years going up and down the long stair.

Old, slow and grey, with reading spectacles on a chain. Smells of tea and of the stuff she cleans cuts with. Asks every child the same question, every time: "What did you learn today?"

#### SEDGE

Highwater. Maker, line: "Us."

At home in the canopy and the sea: climbs like breathing, dives for minutes, ties a knot in one movement and three heartbeats. Freckles that darken in the sun like bark. Unimpressed by boys from domes, and the first person ever to say "Bet you won't" to Bram before he could say it to her.

#### TEASEL

Highwater. Maker, line: "We did."

Tall, long-fingered, grey-bearded, with a white scar down from one ear and a flat punt with a carved heron at the bow. Distrusts anything that smells of the dome. Does not give people pears.

#### LENNA

Highwater. Maker, line: "I did. Sixty-two years. I'd like the credit."

Small, brown as a nut, white hair cropped short, one eye clouded, and a laugh like a gate. Knows every Firstfield saying, in older versions than Firstfield does.

#### VARRO, LISLE

Executive, Varro-Tandem Bioreference. Maker, line: "My mother. And twenty years of the Moriel Bioreference Index."

The man in the colour-changing coat. Grey, close-cropped, with the face of a clerk you would trust, he holds his hands open the way you approach a horse. His coat was bred for beekeepers, to show the hive whether you are frightened. It reads his skin. He stopped hiding from it in his thirties.

#### PENHALLOW, DIRECTOR

Director, Moriel Preserve.

Runs the Preserve from the Upper House, in Heritage Service green, with her silver hair set and her slate on her knee. When something happens that should not, she looks for the regulation that says so. "This is highly irregular."

#### PELL, ASSOCIATE

Recovery Associate, Varro-Tandem Bioreference. Licensed genome and memory, issued in numbered copies (Pell-4, Pell-7, Pell-12 and others). Maker, line: disputed. Pell-4 could not decide which copy did.

The same face, as many times as the company can afford, and never all in agreement. The copies bicker, outrank one another by turns, and file grievances: against each other, against the Examiner, and in one case against an otter.

#### HALLORAN, CHIEF EXAMINER

Inspectorate of Discrepancies, Margin Station.

Mostly a voice on a crackling relay: dry, gravelly, calls her "Pastern". Eats sunflower seeds and spits the shells into a tin you can hear. Hands out impossible cases. Gives her ten days and no budget, which is how he says he trusts her.

#### FERRIER, MAUD

Surveyor. One of the founders, witnessed at the Landing. Maker, line: "Ink on her hands, and she sang flat." (given on her behalf)

Wrote the preface at the front of this book.
